Symplectic monodromy at radius zero and equimultiplicity of μ \mu -constant families Thanks: J.F.B was supported by the Spanish Ministry of Science, Innovation and Universities, project reference MTM2016-76868-C2-1-P (UCM), J.F.B. and T. P. were supported by the Basque Government through the BERC 2018-2021 program, and Gobierno Vasco Grant IT1094-16, by the Spanish Ministry of Science, Innovation and Universities BCAM Severo Ochoa accreditation SEV-2017-0718, by the Spanish Ministry of Science, Innovation and Universities, project reference PID2020-114750GB-C33, and by CIRM Jean Morlet Semester Singularity Theory from Modern Perspectives .
Javier Fernández de Bobadilla, Tomasz Pełka

Abstract
. We show that every family of isolated hypersurface singularities with constant Milnor number has constant multiplicity. To achieve this, we endow the A’Campo model of “radius zero” monodromy with a symplectic structure. This new approach allows to generalize a spectral sequence of McLean converging to fixed point Floer homology of iterates of the monodromy to a more general setting which is well suited to study μ \mu -constant families.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14B05, 14J17, 32S25, 32S30, 32S55, 53D40

1.
Introduction

Let
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[\![z_{1},...,z_{n}]\!]
be a formal power series. We write
f=\sum_{\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}a_{\iota}z^{\iota}
with the usual multi-index notation. The
multiplicity
of
f
is the biggest number
\nu(f)
such that
f\in\mathfrak{m}^{\nu(f)}
, where
\mathfrak{m}
is the maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{C}}[\![x_{1},...,x_{n}]\!]
. The Milnor number of
f
is
\mu(f)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[\![z_{1},\dots,z_{n}]\!]/\langle\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial z_{1}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial z_{n}}\rangle
, see
[
Mil68
, §7]
. A
continuous family of power series
parametrized by
t\in[0,1]
assigns to each
t
a power series
f_{t}=\sum_{\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}a_{\iota}z^{\iota}
in such a way that each coefficient
a_{\iota}(t)
is continuous in
t
.

The main result of this article is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(f_{t})
be a continuous family of power series. If the Milnor number
\mu(f_{t})
is independent of
t
and finite then the multiplicity
\nu(f_{t})
is also independent of
t
.

Theorem
1.1
implies a positive answer to Zariski Multiplicity Question for the case of families of isolated singularities, see Corollary
1.3
. A brief survey on Zariski Question is given in Section
1.3
: for more extensive ones we refer to
[
Eyr07
,
Eyr16
]
or
[
FdB22
]
.

1.1.
Summary of our approach

The starting point of our approach towards proving Theorem
1.1
is a result of McLean
[
McL19
, Corollary 1.4]
, which characterizes the multiplicity of an isolated singularity as the minimal integer
m\geqslant 1
such that a version
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{f}^{m},+)
of the fixed point Floer cohomology of the
m
-th iterate of the symplectic monodromy
\phi_{f}\colon\mathbb{F}_{f}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{f}
of the Milnor fibration does not vanish. Here,
\mathbb{F}_{f}
denotes the symplectic Milnor fiber (the precise definition of
(\mathbb{F}_{f},\phi_{f})
requires some care, but we skip this subtlety for the sake of clarity at the introduction). To prove Theorem
1.1
, we compare the groups
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{f_{t}}^{m},+)
, for different members
f_{t}
of a
\mu
-constant family. We will now outline what is the key difficulty of this approach, why the setting of
[
McL19
]
is not sufficient to cover it, and how our new method allows to overcome this obstacle.

Let
f
be a germ of an isolated hypersurface singularity. It is known that the pair
(\mathbb{F}_{f},\phi_{f})
is a Liouville domain with a compactly supported exact symplectomorphism
[
McL19
]
. Any two such pairs which are symplectically isotopic (in a certain sense) give rise to the same Floer cohomology, see
[
McL19
, Lemma B.3]
or Proposition
6.2
. So, the natural approach to prove Theorem
1.1
would be to prove that, for small
t>0
, the pair
(\mathbb{F}_{f_{t}},\phi_{f_{t}})
is symplectically isotopic to
(\mathbb{F}_{f_{0}},\phi_{f_{0}})
. However, even proving that
\mathbb{F}_{f_{t}}
and
\mathbb{F}_{f_{0}}
are isotopic as Liouville domains seems hard. The main reason is that it is not known whether the Milnor radius
\varepsilon_{t}
for
f_{t}
can be chosen independent of
t
: in principle, we could have
\varepsilon_{t}\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow 0
. This obstructs the direct comparison of Milnor fibrations for
f_{t}
and
f_{0}
.

In
[
McL19
]
, McLean uses an equivalence relation which is weaker than isotopy. Namely, to each pair
(\mathbb{F},\phi)
as above, a construction of Giroux
[
Gir02
,
Gir20
]
associates a pair
L_{\phi}\subseteq S_{\phi}
of contact manifolds. Now,
[
McL19
, Appendix B]
, roughly speaking, shows that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi^{m},+)
depends only on this associated
contact pair
. For a symplectic monodromy of the Milnor fiber,
[
McL19
]
shows that this pair is the link
f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, where
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
is a Milnor radius, with the standard contact structure introduced by Varchenko
[
Var80
]
. This provides the basis for the main theorem of
[
McL19
]
, asserting that the multiplicity only depends on the embedded contact type of the link.

However, the above mentioned McLean’s main result cannot be easily applied in our setting, due to the radius degeneration problem explained in the paragraph above. Indeed, let
(f_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a
\mu
-constant family, and let
\varepsilon_{t}
be a Milnor radius of
f_{t}
. Fix
0<t\ll 1
and choose
\varepsilon_{t}<\varepsilon_{0}
. Then

(1)

(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{t}}},\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{t}}}\cap f_{t}^{-1}(0))

defines a symplectic cobordism between the contact pair of
f_{t}
and a contact pair which is contactomorphic to the one for
f_{0}
. This cobordism is known to be topologically trivial if
n\geqslant 4

[
LR76
]
, but proving its symplectic triviality seems hard.

In order to explain our approach let us briefly recall known facts about the Milnor fibration. Let
f\colon({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)\longrightarrow({\mathbb{C}},0)
be a holomorphic function germ. A classical result
[
Mil68
]
asserts that there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and any
0<\delta\ll\varepsilon
the restrictions

(2)

\frac{f}{|f|}\colon\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon}\setminus f^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}

and

(3)

f\colon\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}

are locally trivial fibrations. The first one (called Milnor fibration
in the sphere
) is smoothly equivalent to the restriction of the second one (called Milnor fibration
in the tube
) to
\operatorname{Int}\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta})
. The numbers
\varepsilon,\delta
are called
Milnor radii
for the ball and the disc, respectively. If
f
has an isolated singularity at the origin then the monodromy of the above fibrations can be chosen compactly supported.

The closures of the fibers of both (
2
) and (
3
) are Liouville domains for the standard Liouville form
-\frac{\pi}{2}d^{c}||z||^{2}
. For the fibration (
2
) McLean associates a monodromy
\phi_{f}:\mathbb{F}_{f}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{f}
which is a compactly supported exact symplectomorphism and studies the Floer cohomology of the pair
(\mathbb{F}_{f},\phi_{f})
.

In turn, we note that the fibration (
3
) will not be, in general, locally trivial in a symplectic sense, since there is no reason why the volume of the fibers should be constant in
z\in\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\delta}
. Nonetheless, in Example
5.22
we fix this issue by slightly modifying the shape of the ball
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
.

In
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
McLean provides a spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{f}^{m},+)
, whose first page allows to compute the multiplicity of
f
. This first page is closely related to the A’Campo formula
[
A’C75
]
for the Lefschetz numbers and zeta function of
\phi_{f}^{m}
, which we now recall.

Let
h\colon X\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be a log resolution of
f
that only modifies
\mathbb{C}^{n}
over the origin. Write
(f{\circ}h)^{-1}(0)=\sum_{i=0}^{N}m_{i}D_{i}
, where
D_{0}
is the strict transform of
f^{-1}(0)
. Then the function
f{\circ}h
can be expressed in local coordinates
(z_{1},...,z_{n})
as
\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}^{m_{i}}
.
Denote by
\tau:\mathbb{D}_{\log}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
the real oriented blow up at the origin. Then
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log}:=\tau^{-1}(0)
can be thought as a circle
“at radius 0”
. Exploiting the local structure of
f{\circ}h
, A’Campo constructed a topological locally trivial fibration

(4)

f_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\log}

whose restriction over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
coincides with (
3
) and whose restriction

(5)

f_{A}|_{f_{A}^{-1}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log})}\colon f_{A}^{-1}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log})\longrightarrow\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log}

over the radius
0
circle admits a monodromy
\phi_{A}
such that the fixed point set of
\phi_{A}^{m}
is equal to

(6)

\bigsqcup_{m_{i}|m}B_{i},

where
B_{i}
is a cyclic covering of degree
m_{i}
of
D_{i}\setminus(\bigcup_{j\neq i}D_{i})
determined by
f
, cf.
[
DL02
, §2.3]
. Therefore, we have the equality of Lefschetz numbers

(7)

\Lambda(\phi_{f}^{m})=\sum_{m_{i}|m}\chi(B_{i}),

where
\chi(\,\cdot\,)
denotes the topological Euler characteristic. Notice that the summand
\chi(B_{0})
appears for every
m
, but it is equal to
0
, since
B_{0}
is homeomorphic to
(f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon_{0}})\times(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
by the conical structure theorem
[
Mil68
, Theorem 2.10]
, and
\chi(f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon_{0}})=0
since
f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon_{0}}
is an odd-dimensional, smooth manifold. At this stage, we would also like to remark that the space
A
constructed by A’Campo is a manifold
with corners
, and
f_{A}
has no further structure than the topological locally trivial fibration.

As we will explain below, the main step of our proof is to endow
A
with a smooth structure, and define a fiberwise symplectic form on
A
such that
\phi_{A}
is realized as the symplectic lift of the angular vector field on
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log}
. In particular, the flow of the symplectic lift of the radial vector field on
\mathbb{D}_{\log}
provides a
symplectic isotopy
between
\phi_{A}
and the monodromy of the Milnor fibration (
3
).

In turn, McLean in
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
constructs a certain Liouville domain
\mathbb{F}
with a compactly supported exact symplectomorphism
\phi
, whose dynamics coincides with the one of
\phi_{A}
; and whose associated contact pair is contactomorphic to the link of
f
. Hence even though
(\mathbb{F}_{f},\phi_{f})
and
(\mathbb{F},\phi)
might not be symplectically isotopic, one still has that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi^{m},+)=\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{f}^{m},+)
is the required Floer cohomology. Now, given such
(\mathbb{F},\phi)
, the first step to define
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi,+)
is to deform
\phi
slightly by a Hamiltonian near
\partial\mathbb{F}
so that the deformed
\check{\phi}
has no fixed points in a collar near
\partial\mathbb{F}
. Therefore, the fixed point set of the deformed McLean monodromy
\check{\phi}^{m}
is of the form

(8)

\bigsqcup_{m_{i}|m,\ i\neq 0}B_{i}

where
B_{i}
have the same properties as above. Note that the term
B_{0}
got removed by the deformation near
\partial\mathbb{F}
. This allows to construct a spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi,+)
whose
E_{1}
-pieces are the ordinary homologies of the components
B_{i}
for
m_{i}|m
,
i\neq 0
, see
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
. Since
\chi(\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi^{m},+))=\Lambda(\phi^{m})
, this result may be regarded as a lifting of the A’Campo formula (
7
) to the setting of Floer cohomology.

Since the component
B_{0}
is
not present
in (
8
), the
E_{1}
-page is empty for
m
is strictly smaller than the multiplicity
\nu(f)
. It is also easy to show that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi^{m},+)\neq 0
for
m=\nu(f)
.

To have a grasp of why the deformation
\check{\phi}
can be performed so that the component
B_{0}
is not present, observe that, exploiting the topological product structure
B_{0}\cong(f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon})\times(0,\varepsilon]
, where
\varepsilon
is the Milnor radius of
f
, the monodromy homeomorphism
\phi_{A}
constructed by A’Campo can be isotoped to a homeomorphism
\check{\phi}_{A}
that eliminates the fixed point component
B_{0}
and leaves the other ones. Now for a sufficiently small Milnor radius
\varepsilon>0
,
B_{0}
becomes symplectically trivial, that is, a piece of a symplectization
(f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon})\times(0,\varepsilon)
of the contact link. This allows to realize the above isotopy in the symplectic category, see
[
McL19
, p. 1023]
.

The Milnor fibration in the tube (
3
) is better suited to study families, in particular
\mu
-constant ones. Indeed, let
F:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi}
be holomorphic of the form
F(x,t)=(f_{t}(x),t)
such that
f_{t}
has a
\mu
-constant isolated singularity at the origin. Then, in
[
LR76
]
it is proved that there exits Milnor radii
\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}
for
f_{0}
such that, after shrinking
\xi>0
, the restriction

(9)

F\colon(\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi})\cap F^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{0}}^{*}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{0}}^{*}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi}

is a locally trivial fibration. So, fixed any
t\in\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\xi}
and any
\delta_{t}<\delta_{0}
, the restriction

(10)

f_{t}\colon\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\cap f_{t}^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{t}}^{*})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{t}}^{*}

is diffeomorphic to the Milnor fibration in the tube for
f_{0}
. Now we pick
\varepsilon_{t}<\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta_{t}<\delta_{0}
, Milnor radii for
f_{t}
. Then, for this particular choice of radii, the Milnor fibration in the tube for
f_{t}
is embedded in the fibration (
10
), and according with
[
LR76
]
, for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{t}}
the difference between the fiber of (
10
) over
z
and the Milnor fiber
f_{t}(z)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{t}}
of
f_{t}
over
z
is a cobordism diffeomorphic to (
1
). Moreover, the monodromy of (
10
) can be chosen with support at the interior of
f_{t}^{-1}(z)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{t}}
. Thus in our case, the component
B_{0}
of the fixed point set (
6
) of
\phi_{A}
corresponds to the cobordism (
1
), which is not known to be symplectically trivial. So,
B_{0}
cannot be eliminated by a small Hamiltonian perturbation.

Our proof of Theorem
1.1

can be summarized in the following steps:

1.

We modify the fibration (
9
) so that it becomes symplectically locally trivial and maintains the topological properties stated above. This is a matter of modifying the shape of the domain
(\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi})\cap F^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{0}}^{*}\times\mathbb{D}_{\xi})
in such a way that the fibers can be symplectomorphic, in particular have constant volume. As a consequence, for each
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\xi}
we define a pair
(\mathbb{F}_{t},\phi_{t})
formed by a Liouville domain and an exact symplectomorphism, whose topology coincides with the monodromy of the fibration (
10
), and such that for any small
t>0
, the pairs
(\mathbb{F}_{t},\phi_{t}^{m})
and
(\mathbb{F}_{0},\phi_{0}^{m})
are symplectically isotopic. In particular,
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{t}^{m},+)\cong\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{0}^{m},+)
for every
m
.

2.

For each fixed
t
, we produce a symplectic version of A’Campo construction. That is, we construct a symplectically locally trivial fibration (
4
) satisfying the following two properties.

(a)

The restrictions of
f_{A}
and of the fibration (
9
) coincide symplectically over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\setminus\mathbb{D}_{\delta^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{D}_{\log}
for a certain
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta
,

(b)

The monodromy of (
5
) induced by the lifting of the unit vector field in
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\log}
has exactly the same dynamical properties as the topological monodromy constructed by A’Campo.

We call this monodromy the
symplectic monodromy at radius zero
and denote it by
(\mathbb{F}^{0}_{t},\phi_{t}^{0})
. Property
(a)
gives an isotopy between
(\mathbb{F}_{t},\phi_{t})
and
(\mathbb{F}^{0}_{t},\phi_{t}^{0})
, so we have
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi_{t}^{m},+)\cong\operatorname{HF}^{*}((\phi_{t}^{0})^{m},+)
for any
m
.

As we explain below, this step gets rather involved, and creates a technique that may be useful for other degeneration problems in algebraic geometry. Our construction is, in fact, performed in a much broader generality, covering e.g. the case when
f
is a function from Stein space
Y
, and both
Y
and
f^{-1}(0)
can have many isolated singularities.

3.

We slightly generalize McLean’s spectral sequence in such a way that the component
B_{0}
, corresponding to the cobordism (
1
), is taken into account. The new terms appearing in the first page of the spectral sequence are summands of the Borel-Moore homology
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{0},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. They vanish because the cobordism (
1
) is homologically trivial. Thus we get the same
E_{1}
page as
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
, hence the same characterization of multiplicity – now for the Milnor radius
\varepsilon_{0}
fixed within the family. This result can also be stated in more generality indicated in Step
2
above, see Propositions
7.1
,
7.4
.

Our proof does not use Giroux construction
[
Gir02
,
Gir20
]
or McLean result
[
McL19
, (HF2)]
asserting that Floer homology depends only on the associated contact link. We directly deform the natural symplectic monodromy arising from the Milnor fibration in the tube (
3
) to a radius zero monodromy. This way, for
any
fixed radius we get a model
(\mathbb{F}_{A},\phi_{A})
which is actually isotopic to the fibration
(\mathbb{F}_{f},\phi_{f})
at that fixed radius, and has dynamical properties which allow to derive McLean spectral sequence. This is done without relying on most constructions in
[
McL19
]
.

As a byproduct of our construction, in Corollary
7.9
we produce, for any
singular
hypersurface germ, a representative of its monodromy in the symplectic mapping class group of its Milnor fiber, which has no fixed points. We also produce one whose fixed point set is precisely the boundary of the Milnor fiber. This extends McLean results, who produces models with the same properties, but instead of being symplectically isotopic to the monodromy, they just share the same contact pairs.

1.2.
Outline

We will now indicate the sections of the article where each of the above steps is realized.

The technical core of the proof lies in Step
2
, that is, in the construction of the symplectic structure on the A’Campo space
A
. It is carried out in Sections
3
and
4
. Definition
3.3
of the space
A
can be explained in two steps. As before, let
h\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
be a log resolution of
f
, and let
D=\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{i}
be the total transform of
f^{-1}(0)
. First, we perform a real oriented blowup
X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
which extends the fibration over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
to one over
\mathbb{D}_{\log}
, see (
4
). This can be phrased conveniently in terms of Kato–Nakayama construction
[
KN99
]
in log geometry, see
[
Cau16
,
CFP21
]
for applications in this context. The resulting monodromy has the required behavior over each
D_{i}
, but is not continuous over the intersections, see Example
4.16
. To remedy this problem, we multiply the preimage of each intersection
\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}
by a corresponding face
\Delta_{I}
of the dual complex of
D
. This face is parametrized by
tropical coordinates

w_{i}
, see (
18
), which measure the relative speed of convergence toward each
D_{i}
. The idea of such a
hybrid
construction, combining classical and tropical coordinates, goes back to
[
Ber71
]
, and has been successfully applied to various degeneration problems, see e.g.
[
BJ17
,
EM21
]
. For our application, we need to push this idea further.

Indeed, the space
A
constructed above is a manifold with corners. We need to choose its smoothing in a very careful way, which allows to introduce a fiberwise symplectic form satisfying restrictive properties
(a)
–
(b)
above. To do this, we introduce smooth charts (
36
) in such a way that both radial coordinates
r_{i}=|z_{i}|
of
X
; and tropical coordinates
w_{i}
of the simplices in
\partial A
, decay exponentially
towards their zero loci. This way, the local coordinate
r_{i}
corresponding to a component
D_{i}
gets replaced by a global smooth function
\overline{v}_{i}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
measuring, in a sense, a
“hybrid”
distance to
D_{i}
. Using these functions in (
44
) we provide an explicit formula for the symplectic form. The resulting monodromy
\phi_{A}|_{\partial A}
at radius zero is then a time one flow of the vector field given by the formula (
48
).

The guiding principle of the technical proofs in Sections
3
–
4
is that all choices made along the construction become irrelevant at radius zero. Hence a local computation can be used to prove that at radius zero,
\phi_{A}
is a rotation by
\tfrac{2\pi}{m_{i}}
about
D_{i}
, and an interpolating Dehn twist in between, see Figure
4(b)
. This way, we get a symplectic representative of the monodromy as required in
(a)
and
(b)
.

Step
1
is addressed in Section
5
, where we recall the necessary notions from the theory of Liouville domains and their fibrations
[
Gir20
,
Sei08
]
. In particular, in Definition
5.2
we recall McLean’s notion of graded abstract contact open book
[
McL19
]
, i.e. pair
(\mathbb{F},\phi)
as above, for which
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi,+)
is defined. Then, we describe a procedure which makes a symplectic monodromy into a graded abstract contact open book, see Definition
5.8
. Our setting is closely related to
Liouville fibrations with singularities
considered in
[
Sei08
]
, see Remark
5.15
.

In Section
6
we recall the definition of Floer cohomology groups
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(\phi,+)
. To be precise, we will use Floer
homology
introduced by Uljarevic in
[
Ulj17
]
, cf.
[
Sei01
]
: in Section
6.2.6
we explain how to compare it with the setting of
[
McL19
]
. In Proposition
6.3
, we prove a slight generalization of McLean spectral sequence
[
McL19
, (HF3)]
to the setting which includes the fixed-point component
B_{0}
. In Proposition
7.1
, we apply this spectral sequence to the monodromy
\phi_{A}
constructed in Step
2
, using its dynamical properties listed in Proposition
7.4
. This way, we get a spectral sequence similar to
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
, but with term
B_{0}
taken into account. Theorem
1.1
is deduced in Section
8
.

In order to make the article more accessible to readers who are not specialists in symplectic geometry, we try to be reasonably self-contained at various points.

1.3.
A brief history of the Zariski multiplicity conjecture

In his seminal paper
[
Zar71
]
, Zariski posed eight questions which had a major impact on the current shape of singularity theory. Out of these questions, the only one which remains open is the following.

Question 1.2
.

Let
f,g\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
be holomorphic germs of the same topological type. Is it true that
f
and
g
have the same multiplicity?

Here,
f,g
are said to be
of the same topological type
if there exists a germ of a homeomorphism
\Phi\colon({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)\longrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)
such that
\Phi(V(f))=V(g)
, where
V(f)
denotes the zero set of
f
.

If the above homeomorphism
\Phi
is assumed to be
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, then the affirmative answer to Question
1.2
was given by Ephraim
[
Eph76
]
, and, independently, by Trotman
[
Tro80
]
. The answer is also known to be positive for
n=2
, i.e. for plane curve singularities
[
Zar32
]
, and if one of the germs is smooth
[
A’C75
,
Lê73
]
: in fact, the latter result follows from the A’Campo formula (
7
) for the Lefschetz number. It is also true if
n=3
and one of the germs has multiplicity two, see Navarro-Aznar
[
Nav80
]
. If
n=3
and the homeomorphism
\Phi
is additionally bilipschitz, then a positive answer to Question
1.2
was given in
[
FdBFS18
, Theorem 3.4(1)]
by Fernandes, Sampaio and the first author.

Assume that
n=3
, and both
f
and
g
are germs of isolated surface singularities. Then, the answer to Question
1.2
is known to be positive if both
f,g
are quasihomogeneous (Xu–Yau
[
XY89
,
Yau88
]
); if one has arithmetic genus at most two (Yau
[
Yau89
]
), or geometric genus at most
3
, or Milnor number at most 26 (Yau–Zuo
[
YZ18
]
). It is also true for suspensions of irreducible plane curve singularities (Mendris–Némethi
[
MN05
]
). For
n>3
, a positive answer is known if one of the germs has Milnor number at most
2^{n}-1

[
YZ18
]
.

In general, if
f
and
g
are topologically right-left equivalent by bilipschitz homeomorphisms, Risler–Trotman
[
RT97
]
proved that they have the same multiplicity.

An important special case of Question
1.2
, which was also widely studied since
[
Zar71
]
, concerns families of topologically equivalent germs. Theorem
1.1
gives a positive answer to this question in case of isolated singularities. More precisely, we have the following.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
f_{t}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
be a continuous family of holomorphic germs of isolated singularities, parametrized by
t\in[0,1]
. Assume that the topological type of
f_{t}
is independent of
t
. Then the multiplicity
\nu(f_{t})
is independent of
t
, too.

Indeed, families of isolated singularities with constant topological type have constant Milnor number
\mu
, because
\mu
is a topological invariant
[
Mil68
, 7.2]
, see
[
Lê73
,
Tei80
]
. Thus Corollary
1.3
follows from Theorem
1.1
. We remark that the question whether every
\mu
-constant family has constant topological type has positive answer if
n\neq 3

[
LR76
]
, and is open if
n=3
, i.e. for families of surface singularities.

In Example
8.6
, we show that there do exist isolated singularities of the same topological type, which are not connected by a
\mu
-constant family. Therefore, while Corollary
1.3
gives a positive answer to Question
1.2
for families of isolated singularities, the non-family version remains open. The
non-isolated
case of Question
1.2
, even for families, remains open, too. Nonetheless, David Massey observed that our results imply a positive answer for a particular class of
aligned
singularities, see Corollary
8.5
.

Up to now, Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.3
were established only in certain special cases. Gabrielov and Kushnirenko
[
GK75
]
proved Theorem
1.1
for
f_{0}
homogeneous. Greuel
[
Gre86
]
and O’Shea
[
O’S87
]
proved it for
\mu
-constant deformations of functions
f_{0}
, with the only hypothesis that
f_{0}
is quasi-homogeneous. The case when
f_{0}
is Newton-nondegenerate was settled by Abderrahmane
[
Abd16
]
, cf.
[
ST04
]
. Other particular cases were studied e.g. by Greuel–Pfister
[
GP96
, §3]
, Massey
[
Mas95
, 7.9]
or Plenat–Trotman
[
PT13
]
. Let us also remark that classical results of Hironaka
[
Hir69
]
imply that Whitney equisingular families are equimultiple. In
[
TvS16
]
, Trotman and van Straten proved that
weak
Whitney equisingularity implies equimultiplicity, too.

As a final remark, we note that Theorem
1.1
is easily reduced to the case when the family
(f_{t})
is algebraic, i.e. each coefficient
a_{\iota}
is a polynomial in
t
. This way, Theorem
1.1
becomes a purely algebraic statement, which can be formulated over any field. Nonetheless, even in the curve case (
n=2
), no purely algebraic proof of this statement is known. In fact, the only proof over the complex numbers consists in proving that a
\mu
-constant family is topologically trivial, and then applying the affirmative answer to Question
1.2
, known for
n=2
. For families of functions defined over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, an application of Lefschetz Principle gives the same conclusion, see Remark
8.4
. Answering this question was one of the main motivations for development of the computer algebra program
Singular

[
DGPS22
]
, see
[
GP21
]
for a historical account.

Recently, Nero Budur, Lê Quy Thuong, Honc Duc Nguyen and the first author have conjectured that the fixed point Floer cohomology of the
m
-th iterate of the monodromy coincides with the compactly supported cohomology of the
m
-th restricted contact locus
[
BFdB+22
, Conjecture 1.5]
. This might suggest an algebraic approach to the proof of Theorem
1.1
, based on studying variation of contact loci in
\mu
-constant families. However, this problem does not seem easy at all.

Acknowledgments

Part of this project was realized during the semester
Singularity Theory from Modern Perspectives
at Centre International de Rencontres Mathématiques in Marseille. We would like to thank CIRM for their hospitality and great working conditions. We would also like to thank Norbert A’Campo, Enrique Artal, Nero Budur, Ailsa Keating, Ignacio Luengo, David Massey, András Némethi, Adam Parusiński, María Pe Pereira, Patrick Popescu-Pampu, Edson Sampaio, and Duco van Straten for helpful discussions. We also thank the referees for valuable comments.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we review some standard notation which used throughout the article; and recall those basic definitions from symplectic geometry which will be needed to introduce the fiberwise symplectic structure on the A’Campo space in Section
4
. Further notions, needed to define symplectic monodromy and McLean spectral sequences, will be introduced in Sections
5
and
6
.

2.1.
Basic notation

We write
\log
for the
natural
logarithm. For
r\geqslant 0
we denote by
\mathbb{B}_{r}^{n}
, or simply
\mathbb{B}_{r}
, the closed Euclidean ball of radius
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and by
\mathbb{D}_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
the closed disk of radius
r
in
\mathbb{C}
. We put
\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}=\mathbb{D}_{r}\setminus\{0\}
.

We denote by
\mathrm{id}_{X}
the identity map
X\longrightarrow X
; and by
\mathrm{id}_{k\times k}
the
k\times k
identity matrix. If
X
or
k
is clear from the context, we will sometimes write
\mathrm{id}
for
\mathrm{id}_{X}
or
\mathrm{id}_{k\times k}
. For a map
f\colon X\longrightarrow X
, we denote its fixed point set by
\operatorname{Fix}f\coloneqq\{x\in X:f(x)=x\}
. We write
\mathrm{pr}_{X}
,
\mathrm{pr}_{Y}
for the projections from a subset of
X\times Y
to
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Let
X
be a topological space. For a subset
U\subseteq X
, we denote by
\overline{U}
its topological closure, and by
\operatorname{Int}U
(or
\operatorname{Int}_{X}U
) its interior. Given a sequence
(x_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{\infty}
of elements of
X
(which we abbreviate by
(x_{\nu})\subseteq X
), we write
x_{\nu}\rightarrow x
for the convergence
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}x_{\nu}=x
. Similarly, for a subset
Z\subseteq X
and continuous functions
h
and
a
on
X\setminus Z
and
Z
, respectively, we say that
“
h\rightarrow a
on
Z
”
if
h
extends to a continuous function on
X
which restricts to
a
on
Z
. We denote this continuous extension by
h
, too.

For a smooth manifold
X
, we denote by
\Omega^{k}
the sheaf of smooth
k
-forms, and put
\Omega^{*}=\bigoplus_{k\geqslant 0}\Omega^{k}
. Thus for an open subset
U\subseteq X
, the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\Omega^{0}(U)
is the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U)
of smooth functions on
U
. Whenever no confusion is likely to occur, we will identify a function with its restriction, e.g. by writing
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U)
for an open subset
V\subseteq U
.

2.2.
Symplectic manifolds

A
2
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is
symplectic
if it is closed and nondegenerate, i.e.
T_{x}M\ni v\mapsto\omega(v,\,\cdot\,)\in T^{*}_{x}M
is an isomorphism for all
x\in M
. A
1
-form
\lambda
is
Liouville
if
d\lambda
is symplectic. Its
Liouville vector field

X_{\lambda}
is defined by
d\lambda(X_{\lambda},\,\cdot\,)=\lambda
. We say that
(M,\lambda)
is a
Liouville domain
if
M
is a compact connected manifold with boundary and
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
is a Liouville form such that
X_{\lambda}
points outwards
\partial M
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{End}(TM)
be an almost complex structure, i.e.
J^{2}=-\mathrm{id}
. We say that
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)

tames

J
if
\omega(v,Jv)>0
for all nonzero
v\in T_{x}M
,
x\in M
: in this case,
\omega
is nondegenerate. We say that
\omega
is
J
-compatible if it tames
J
and
\omega(J\,\cdot\,,J\,\cdot\,)=\omega(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
. Note that these definitions vary slightly among the literature; here we follow
[
MS17
, §4.1]
.

Assume that
M
is a complex manifold, and let
J
be the multiplication by
\imath=\sqrt{-1}
. A
2
-form on
M
is
Kähler
if it is
J
-compatible and closed, hence symplectic.

For a smooth function
\varrho\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
we write
d^{c}\varrho=d\varrho\circ J
. Thus
d^{c}=\imath(\partial-\overline{\partial})
. Again, we warn the reader that the definition of
d^{c}
varies among the literature, e.g.
[
Huy05
, Definition 3.13]
defines
d^{c}
as
-\imath(\partial-\overline{\partial})
, and
[
GH78
, p. 109]
as
-\frac{\imath}{4\pi}(\partial-\overline{\partial})
. Our definition of
d^{c}
, adapted from
[
CE12
, §2.2]
, is used e.g. in
[
CNPP06
, §3.1]
in a context which is very similar to ours, see Example
5.20
.

We say that a smooth function
\varrho\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
strictly plurisubharmonic
if
-dd^{c}\varrho
tames
J
, see
[
MS17
, Definition 7.4.7]
. In this case, the form
-dd^{c}\varrho
is Kähler. A function
\varrho
is
exhaustive
if
\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,c]
is compact for every
c\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\varrho
is an exhaustive strictly plurisubharmonic function then
\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,c]
with
1
-form
-d^{c}\varrho
is a Liouville domain. Indeed, the Liouville vector field
X_{-d^{c}\varrho}
is the gradient field of
\varrho
with respect to the metric
-dd^{c}\varrho(\,\cdot\,,J\,\cdot\,)
, see
[
CE12
, §2.8]
. Every Stein manifold admits an exhaustive strictly plurisubharmonic function, see
[
CE12
, §5.3]
.

Example 2.1
.

Let
M=\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and let
J(v)=\imath\cdot v
, i.e.
J
is a rotation by
\pi
. Then in polar coordinates
z=r\cdot\exp(2\pi\imath\cdot\theta)
we have
J\frac{\partial}{\partial r}=\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
and
J\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}=-r\tfrac{\partial}{\partial r}
. Hence
d^{c}r=-r\,d\theta
and
d^{c}\theta=\frac{1}{r}\,dr
.

Now if
M=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\varrho(z)=\frac{\pi}{2}\cdot||z||^{2}=\frac{\pi}{2}\cdot\sum_{i}r_{i}^{2}
, then

\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}\coloneqq-d^{c}\varrho=\pi\cdot\sum_{i}r_{i}^{2}d\theta_{i}
,

\omega_{\mathrm{std}}\coloneqq-dd^{c}\varrho=2\pi\cdot\sum_{i}r_{i}\,dr_{i}\wedge d\theta_{i}
is the standard area form, and
X_{\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}}=\pi\cdot\sum_{i}r_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{i}}=\nabla\varrho
is the radial vector field.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function with critical value
0\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\varepsilon>0
be such that
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\pitchfork f^{-1}(0)
. Then for
0<\delta\ll\varepsilon
, the
Milnor fiber

F\coloneqq f^{-1}(\delta)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
is smooth, and
f^{-1}(\delta)\pitchfork\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
, so
(F,\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}|_{F})
, is a Liouville domain.

For a more general example, replace
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by a Stein space
Y
, smooth off
f^{-1}(0)
;
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
by a sub-level set of an exhaustive strictly plurisubharmonic function
\varrho
; and the standard Liouville form by
-d^{c}\varrho
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow B
be a submersion. A form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is
fiberwise symplectic
if it is closed, and its restriction to each fiber of
f
is nondegenerate (hence symplectic). A fiberwise symplectic form defines an Ehressmann connection
\{v\in TM:\forall_{w\in\ker f_{*}}\colon\omega(v,w)=0\}
, called a
symplectic connection

[
MS17
, §6.3]
. Given a vector field on the base
B
, its lifting to the symplectic connection is called the
symplectic lift
. If the flow the symplectic lift of a vector field in
B
exists at a given time, then it induces symplectomorphisms between fibers, see
[
MS17
, Lemma 6.3.5]
.

3.
The A’Campo space:
“Milnor fibration at radius zero”

Let
X
be a complex manifold. Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function with only one critical value
0\in\mathbb{C}
, such that the central fiber
f^{-1}(0)
is snc. In this section, we recall the A’Campo
[
A’C75
]
model
A
of the
“radius zero”
monodromy of
f
, see Figure
4
, and construct a smooth atlas on it.

After some preparation in Section
3.1
, we introduce the topological space
A
in Definition
3.3
by a hybrid procedure using tropical coordinates and Kato-Nakayama spaces at radius
0
, and classical geometry at positive radius. We list its basic properties in Section
3.2
. In Section
3.3
, we endow
A
with a structure of a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-manifold with boundary, which depends only on
f
. In Section
3.4
we improve this structure to a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
one, designed so that we can introduce a well-behaved fiberwise symplectic form (
45
) in Section
4
. This
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure will depend on some additional data chosen along the construction.

In a topological setting,
A
was introduced by A’Campo in
[
A’C75
]
, who used it to compute the zeta function of the monodromy. We note that loc. cit. predates the Kato–Nakayama construction
[
KN99
]
, so instead of
X_{\log}
it uses a real blowup. In turn,
“tropical”
(or
“hybrid”
) constructions have recently been used, for example in
[
BJ17
,
AN20
,
EM21
]
, to define topological spaces related to our space
\Gamma
in Definition
3.3
, together with some additional structure inherited from
f^{-1}(\delta)
as
\delta\rightarrow 0
: e.g. a measure in
[
BJ17
, 3.4]
or a Lagrangian torus fibration in
[
EM21
]
.

3.1.
Construction of the A’Campo space

Let
X
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
. Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function, and let
D
be the (scheme-theoretic) fiber
f^{-1}(0)
. Assume that
D_{\mathrm{red}}
is snc. Since we are only interested in
f^{-1}(0)
and nearby fibers, we assume
\log|f|<-1
and that
f|_{X\setminus D}
is a submersion onto
\mathbb{D}_{e^{-1}}^{*}
, so
0\in\mathbb{C}
is its only critical value. Whenever no confusion is likely to occur, we identify divisors with their supports, e.g. by writing
X\setminus D
for
X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}D
, i.e. for
f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{e^{-1}}^{*})
; or writing
x\in D
instead of
x\in\operatorname{Supp}D
.

Let
D_{1},\dots,D_{N}
be the irreducible components of
D\coloneqq f^{-1}(0)
. Write

D=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}D_{i},

where each
m_{i}
is a positive integer. Put
X_{\emptyset}=X\setminus D
, and for a nonempty subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
put

(11)

X_{I}^{\circ}=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{j\not\in I}D_{j}.

Then
X=\bigsqcup_{I}X_{I}^{\circ}
is a stratification of
X
.

3.1.1.
Charts adapted to
f

In order to fix the notation, we recall some basic definitions. We say that
(U_{X},\psi_{X})
is a
holomorphic chart
for
X
if
U_{X}\subseteq X
is an open set, and
\psi_{X}\colon U_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a biholomorphism onto its image. The map
\psi_{X}
will often be clear from the context, in which case we will abuse the language and use the term
chart
to refer to
U_{X}
. We will usually keep the subscript
“
X
”
when referring to a chart in
X
, and drop it when taking its preimage in the A’Campo space.

A
holomorphic atlas
is a collection
\mathcal{U}_{X}=\{(U_{X}^{p},\psi_{X}^{p}):p\in R\}
of holomorphic charts which cover
X
, i.e.
X=\bigcup_{p\in R}U^{p}_{X}
.
A
partition of unity inscribed in
\mathcal{U}_{X}
is a collection
\{\tau^{p}:p\in R\}
of smooth functions
\tau^{p}\colon X\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that
\tau^{p}|_{X\setminus U_{X}^{p}}=0
, the set
\{p\in R:\tau^{p}(x)\neq 0\}
is finite for every
x\in X
, and
\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}(x)=1
.
We will use charts of the following form.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(U_{X},\psi_{X})
be a holomorphic chart for
X
. Its
associated index set
is

(12)

S=\{i:D_{i}\cap\overline{U}_{X}\neq\emptyset\}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}.

Write
\psi_{X}=(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})\colon U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
for some positive integers
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}
. We say that the chart
(U_{X},\psi_{X})
is
adapted to
f
if the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, we have
D_{i}\cap U_{X}=\{x\in U_{X}:z_{i}(x)=0\}
.

(b)

If
S\neq\emptyset
then the function
f
restricts to
f|_{U_{X}}=\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}^{m_{i}}
.

(c)

For every
i\in S
, we have
\log|z_{i}|<-1
.

(d)

The set
U_{X}
has compact closure
\overline{U}_{X}
, and the map
\psi_{X}
extends continuously to
\overline{U}_{X}
.

A holomorphic atlas is
adapted to
f
if all its charts are. We say that a pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
is
adapted to
f
if
\mathcal{U}_{X}
is a holomorphic atlas adapted to
f
, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is a partition of unity inscribed in
\mathcal{U}_{X}
.

Note that, by
(a)
, the indices of
z_{j}
’s are either in
S
– in which case they correspond to the fixed order of components of
D
– or are integers bigger than
N
. Although a bit unusual, this convention will make further notation much more convenient. We also note that by
(b)
, each component of
D
meeting the closure
\overline{U}_{X}
(i.e. whose index is in
S
) actually meets
U_{X}
.

Because
D_{\mathrm{red}}
is snc,
X
admits an atlas adapted to
f
. Indeed, the definition of snc implies that, after suitably indexing the variables, every point of
X
lies in a chart satisfying
(a)
,
(b)
. Then, conditions
(c)
,
(d)
can be achieved by shrinking those charts.

3.1.2.
The basic functions

We will use a continuous bijection
\eta\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1]
, given by

(13)

\eta(0)=0,\quad\eta(s)=(1-\log s)^{-1}\mbox{ for }s>0,

see Figure
1
. Note that the restriction
\eta|_{(0,1]}
is smooth.
Since we have assumed
\log|f|<-1
, we have smooth functions

(14)

t\coloneqq(-\log|f|)^{-1}\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow[0,1),\qquad g\coloneqq\eta(t)\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow[0,1).

We extend them to continuous functions on
X
by putting
t|_{D}=0
,
g|_{D}=0
. We have a smooth map

(15)

\theta\coloneqq\frac{f}{|f|}\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Fix a chart
(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})\colon U_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
adapted to
f
. Fix
i
in its index set
S=\{i:\overline{U}_{X}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset\}
, see (
12
). By Definition
3.1
(c)
,
\log|z_{i}|<-1
, so in particular
|z_{i}|<1
. We have radial coordinates

(16)

r_{i}\coloneqq|z_{i}|\colon U_{X}\longrightarrow[0,1),\quad\mbox{and}\quad t_{i}\coloneqq(-m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow(0,1).

We extend
t_{i}
to a continuous function
U_{X}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
by setting
t_{i}|_{U_{X}\cap D_{i}}=0
. We also have an angular coordinate:

(17)

\theta_{i}\coloneqq\frac{z_{i}}{|z_{i}|}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}.

We will compare the speeds of convergence towards
D_{i}
using
“tropical”
functions:

(18)

w_{i}\coloneqq\frac{t}{t_{i}}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow[0,1],\qquad u_{i}\coloneqq\eta(w_{i})\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow[0,1].

Note that
\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}=1
, see Lemma
3.6
(c)
. Eventually, we introduce a
“hybrid”
coordinate:

(19)

v_{i}\coloneqq t_{i}-u_{i}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow[-1,1).

To simplify the notation, we introduce a map

(20)

\vartheta\coloneqq((\theta_{i})_{i\in S},(z_{j})_{j\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}\setminus S}).

It puts together those coordinates which have no
“tropical”
part: as we will see, they are easier to handle when defining a smooth structure on the A’Campo space
A
.

Notation 3.2
.

Throughout the article, we will identify
\mathbb{S}^{1}
with the
additive
group
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. This way,
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
denotes the angle
2\pi\theta
, and we have
\theta+1=\theta
.

3.1.3.
The Kato–Nakayama space
X_{\log}

As a first step of our construction, we will extend polar coordinates on
X
to radius zero. This can be conveniently phrased in terms of the Kato–Nakayama construction
[
KN99
]
. In general, it associates to a log structure (i.e. a scheme with a sheaf of monoids, subject to some additional conditions), a topological space. We will use it only for the monoid sheaf
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{X}\cap i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X\setminus D}^{*}
, where
i\colon X\setminus D\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion. We follow
[
KN99
, 1.2]
.

For
x\in X
, let
\mathcal{M}_{x}
be the stalk of
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\cap i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X\setminus D}^{*}
at
x
, so
\mathcal{M}_{x}
consists of holomorphic germs which are invertible off
D
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{x}^{\mathrm{gp}}
be the group associated to the monoid
\mathcal{M}_{x}
, i.e.
\mathcal{M}_{x}^{\mathrm{gp}}=\{ab^{-1}:a,b\in\mathcal{M}_{x}\}
with a natural identification
(ab^{-1})\cdot(ba^{-1})=1
. Let

X_{\log}=\{(x,h):x\in X,\ h\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\mathrm{gp}},\mathbb{S}^{1}),\ h(f)=\tfrac{f(x)}{|f(x)|}\mbox{ if }f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{*}\},

and let
\tau\colon X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
be the first projection. To define a topology on
X_{\log}
, fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, see Definition
3.1
. Reordering the components of
D
if needed, we can assume that its associated index set is
\{1,\dots,k\}
. Put
U_{\log}=\tau^{-1}(U_{X})\subseteq X_{\log}
. A
Kato–Nakayama chart over
U_{X}
is a map
U_{\log}\longrightarrow[0,1)^{k}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-k}
, given by

(21)

(x,h)\mapsto(r_{1},\dots,r_{k};\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k};z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}}),\quad\mbox{where}\quad r_{i}=|z_{i}|,\quad\theta_{i}=h(z_{i}).

Note that the coordinates
r_{i}
and
\theta_{i}
extend to
U_{\log}
the pullbacks of the maps
r_{i}
,
\theta_{i}
defined in (
16
), (
17
). The topology on
(X,D)_{\log}
is the coarsest one which makes these charts continuous. Clearly,
X_{\log}
is a topological manifold with boundary, whose interior is homeomorphic with
X\setminus D
.

Applying this construction to
(X,D)=(\mathbb{C},0)
, we get

\mathbb{C}_{\log}\coloneqq(\mathbb{C},0)_{\log}\cong[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1},

so
\mathbb{C}_{\log}
extends the polar coordinates to a
radius zero circle

\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, cf.
[
Ogu18
, V.1.2]
. The holomorphic function
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extends to a continuous map
f_{\log}\colon X_{\log}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}
. In local coordinates (
21
) over an adapted chart meting
D
, the map
f_{\log}
is given by
(r_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot r_{k}^{m_{k}},m_{1}\theta_{1}+\ldots+m_{k}\theta_{k})
: recall from Notation
3.2
that we write the angular coordinates additively. We get a commutative diagram

(22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\log}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{\log}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

For a subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
we define
(X_{I}^{\circ})_{\log}\subseteq X_{\log}
as the preimage of the stratum
X_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq X
, see (
11
). In coordinates (
21
),
(X_{I}^{\circ})_{\log}
is defined by
r_{i}=0
for
i\in I
and
r_{j}\neq 0
for
j\not\in I
.

3.1.4.
Definition of the A’Campo space
A
.

Fix a holomorphic atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}=\{(U^{p}_{X},\psi^{p}_{X}):p\in R\}
adapted to
f
, and a partition of unity
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\{\tau^{p}:p\in R\}
inscribed in
\mathcal{U}_{X}
. We write
u_{i}^{p}
,
v_{i}^{p}
etc. for the corresponding maps from Section
3.1.2
. For convenience, if
U_{X}^{p}\cap D_{i}=\emptyset
we set
u_{i}^{p}=0
,
v_{i}^{p}=0
.

Now, we
define smooth maps
\overline{u}_{i},\overline{v}_{i}\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\mu\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
by

(23)

\overline{u}_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}u_{i}^{p},\quad\overline{v}_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p},\quad\mu\coloneqq(\overline{u}_{1},\dots,\overline{u}_{N}).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
X_{\log}
be the Kato–Nakayama space associated to
(X,D)
, see Section
3.1.3
. Let
\Gamma\subseteq X\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the closure of the graph of
\mu
. The
A’Campo space

A
is the topological space
A=\Gamma\times_{X}X_{\log}
, i.e. such that the top-left square of (
24
) is cartesian.

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\coloneqq\overline{\mbox{\small{graph}}(\mu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\log}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{\log}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\theta)\sim f_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{u}_{1},\ldots,\overline{u}_{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Put
f_{A}=(|f|\circ\pi,\theta)\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. It is a composition of the natural map
A\longrightarrow X_{\log}
with the map
f_{\log}
induced by
f
between the Kato–Nakayama spaces, see (
22
). Fibers of
f_{A}
agree with those of
(t,\theta)
and
(g,\theta)
. Figure
4(a)
in Example
4.16
shows the fibers of
f_{A}
and
f_{\log}
over
\partial\mathbb{C}_{\log}
.

We pull back the stratification (
11
) of
X
by putting

(25)

A_{I}^{\circ}=\pi^{-1}(X_{I}^{\circ}).

The partition
A=\bigsqcup_{I}A_{I}^{\circ}
obtained this way is not a stratification anymore.

We have
A\setminus\pi^{-1}(D)=A_{\emptyset}^{\circ}
and
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}A_{I}^{\circ}
. Thus, for each chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, the zero locus of the function
t_{i}
introduced in (
16
) is
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}A_{I}^{\circ}\cap U
, where
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. The
“deepest”
subset of this partition, namely the one corresponding to the associated index set (
12
), equals
A_{S}^{\circ}\cap U=\{x\in U:\forall_{i\in S}\colon\,t_{i}(x)=0\}
and is closed in
U
.

We put
\partial A\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(D)=\bigsqcup_{I\neq\emptyset}A_{I}^{\circ}
. In the manifold with boundary structure that we will define in Section
3.3
, the set
\partial A
will indeed be the boundary of
A
. Note that
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow X\setminus D
is a homeomorphism. Whenever no confusion is likely to occur, we will identify
A\setminus\partial A
with
X\setminus D
. In particular,
A\setminus\partial A
has a
natural smooth structure
, pulled back from
X\setminus D
via
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
.

Remark 3.4
.

The topological space
A
introduced in Definition
3.3
agrees with the one originally constructed by A’Campo, see
[
A’C75
, p. 238]
.

3.2.
Elementary properties of the A’Campo space

In this section, we list some simple properties of the A’Campo space
A
introduced in Definition
3.3
. They are summarized in Lemma
3.6
, which studies the lifts of basic functions introduced in Section
3.1.2
; and in Proposition
3.7
, which describes the topological structure of
A
.

3.2.1.
Lifts of the basic functions

The following notation, which we will keep throughout Section
3
, will help to make our formulas lighter.

Notation 3.5
.

For a subset
U_{X}\subseteq X
, we denote a map from
U_{X}
and its pullback to
U\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
by the same letter. This way, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we have continuous functions
\overline{u}_{i},\overline{v}_{i},g\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by the formulas (
14
) and (
23
).

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
. Pulling back the functions (
16
)–(
19
), we get functions
r_{i},t_{i}\colon U\longrightarrow[0,1)
,
\theta_{i}\colon U\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
w_{i},u_{i},v_{i}\colon U\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. In Lemma
3.6
(a)
we will see that they extend continuously to
U
, and we will denote these extensions by the same letters, too.

Whenever a chart
U_{X}
is fixed, we will put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
, denote its associated index set (
12
) by
S
, write
k=\#S
and use the maps
t_{i},w_{i}
introduced in Section
3.1.2
without further emphasis on the dependence on
U_{X}
. If the chart is called
U_{X}^{\prime}
or
U_{X}^{p}
for some index
p
, we will call the corresponding maps
t_{i}^{\prime},w_{i}^{\prime},\dots
or
t_{i}^{p},w_{i}^{p},\dots
, respectively.

In Section
3.2.4
we will prove the following result.

Lemma 3.6
.

Fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, not necessarily one from the atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
fixed in Definition
3.3
. Let
S
be its associated index set (
12
), and let
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. Assume
S\neq\emptyset
. The following hold.

(a)

For every
i\in S
, the maps
t_{i},w_{i},u_{i},v_{i},\theta_{i}
from Section
3.1.2
extend to continuous maps on
U
.

(b)

Fix
i\in S
and a nonempty subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
i\not\in I
. We have
w_{i}|_{A_{I}^{\circ}\cap U}\equiv 0
.

(c)

For every
i\in S
we have
w_{i}\in[0,1]
, and
\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}=1
.

(d)

Let
U_{X}^{\prime}
be another chart adapted to
f
, let
U^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(U_{X}^{\prime})
, and let
w_{i}^{\prime}
be the corresponding map from (
18
). Then on
\partial A\cap U\cap U^{\prime}
we have
w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}
for all
i
such that
U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{\prime}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

(e)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we have
\overline{u}_{i}|_{U\cap\partial A}=u_{i}|_{U\cap\partial A}
if
i\in S
and
\overline{u}_{i}|_{U\cap\partial A}=0
if
i\not\in S
.

Very importantly for us, part
(d)
expresses the idea that
“at radius zero”
, the values of
w_{i}
do not depend on the choice of the chart. Before entering in the technicalities of the proof, we proceed with the topological description of the A’Campo space.

3.2.2.
Rounded simplices

To state the next result, we introduce the following additional notation. By
\Delta^{N-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we denote the image of the standard (
N-1
)-simplex through
(\eta,\ldots,\eta)
, i.e.

\Delta^{N-1}=\{(\eta(s_{1}),\dots,\eta(s_{N})):s_{i}\geqslant 0,\ \textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}=1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N},

see Figure
1
.
For
\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
we denote by
\Delta_{I}^{N-1}
(or simply
\Delta_{I}
) the face
\{u_{j}=0,j\not\in I\}\subseteq\Delta^{N-1}
of
\Delta^{N-1}
corresponding to
I
, and put
\Delta_{I}^{\circ}=\{u_{i}\neq 0,u_{j}=0:i\in I,j\not\in I\}\subseteq\Delta_{I}
. Hence e.g.
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1}^{\circ}=\{(1,0,\dots,0)\}
. Note that we identify a subscript
\{i\}
with
i
.

\eta(t)
1
1
t
1
1
t_{2}
t_{1}
(\eta,\ldots,\eta)
1
1
t_{2}
t_{1}
\Delta^{1}
\Delta^{1}_{1}
\Delta^{1}_{2}

Figure 1
.
Function
\eta
from (
13
) gives a convenient embedding of an (
N-1
)-simplex to
\mathbb{R}^{N}

Let
X=\bigsqcup_{I}X_{I}
be the stratification (
11
) of
X
associated to
D
. The
dual complex
of
D
is

(26)

\bigcup_{\{I\neq\emptyset\ :\ X_{I}\neq\emptyset\}}\Delta_{I}\subseteq\Delta^{N-1}.

3.2.3.
Topological structure.

The second main result of the current Section
3.2
is the following.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
be a pair adapted to
f
and let
A
be the A’Campo space corresponding to
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The maps
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
,
\mu\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\overline{v}_{i}\colon A\longrightarrow[-1,1]
for
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
are continuous.

(b)

The map
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
is proper. Its restriction
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow X\setminus D
is a homeomorphism.

(c)

For every nonempty subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, the restriction
(\pi,\mu)|_{A_{I}^{\circ}}\colon A_{I}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{I}^{\circ}\times\Delta_{I}
is a topological locally trivial fibration, with fiber
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\#I}
. Hence
\mu(\partial A)
is the dual complex of
D
.

(d)

The subset
\partial A\subseteq X_{\log}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}

does not depend on the choice of
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
.

(e)

Let
A^{\prime}
be the A’Campo space corresponding to another pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime})
adapted to
f
. Using
(b)
and
(d)
, we can define a map

\Phi\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
by

(27)

\Phi|_{\partial A}=\mathrm{id}_{\partial A}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Phi|_{A\setminus\partial A}=\pi^{\prime}|_{A^{\prime}\setminus\partial A}\circ\pi^{-1}|_{X\setminus D}.

Then
\Phi
is a homeomorphism.

Later, we will show that
A
is a topological manifold with boundary. We postpone this statement, since at the same time we will endow
A
with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure; in such a way that the map (
27
) becomes a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism. In Section
3.4
, we will even make
A
a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-manifold with boundary. As we will see, the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure is harder to produce, and the map (
27
) will not be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism in general.

Example 3.8
.

Let
X=\mathbb{D}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{e})
, and let
f=z_{1}z_{2}
. Consider
\mathcal{U}_{X}
consisting of just one chart
(z_{1},z_{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\{1\}
. Then
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
is adapted to
f
. The corresponding A’Campo space
A
is the shaded part of Figure
2(a)
, multiplied by
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{2}
. Similarly, for
X=\mathbb{D}^{3}_{\varepsilon}
,
f=z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}
and trivial
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
, the corresponding
A
is Figure
2(b)
times
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{3}
.

\overline{v}_{1}
-1

.

3.2.4.
Proofs of
3.6
–
3.7

The remaining part of Section
3.2
is devoted to the proof of Lemma
3.6
and Proposition
3.7
. We remind the reader to use Notation
3.5
.

First, we give a precise formula relating
t_{i}
,
w_{i}
with corresponding
t_{i}^{\prime}
,
w_{i}^{\prime}
defined by another chart. We will use it both to prove the equality
w_{i}|_{\partial A}=w_{i}^{\prime}|_{\partial A}
asserted in Lemma
3.6
(d)
, and to make further computations in Lemma
3.26
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
U_{X},U^{\prime}_{X}
be two charts adapted to
f
. Put
V_{X}=U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{\prime}
,
V=\pi^{-1}(U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{\prime})
, and use Notation
3.5
. For every
i\in S\cap S^{\prime}
, the following hold.

(a)

The functions
t_{i}
,
t_{i}^{\prime}
are continuous on
V
.

(b)

There is a nonvanishing holomorphic function
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}(V_{X})
such that
z_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda\cdot z_{i}
.

(c)

Let
\lambda
be as in
(b)
, and let
a=-m_{i}\log|\lambda|
. Then
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X})
,
1+at_{i}\neq 0
, and on
V
we have:

t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}\cdot(1+a\cdot t_{i})^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}\cdot(1+a\cdot t_{i}).

(d)

The functions
w_{i}
and
w_{i}^{\prime}
extend to continuous functions on
V
, such that
w_{i}|_{V\cap\partial A}=w_{i}^{\prime}|_{V\cap\partial A}
.

(a)
Recall that
t_{i}
,
t_{i}^{\prime}
denote the pullbacks by
\pi
of continuous functions
t_{i}
,
t_{i}^{\prime}
introduced in (
35
). By Definition
3.3
of
A
, the map
\pi
is continuous, so
t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime}
are continuous, too.

(b)
By Definition
3.1
(b)
, we have
D_{i}\cap V=\{z_{i}=0\}=\{z_{i}^{\prime}=0\}
.
Hence
z_{i}=\lambda z_{i}^{\prime}
for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}(V)
, as claimed.

(c)
Since
\lambda
is smooth and nonvanishing, the function
a=-m_{i}\log|\lambda|
is smooth. By definition, on
V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
both
t_{i}
and
t_{i}^{\prime}
are zero, so
1+at_{i}\neq 0
and the first equality of
(c)
holds trivially there. Away from
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, we have
t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
and
t_{i}^{\prime}=-(m_{i}\log|z_{i}^{\prime}|)^{-1}
by formula (
16
). Part
(b)
and the definition of
a
give

t_{i}^{\prime}\cdot(1+at_{i})=-\frac{1+at_{i}}{m_{i}\log|\lambda z_{i}|}=-\frac{1+at_{i}}{m_{i}\log|z_{i}|+m_{i}\log|\lambda|}=-\frac{1+at_{i}}{-t_{i}^{-1}-a}=t_{i},

so
1+at_{i}\neq 0
and
t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}\cdot(1+at_{i})^{-1}
, as needed.

We will now prove that the equality
w_{i}^{\prime}=w_{i}\cdot(1+at_{i})
holds on
V\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
. There, substituting the equality
t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}\cdot(1+at_{i})^{-1}
to the definition (
18
) of
w_{i}^{\prime}
, we get

w_{i}^{\prime}=t\cdot(t_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}=t\cdot t_{i}^{-1}\cdot(1+at_{i})=w_{i}\cdot(1+at_{i}).

Once we prove in
(d)
that
w_{i}
and
w_{i}^{\prime}
are continuous, the above equality will extend to
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
.

(d)
As in the proof of
(a)
, continuity of
\pi
implies that
w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}
are continuous on
V\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
. Fix
x\in V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
and a sequence
(x_{\nu})\subset V\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
such that
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}x_{\nu}=x
. Since
w_{i}(x_{\nu})\in[0,1]
, after passing to a subsequence we can assume that both
w_{i}(x_{\nu})
and
w_{i}^{\prime}(x_{\nu})
have limits in
[0,1]
. Call these limits
w
and
w^{\prime}
, respectively. Since
t_{i}(x)=0
, applying the second formula in
(c)
away from
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
and passing to the limit, we get
w=w^{\prime}
. Taking for
U_{X}^{\prime}
a chart
U_{X}^{p}
of the atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
fixed in Definition
3.3
, and writing
w_{i}^{p}
for the corresponding map, we get
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}w_{i}^{p}(x_{\nu})=w
, for every
p\in R
such that
x\in U^{p}
. Continuity of
\eta
implies that
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}u_{i}^{p}(x_{\nu})=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}\eta(w_{i}^{p}(x_{\nu}))=\eta(w)
. Since each
\tau^{p}
is continuous and
supported in
U_{X}^{p}
, we get
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}\tau^{p}(x_{\nu})u_{i}^{p}(x_{\nu})=\tau^{p}(x)\eta(w)
for all
p\in R
.

By Definition
3.3
, the function
\overline{u}_{i}
is continuous. Hence
\overline{u}_{i}(x)=\sum_{p}\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}\tau^{p}(x_{\nu})u_{i}^{p}(x_{\nu})=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}(x)\eta(w)=\eta(w)
. Bijectivity of
\eta
gives
w=\eta^{-1}(\overline{u}_{i}(x))
.

Thus from any sequence
(x_{\nu})\subset V\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
such that
\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}x_{\nu}=x
, we can choose a subsequence
(x_{\nu^{\prime}})
such that
\lim_{\nu^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty}w_{i}(x_{\nu^{\prime}})=\eta^{-1}(\overline{u}_{i}(x))
. In fact, the same is true for all sequences
(x_{\nu})\subset V
, because by Definition
3.3
of
A
, every point of
V
is a limit of a sequence of points in
V\setminus\partial A
. It follows that the function
w_{i}
is continuous at
x
, and the value of
w_{i}
at
x
equals
\eta^{-1}(\overline{u}_{i}(x))
, so it does not depend on the choice of the chart
U_{X}
defining
w_{i}
, as claimed.

It follows that the second formula in
(c)
holds on
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, too, which completes its proof.

∎

(a)
Applying Lemma
3.9
(a)
,
(d)
to
U_{X}
, we infer that
t_{i}
,
w_{i}
extend to continuous functions on
U
. Since
\eta
is continuous, the functions
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})
and
v_{i}=t_{i}-u_{i}
are continuous, too. Eventually,
\theta_{i}
pulls back to a continuous coordinate on the preimage of
U
in the Kato–Nakayama space
X_{\log}
, see (
21
), so its further pullback to
A
is continuous, too.

(b)
Since
I\neq\emptyset
, we have
A_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq\partial A
, so
t|_{A_{I}^{\circ}}=0
. For every
i\not\in I
, definition of
A_{I}^{\circ}
gives
U\cap A_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq U\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, so
t_{i}|_{U\cap A_{I}^{\circ}}>0
. By definition (
18
) of
w_{i}
, on
U\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
we have
w_{i}=t\cdot t_{i}^{-1}=0
.

(c)
Because
U_{X}
is adapted to
f
, by Definition
3.1
(b)
we have
|f|_{U}|=\prod_{i\in S}|z_{i}|^{m_{i}}
. Hence
\log|f|_{U}|=\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\log|z_{i}|
. On
U\setminus\partial A
, substituting the formulas (
14
), (
16
) for
t
and
t_{i}
, gives
-t^{-1}=-\sum_{i\in S}t_{i}^{-1}
, so
1=\sum_{i\in S}tt_{i}^{-1}=\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}
by definition (
18
) of
w_{i}
. Since by
(a)
the functions
w_{i}
are continuous on
U
, we infer that the equality
1=\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}
holds on
U\cap\partial A
, too, as claimed.

(d)
Put
V=U\cap U^{\prime}
, and fix
i
such that
D_{i}\cap V\neq\emptyset
. The set
V\cap\partial A
is a disjoint union of
V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
and
V\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
for all nonempty
I
not containing
i
. By Lemma
3.9
(d)
,
w_{i}=w_{i}^{\prime}
on
V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
. In turn, by
(b)
, both
w_{i}
and
w_{i}^{\prime}
are zero on
V\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
whenever
i\not\in I
. Thus
w_{i}=w_{i}^{\prime}
on the whole set
V\cap\partial A
.

(e)
Fix
i\in S
. By
(d)
, for every chart
U_{X}^{p}
of the atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
fixed in Definition
3.3
, the corresponding function
w_{i}^{p}
satisfies
w_{i}^{p}|_{U\cap U^{p}\cap\partial A}=w_{i}|_{U\cap U^{p}\cap\partial A}
. Hence
\overline{u}_{i}=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}\eta(w_{i}^{p})=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}\eta(w_{i})=\eta(w_{i})=u_{i}
on
U\cap\partial A
, as claimed.

Now, fix
i\not\in S
, and
x\in U\cap\partial A
. Since
i\not\in S
, we have
x\in A_{I}^{\circ}
for some nonempty
I
not containing
i
. Take any
p\in R
such that
\tau^{p}(x)\neq 0
, so
x\in U^{p}
. Applying
(b)
to
U^{p}
, we infer that
w_{i}^{p}(x)=0
, so
u_{i}^{p}(x)=0
. Therefore,
\overline{u}_{i}(x)=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}(x)u_{i}^{p}(x)=0
, as claimed.

∎

Lemma 3.10
.

For a nonempty subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, the following hold.

(a)

For every
x\in X_{I}^{\circ}
, we have
\mu(\pi^{-1}(x))=\Delta_{I}\subseteq\Delta^{N-1}
.

(b)

The projection
\Gamma\longrightarrow X
is proper.

(c)

The subset
A_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq X_{\log}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
equals
(X_{I}^{\circ})_{\log}\times\Delta_{I}
.

(a)
Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
containing
x
, let
S
be its associated index set, and let
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. Fix
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)\subseteq U\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
. For any
i\not\in S
, we have
\overline{u}_{i}(y)=0
by Lemma
3.6
(e)
. Fix
i\in S
. By Lemma
3.6
(e)
, we have
\overline{u}_{i}(y)=u_{i}(y)=\eta(w_{i}(y))
. Thus Lemma
3.6
(c)
implies that
\mu(y)\in\Delta^{N-1}
. Moreover, if
i\not\in I
then by Lemma
3.6
(b)
we have
w_{i}(y)=0
, so
\overline{u}_{i}(y)=0
. We conclude that
\mu(y)\in\Delta_{I}
, so
\mu(\pi^{-1}(x))\subseteq\Delta_{I}
.

For the reverse inclusion, note that the subset
\mu(\pi^{-1}(x))\subseteq\Delta_{I}
is closed. Indeed, it consists of all possible limits of
\mu(x_{\nu})
, where
(x_{\nu})\subset X\setminus D
is a sequence converging to
x
. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that
\Delta_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq\mu(\pi^{-1}(x))
. Fix
b\in\Delta_{I}^{\circ}
. Since the function
\eta|_{[0,1]}
is bijective, for each
i\in I
we get a unique
b_{i}\in(0,1]
such that
\eta(b_{i})
is the
i
-th coordinate of
b
; and
\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}=1
. Put
b_{i}=0
for
i\not\in I
.

Recall that
z_{i}
, for
i\in S
, are some of the coordinates of the chart
U_{X}
. Hence for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there is a curve
\gamma\colon[0,\varepsilon]\longrightarrow U_{X}
such that
z_{i}(\gamma(s))=s^{b_{i}/m_{i}}
for all
s\in[0,\varepsilon]
and all
i\in S
. By Definition
3.1
(b)
, we have
f(\gamma(s))=\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}(\gamma(s))^{m_{i}}=s^{\sum_{i\in S}b_{i}}=s
, so
t(\gamma(s))=-(\log s)^{-1}
for
s>0
. By definition (
16
) of
t_{i}
, for
s>0
we have
t_{i}(\gamma(s))=-(m_{i}\log(s^{b_{i}/m_{i}}))^{-1}=-(b_{i}\log s)^{-1}
, and by (
18
),
w_{i}(\gamma(s))=t(\gamma(s))\cdot t_{i}(\gamma(s))^{-1}=b_{i}
. Therefore,
\mu(\gamma(s))=b
for all
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
. Passing with
s
to
0
we get
y\in A
such that
\pi(y)=\gamma(0)=x
and
\mu(y)=b
, as needed.

(b)
,
(c)
Follow immediately from
(a)
.

∎

(a)
Follows directly from Definition
3.3
.

(b)
Properness follows from the properness of the maps
\Gamma\longrightarrow X
(see Lemma
3.10
(b)
) and
X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
, and the rest is immediate by the definitions.

(c)
Let
U_{X}\subseteq X
be a chart adapted to
f
. Let
\tau\colon X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
be the natural map from the Kato–Nakayama space. In coordinates (
21
) on
\tau^{-1}(U_{X})
, the restriction of
\tau
to
\tau^{-1}(U\cap X_{I}^{\circ})
maps
\theta_{i}
to
0
for
i\in I
, and keeps the remaining coordinates intact. It follows that
\tau|_{\tau^{-1}(X_{I}^{\circ})}\colon\tau^{-1}(X_{I}^{\circ})\longrightarrow X_{I}^{\circ}
is a topological
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\#I}
-bundle. Thus the first part of
(c)
follows Lemma
3.10
(c)
. The second follows from the definition (
26
) of the dual complex.

(d)
By Lemma
3.10
(c)
, for every nonempty
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, the subset
A_{I}^{\circ}\subseteq X_{\log}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
does not depend on the choice of
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
. Therefore, neither does their disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{I\neq\emptyset}A_{I}^{\circ}=\partial A
.

(e)
Let
\pi_{\log}\colon A\longrightarrow X_{\log}
be the natural map induced by
\pi
in Definition
3.3
. Let
\pi_{\log}^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime}
,
\overline{u}_{i}^{\prime}
etc. denote the maps from Definition
3.3
associated to
A^{\prime}
. By
(d)
, the subsets
\partial A,\partial A^{\prime}\subseteq X_{\log}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
are equal, so the restriction
\Phi|_{\partial A}
, defined as
\mathrm{id}|_{\partial A}
, indeed maps
\partial A
to
\partial A^{\prime}
. Clearly, this restriction is continuous. In turn,
\Phi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}\setminus\partial A^{\prime}
is continuous by
(b)
. Since
\partial A
is a closed subset of
A
, to prove continuity of
\Phi
it is sufficient to prove that, for every
y\in\partial A
, and every sequence
(y_{\nu})\subset A\setminus\partial A
converging to
y
, the values
y_{\nu}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Phi(y_{\nu})
converge to
y
, too.

Since a point
y^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
is by definition a pair
(\pi^{\prime}_{\log}(y^{\prime}),\mu^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))
, we need to show that
\pi_{\log}^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})\rightarrow\pi_{\log}(y)
and
\mu^{\prime}(y^{\prime}_{\nu})\rightarrow\mu(y)
. By definition of
\Phi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
, we have
\pi(y_{\nu})=\pi^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})\in X\setminus D
, so
\pi_{\log}(y_{\nu})=\pi^{\prime}_{\log}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})
, because the map
X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
is a homeomorphism away from
D
. This proves the first convergence.

For the second one, choose a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
containing
\pi^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})
for
\nu\gg 1
, and fix
i
in the associated index set (
12
). By Lemma
3.6
(a)
, the function
u_{i}
from (
18
) extends to continuous functions
u_{i}\colon U\longrightarrow[0,1]
and
u_{i}^{\prime}\colon U^{\prime}\longrightarrow[0,1]
, where
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
,
U^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime})^{-1}(U_{X})
. Put
x_{\nu}=\pi(y_{\nu})
,
x_{\nu}^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})
. Clearly,
x_{\nu}=x_{\nu}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we have the equality

\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}u_{i}^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}u_{i}(x_{\nu}^{\prime})=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}u_{i}(x_{\nu})=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}u_{i}(y_{\nu})=u_{i}(y)=\overline{u}_{i}(y),

where the last equality follows from Lemma
3.6
(e)
. Applying it to all charts
U_{X}
of
\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\prime}
, we infer that
\overline{u}_{i}^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})\rightarrow\overline{u}_{i}(y)
. Thus by definition (
23
) of
\mu
we get
\mu^{\prime}(y_{\nu}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mu(y)
, as needed.

Therefore,
\Phi
is continuous. Continuity of the inverse follows by reversing the roles of
A
and
A^{\prime}
.

∎

3.3.
The
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas on the A’Campo space

Fix a pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
adapted to
f
, see Definition
3.1
. In this section we will introduce a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas on the A’Campo space and we will show that if
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime})
is another pair adapted to
f
, then the homeomorphism (
27
) connecting the corresponding A’Campo spaces is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism. As the reader may expect, this
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure agrees outside
\partial A
with the natural smooth structure in
A\setminus\partial A
, i.e. the one pulled back from
X\setminus D
by the homeomorphism
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow X\setminus D
.

3.3.1.
Definition of the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas

Fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, not necessarily one from the fixed atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
. Put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
, let
S=\{i:U_{X}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset\}
be the associated index set, and let
w_{i},v_{i},\vartheta,\dots
be the maps introduced in Section
3.1.2
. Assume
S\neq\emptyset
. For every
i\in S
, we define

(28)

U_{i}=\{x\in U:w_{i}(x)>\tfrac{1}{n+1}\}.

By Lemma
3.6
(a)
, the subset
U_{i}\subseteq U
is open. By Lemma
3.6
(c)
, the open sets
U_{i}
for
i\in S
cover
U
. Put
k=\#S
and
Q_{k,n}\coloneqq[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{n-k}
. By Lemma
3.6
(a)
, for every
i\in S
, we have a continuous map

(29)

\psi_{i}=(g,({v}_{j})_{j\in S\setminus\{i\}};\vartheta)\colon U_{i}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n},

see (
14
), (
19
) and (
20
) for definitions of
g
,
v_{i}
and
\vartheta
, respectively.

The pairs
(U_{i},\psi_{i})
will be called the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts corresponding to
the chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
. The following lemmas, which we will prove later in this section, assert that these charts indeed make
A
a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-manifold with boundary.

Lemma 3.11
.

Each map (
29
) is a homeomorphism onto an open subset of
Q_{k,n}
. Moreover the restriction
\psi_{i}
to
U_{i}\setminus\partial A
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
for the natural smooth structure in
A\setminus\partial A
.

Lemma
3.11
endows
A
with a structure of topological manifold with boundary
\partial A=\{g=0\}=\pi^{-1}(D)
. The
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure is provided by Lemma
3.12
below, which studies the transition functions between charts (
29
), possibly associated with different charts adapted to
f
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
U_{X}
,
U^{\prime}_{X}
be charts adapted to
f
, let
S
,
S^{\prime}
be their associated index sets (
12
), and let
k=\#S
,
k^{\prime}=\#S^{\prime}
. For
i\in S
and
j\in S^{\prime}
let
(U_{i},\psi_{i})
,
(U_{j}^{\prime},\psi_{j}^{\prime})
be the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts defined in (
29
) above. Then the transition map

\psi^{\prime}_{j}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}|_{\psi_{i}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})}\colon\psi_{i}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})\longrightarrow\psi^{\prime}_{j}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})

is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism between open subsets of
Q_{k,n}
and
Q_{k^{\prime},n}
.

Figure 3
.
Coordinate charts (
29
) of the A’Campo space, cf. Figure
2
.

Since for every
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the space
Q_{k,n}
is a
2n
-manifold with boundary, refining the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts corresponding to each adapted chart
U_{X}
, we obtain an honest
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas for a neighborhood of
\partial A
, whose charts have images in half-euclidean spaces. In order to have a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas of the whole
A
, we notice that by Lemma
3.11
this
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure agrees with the natural smooth structure in
A\setminus\partial A=X\setminus D
.

Proposition
3.13
below allows to speak about
the A’Campo space of
f
, as a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-manifold with boundary. The key properties of this space are listed in Proposition
3.14
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Fix two pairs
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime})
adapted to
f
. Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be the corresponding A’Campo spaces, each endowed with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure defined above. Then the homeomorphism
\Phi:A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
introduced in Proposition
3.7
(e)
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
diffeomorphism.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
A
be the A’Campo space of
f
, with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure defined above.

(a)

The map
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Its restriction
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow X\setminus D
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

(b)

Let
g,\theta
be as in (
14
), (
15
). Then the map
(g,\theta)\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-submersion. In particular, the map
f_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}
extending
f
in (
24
) is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

(c)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, the function
\overline{v}_{i}:A\longrightarrow[-1,1]
associated in (
23
) to the pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
adapted to
f
, is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Example 3.15
.

The
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure on
A
constructed above may not be
\mathcal{C}^{2}
. Indeed, let
X=\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{e})
, and let
f=z_{1}z_{2}
. Consider two charts
U_{X}
,
U_{X}^{\prime}
adapted to
f
: one with the standard coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2})
, and the other with coordinates
(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})
given by

(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})=(e^{-1}\cdot z_{1},e\cdot z_{2}).

Let
(U_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(U_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{1}^{\prime})
be the corresponding
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts. We will show that the transition map
\psi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}\colon(g,v_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(g,v_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2},\theta_{2}^{\prime})
is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
along
\{t_{2}=0,u_{2}=0\}
.

Using definitions (
16
), (
18
) of
t_{2}
and
u_{2}
, we compute

\begin{split}t_{2}^{\prime}&=-\frac{1}{\log|z_{2}^{\prime}|}=-\frac{1}{\log|z_{2}|+1}=-\frac{1}{-t_{2}^{-1}+1}=\frac{t_{2}}{1-t_{2}},\\
u_{2}^{\prime}&=\eta\left(\frac{t}{t_{2}^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{1}{1-(\log\frac{t}{t_{2}}+\log(1-t_{2}))}=\frac{1}{u_{2}^{-1}-\log(1-t_{2})}=\frac{u_{2}}{1-u_{2}\log(1-t_{2})},\end{split}

so
t_{2}^{\prime}-t_{2}=\frac{t_{2}^{2}}{1-t_{2}}
and
u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{2}=\frac{u_{2}^{2}\log(1-t_{2})}{1-u_{2}\log(1-t_{2})}
, cf. Lemmas
3.9
(c)
and
3.26
(c)
. Substituting these equalities to the definition (
19
) of
v_{2}
, we get

(30)

v_{2}^{\prime}=v_{2}+\frac{t_{2}^{2}}{1-t_{2}}-\frac{u_{2}^{2}\log(1-t_{2})}{1-u_{2}\log(1-t_{2})}.

Now, we will approach
\{t_{2}=0,u_{2}=0\}
along
\{t_{2}=0,u_{2}>0\}=\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}A_{1,2}^{\circ}
and
\{t_{2}>0,u_{2}=0\}=A_{1}^{\circ}
. In Figure
2(a)
, these are, respectively, the circle-arc and the bold vertical line. If
t_{2}=0
,
u_{2}>0
then (
30
) reads as
v_{2}^{\prime}=v_{2}
, so
(\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{2}})^{2}v_{2}^{\prime}=0
. In turn, if
t_{2}>0
,
u_{2}=0
then
v_{2}=t_{2}
, so (
30
) reads as
v_{2}^{\prime}=v_{2}+\frac{v_{2}^{2}}{1-v_{2}}=v_{2}+v_{2}^{2}+v_{2}^{3}+\dots
, and therefore
(\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{2}})^{2}v_{2}^{\prime}=1
. This shows that the transition map
v_{2}\mapsto v_{2}^{\prime}
is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, as claimed.

In the remaining part of Section
3.3
, we prove Lemmas
3.11
,
3.12
and Propositions
3.13
,
3.14
stated above. First, in Section
3.3.2
we prove Lemma
3.11
, which makes
A
a topological manifold with boundary. To prove that this manifold is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, we will need some preparatory results, which will be useful for the construction of a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
atlas, too. In Section
3.3.3
, we gather some handy identities. Next, in Section
3.3.4
we prove that, as we approach
\partial A
, the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts corresponding to different charts adapted to
f
become
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-close to each other. To state it in a precise way, we introduce a technical notion of differential forms
bounded from
X
. With these preparations at hand, we will prove Lemma
3.12
and Propositions
3.13
,
3.14
in Section
3.3.6
.

3.3.2.
The A’Campo space is a topological manifold

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.11
, which implies that the formulas (
29
) define topological charts. We begin with two simple observations.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
. Let
S=\{i:U_{X}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset\}
be the associated index set (
12
), and for
i\in S
let
U_{i}=\{w_{i}>\frac{1}{n+1}\}
be the open subset of
U\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
defined in (
28
). Then

(a)

The intersection
U_{i}\cap\partial A
is an open subset of
\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
.

(b)

Fix
x\in U_{i}\cap\partial A
. Then
x\in A_{I}^{\circ}
for some
I\subseteq S
containing
i
. Moreover, for every
j\in S
we have
v_{j}(x)=-u_{j}(x)\leqslant 0
if
j\in I
and
v_{j}(x)=t_{j}(x)>0
if
j\not\in I
.

(c)

Fix
i\in S
and
I\subseteq S
not containing
i
. Then the restrictions of
t_{i},w_{i},u_{i}
and
v_{i}
to
A_{I}^{\circ}\cap U
are pullbacks of smooth functions on
X_{I}^{\circ}\cap U_{X}
