.

(a)
By Lemma
3.6
(a)
, the function
w_{i}
is continuous, so
U_{i}=\{w_{i}>\frac{1}{n+1}\}
is an open subset of
A
. To see that
U_{i}\cap\partial A\subseteq\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, fix
x\in U_{i}\cap\partial A
. Since
w_{i}(x)>0
, the formula (
18
) implies that
t_{i}(x)=t(x)\cdot w_{i}(x)^{-1}
. If
x\in\partial A
then
t(x)=0
, so we get
t_{i}(x)=0
, i.e.
x\in\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, as claimed.

(b)
By
(a)
, we have
i\in I
. If
j\in I
then by definition (
16
) of
t_{j}
we have
t_{j}(x)=0
, so
v_{j}(x)=-u_{j}(x)\leqslant 0
by (
19
). If
j\not\in I
then
u_{j}(x)=0
by Lemma
3.6
(b)
, so
v_{j}(x)=t_{j}(x)>0
.

(c)
For
I=\emptyset
we have
A_{I}^{\circ}\cap U=U\setminus\partial A=U_{X}\setminus D
, where the assertion is clear. Assume
I\neq\emptyset
. Since
i\not\in I
, by Lemma
3.6
(b)
the restrictions to
A_{I}^{\circ}\cap U
of
w_{i}
, and hence of
u_{i}
, are zero. Thus
v_{i}
restricts to
t_{i}
, which is a smooth function on
U_{X}\setminus D_{i}
, in particular on
X_{I}^{\circ}\cap U_{X}
.

∎

The function defining the
“hybrid”
coordinate
v_{i}
in (
19
) has the following useful property.

Lemma 3.17
.

Fix a positive number
\check{t}>0
and consider a function
\check{v}_{\check{t}}\colon[\check{t},\infty)\ni s\mapsto s-\eta(\check{t}\cdot s^{-1})\in\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

The function
\check{v}_{\check{t}}
is strictly increasing. Its image equals
[\check{t}-1,\infty)
.

(b)

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
, and for
i
in the associated index set (
12
) let
v_{i}
be the function defined in (
19
). Then
v_{i}(x)=\check{v}_{t(x)}(t_{i}(x))
for every
x\in U_{X}\setminus D
.

For
s\in[\check{t},\infty)
we have
\check{t}s^{-1}\in(0,1]
, so the function
\check{v}_{\check{t}}
is well defined. Its derivative is
\check{v}_{\check{t}}^{\prime}(s)=1+\eta^{\prime}(\check{t}s^{-1})\cdot\check{t}s^{-2}>1
, since
\eta
is strictly increasing. Thus
\check{v}_{\check{t}}
is strictly increasing, too. Since
\check{v}_{\check{t}}(\check{t})=\check{t}-\eta(1)=\check{t}-1
, this proves
(a)
. Part
(b)
follows directly from the definition (
19
) of
v_{i}
.

∎

Recall that on
A\setminus\partial A
we have the natural smooth structure pulled back from
X\setminus D
. The following lemma asserts that the candidate charts (
29
) are compatible with this structure.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
, and let
\psi_{i}\colon U_{i}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
be an associated chart (
29
). Then the restriction
\psi_{i}|_{U_{i}\setminus\partial A}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

Reordering the components of
D
if needed, we can assume that the index set associated to
U_{X}
is
\{1,\dots,k\}
, and our fixed chart is
\psi_{1}=(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
.

Recall from Definition
3.1
that
(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
is a holomorphic coordinate system on
U_{X}
, and
D\cap U_{X}
is the zero locus of
f|_{U_{X}}=\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}^{m_{i}}
. Therefore, the map
U_{X}\setminus D\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
given by polar coordinates
(r_{1},\dots,r_{k},\vartheta)
is a diffeomorphism onto its image.
Since by definition (
16
) we have
t_{i}=-(m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}
, the same is true for the map
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
. Thus
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
is a smooth coordinate system on
U_{1}\setminus\partial A
, for the smooth structure inherited from
X
.

We claim that the map
\varphi\coloneqq(t,t_{2},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)\colon U_{1}\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image as well. Recall that
|f||_{U_{X}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}^{m_{i}}
,
t=-(\log|f|)^{-1}
and
t_{i}=-(m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}
, so

(31)

t^{-1}=-\log|f|=-\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}\log|z_{i}|=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}^{-1},

thus in our smooth coordinates
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
we have

\tfrac{\partial t}{\partial t_{1}}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\textstyle(\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}^{-1})^{-1}=-t^{2}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}t_{1}^{-1}=t^{2}t_{1}^{-2}>0.

Therefore,
\varphi
is a local diffeomorphism. Suppose that for some
x,y\in U\setminus\partial A
we have
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
. Then
t(x)=t(y)>0
and
t_{i}(x)=t_{i}(y)>0
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, so
t_{1}(x)=t_{1}(y)
by (
31
). Since
\vartheta(x)=\vartheta(y)
, too, we get
x=y
. Thus
\varphi
is injective, hence a diffeomorphism onto its image, as claimed.

The above claim shows that
(t,t_{2},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
is another smooth coordinate system on
U\setminus\partial A
. With respect to these coordinates, we have
\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}=\eta^{\prime}(g)>0
by (
14
) and
\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial t_{j}}=0
for
i\neq j
by (
19
). For a fixed
t>0
, the function
t_{i}\mapsto t_{i}-\eta(t^{-1}\cdot t_{i})=v_{i}
is strictly increasing by Lemma
3.17
, so
\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial t_{i}}>0
. Thus the Jacobian determinant of
\psi_{1}
in coordinates
(t,t_{2},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
is
\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{k}\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial t_{i}}>0
, so
\psi_{1}
is a local diffeomorphism.

Assume
\psi_{1}(x)=\psi_{1}(y)
for some
x,y\in U\setminus\partial A
. Then
g(x)=g(y)>0
, so both
t(x)
and
t(y)
are equal to some number
\check{t}>0
. By Lemma
3.17
(b)
,
v_{i}(x)=\check{v}_{\check{t}}(t_{i}(x))
for every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, and similarly
v_{i}(y)=\check{v}_{\check{t}}(t_{i}(y))
. Since
v_{i}(x)=v_{i}(y)
, Lemma
3.17
(a)
implies that
t_{i}(x)=t_{i}(y)
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. The equality
\psi_{1}(x)=\psi_{1}(y)
implies that
\vartheta(x)=\vartheta(y)
, too, so the values of all coordinates
(t,t_{2},\dots,t_{k},\vartheta)
at
x
and
y
are equal. Thus
x=y
, so
\psi_{1}
is injective, as claimed.

∎

Lemma 3.19
.

The image of each chart (
29
) is an open subset of
Q_{k,n}
.

Fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
. As before, we order the components of
D
so that the index set (
12
) associated to
U_{X}
is
\{1,\dots,k\}
, and study the map
\psi_{1}\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
introduced in (
29
).

Fix a point
x\in U_{1}
. We need to prove that some neighborhood of
\psi_{1}(x)
in
Q_{n,k}
lies in
\psi_{1}(U_{1})
. This is clear if
x\not\in\partial A
, since by Lemma
3.18
the restriction
\psi_{1}|_{U\setminus\partial A}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Hence we can assume
x\in\partial A
. Take a sequence
(y^{\nu})\subseteq Q_{k,n}
such that
y^{\nu}\rightarrow\psi_{1}(x)
. We need to show that
y^{\nu}\in\psi_{1}(U_{1})
for all
\nu\gg 1
. To do this, we will construct a sequence
(x^{\nu})
such that
\psi_{1}(x^{\nu})=y^{\nu}
and
x^{\nu}\in U_{1}
for
\nu\gg 1
.

Write
y^{\nu}=(g^{\nu},v^{\nu}_{1},\dots,v_{k}^{\nu},\vartheta^{\nu})
for some
g^{\nu}\geqslant 0
,
v_{i}^{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\vartheta^{\nu}\in(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{n-k}
. Then
g^{\nu}\rightarrow g(x)=0
,
v_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow v_{i}(x)
and
\vartheta^{\nu}\rightarrow\vartheta(x)
. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume that either
g^{\nu}>0
for all
\nu
, or
g^{\nu}=0
for all
\nu
: in other words,
y^{\nu}
approaches
\psi_{1}(x)
either from
\operatorname{Int}Q_{k,n}
, or from
\partial Q_{k,n}
.

Consider the case
g^{\nu}>0
. Put
t^{\nu}:=\eta^{-1}(g^{\nu})
. Since
\eta^{-1}
is continuous, we have
t^{\nu}\rightarrow\eta^{-1}(0)=0
. By definition (
28
) of
U_{i}
, we have
w_{1}(x)>\tfrac{1}{n+1}
, so by Lemma
3.6
(c)
, for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
w_{i}(x)=1-w_{1}(x)-\sum_{j\neq 1,i}w_{j}(x)<1-\tfrac{1}{n+1}<1
. The formula (
19
) for
v_{i}
implies that
v_{i}(x)>-1
. Hence there is a positive number
\varepsilon>0
such that, after taking
\nu
sufficiently large, we have
t^{\nu}<\varepsilon
and
v_{i}^{\nu}>\varepsilon-1
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Therefore, each number
v_{i}^{\nu}
lies in the image of the function
\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}
from Lemma
3.17
(a)
. In other words, there is a number
t_{i}^{\nu}\geqslant t^{\nu}
such that
\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}(t_{i}^{\nu})=v_{i}^{\nu}
.

We claim that for each
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, the sequence
(t_{i}^{\nu})
is bounded. Suppose the contrary. Passing to a subsequence, we get
t_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow\infty
. Since
t^{\nu}\rightarrow 0
, we get
t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}\rightarrow 0
, so
\eta(t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1})\rightarrow\eta(0)=0
. Now
v_{i}^{\nu}=\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}(t_{i}^{\nu})=t_{i}^{\nu}-\eta(t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1})\rightarrow\infty-0=\infty
, a contradiction since
v_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow v_{i}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
.

Thus passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
t_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow\check{t}_{i}
for some
\check{t}_{i}\geqslant 0
. We claim that
\check{t}_{i}=t_{i}(x)
. If
\check{t}_{i}>0
, then the sequence
(t_{i}^{\nu})
is bounded from below by a positive number, so
t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}\rightarrow 0
, and therefore
\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}(t_{i}^{\nu})=t_{i}^{\nu}-\eta(t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1})\rightarrow\check{t}_{i}
. Hence
\check{t}_{i}=v_{i}(x)
. In particular,
v_{i}(x)>0
, so by Lemma
3.16
(b)
we have
v_{i}(x)=t_{i}(x)
, and therefore
\check{t}_{i}=t_{i}(x)
, as claimed. On the other hand, if
\check{t}_{i}=0
then
v_{i}(x)=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}(t_{i}^{\nu})=\lim_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}-\eta(t^{\nu}\cdot(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1})\leqslant 0
, so Lemma
3.16
(b)
gives
t_{i}(x)=0
, as needed.

Thus for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
t_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow t_{i}(x)
. Put
w_{i}^{\nu}:=t^{\nu}\cdot(t^{\nu}_{i})^{-1}
. By definition of
t_{i}^{\nu}
we have
v_{i}^{\nu}=\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}(t_{i}^{\nu})
, so
v_{i}^{\nu}=t_{i}^{\nu}-\eta(w_{i}^{\nu})
by definition of the function
\check{v}_{t^{\nu}}
. Hence
w_{i}^{\nu}=\eta^{-1}(t_{i}^{\nu}-v_{i}^{\nu})\rightarrow\eta^{-1}(t_{i}(x)-v_{i}(x))=\eta^{-1}(u_{i}(x))=w_{i}(x)
.

By definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
, we have
w_{1}(x)>\frac{1}{n+1}
. Lemma
3.6
(c)
implies that
\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}(x)=1-w_{1}(x)<\tfrac{n}{n+1}
. Hence for
\nu\gg 1
we have
\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}^{\nu}<\tfrac{n}{n+1}
, too. By definition of
w_{i}^{\nu}
we get
\sum_{i=2}^{k}(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}<\tfrac{n}{n+1}(t^{\nu})^{-1}<(t^{\nu})^{-1}
, so the positive number
t^{\nu}_{1}\coloneqq((t^{\nu})^{-1}-\sum_{i=2}^{k}(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1})^{-1}
satisfies
(t^{\nu}_{1})^{-1}=(t^{\nu})^{-1}-\sum_{i=2}^{k}(t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}>\tfrac{1}{n+1}(t^{\nu})^{-1}
, and therefore
t_{1}^{\nu}<(n+1)t^{\nu}
. Since
t^{\nu}\rightarrow 0
, we infer that
t_{1}^{\nu}\rightarrow 0
, too. By Lemma
3.16
(a)
we have
t_{1}(x)=0
, so the convergence
t_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow t_{i}(x)
holds for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
put
r_{i}^{\nu}:=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}}
. By (
16
), we have
r_{i}(x)=0
if
t_{i}(x)=0
and
r_{i}(x)=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i}(x))^{-1}}
otherwise, so
r_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow r_{i}(x)
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Let
x_{\log}
be the image of
x
in the Kato–Nakayama space
X_{\log}
. In the chart (
21
), the coordinates of
x_{\log}
are
(r_{1}(x),\dots,r_{k}(x),\vartheta(x))
. Since
r_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow r_{i}(x)
and
\vartheta^{\nu}\rightarrow\vartheta(x)
by definition of
\vartheta^{\nu}
, for
\nu\gg 1
the point
(r_{1}^{\nu},\dots,r_{k}^{\nu},\vartheta^{\nu})
lies in the image of that chart, too. Let
x_{\log}^{\nu}
be the corresponding point of
X_{\log}
, and let
x^{\nu}
be its image in
X
. Since
{r}^{\nu}_{i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
x^{\nu}\not\in D
, so we can identify
x^{\nu}
with its preimage in
A\setminus\partial A
. This way,
\psi_{1}(x^{\nu})=y^{\nu}
and
x^{\nu}\rightarrow x
, so
x^{\nu}\in U_{1}
for
\nu\gg 1
because
U_{1}
is open. This ends the proof in case
g^{\nu}>0
.

Consider now the case when
g^{\nu}=0
for all
\nu
. Say that
x\in A_{I}^{\circ}
for some
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}
. By Lemma
3.16
(b)
we have
1\in I
,
v_{i}(x)=-u_{i}(x)
if
i\in I
and
v_{i}(x)=t_{i}(x)>0
if
i\not\in I
. Since
v_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow v_{i}(x)
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, after passing to a subsequence we get a subset
J\subseteq I\setminus\{1\}
such that
v_{i}^{\nu}\leqslant 0
if
i\in J
and
v_{i}^{\nu}>0
if
i\not\in J
.

For
i\in J
put
w_{i}^{\nu}=\eta^{-1}(-v_{i}^{\nu})
. Since
\eta^{-1}
is continuous, we get
w_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow\eta^{-1}(-v_{i}(x))=w_{i}(x)
. For
i\in I\setminus J
we have
-u_{i}(x)=v_{i}(x)=0
, so
w_{i}(x)=0
. For
i\not\in I
we have
w_{i}(x)=0
by Lemma
3.6
(b)
. Thus by Lemma
3.6
(c)
we have
\sum_{i\in J}w_{i}(x)=1-w_{1}(x)<\tfrac{n}{n+1}
, where the inequality follows from definition (
28
) of
U_{i}
. Since
w_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow w_{i}(x)
for
i\in J
, we infer that for
\nu\gg 1
, the numbers
w_{1}^{\nu}\coloneqq 1-\sum_{i\in J}w_{i}^{\nu}
are positive. Put
v_{1}^{\nu}=-\eta(w_{1}^{\nu})
. Then
v_{1}^{\nu}\rightarrow-\eta(w_{1}(x))=v_{1}(x)
by Lemma
3.16
(b)
. Let
u^{\nu}
be the point of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
whose
i
-th coordinate, for
i\in J\cup\{1\}
, is
-v_{i}^{\nu}
, and the remaining coordinates are zero. Then
u^{\nu}\in\Delta_{J\cup\{1\}}\subseteq\Delta^{N-1}
.

For
i\not\in J
put
r_{i}^{\nu}=e^{-(m_{i}v_{i}^{\nu})^{-1}}
. Like before, we have
r_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow e^{-(m_{i}v_{i}(x))^{-1}}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i}(x))^{-1}}=r_{i}(x)
. For
i\in J\cup\{1\}
put
r_{i}^{\nu}=0
, so
r_{i}^{\nu}\rightarrow r_{i}(x)
, too. The image of
x
in the chart (
21
) of
X_{\log}
has coordinates
(r_{1}(x),\dots,r_{k}(x),\vartheta(x))
, so for
\nu\gg 1
the point
(r_{1}^{\nu},\dots,r_{k}^{\nu},\vartheta^{\nu})
lies in the image of this chart, too. The corresponding point
x_{\log}^{\nu}\in X_{\log}
lies in
(X_{J\cup\{1\}}^{\circ})_{\log}
. Since
u^{\nu}\in\Delta_{J\cup\{1\}}
, the pair
x^{\nu}\coloneqq(x_{\log}^{\nu},u^{\nu})\in X_{\log}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
lies in
A_{J\cup\{1\}}^{\circ}
by Lemma
3.10
(c)
. By construction,
x^{\nu}\rightarrow x
and
\psi_{1}(x^{\nu})=y^{\nu}
. Since
U_{1}
is open, we conclude that
x^{\nu}\in U_{1}
for
\nu\gg 1
, as required.

∎

Lemma 3.20
.

Each chart (
29
) is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Fix a chart (
29
) corresponding to a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, say
\psi_{1}\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{k.n}
. Let
S
be the index set (
12
) associated to
U_{X}
. By Definition
3.1
(d)
, the coordinates of
U_{X}
extend continuously to the compact closure
\overline{U}_{X}
. Together with Lemma
3.6
(a)
, this implies that
\psi_{1}
extends to a continuous map
\overline{U}_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
, which we will denote by the same letter. Since
\overline{U}_{1}
is compact, it is sufficient to prove that this extension is injective.

For a subset
I\subseteq S
put
V_{I}=\overline{U}_{1}\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
. We have
\psi_{1}(V_{\emptyset})\subseteq\operatorname{Int}Q_{k,n}
and
\psi(V_{I})\subseteq\partial Q_{k,n}
for all
I\neq\emptyset
, so
\psi_{1}(V_{\emptyset})\cap\psi_{1}(V_{I})=\emptyset
. Lemma
3.16
(b)
implies that
\psi_{1}(V_{I})\cap\psi_{1}(V_{J})=\emptyset
for all
I\neq J
. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove the injectivity of each restriction
\psi_{1}|_{V_{I}}
.

For
I=\emptyset
, this follows Lemma
3.18
. Assume
I\neq\emptyset
and fix
x\in V_{I}
. By Lemma
3.10
(c)
, the point
x
is a pair
(x_{\log},x_{\Delta})\in(X_{I}^{\circ})_{\log}\times\Delta_{I}
. In coordinates (
21
) on
X_{\log}
, the point
x_{\log}
is given by
((r_{i}(x))_{i\in S},\vartheta(x))
, where
r_{i}(x)=0
if
i\in I
and, by (
16
),
r_{i}(x)=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i}(x))^{-1}}
if
i\not\in I
. By Lemma
3.16
(b)
, for
i\not\in I
we have
i\neq 1
and
t_{i}(x)=v_{i}(x)
, so the point
x_{\log}
is determined by the value of
\psi_{1}
at
x
. In turn, the
i
-th coordinate of
x_{\Delta}\in\Delta_{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
is
\overline{u}_{i}(x)
if
i\in I
and zero otherwise. For
i\in I
, we have
\overline{u}_{i}(x)=u_{i}(x)
by Lemma
3.6
(e)
and
u_{i}(x)=-v_{i}(x)
by Lemma
3.16
(b)
. Hence for
i\neq 1
, the
i
-th coordinate of
x_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is determined by the value of
\psi_{1}
at
x
. The remaining coordinate is determined by the fact that
x_{\Delta}
lies in the rounded simplex
\Delta_{I}
.

∎

By Lemma
3.20
, each chart (
29
) is a homeomorphism onto its image, which is an open subset of
Q_{k,n}
by Lemma
3.19
. By Lemma
3.18
, the chart (
29
) restricts to a diffeomorphism away from
\partial A
, as needed.

∎

3.3.3.
Useful computations

By Lemma
3.11
, our atlas makes
A
a topological manifold. Now, our goal is to show that this manifold is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Lemmas
3.21
–
3.22
gather some technical computations which will be useful throughout the remaining part of Section
3
. To state them in a convenient way, we introduce the following notation.

Recall that in (
14
) we defined
t=-(\log|f|)^{-1}
and
g=\eta(t)
. We put
t^{\prime}=\tfrac{d}{dg}t
, so
t^{\prime}=\zeta(g)
, where
\zeta\colon s\mapsto s^{-2}e^{1-s^{-1}}
is the derivative of the inverse of
\eta
, see (
13
). More generally, for every
l\geqslant 0
we put

(32)

t^{(l)}=(\tfrac{d}{dg})^{l}\,t=\zeta^{(l-1)}(g).

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
. For every
i
in its associated index set (
12
), we define functions

(33)

\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2},

where
t_{i},u_{i}
are the functions introduced in (
16
) and (
18
). Note that
\sigma_{i}=t_{i}\rho_{i}^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
. Fix
i
in its associated index set (
12
). The functions introduced in Section
3.1.2
and in (
32
), (
33
) above satisfy the following identities on
U_{X}\setminus D
:

(a)

r_{i}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
,

(b)

t=t_{i}\cdot w_{i}
,

(c)

t^{\prime}=t\cdot g^{-2}=t\cdot(1-\log t)^{2}
,

(d)

for every integer
l\geqslant 0
, there is a polynomial
p_{l}\in\mathbb{R}[s]
such that
t^{(l)}=t\cdot p_{l}(\log t)
,

(e)

u_{i}=g\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1}
,

(f)

\rho_{i}=(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}\cdot(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}
,

(g)

\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}
, where
\eta\colon s\mapsto(1-\log s)^{-1}
is the function defined in (
13
),

(h)

\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}
,

(i)

for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a bounded function
l\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\setminus D)
such that
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot l
.

(a)
In (
16
), the function
t_{i}
was defined as
t_{i}=-(m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}
, so
\log r_{i}=-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}
, and therefore
r_{i}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
, as claimed.

(b)
In (
18
), the function
w_{i}
was defined as
w_{i}=tt_{i}^{-1}
, so
t=t_{i}w_{i}
.

(c)
Recall from (
14
) and (
13
) that
g=\eta(t)=(1-\log t)^{-1}
, so
t=e^{1-g^{-1}}
. Now
t^{\prime}=\frac{dt}{dg}=e^{1-g^{-1}}g^{-2}=tg^{-2}=t(1-\log t)^{2}
, as needed.

(d)
We argue by induction on
l\geqslant 0
. For
l=0
we put
p_{0}=1
. Assume that for some
l\geqslant 0
, there is
p_{l}\in\mathbb{R}[s]
such that
t^{(l)}=p_{l}(\log t)t
. Since
t=e^{1-g^{-1}}
and
\log t=1-g^{-1}
, we have

\begin{split}t^{(l+1)}&=\tfrac{d}{dg}t^{(l)}=\tfrac{d}{dg}[p_{l}(1-g^{-1})e^{1-g^{-1}}]=p_{l}^{\prime}(1-g^{-1})g^{-2}e^{1-g^{-1}}+p_{l}(1-g^{-1})g^{-2}e^{1-g^{-1}}=\\
&=(p_{l}(1-g^{-1})+p_{l}^{\prime}(1-g^{-1}))g^{-2}e^{1-g^{-1}}=(p_{l}(\log t)+p_{l}^{\prime}(\log t))(1-\log t)^{2}t.\end{split}

The inductive claim follows by putting
p_{l+1}(s)=(p_{l}(s)+p_{l}^{\prime}(s))(1-s)^{2}\in\mathbb{R}[s]
.

(e)
The function
u_{i}
is defined in (
18
) as
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
. By (
18
), we have
(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}=(1-\log t+\log t_{i})^{-1}
. Since by definition
g=(1-\log t)^{-1}
, we have
(1-\log t+\log t_{i})^{-1}=(g^{-1}+\log t_{i})^{-1}=g\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1}
. Thus eventually
u_{i}=g\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1}
, as claimed.

(f)
The function
\rho_{i}
was defined in (
33
) by
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
. Substituting the formula for
u_{i}
from
(e)
, we get
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+g^{2}\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2})^{-1}=(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}\cdot(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}
.

(g)
By (
18
), we have
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
, so
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-2}w_{i}^{-1}=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}
.

(h)
Substituting the formula for
u_{i}
from
(e)
to
(g)
, we get
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=g^{2}\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}
. By (
18
) we have
w_{i}=t\cdot t_{i}^{-1}
, so
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=g^{2}\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}\cdot t^{-1}t_{i}
. In turn, by
(c)
we have
t^{\prime}=tg^{-2}
, so
t^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=tg^{-2}\cdot g^{2}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}\cdot t^{-1}t_{i}=t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}
, as claimed.

(i)
Recall from (
14
) that
g=\eta(t)=(1-\log t)^{-1}
. By
(b)
, we have
t=t_{i}w_{i}
, so
g=(1-\log t_{i}-\log w_{i})^{-1}
. Substituting this formula to
(h)
, we get

\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\log t_{i}}{1-\log t_{i}-\log w_{i}}\right)^{-2}=t_{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1-\log w_{i}}{1-\log t_{i}-\log w_{i}}\right)^{-2}=t_{i}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\log t_{i}}{1-\log w_{i}}\right)^{2}.

Since
w_{i}\in(0,1]
, we have
\log w_{i}\leqslant 0
, so the function
s\coloneqq(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
is bounded. Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and put
\delta=\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)>0
. Then

\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}\cdot(1-s\cdot\log t_{i})^{2}=t_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot(t_{i}^{\delta}-s\cdot t_{i}^{\delta}\log t_{i})^{2}.

Since
\delta>0
, we have
t_{i}^{\delta}\log t_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, so
l\coloneqq(t_{i}^{\delta}-s\cdot t_{i}^{\delta}\log t_{i})^{2}
is bounded, as claimed.

∎

We write
d
for the exterior derivative. We will apply it to smooth functions on (subsets of)
X\setminus D
, and study the behavior of the resulting forms as we approach
D
.

Lemma 3.22
.

In the setting of Lemma
3.21
, the following identities hold on
U_{X}\setminus D
:

(a)

dr_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{-2}r_{i}\,dt_{i}
,

(b)

dv_{i}=(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})\,dt_{i}-t_{i}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg
,

(c)

dt_{i}=\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg)
,

(d)

dt_{i}=t_{i}\cdot(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}\cdot((1+g\log t_{i})^{2}\,dv_{i}+dg)
,

(e)

dw_{i}=-\rho_{i}(w_{i}\,dv_{i}-t^{\prime}\,dg)
,

(f)

du_{i}=-\rho_{i}(u_{i}^{2}\,dv_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)
,

(g)

du_{i}=-t_{i}^{-1}(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)
.

(a)
By Lemma
3.21
(a)
, we have

r_{i}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
, so
dr_{i}=m_{i}\cdot(m_{i}t_{i})^{-2}\cdot e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}\,dt_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{-2}r_{i}\,dt_{i}
, as claimed.

(b)
The functions
v_{i}
,
w_{i}
were defined in (
19
) and (
18
) as
v_{i}=t_{i}-\eta(w_{i})
and
w_{i}=tt_{i}^{-1}
. Hence

dv_{i}=dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})\cdot d(tt_{i}^{-1})=dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})\cdot(-tt_{i}^{-2}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}\,dt)=(1+\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})tt_{i}^{-2})\,dt_{i}-t_{i}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})\,dt.

By Lemma
3.21
(g)
, we have
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}
. Since
t=t_{i}w_{i}
by Lemma
3.21
(b)
, we get
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})tt_{i}^{-2}=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\cdot t_{i}w_{i}\cdot t_{i}^{-2}=u_{i}^{2}t_{i}^{-1}
.
In turn,
dt=t^{\prime}dg
by definition (
33
) of
t^{\prime}
. Thus
dv_{i}=(1+u_{i}^{2}t_{i}^{-1})dt_{i}-t_{i}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg
, as claimed.

(c)
Solving
(b)
for
dt_{i}
, we get

dt_{i}=(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})^{-1}\cdot(dv_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}\cdot(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg).

We have
(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}=\rho_{i}
by (
33
) and
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}
by Lemma
3.21
(g)
, so
dt_{i}=\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg)
, as claimed.

(d)
Substituting the formula for
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})
from Lemma
3.21
(g)
to Lemma
3.21
(h)
, we get the identity
u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}=t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}
. Substituting it to
(c)
gives

dt_{i}=\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}\,dg)=\rho_{i}t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-2}\cdot((1+g\log t_{i})^{2}dv_{i}+dg).

The claim follows by substituting the formula for
\rho_{i}
from Lemma
3.21
(f)
.

(e)
The function
w_{i}
was defined in (
18
) as
w_{i}=tt_{i}^{-1}
, so
dw_{i}=-tt_{i}^{-2}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}\,dt=-w_{i}t_{i}^{-1}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}\,dt
. We have
dt_{i}=\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg)
by
(c)
and
dt=t^{\prime}\,dg
by definition (
33
) of
t^{\prime}
, so

dw_{i}=-w_{i}t_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg)+t_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg=-\rho_{i}(w_{i}\,dv_{i}+(u_{i}^{2}-\rho_{i}^{-1})t_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg).

By definition (
33
) of
\rho_{i}
, we have
(u_{i}^{2}-\rho_{i}^{-1})t_{i}^{-1}=(u_{i}^{2}-t_{i}-u_{i}^{2})t_{i}^{-1}=-1
, which proves the claim.

(f)
By (
18
), we have
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})
, so using
(e)
we get
du_{i}=\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})dw_{i}=-\rho_{i}(\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})w_{i}\,dv_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)
. By Lemma
3.21
(g)
, we have
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})w_{i}=u_{i}^{2}
, so
du_{i}=-\rho_{i}(u_{i}^{2}\,dv_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)
, as claimed.

(g)
Substituting
(b)
to
(f)
, we get

\begin{split}du_{i}&=-\rho_{i}(u_{i}^{2}\cdot((1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})\,dt_{i}-t_{i}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)=\\
&=-\rho_{i}(u_{i}^{2}(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})\,dt_{i}-(u_{i}^{2}t_{i}^{-1}+1)\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)=\\
&=-\rho_{i}(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)=-t_{i}^{-1}(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg),\end{split}

where the last equality follows from definition (
33
) of
\rho_{i}
.

∎

3.3.4.
Forms bounded from
X

Once we endow
A
with a smooth structure, it will be important to distinguish those forms on
A
which come from bounded forms on
X
. To this end, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
, and let
V
be an open subset of
\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. On
V\setminus\partial A
, we consider the natural smooth structure pulled back from
\pi(V)\setminus D
. We say that a form
\beta\in\Omega^{*}(V\setminus\partial A)
is
bounded from
X
if
\beta=\pi^{*}\beta^{\prime}
for some
\beta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{*}(\pi(V)\setminus D)
which is bounded in the usual sense, with respect to coordinates
(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
of the adapted chart
U_{X}
.

Remark 3.24
.

Let
U_{i}
be a domain of a chart (
29
). A function on
U_{i}\setminus\partial A
is bounded from
X
if and only if it is bounded. Since the closure
\overline{U}_{i}
is compact by Definition
3.1
(d)
, all continuous functions on
\overline{U}_{i}
, in particular those from Lemma
3.6
(a)
, are bounded from
X
.

If two forms
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are bounded from
X
, then so are
\beta+\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta\wedge\beta^{\prime}
. Nonetheless,
d\beta
might not be bounded from
X
. For example, functions
\theta_{i}
,
t_{i}
are bounded from
X
, but the
1
-forms
d\theta_{i}
,
dt_{i}
are not. Indeed,
d\theta_{i}=-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\,d^{c}r_{i}
has a
“logarithmic pole”
along
D_{i}
. For
dt_{i}
, note that by Lemma
3.22
(a)
we have
dt_{i}=m_{i}t_{i}^{2}r_{i}^{-1}\,dr_{i}
, so Lemma
3.21
(a)
gives

dt_{i}=m_{i}t_{i}^{2}e^{(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}\,dr_{i}\rightarrow\infty\quad\mbox{as }t_{i}\rightarrow 0.

In principle, one should think of
1
-forms bounded from
X
as the ones
“decaying exponentially fast as we approach
\partial A
”
. One way of making this precise will be given in Lemma
4.9
(b)
.

In the next computation, the following elementary observation will be useful.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
a,b,x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume
a,b\geqslant 0
and
a+b>0
. Then the following inequality holds:

\frac{|ax+by|}{a+b}\leqslant|x|+|y|.

By symmetry, we can assume
a\geqslant b
. Then
a>0
and
1\geqslant\frac{b}{a}\geqslant 0
, so

\frac{|ax+by|}{a+b}=\frac{|x+\frac{b}{a}y|}{1+\frac{b}{a}}\leqslant\frac{|x|+\frac{b}{a}|y|}{1+\frac{b}{a}}\leqslant|x|+|y|.\qed

We will now use Lemma
3.9
(c)
to compare the
1
-forms
dv_{i}
defined by two charts adapted to
f
. We warn the reader that functions
b,c,q,s
and a
1
-form
\gamma
introduced below depend on the fixed charts
U_{X}
,
U_{X}^{\prime}
and index
i
; even though the notation does not reflect this explicitly. The same warning applies to functions
\lambda,a
from Lemma
3.9
(b)
,
(c)
.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
U_{X},U^{\prime}_{X}
be two charts adapted to
f
. As in Lemma
3.9
, we put
V_{X}=U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{\prime}
,
V=\pi^{-1}(U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{\prime})
, and use Notation
3.5
. Fix
i\in S\cap S^{\prime}
and a subset
I\subseteq S\cap S^{\prime}
not containing
i
. Put
V_{X,I}^{\circ}=V_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The map
\theta^{\prime}_{i}-\theta_{i}\colon V\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a pullback of a smooth map
V_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

(b)

Let
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X})
be as in Lemma
3.9
(c)
. Then the functions
|a|
,
|1+at_{i}|^{-1}
,
|\log(1+at_{i})|
and
|1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})|^{-1}
are continuous and bounded on
V
.

(c)

Let
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X})
be as in
(b)
. The following identity holds on
V

v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{at_{i}^{2}}{1+at_{i}}+\frac{u_{i}^{2}\log(1+at_{i})}{1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})}.

(d)

There is a bounded function
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
such that
v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}=\sigma_{i}b
on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
.

(e)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there are bounded functions
c,q\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
, and a bounded

1
-form
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
, such that the following equality holds on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
:

d(v_{i}-v^{\prime}_{i})=t_{i}\cdot c\,dv_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot q\,dg+\sigma_{i}\cdot\gamma.

In particular, taking
I=\emptyset
, we get that the equalities from
(d)
,
(e)
hold on
V_{X}\setminus D=V\setminus\partial A
, where functions
b,c,q
are bounded, and the
1
-form
\gamma
is bounded from
X
.

(a)
By Lemma
3.9
(b)
, there is a nonvanishing holomorphic function
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}(V_{X})
such that
z_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda z_{i}
. In polar coordinates, we have
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{2\pi\imath\cdot\beta}
for some smooth function
\beta
. Using the additive notation
3.2
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we get
\theta^{\prime}_{i}-\theta_{i}=\beta
, as claimed.

(b)
Recall that by Definition
3.1
(d)
, the coordinates of an adapted chart
U_{X}
extend continuously to the closure
\overline{U}_{X}
, which is compact. Therefore, the function
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X})
introduced in Lemma
3.9
(c)
extends to a continuous function on a compact set
\overline{V}_{X}
. In particular, the function
|a|
is bounded.

Similarly, the function
1+at_{i}
extends to a continuous function on
\overline{V}_{X}
, which does not vanish by Lemma
3.9
(c)
. Thus the functions
|1+at_{i}|^{-1}
and
|\log(1+at_{i})|
are continuous on
\overline{V}_{X}
, hence bounded because
\overline{V}_{X}
is compact.

On
U_{X}\setminus D_{i}
, we have
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
by the formulas (
18
) and (
13
). Using the second equation from Lemma
3.9
(c)
, we get that on
V\setminus\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, the following identity holds:

u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i}=\frac{1}{1-\log w_{i}-\log(1+at_{i})}-u_{i}=\frac{1}{u_{i}^{-1}-\log(1+at_{i})}-u_{i}=\frac{u_{i}}{1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})}-u_{i}.

Therefore,

(34)

u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i}=\frac{u_{i}^{2}\log(1+at_{i})}{1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})}.

On
V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
we have
t_{i}=0
by definition (
16
) of
t_{i}
, so the right-hand side of (
34
) is well defined there. By Lemma
3.6
(d)
we have
u^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i}
on
V\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{i})
, so both sides of (
34
) are zero. Thus (
34
) holds everywhere on
V
. As before, it extends to continuously to
\overline{V}
. In particular, the function
|1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})|^{-1}
is continuous on
\overline{V}
: to see this, divide both sides of (
34
) by
u_{i}^{2}\log(1+at_{i})
and use the right or left side of the equality when
u_{i}
is close or away from
0
, respectively. Since the map
\pi
is proper by Proposition
3.7
(b)
, the set
\overline{V}=\pi^{-1}(\overline{V}_{X})
is compact. Thus
|1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})|^{-1}
is bounded, as claimed.

(c)

By Lemma
3.9
(c)
, we have

(35)

t_{i}-t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}-\frac{t_{i}}{1+at_{i}}=\frac{at_{i}^{2}}{1+at_{i}}.

By (
19
), we have
v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}=(t_{i}-u_{i})-(t_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i}^{\prime})=(t_{i}-t_{i}^{\prime})+(u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i})
, so
(c)
follows from (
35
) and (
34
).

(d)

Since
i\not\in I
, by Lemma
3.16
(c)
the functions
t_{i}
,
u_{i}
,
v_{i}
,
v_{i}^{\prime}
are smooth on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
. Moreover, since
t_{i}>0
on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
, it follows that
\sigma_{i}|_{V_{X,I}^{\circ}}
is a smooth, positive function, hence the restriction
\sigma_{i}^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime})|_{V_{X,I}^{\circ}}
is smooth. We need to prove that it is bounded.

By
(b)
, the functions
\check{b}\coloneqq\frac{a}{1+at_{i}}
and
(1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i}))^{-1}
are bounded. Since
\frac{1}{t_{i}}\log(1+at_{i})=a-\tfrac{1}{2}a^{2}t_{i}+\dots
is bounded, too, we infer that
\check{h}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t_{i}}\cdot\frac{\log(1+at_{i})}{1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})}
is bounded as well.
By
(c)
, we have
v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}=\check{b}t_{i}^{2}+\check{h}t_{i}u_{i}^{2}
. By definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
, we have
\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}
. Lemma
3.25
implies that

\frac{|v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}|}{\sigma_{i}}=\frac{|\check{b}t_{i}^{2}+\check{h}t_{i}u_{i}^{2}|}{t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}}\leqslant|\check{b}|+|\check{h}|.

Therefore,
v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime}=\sigma_{i}b
for some bounded function
b
, as claimed.

(e)
As before, we note that since
i\not\in I
, the functions
t_{i}
and
u_{i}
are smooth on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
. Thus we can apply the exterior derivative
d
to the formulas (
35
) and (
34
). We get bounded functions
\widetilde{c},\widetilde{q},\widetilde{s}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
and bounded
1
-forms
\widetilde{\beta}
,
\widetilde{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
, such that the following identities hold on
V_{X,I}^{\circ}
:

\begin{split}&d(t_{i}-t_{i}^{\prime})=\frac{t_{i}^{2}\,da+2at_{i}\,dt_{i}}{1+at_{i}}-\frac{at_{i}^{2}(a\,dt_{i}+t_{i}\,da)}{(1+at_{i})^{2}}=t_{i}\widetilde{c}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\beta},\\
&d(u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i})=\frac{2u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})\,du_{i}}{1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})}+\frac{u_{i}^{2}(a\,dt_{i}+t_{i}\ da)}{(1+at_{i})(1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i}))}+\\
+&\frac{u_{i}^{2}\log(1+at_{i})}{(1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i}))^{2}}\cdot\left(\log(1+at_{i})\,du_{i}+\frac{u_{i}(a\,dt_{i}+t_{i}\,da)}{1+at_{i}}\right)=u_{i}t_{i}\widetilde{s}\,du_{i}+u_{i}^{2}\widetilde{q}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\gamma}.\end{split}

By Lemma
3.22
(g)
, we have
du_{i}=-t_{i}^{-1}(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}\,dg)
. Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. By Lemma
3.21
(i)
,
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l
for some bounded function
l
. Therefore,
du_{i}=-t_{i}^{-1}(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l\,dg)
. Substituting this to the above formula for
d(u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i})
, we get that for some bounded functions
\check{q}
,
s
, the following holds:

d(u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{i})=-u_{i}\widetilde{s}(u_{i}^{2}\,dt_{i}-t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l\,dg)+u_{i}^{2}\widetilde{q}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\gamma}=u_{i}^{2}\check{q}\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\gamma}.

Adding this equality to the above formula for
d(t_{i}-t_{i}^{\prime})
gives

d(v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i})=(t_{i}\widetilde{c}+u_{i}^{2}\check{q})\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg+(t_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\beta}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\,\widetilde{\gamma}).

By Lemma
3.25
,
\frac{|t_{i}\widetilde{c}+u_{i}^{2}\check{q}|}{t_{i}+u_{i}^{2}}\leqslant|\widetilde{c}|+|\check{q}|
, so
t_{i}\widetilde{c}+u_{i}^{2}\check{q}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})c
for some bounded function
c
. By definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
, we get
t_{i}\widetilde{c}+u_{i}^{2}\check{s}=\sigma_{i}t_{i}^{-1}c
.

Recall that, by Definition
3.1
, the holomorphic coordinates on
U_{X}
are
(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
. Write
x_{j}
,
y_{j}
for the real and imaginary part of
z_{i_{j}}
, respectively, and put
\gamma_{2j-1}=dx_{j}
,
\gamma_{2j}=dy_{j}
. Then we can write
\widetilde{\beta}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\widetilde{b}_{j}\gamma_{j}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\widetilde{q}_{j}\gamma_{j}
for some bounded functions
\widetilde{b}_{j}
,
\widetilde{q}_{j}
. As before, Lemma
3.25
shows that there is a bounded function
\widetilde{c}_{j}
such that
t_{i}^{2}\widetilde{b}_{j}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\widetilde{q}_{j}=(t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2})\widetilde{c}_{j}=\sigma_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j}
, where the last equality follows from definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
. Therefore,
t_{i}^{2}\widetilde{\beta}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}\widetilde{\gamma}=\sigma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\widetilde{c}_{j}\gamma_{j}=\sigma_{i}\gamma
for some
1
-form
\gamma
bounded from
X
. Substituting the above equalities to the formula for
d(v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i})
, we get

d(v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i})=\sigma_{i}t_{i}^{-1}c\,dt_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg+\sigma_{i}\gamma=\sigma_{i}(ct_{i}^{-1}\,dt_{i}+\gamma)+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg.

In case
I\neq\emptyset
, we have
u_{i}=0
, so
v_{i}=t_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}
, and therefore, the above equality reads as
d(v_{i}-v_{i}^{\prime})=t_{i}c\,dv_{i}+\sigma_{i}\gamma
, which is what we wanted to prove. In case
I=\emptyset
, we substitute the formula for
dt_{i}
from Lemma
3.22
(c)
. Since by (
33
) we have
\sigma_{i}t_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}=1
, we get

\begin{split}&d(v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i})=\sigma_{i}(ct_{i}^{-1}\,dt_{i}+\gamma)+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg=\\
=\ &\sigma_{i}ct_{i}^{-1}\,(\rho_{i}(t_{i}\,dv_{i}+u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\,dg))+\sigma_{i}\gamma+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s\,dg=\\
=\ &ct_{i}\,dv_{i}+(cu_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}u_{i}s)\,dg+\sigma_{i}\gamma.\end{split}

By Lemma
3.21
(g)
,
u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}=\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})
, and by Lemma
3.21
(i)
,
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l
for some bounded function
l
. Hence the coefficient near
dg
above equals
t_{i}^{\varepsilon}q
for some bounded function
q
, which proves
(e)
.

∎

3.3.5.
The functions
r_{j}
,
w_{j}
are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

We aim at proving Lemma
3.12
, which asserts that the transition maps for our atlas are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. The key step is to prove that in a chart
(U_{i},\psi_{i})
introduced in (
29
), all functions
r_{j}
,
w_{j}
for
j\neq i
are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. As a consequence, in Lemma
3.29
we will infer that the function
v_{i}
, which is not a coordinate of
\psi_{i}
(see formula (
29
)); as well as the pullbacks of all smooth functions from
U_{X}
, are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. In Lemmas
3.40
(d)
and
3.41
(e)
we will prove a stronger result, replacing
\mathcal{C}^{1}
by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. Nonetheless, we provide an independent, direct argument here, too. This way, we give a complete construction of a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas without introducing further technical tools, and illustrate computations behind the proof of Lemmas
3.40
,
3.41
, which is more complicated.

Notation 3.27
.

Fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
such that
U_{X}\cap D\neq\emptyset
. We reorder the components of
D
so that the associated index set (
12
) is
S=\{1,\dots,k\}
. By Lemma
3.11
the map
\psi_{1}\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
is a homeomorphism onto an open subset of a smooth manifold with boundary, so it endows
U_{1}
with a smooth structure.
In other words, we take
(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
as smooth coordinates on
U_{1}
.

For a subset
V\subseteq U_{1}
, we denote by
\mathcal{S}(V)
(respectively,
\mathcal{S}^{l}(V)
for
l\in\mathbb{N}
) the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of functions on
V
which are smooth (respectively,
\mathcal{C}^{l}
), with respect to this smooth structure. We do not use the symbol
“
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
”
to avoid confusion with the eventual
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-structure on
A
, which we will introduce in Section
3.4
, and whose restriction to
U_{1}
will differ from the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-structure that
U_{1}
inherits via
\psi_{1}
. Since by Lemma
3.11
the map
\pi|_{U_{1}\setminus\partial A}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image, we can – identifying functions on
X
with their pullbacks to
A
– write an inclusion
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\setminus D)\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus\partial A)
.

For
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we introduce differential operators
\partial_{j}\colon\mathcal{S}^{l}(V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{l-1}(V)
by

\partial_{1}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial g},\quad\partial_{i}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}\quad\mbox{for }i\in\{2,\dots,k\},

and for
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{k}
we write
\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial^{\alpha_{k}}_{k}
,
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}
.

For
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, we put

W_{i}=\{x\in U_{1}:w_{i}(x)>0\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad T_{i}=\{x\in U_{1}:t_{i}(x)>0\}.

Using the above notation, we have the following immediate corollary of Lemma
3.22
.

Lemma 3.28
.

For every
i,j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, such that
i\neq j
, we have

\partial_{j}v_{i}=0,\quad\partial_{j}r_{i}=0,\quad\partial_{j}t_{i}=0,\quad\partial_{j}w_{i}=0,\quad\partial_{j}u_{i}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{j}t^{(l)}=0\mbox{ for any }l\geqslant 0.

Since
(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k})
are coordinates on
U_{1}
, we have
\partial_{j}v_{i}=0
for
j\neq i
and
\partial_{j}g=0
for
j\neq 1
. This proves the first equality. The last one follows by definition (
32
) of
t^{(l)}
.

For the remaining ones, note that the basis of
T^{*}_{y}U_{1}
at any point
y\in U_{1}
is given by
dg,dv_{2},\dots,dv_{k}
and
1
-forms
dz
, where
z
ranges through the coordinates of
\vartheta
. For a function
h
, the coefficient of
dh
near
dv_{j}
is
\partial_{j}h
. By Lemma
3.22
(a)
,
(c)
,
(e)
and
(f)
, this coefficient is zero for
h=r_{i},t_{i},w_{i},u_{i}
whenever
i\neq 1,j
.
∎

Lemma 3.29
.

In the setting of Notation
3.27
, we have

(a)

The function
t
is smooth on
U_{1}
, i.e.
t\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
,

(b)

For all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the functions
v_{i},w_{i}
are in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

(c)

For all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the function
r_{i}
is in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

(d)

The pullback of any
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-function
h
on
U_{X}
is in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, i.e.
\mathcal{C}^{1}(U_{X})\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

(a)
Since
t=\eta^{-1}(g)=e^{1-g^{-1}}
by (
14
), and
g
is one of the coordinates on
U_{1}
, we have
t\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(b)
Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. By definition (
29
) of the coordinate chart
\psi_{1}
, we have
v_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

By Lemma
3.22
(e)
, we have
\partial_{i}w_{i}=-\rho_{i}w_{i}
,
\partial_{1}w_{i}=\rho_{i}t^{\prime}
and
\partial_{j}w_{i}=0
for
j\neq i,1
. Recall that
\rho_{i}
was defined in (
33
) by
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
, so since
t_{i},u_{i}
are continuous by Lemma
3.6
(a)
, the function
\rho_{i}
extends to a continuous function away from the common zero locus of
t_{i}
and
u_{i}
, i.e. on
T_{i}\cup W_{i}
. It follows that
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i}\cup W_{i})
. We claim that
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

By definitions (
33
) and (
18
), we have
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
and
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
, so

-\partial_{i}w_{i}=\rho_{i}w_{i}=\frac{w_{i}}{t_{i}+u_{i}^{2}}=\frac{w_{i}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}}{t_{i}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}+1}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as }w_{i}\rightarrow 0.

By Lemma
3.21
(c)
and
(b)
, we have
t^{\prime}=t(1-\log t)^{2}
and
t=t_{i}w_{i}
, so, using Lemma
3.22
(e)
, we get

\partial_{1}w_{i}=\rho_{i}t^{\prime}=\frac{t^{\prime}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}}{t_{i}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}+1}=\frac{t_{i}w_{i}(1-\log t_{i}-\log w_{i})^{2}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}}{t_{i}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}+1}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as }t_{i},w_{i}\rightarrow 0.

Therefore,
\partial_{i}w_{i}
and
\partial_{1}w_{i}
extend to continuous functions on
U_{1}
, so
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

By Lemma
3.6
(c)
, we have
w_{1}=-\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}
, so
w_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, too. The remaining function
v_{1}
is defined in (
19
) as
v_{1}=t_{1}-u_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}-\eta(w_{1})
. Recall that
t\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by
(a)
, and the restriction
\eta|_{(0,1]}
is smooth. Therefore, since
w_{1}|_{U_{1}}>0
by definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
, we infer that
v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

(c)
Consider first the case
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Lemma
3.22
(d)
implies that
\partial_{1}t_{i}
and
\partial_{i}t_{i}
are continuous on
T_{i}
, and
\partial_{j}t_{i}=0
for
j\neq i,1
, so like before we conclude that
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
. By Lemma
3.21
(a)
, we have
r_{i}=e^{(-m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
, so
r_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
, too. We need to show that
r_{i}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on its zero locus
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
.

First, we claim that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the function
t_{i}\cdot\partial_{j}t_{i}
is bounded. By Lemma
3.22
(c)
, we have
\partial_{i}t_{i}=\rho_{i}t_{i}
,
\partial_{1}t_{i}=\rho_{i}u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}
and
\partial_{j}t_{i}=0
for
j\neq 1,i
. By definition (
33
) of
\rho_{i}
, we have
\rho_{i}t_{i}=(1+u_{i}^{2}t_{i}^{-1})^{-1}\leqslant 1
, so
\partial_{i}t_{i}
is bounded, hence
t_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}t_{i}
is bounded, too, as needed. To deal with
\partial_{1}t_{i}=\rho_{i}u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}
, recall that
u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}=\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})
by Lemma
3.21
(g)
, and by Lemma
3.21
(i)
, we have
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l
for some bounded function
l
and
\varepsilon>0
. It follows that
\partial_{1}t_{i}=\rho_{i}t_{i}^{\varepsilon}l
. We have seen that
\rho_{i}t_{i}=(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
is bounded, so
t_{i}\partial_{1}t_{i}=\rho_{i}t_{i}^{\varepsilon+1}l
is bounded, too, which proves the claim.

Now by Lemma
3.22
(a)
, we have
\partial_{j}r_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}\cdot t_{i}^{-3}r_{i}\cdot t_{i}\partial_{j}t_{i}
. By Lemma
3.21
(a)
, we have
r_{i}=e^{(-m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
, so
t_{i}^{-3}r_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
. Since we have shown that
t_{i}\partial_{j}t_{i}
is bounded, we infer that
\partial_{j}r_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, so
r_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, as needed.

It remains to show that
r_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
. By Lemma
3.21
(b)
we have
t_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}
, and
w_{1}>0
on
U_{1}
by definition (
28
). By
(a)
and
(b)
, we have
t,w_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, so
t_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
. Now Lemma
3.21
(a)
gives
r_{1}=e^{-(m_{1}t_{1})^{-1}}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

(d)
For
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
r_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
by
(e)
and
\theta_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
by definition (
29
) of the chart. Thus the smooth coordinates
r_{i}\cos(2\pi\theta_{i})
and
r_{i}\sin(2\pi\theta_{i})
on
U_{X}
are in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
. By (
29
), the remaining coordinates
z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}}
are in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, too. Now, part
(d)
follows from the chain rule.

∎

3.3.6.
The A’Campo space is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-manifold

We are now in the position to prove the remaining results stated in Section
3.3.1
, which endow
A
with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure.

Fix two
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts
(U_{i},\psi_{i})
and
(U_{j}^{\prime},\psi_{j}^{\prime})
associated to charts
(U_{X},\psi_{X})
and
(U^{\prime}_{X},\psi_{X}^{\prime})
adapted to
f
. By Lemma
3.11
, the transition map
\psi_{j}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}|_{\psi_{i}(U_{i}\cap U_{j}^{\prime})}
is a homeomorphism between open subsets of
Q_{k,n}
and
Q_{k^{\prime},n}
. To prove that it is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism, we need to prove that it is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
\psi_{i}(x)
for any
x\in U_{i}\cap U_{j}^{\prime}
, and that its Jacobian determinant does not vanish there.

If
x\not\in\partial A
then near
x
we have the natural smooth structure pulled back from
X\setminus D
, and by Lemma
3.11
both
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{j}
are diffeomorphisms with respect to this structure; so the assertion follows.

Assume
x\in\partial A
, so
x\in A_{I}^{\circ}
for some nonempty
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, say
I=\{1,\dots,k\}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
U_{X}\subseteq U_{X}^{\prime}
, and that the associated index set (
12
) for
U_{X}
is
\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Assume first that
\psi_{X}=\psi_{X}^{\prime}|_{U_{X}}
. Then the index set associated to
U_{X}^{\prime}
is
\{1,\dots,k\}
, too. Say that
i=1
,
j=2
, and put
V=U_{1}\cap U_{2}
. The map
\psi_{1}
is the smooth chart used in Notation
3.27
, and
\psi_{2}
differs from it by replacing
v_{2}
with
v_{1}
. Thus it is sufficient to show that
v_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(V)
, and that the Jacobian determinant of
\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}
, which equals
\partial_{2}v_{1}
, does not vanish on
V
.

The first assertion follows from Lemma
3.29
(b)
. For the second one, recall from (
19
) that
v_{1}=t_{1}-u_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}-\eta(w_{1})
, and this formula is valid on
U_{1}
since
w_{1}|_{U_{1}}>0
by definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
. By Lemma
3.28
we have
\partial_{2}t=0
, so by Lemma
3.21
(b)
and
(g)
:

\partial_{2}v_{1}=-(tw_{1}^{-2}+\eta^{\prime}(w_{1}))\cdot\partial_{2}w_{1}=-w_{1}^{-1}\cdot(t_{1}+u_{1}^{2})\cdot\partial_{2}w_{1}=-w_{1}^{-1}\rho_{1}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{2}w_{1},

where the last equality follows from definition (
33
) of
\rho_{1}
.

By Lemma
3.6
(c)
, we have
w_{1}=-\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}
, so Lemma
3.28
implies that
\partial_{2}w_{1}=-\partial_{2}w_{2}
. Now by Lemma
3.22
(e)
, we have
\partial_{2}w_{2}=-\rho_{2}w_{2}
, so
\partial_{2}w_{1}=\rho_{2}w_{2}
, and therefore

\partial_{2}v_{1}=-w_{1}^{-1}\rho_{1}^{-1}\cdot w_{2}\rho_{2}.

By definition (
28
) of
U_{i}
, we have
w_{1},w_{2}>0
on
V=U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, hence
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}>0
on
V
by definition (
33
) of
\rho_{i}
. We conclude that
\partial_{2}v_{1}\neq 0
, as needed.

Consider now the general case, where
\psi_{X}
may be different than
\psi_{X}^{\prime}
. Since
U_{X}\subseteq U_{X}^{\prime}
, the index set
S^{\prime}
associated to
U_{X}^{\prime}
contains
\{1,\dots,k\}
, so, say,
S^{\prime}=\{1,\dots,l\}
for some
l\geqslant k
. The special case considered above allows to assume
i=j=1
. We can write the transition map
\psi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}
as a composition
\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
, where

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,v_{2}^{\prime},\dots,v_{k}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\theta^{\prime}_{k},z^{\prime}_{i_{k+1}},\dots z^{\prime}_{i_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,v_{2}^{\prime},\dots,v_{l}^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

We will first show that
\varphi_{1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism at
x
. By Lemma
3.29
(d)
, the pullbacks of smooth functions on
U_{X}
are in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, so
z_{i_{k+1}}^{\prime},\dots,z_{i_{n}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, and, by Lemma
3.26
(a)
,
\theta_{i}^{\prime}-\theta_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Since
g,\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}
are coordinates on
U_{1}
, we infer that
g,\theta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\theta_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
.

Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. We claim that
v_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
. By Lemma
3.26
(c)
, it is enough to show that the functions
a
,
t_{i}
and
u_{i}
are in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
. Since the function
a
is smooth on
U_{X}
, it is in
\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.29
(d)
. By Lemma
3.29
(c)
,
r_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, so since
t_{i}=-(m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}
, we have
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
. Now by Lemma
3.22
(f)
we have
\partial_{j}u_{i}=0
for
j\neq 1,i
and
\partial_{i}u_{i}=-\rho_{i}u_{i}^{2}=-(t_{i}u_{i}^{-2}+1)^{-1}
, so
\partial_{j}u_{i}
is continuous on
U_{1}
for
j\neq 1
. Eventually,
\partial_{1}u_{i}=\rho_{i}\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=\rho_{i}t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1}
by Lemma
3.21
(h)
, so
\partial_{1}u_{i}
is continuous on
T_{i}
. Hence
u_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
, and therefore
v_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(T_{i})
, as claimed.
In turn, by Lemma
3.26
(e)
we have
d(v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i})\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, so
v_{i}^{\prime}-v_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
, and therefore
v_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}(U_{1})
. Thus
\varphi_{1}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

To prove that
\varphi_{1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism at
x
, it remains to show that its Jacobian matrix is invertible at
x
. To this end, we will first write the Jacobian matrix of the transition map
\psi_{X}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{X}^{-1}\colon(z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})\mapsto(z_{i_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,z_{i_{n}}^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
3.9
(b)
, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
there is a nonvanishing holomorphic function
\lambda_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}(V)
such that
z_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda_{i}z_{i}
. Hence for
j\neq i
we have
\frac{\partial z_{i}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{j}}=z_{i}\frac{\partial\lambda_{i}}{\partial z_{j}}
, so
\frac{\partial z_{i}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{j}}|_{D_{i}}=0
. It follows that the Jacobian matrix of
\psi_{X}^{-1}\circ\psi_{X}^{\prime}
at
\pi(x)
has a block form
\begin{pmatrix}*&0\\
*&Z\end{pmatrix}
, for some
2(n-k)\times 2(n-k)
matrix
Z
. Since this block matrix is invertible, so is
Z
.

Now, we claim that the Jacobian matrix of
\varphi_{1}
at
x
has the form

\left[\begin{matrix}\mathrm{id}_{k\times k}&0&0\\
*&\mathrm{id}_{k\times k}&*\\
*&0&Z\end{matrix}\right].

The first row consists of partial derivatives of the first coordinate
g
, so it is the first unit vector. For
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
t_{i}(x)=0
, so
\sigma_{i}(x)=0
by definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
. Thus by Lemma
3.26
(e)
we have
dv_{i}^{\prime}=dv_{i}
at
x
, so the
i
-th row is the
i
-th unit vector, as claimed.

The middle
k\times k
block is
[\frac{\partial\theta_{j}^{\prime}}{\partial\theta_{i}}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant k}
. By Lemma
3.26
(a)
, we have
\theta_{j}^{\prime}=\theta_{j}+\beta
for some smooth
\beta\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
(recall that we use additive notation
3.2
for
\mathbb{S}^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
). Write
z_{i}=x_{i}+\imath y_{i}
. Then
\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial\theta_{i}}=-r_{i}\sin(2\pi\theta_{i})\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial x_{i}}+r_{i}\cos(2\pi\theta_{i})\tfrac{\partial\beta}{\partial y_{i}}
, so
\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial\theta_{i}}=0
at
x
. Thus the middle block is indeed
\mathrm{id}_{k\times k}
.

The last
2(n-k)
rows correspond to the (real and imaginary parts of) the functions
z_{i_{k+1}}^{\prime},\dots,z_{i_{n}}^{\prime}
. The above computation shows that their partial derivatives with respect to
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}
vanish. The ones with respect to (real and imaginary parts of)
z_{i_{j}}
give the matrix
Z
. This proves that the Jacobian of
\varphi_{1}
has the required form, so it is invertible because
Z
is.

Now, consider the map
\varphi_{2}
. Its inverse
\varphi_{2}^{-1}
is defined away from the zero locus of
r_{j}
, for
j\in\{k+1,\dots,l\}
, and it replaces the coordinates
(v_{j}^{\prime},\theta_{j}^{\prime})
of
U_{1}^{\prime}
by real and imaginary parts of
r_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\exp(2\pi\imath\theta_{j}^{\prime})
. We will show
\varphi_{2}^{-1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism. It is sufficient to show it for the map
v_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto r_{j}^{\prime}
: the remaining part is passing from polar to standard coordinates. Since
r_{j}^{\prime}
is a pullback of a smooth function on
U_{X}^{\prime}
, by Lemma
3.29
(c)
it is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
in the coordinates of
U_{1}^{\prime}
. Hence our map is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. By Lemma
3.28
, we have
\frac{\partial r_{j}^{\prime}}{\partial v_{i}^{\prime}}=0
if
i\neq j
, so its Jacobian determinant is
\prod_{j=k+1}^{l}\frac{\partial r_{j}^{\prime}}{\partial v_{j}^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
3.22
(a)
,
(c)
, we have
\frac{\partial r_{j}^{\prime}}{\partial v_{j}^{\prime}}=m_{j}^{-1}(t_{j}^{\prime})^{-2}r_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{\partial t_{j}^{\prime}}{\partial v_{j}^{\prime}}=m_{j}^{-1}(t_{j}^{\prime})^{-2}r_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{j}^{\prime}t_{j}^{\prime}
, which is nonzero whenever
r_{j}^{\prime}
is, as needed.

∎

Since the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure on
A\setminus\partial A
agrees with the one on
X\setminus D
, the restriction
\Phi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism. We will show that
\Phi
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism at every point
x\in\partial A
. Fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
containing
\pi(x)
, and let
U
and
U^{\prime}
be its preimages in
A
and
A^{\prime}
, respectively. Fix
i
in the associated index set such that
w_{i}(x)>\tfrac{1}{n+1}
. Then
x
lies in the subset
U_{i}\subseteq U
defined in (
28
), as well as in its counterpart
U_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
. To prove that
\Phi
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
diffeomorphism at
x
, we need to prove that the composition
\psi_{i}^{\prime}|_{\Phi(U_{i})\cap U_{i}^{\prime}}\circ\Phi|_{U_{i}}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism between open subsets of
Q_{k,n}
. Since both
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
correspond to the same adapted chart
U_{X}
, this map is the identity.

∎

(a)
By Lemma
3.29
(e)
, the map
\pi
pulls back
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-functions from the charts
U_{X}\subseteq X
to
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-functions on their preimages in
A
, so it is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Its restriction
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism by Lemma
3.11
.

(b)
The function
g\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)
is a coordinate in each chart (
29
) meeting
\partial A
, hence it is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-submersion (onto its image) near
\partial A
. Away from
\partial A
, it is a composition of a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
with a submersion
|f||_{X\setminus D}
, so it is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-submersion there, too. Similarly, over each chart adapted to
f
we have
\theta=\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\theta_{i}
by Definition
3.1
(b)
, so
\theta
is a nonzero linear combination of coordinates of each chart (
36
), hence a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-submersion near
\partial A
. In turn,
\theta|_{A\setminus\partial A}
is a pullback of a submersion
\frac{f}{|f|}
. Thus
(g,\theta)
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-submersion. It follows that the map
f_{A}=(\exp(-\exp(g^{-1}-1)),\theta)
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

(c)
Let
U_{X}^{p}
be the chart adapted to
f
from the atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
used in Definition
3.23
, and for
i
in the associated index set let
v_{i}^{p}
be the corresponding function (
19
). By Lemma
3.29
(b)
,
v_{i}^{p}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\pi^{-1}(U_{X}^{p})
. Let
\tau^{p}
be the element of the smooth partition of unity
\boldsymbol{\tau}
inscribed in
\mathcal{U}_{X}
, supported in
U_{X}^{p}
. Since the map
\pi
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
by
(a)
, the pullback of
\tau^{p}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
; hence
\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-function on
A
. Therefore, the function
\overline{v}_{i}
defined in (
23
) as
\sum_{p}\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, too, as needed.

∎

3.4.
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-atlases on the A’Campo space

Let
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
be a pair adapted to
f
, and let
A
be its associated A’Campo space. We will now use the function
g\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)
, see (
14
), and the distinguished global functions
\overline{v}_{i}\colon A\longrightarrow[-1,1]
corresponding to
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
, see (
23
), to produce a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-atlas on
A
. This atlas will be compatible with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas defined in Section
3.3
. However, if
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime})
are pairs adapted to
f
, and
A
,
A^{\prime}
are corresponding A’Campo spaces, then the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
from Proposition
3.13
will not be
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in general.

3.4.1.
Definition of the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-atlas associated with
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})

Fix a pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
adapted to
f
. Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
, which perhaps does not belong to the fixed atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
. Let
S
be the index set (
12
) associated with
U_{X}
. Assume
S\neq\emptyset
, let
U\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
and let
\bigcup_{i\in S}U_{i}
be the covering of
U
introduced in (
28
). For every
i\in S
, we define

(36)

\overline{\psi}_{i}=(g,(\overline{v}_{j})_{j\in S\setminus\{i\}};\vartheta)\colon U_{i}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}.

It differs from the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-chart
\psi_{i}
introduced in (
29
) by replacing local functions
v_{j}
by global
\overline{v}_{j}
. In particular, by Proposition
3.14
(c)
,
\overline{\psi}_{i}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
with respect to the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure on
A
introduced in Section
3.3
. Note that the formula (
23
) for the function
\overline{v}_{j}
used in (
36
) depends on the fixed pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
.

We will now use charts (
36
) to define a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure on
A
. As in Section
3.3
, we state the main results first, and postpone their proofs to subsequent Sections
3.4.2
–
3.4.7
.

Lemma 3.30
.

Consider in
A
the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure constructed in the previous section. For every
x\in D
there exists a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
containing
x
, such that each map (
36
) is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto an open subset of
Q_{k,n}
. Moreover, the restriction
\psi_{i}|_{U_{i}\setminus\partial A}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
for the natural smooth structure in
A\setminus\partial A
.

By Lemma
3.30
, pairs
(U_{i},\overline{\psi}_{i})
defined in (
36
) are candidates for
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
charts on
A
. We will call them the
smooth charts
corresponding to the adapted chart
U_{X}

and to the pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
. As before, we abuse the definition of a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
atlas by allowing open subsets of
Q_{k,n}
as targets for the charts. Nonetheless, the obvious refinement produces charts whose targets are open subsets of half-euclidean spaces.

In order to complete the definition of the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
atlas we need to prove that the corresponding transition functions are
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. This is the content of Lemma
3.31
below.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
U_{X}
,
U^{\prime}_{X}
be charts adapted to
f
, satisfying the statement of Lemma
3.30
. Let
S
,
S^{\prime}
be their associated index sets (
12
) and let
k=\#S^{\prime}
,
k^{\prime}=\#S^{\prime}
. For
i\in S
and
j\in S^{\prime}
let
(U_{i},\psi_{i})
,
(U_{j}^{\prime},\psi_{j}^{\prime})
be the charts defined in (
36
) above. Then the transition map

\overline{\psi}^{\prime}_{j}\circ\overline{\psi}_{i}^{-1}|_{\overline{\psi}_{i}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})}\colon\overline{\psi}_{i}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})\longrightarrow\overline{\psi}^{\prime}_{j}(U_{i}\cap U^{\prime}_{j})

is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism between open subsets of
Q_{k,n}
and
Q_{k^{\prime},n}
.

Lemmas
3.30
,
3.31
endow
A
with a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure, compatible with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure introduced in Section
3.3
, and with the natural smooth structure on
A\setminus\partial A=X\setminus D
.

Remark 3.32
.

Example
3.15
shows that the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi
comparing the A’Campo spaces defined using different adapted pairs is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
in general. Indeed, let
X=\mathbb{D}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
,
f\colon\mathbb{D}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\ni(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto z_{1}z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
; and let
\mathcal{U}_{X}
,
\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\prime}
be atlases adapted to
f
, each consisting of one chart: for
\mathcal{U}_{X}
, we take the standard one
(z_{1},z_{2})
, and for
\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\prime}
we take
(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})=(e^{-1}z_{1},ez_{2})
as in Example
3.15
. Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be the A’Campo spaces corresponding to
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\{1\})
and
(\mathcal{U}_{X}^{\prime},\{1\})
. Since the partition of unity is trivial in each case, the smooth charts (
36
) are the same as the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-charts (
29
). The map
\Phi\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
is defined in (
27
) so that
\Phi|_{\partial A}=\mathrm{id}_{\partial A}
. Example
3.15
shows that the identity is not a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-diffeomorphism between these two smooth structures on
\partial A
: thus
\Phi
is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
.

The
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure defined above has the following important properties, cf. Proposition
3.14
.

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
be a pair adapted to
f
, and let
A
be the corresponding A’Campo space, with the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-structure defined above.

(a)

The map
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
is smooth. Its restriction
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\colon A\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow X\setminus D
is a diffeomorphism.

(b)

The map
(g,\theta)\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a smooth submersion. In particular,
f_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}
is smooth.

(c)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
the function
\overline{v}_{i}:A\longrightarrow[-1,1]
defined in (
23
) for
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
is smooth.

(d)

For every nonempty subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, the restriction
(\pi,\mu)\colon\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}A_{I}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{I}^{\circ}\times\Delta_{I}^{\circ}
is a smooth
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\#I}
-bundle.

Remark 3.34
.

Proposition
3.33
(b)
and the Ehressmann lemma give a diffeomorphism
A\cong g^{-1}(\delta)\times[0,1)
for any
\delta\in[0,1)
. By definition (
14
) of
g
, for every
\delta>0
we have
g^{-1}(\delta)=f^{-1}(\mathbb{S}_{\varepsilon}^{1})
, where
\varepsilon=\exp(-\exp(\delta^{-1}-1))>0
. Therefore, the A’Campo space
A
is diffeomorphic to
f^{-1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\times[0,1)
for any
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. In particular, its diffeomorphism type does not depend on the choice of
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
. Nonetheless, as we have seen in Remark
3.32
, given two A’Campo spaces
A
and
A^{\prime}
the composition of any diffeomorphism from
A
to
f^{-1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\times[0,1)
with the inverse of any diffeomorphism from
A^{\prime}
to
f^{-1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\times[0,1)
cannot be equal to the canonical map
\Phi\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
defined in (
27
).

Lemma
3.30
will be proved in Section
3.4.2
below. The proofs of Lemma
3.31
and Proposition
3.33
are somewhat intricate and need preparation which will be developed in the next sections.

3.4.2.
Compatibility with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.30
. It asserts that charts (
36
) yield a particular
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-atlas on for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-structure on
A
defined in Section
3.3
. To this end, we will use the following consequence of Lemma
3.26
(d)
,
(e)
. We use the notion of forms bounded from
X
, introduced in Definition
3.23
, and the function
\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}
defined in (
33
).

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
, with associated index set
S
. Fix
i\in S
, and a subset
I\subseteq S
not containing
i
. Put
U_{X,I}^{\circ}=U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

There is a bounded function
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
such that on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
we have
v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}b
.

(b)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there are bounded functions
c,q\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
, and a bounded
1
-form
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
such that on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
we have

d(v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i})=t_{i}\cdot c\,dv_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot q\,dg+\sigma_{i}\cdot\gamma.

In particular, taking
I=\emptyset
, we get that the above equalities hold on
U_{X,\emptyset}^{\circ}=U_{X}\setminus D=U\setminus\partial A
, with
b,c,q
and
\gamma
bounded from
X
.

For
p\in R
, let
U_{X}^{p}
be the corresponding chart from
\mathcal{U}_{X}
, and let
v_{i}^{p}
be its associated function (
19
). By (
23
), we have
\overline{v}_{i}=\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p}
. Put
V_{X,I}^{p}=U_{X}^{p}\cap U_{X,I}^{\circ}
.

(a)
Since
\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}=1
, we have
v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i}=\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})
. For every
p\in R
there is a bounded function
b^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X,I}^{p})
such that
v_{i}-v_{i}^{p}=\sigma_{i}b^{p}
: indeed, if
D_{i}
meets
\overline{U}_{X}^{p}
then it follows from Lemma
3.26
(d)
; otherwise
v_{i}^{p}=0
and
b^{p}\coloneqq v_{i}\sigma_{i}^{-1}|_{V_{X,I}^{p}}
is bounded. Since
\tau^{p}|_{U_{X,I}^{\circ}}
is supported on
V_{X,I}^{p}
, the equality
\tau^{p}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})=\sigma_{i}\tau^{p}b^{p}
holds on the whole
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
. Thus
v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}b^{p}=\sigma_{i}b
, where
b\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}b^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
is bounded, as needed.

(b)
Since
\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}=1
, we have
d(v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i})=\sum_{p\in R}d[\tau^{p}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})]=\sum_{p\in R}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})\,d\tau^{p}+\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}\,d(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})
. As before, Lemma
3.26
(d)
implies that on
V^{p}_{X,I}
we have
v_{i}-v_{i}^{p}=\sigma_{i}b^{p}
for some bounded
b^{p}
, so on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
we have
\sum_{p\in R}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})d\tau^{p}=\sigma_{i}\sum_{p\in R}b^{p}d\tau^{p}=\sigma_{i}\gamma_{0}
for some
1
-form
\gamma_{0}
bounded from
X
.

Similarly, Lemma
3.26
(e)
implies that on
V^{p}_{X,I}
we have
d(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})=t_{i}c^{p}\,dv_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}q^{p}\,dg+\sigma_{i}\gamma^{p}
for some bounded
c^{p},q^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V_{X,I}^{p})
and
\gamma^{p}\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X,I}^{p})
. Indeed, if
D_{i}
meets
\overline{U}_{X}^{p}
then this is the statement of
3.26
(e)
for
U_{X}^{\prime}=U_{X}^{p}
. Otherwise, the restriction of
t_{i}^{-1}
to
V_{X,I}^{p}
is bounded, and by convention
v_{i}^{p}=0
, so we can take
c^{p}=t_{i}^{-1}
,
q^{p}=0
and
\gamma^{p}=0
. Multiplying by
\tau^{p}
and taking the sum we get a formula
\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}d(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})=t_{i}c\,dv_{i}+t_{i}^{\varepsilon}q\,dg+\sigma_{i}\gamma_{1}
valid on the whole
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
, where the functions
c\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}c^{p}
,
q\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}q^{p}
and
1
-form
\gamma_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}\gamma^{p}
are smooth and bounded on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
.

Adding these formulas together and taking
\gamma=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}
gives
(b)
.
∎

Fix
x\in D
, so
x\in X_{S}^{\circ}
for some nonempty
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
. Let
U_{X}
be a chart adapted to
f
containing
x
, whose associated index set is
S
. For
i\in S
, let
U_{i}
be the domain of the corresponding chart
\overline{\psi}_{i}
introduced in (
36
). We need to show that there is a neighborhood
V_{X}
of
x
in
U_{X}
, such that for every
i\in S
, the map
\overline{\psi}_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism on
U_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(V_{X})
.

Fix
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)\cap U_{i}
. Then
t_{j}(y)=0
for all
j\in S
, so
\sigma_{j}(y)=0
by definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{j}
. Thus by Lemma
3.35
(b)
, the forms
dv_{j}
and
d\overline{v}_{j}
are equal at
y
. Since the map
\overline{\psi}_{i}
introduced in (
36
) differs from the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-chart
\psi_{i}
introduced in (
29
) only by replacing
v_{j}
by
\overline{v}_{j}
, we infer that the Jacobian matrix of
\overline{\psi}_{i}
with respect to the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-coordinates
\psi_{i}
is the identity at
y
. By the inverse function theorem, there is a neighborhood
V_{y}
of
y
in
U_{i}
such that
\overline{\psi}_{i}|_{V_{y}}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

We claim that there is a neighborhood
V_{X}
of
x
in
U_{X}
, such that
\overline{\psi}_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(V_{X})}
is injective. Suppose the contrary. Then there are sequences
(y^{\nu}_{1}),(y^{\nu}_{2})\subseteq U_{i}
such that both
\pi(y^{\nu}_{1})
and
\pi(y^{\nu}_{2})
converge to
x
, and we have
y_{1}^{\nu}\neq y_{2}^{\nu}
,
\overline{\psi}_{1}(y^{\nu}_{1})=\overline{\psi}_{1}(y^{\nu}_{2})
for all
\nu
. By Proposition
3.7
(b)
, the map
\pi
is proper, so passing to subsequences we can assume that for
l\in\{1,2\}
we have
y^{\nu}_{l}\rightarrow y_{l}
for some
y_{l}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
. Since
\overline{\psi}_{i}
is continuous, we have
\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{1})=\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{2})
.

Since
x\in X_{S}^{\circ}
, for every
j\in S
and
l\in\{1,2\}
we have, as before,
t_{j}(y_{l})=0
and
\sigma_{j}(y_{l})=0
. Lemma
3.35
(a)
implies that
\overline{v}_{j}(y_{l})={v}_{j}(y_{l})
, so
\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{l})=\psi_{i}(y_{l})
. Therefore,
\psi_{i}(y_{1})=\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{1})=\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{2})=\psi_{i}(y_{2})
. Since
\psi_{i}
is injective by Lemma
3.11
, we get
y_{1}=y_{2}
, so the sequences
(y_{1}^{\nu})
and
(y_{2}^{\nu})
converge to the same limit
y_{1}
. Thus for
\nu\gg 1
the points
y_{l}^{\nu}
lie in the neighborhood
V_{y_{1}}
of
y_{1}
where
\psi_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism. Since by assumption
\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{1}^{\nu})=\overline{\psi}_{i}(y_{2}^{\nu})
, we get
y_{1}^{\nu}=y_{2}^{\nu}
, a contradiction.

By Proposition
3.7
(b)
the map
\pi
is proper, so we can shrink the neighborhood
V_{X}
of
x
so that
\pi^{-1}(V_{X})\cap U_{i}\subseteq\bigcup_{y\in\pi^{-1}(x)}V_{y}
. This way,
\overline{\psi}_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(V_{X})}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism, as needed.

∎

The remaining part of Section
3.4
is devoted to the proof of Lemma
3.31
and Proposition
3.33
. Throughout this proof, we will use the following notation.

Notation 3.36
.

We fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
satisfying the statement of Lemma
3.30
, and put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. We reorder the components of
D
so that the associated index set (
12
) of
U_{X}
is
S=\{1,\dots,k\}
. As in (
28
), we put
U_{1}=\{w_{1}>\tfrac{1}{n+1}\}\subseteq U
. Now on
U_{1}
, we have a smooth structure given by the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-chart
\psi_{1}=(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
, see (
29
). We use Notation
3.27
for this chart. That is, for an open subset
V\subseteq U_{1}
we denote by
\mathcal{S}(V)
and
\mathcal{S}^{l}(V)
the algebras of smooth (resp.
\mathcal{C}^{l}
) functions on
V
; put
\partial_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial g}
,
\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}
and
T_{i}=\{t_{i}>0\}\subseteq U_{1}
,
W_{i}=\{w_{i}>0\}\subseteq U_{1}
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
.

Recall that by Lemma
3.11
, the restriction
\pi|_{U_{1}\setminus\partial A}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism. Thus, with our usual abuse of notation, we can write
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\setminus D)\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus\partial A)
.

We end this section with a simple consequence of Lemma
3.22
.

Lemma 3.37
.

For all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, we have
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
and
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(T_{i}\cup W_{i})
.

Recall that
w_{i},t_{i}
are continuous on
U_{1}
by Lemma
3.6
(a)
. Assume that for some
l\geqslant 0
we have
w_{i},t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{l}(W_{i})
, that is, the restrictions
w_{i}|_{W_{i}}
,
t_{i}|_{W_{i}}
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{l}
with respect to the smooth coordinates
(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
. Then by Lemma
3.22
(e)
,
(c)
, all partial derivatives of
w_{i}|_{W_{i}}
,
t_{i}|_{W_{i}}
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{l}
, too. Hence
w_{i}|_{W_{i}},t_{i}|_{W_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}^{l+1}(W_{i})
. By induction,
t_{i},w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
. Similarly, Lemma
3.22
(d)
implies that on
T_{i}
, all partial derivatives of
t_{i}
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{l}
whenever
t_{i}
is, so
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
.

∎

3.4.3.
Flattening algebras

We will now introduce a practical tool which allows to extend the smoothness domain of a function along its zero locus. First, we settle the following notation.

Let
V
be a subset of
U_{1}
. Given a subset
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(V)
, we denote by
\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra (possibly without
1
) generated by
\mathcal{F}
. For two
\mathbb{R}
-subalgebras
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(V)
we put
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}=\langle\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\rangle
and
\mathcal{A}\cdot\mathcal{B}=\langle a\cdot b:a\in\mathcal{A},b\in\mathcal{B}\rangle=\{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}a_{i}b_{i}:\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A},b_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\}
. In particular, if
\mathcal{F}
is a subset of
\mathcal{A}
then
\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle\cdot\mathcal{A}
is the ideal of
\mathcal{A}
generated by
\mathcal{F}
. For a function
h\in\mathcal{S}(V)
, we write
h\cdot\mathcal{A}
for the algebra
\langle h\rangle\cdot\mathcal{A}
. Thus if
h\in\mathcal{A}
, then
h\cdot\mathcal{A}
is, as usual, the principal ideal of
\mathcal{A}
generated by
h
.

Recall that we have introduced differential operators
\partial_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial g}
,
\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. For an
\mathbb{R}
-subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(V)
we write
\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}=\{\partial_{j}a:a\in\mathcal{A}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(V)
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
closed under derivation by
\partial
(or simply
closed under
\partial
) if
\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. An easy application of Leibniz rule shows that an algebra
\mathcal{A}=\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
generated by
\mathcal{F}
is closed under
\partial
if and only if
\partial_{j}h\in\mathcal{A}
for every generator
h\in\mathcal{F}
and every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Definition 3.38
.

Fix a closed subset
Z\subseteq U_{1}\cap\partial A
and a function
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus Z)
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus Z)
which is closed under derivation by
\partial
.

We say that
h
flattens
\mathcal{A}
on
Z
if for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and every
\varepsilon>0
we have

(37)

|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot a\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{ on }Z.

The following Lemma
3.39
lists some elementary consequences of Definition
3.38
. Part
(a)
asserts that condition (
37
) – just like closedness under
\partial
– can be checked on generators. Part
(b)
gives a practical condition under which not only
h
, but all its derivatives flatten
\mathcal{A}
. Together with part
(c)
, it will be used to infer smoothness of
h
.

Lemma 3.39
.

Fix a closed subset
Z\subseteq U_{1}
and a continuous function
h
on
U_{1}
such that
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus Z)
and
h|_{Z}=0
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus Z)
, and let
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
be the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{F}
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is closed under
\partial
. Then the following hold.

(a)

If for every
a\in\mathcal{F}
and every
\varepsilon>0
we have
|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot a\rightarrow 0
on
Z
, then
h
flattens
\mathcal{A}
on
Z
.

(b)

Assume that
h
flattens
\mathcal{A}
on
Z
, and for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
h^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}h\in\mathcal{A}
. Then for all
l\geqslant 0
and all
j_{1},\dots,j_{l}\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the function
\partial_{j_{1}}\dots\partial_{j_{l}}h
flattens
\mathcal{A}
on
Z
.

(c)

Assume that all derivatives
\partial_{j_{1}}\dots\partial_{j_{l}}h
flatten
\mathcal{A}
on
Z
. Then for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
ha\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, and
\partial^{\alpha}(ha)|_{Z}=0
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. In particular, if
1\in\mathcal{A}
then
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
and all derivatives of
h
vanish on
Z
.

(a)
Since by assumption
h|_{Z}=0
and
h
is continuous, all constant functions
a
satisfy (
37
). Now if
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
satisfy (
37
) then
|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot(a+b)=|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot a+|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot b\rightarrow 0
, and
|h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot(ab)=(|h|^{\varepsilon/2}\cdot a)\cdot(|h|^{\varepsilon/2}\cdot b)\rightarrow 0
on
Z
, so
a+b
and
ab
satisfy (
37
), too, as needed.

(b)
We need to prove that for every
\varepsilon>0
and every
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
|\partial_{j_{1}}\dots\partial_{j_{l}}h|^{\varepsilon}\cdot a\rightarrow 0
on
Z
. It is sufficient to prove this claim for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\varepsilon=1
. Indeed, by continuity it is sufficient to consider the case
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
, which follows from case
\varepsilon=1
applied to some power of
a
.

For
\boldsymbol{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l})\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{l}
put
\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}=\partial_{j_{1}}\dots\partial_{j_{l}}
and
|\boldsymbol{j}|=l
.

We argue by induction on
l\geqslant 0
. Case
l=0
holds by assumption. Fix
l\geqslant 1
and assume that the claim holds for all
l^{\prime}<l
. Fix
\boldsymbol{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l})
and put
\boldsymbol{j}^{\prime}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l-1})
. By assumption, the function
b\coloneqq h^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j_{l}}h
belongs to
\mathcal{A}
. Now
a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}h=a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}^{\prime}}(hb)=a\cdot\sum_{|\boldsymbol{i}|,|\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}|<l}c_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}}h\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}b
for some
c_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is closed under
\partial
, we have
a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}b\in\mathcal{A}
, so by inductive assumption,
\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}}h\cdot(a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}b)\rightarrow 0
on
Z
. Thus
a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}h\rightarrow 0
on
Z
, as needed.

(c)
By assumption,
ha\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus Z)
. As before, for any
\boldsymbol{j}\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{l}
we can write
