\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}(ah)=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{i}|,|\boldsymbol{i^{\prime}}|}c_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}}a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}h
for some
c_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is closed under
\partial
, we have
\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}}a\in\mathcal{A}
. Since
\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}h
flattens
\mathcal{A}
, we have
\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}}a\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{i}^{\prime}}h\rightarrow 0
on
Z
. Hence
\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}(ah)
extends to a continuous function on
U_{1}
, which vanishes on
Z
, as claimed. If furthermore
1\in\mathcal{A}
then substituting
a=1
to the first claim we get
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
and
\partial^{\alpha}h|_{Z}=0
, as claimed.
∎

3.4.4.
The functions
r_{j}
,
w_{j}
are smooth.

In this section, we establish a
“smooth version”
of Lemma
3.29
. Its most important consequence will be the fact that, for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, the functions
w_{i},r_{i}
are in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, and for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, their derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}w_{i}
,
\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}
, vanish on the zero locus of
w_{i}
and
r_{i}
, respectively. To prove this result, for each
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we introduce the following
\mathbb{R}
-algebras:

(38)

\begin{split}\mathcal{W}_{i}&=\langle 1,t_{i},w_{i},u_{i},\rho_{i},t^{(l)}w_{i}^{-1}\ :\ l\geqslant 0\rangle,\\
\mathcal{R}_{i}&=\langle 1,t_{i},t_{i}^{-1},\log t_{i},g,(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1},(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}\rangle.\end{split}

We will study their properties in Lemmas
3.40
and
3.41
, respectively.

Lemma 3.40
.

Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, and let
\mathcal{W}_{i}
be as in (
38
). Then the following hold.

(a)

For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
w_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
.

(b)

The algebra
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is contained in
\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
and is closed under derivation by
\partial
.

(c)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the function
\partial^{\alpha}w_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{W}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
.

(d)

We have
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, for every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
.

(e)

We have
w_{1},t_{1},v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(a)
Recall that by Lemma
3.28
we have
\partial_{j}w_{i}=0
for all
j\neq i,1
. By Lemma
3.22
(e)
, we have

w_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{i}w_{i}=-\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i},\quad\mbox{and}\quad w_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{1}w_{i}=\rho_{i}\cdot(t^{\prime}w_{i}^{-1})\in\mathcal{W}_{i}.

(b)
By Lemma
3.29
(a)
, we have
t^{(l)}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
for all
l\geqslant 0
. By Lemma
3.37
, we have
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
: recall that, by definition,
W_{i}=\{w_{i}>0\}
is the complement of the zero locus of
w_{i}
. Hence
t^{(l)}w_{i}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
. The function
u_{i}
was defined in (
18
) as
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})
, where
\eta|_{(0,1]}\colon(0,1]\longrightarrow(0,1]
is smooth. Therefore,
u_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
and
u_{i}|_{W_{i}}>0
. It follows that
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
, so
\mathcal{W}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
, as claimed.

Now, we check that
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
. It is sufficient to check
\partial_{j}a\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
for all generators
a
of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
, and all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. By Lemma
3.22
(c)
, we have

\partial_{i}t_{i}=\rho_{i}t_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i},\quad\partial_{1}t_{i}=\rho_{i}u_{i}^{2}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{i},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{j}t_{i}=0\mbox{ for }j\neq i,1.

Thus
\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Since
w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
, part
(a)
implies that
\partial_{j}w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
, too. Recall that
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})
. By Lemma
3.21
(g)
, we have
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})=u_{i}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}
, so
\partial_{j}u_{i}=\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})\partial_{j}w_{i}=u_{i}^{2}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}\partial_{j}w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
by
(a)
. In (
33
), we have defined
\rho_{i}
as
(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
, so
\partial_{j}\rho_{i}=-\rho_{i}^{2}(\partial_{j}t_{i}+2u_{i}\partial_{j}u_{i})\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Eventually,

\partial_{j}(w_{i}^{-1}t^{(l)})=-w_{i}^{-1}t^{(l)}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}\partial_{j}w_{i}+\delta_{j}^{1}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}t^{(l+1)}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}.

(c)
,
(d)
It is sufficient to show that
w_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{W}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
. Indeed, since by
(b)

\mathcal{W}_{i}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
closed under
\partial
, and by
(a)
it contains
w_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}w_{i}
, Lemma
3.39
(b)
shows that if
w_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{W}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
, then so do all its partial derivatives (taken in any order). Once this is shown, Lemma
3.39
(c)
will imply that
w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

By Lemma
3.39
(a)
, it is sufficient to check that, for every generator
a
of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
and every
\varepsilon>0
, we have
w_{i}^{\varepsilon}a\rightarrow 0
as
w_{i}\rightarrow 0
. This is clear for
all bounded generators, i.e. for
1
,
t_{i}
,
w_{i}
and
u_{i}
. Recall from (
33
) and (
18
) that
\rho_{i}=(t_{i}+u_{i}^{2})^{-1}
and
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})=(1-\log w_{i})^{-1}
, so

\rho_{i}w_{i}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{w_{i}^{\varepsilon}}{t_{i}+(1-\log w_{i})^{-2}}=\frac{w_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot(1-\log w_{i})^{2}}{t_{i}(1-\log(w_{i}))^{2}+1}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as }w_{i}\rightarrow 0,

because
t_{i}(1-\log w_{i})^{2}\geqslant 0
. Eventually, by Lemma
3.21
(d)
there is a polynomial
p\in\mathbb{R}[s]
such that
t^{(l)}=tp(\log t)
. Since by Lemma
3.21
(b)
we have
t=t_{i}w_{i}
, we get

w_{i}^{\varepsilon}\cdot t^{(l)}w_{i}^{-1}=w_{i}^{\varepsilon}t_{i}\cdot p(\log t_{i}+\log w_{i})\rightarrow 0,\quad\mbox{as }w_{i}\rightarrow 0.

(e)
We argue as in the proof of Lemma
3.29
(b)
. Part
(d)
applied to
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
gives
\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
. By Lemma
3.6
(c)
, we have
w_{1}=1-\sum_{i=2}^{k}w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
. Because
t\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.29
(a)
, and
w_{1}|_{U_{1}}>0
by (
28
), we have
t_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, and
v_{1}=t_{1}-\eta(w_{1})\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

∎

Lemma 3.41
.

Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, and let
\mathcal{R}_{i}
be as in (
38
). Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
u_{i},\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and
r_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}r_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

(b)

The algebra
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is contained in
\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
and is closed under derivation by
\partial
.

(c)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the functions
\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}
and
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}
flatten
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
and on
U_{1}\cap\partial A
, respectively.

(d)

We have
r_{i},r_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(e)

For every
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, the pullback of
h
to
U_{1}
is in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
(in short:
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
).

Denote the last two generators of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
by
b=(1+g\log t_{i})^{-1}
and
c=(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}
.

(a)

By Lemma
3.21
(e)
we have
u_{i}=gb\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and by Lemma
3.21
(f)
we have
\rho_{i}=(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}\cdot c\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
. By Lemma
3.22
(d)
, we have

\partial_{i}t_{i}=t_{i}c(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{i},\quad\partial_{1}t_{i}=t_{i}c\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{j}t_{i}=0\mbox{ for }j\neq 1,i.

Hence by Lemma
3.22
(a)
,
r_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}r_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{-2}\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

(b)
Recall that
T_{i}=\{t_{i}>0\}
. Clearly, the coordinate
g
is in
\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
. By Lemma
3.37
we have
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
, so
t_{i}^{-1},\log t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
, and in consequence
b,c\in\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
, so
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
, as needed.

Now, we check that
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
. We have already seen that
\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Hence
\partial_{j}t_{i}^{-1}=-t_{i}^{-2}\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
and
\partial_{j}\log t_{i}=t_{i}^{-1}\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
. By definition of
\partial_{j}
, we have
\partial_{j}g=\delta_{j}^{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Eventually,

\begin{split}\partial_{j}b&=-b^{2}\cdot(\partial_{j}g\cdot\log t_{i}+gt_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}t_{i})\in\mathcal{R}_{i},\\
\partial_{j}c&=-c^{2}\cdot(\partial_{j}t_{i}\cdot(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+2t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})(\log t_{i}\cdot\partial_{j}g+gt_{i}^{-1}\cdot\partial_{j}t_{i})+2g\partial_{j}g)\in\mathcal{R}_{i}.\end{split}

(c)
,
(d)
We claim that
r_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1}\setminus T_{i})
, by Lemma
3.39
(a)
it is enough to show that all generators of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
satisfy (
37
). Since
r_{i}|_{U_{1}\setminus T_{i}}=0
, this condition is clear for all bounded functions, hence for
1
,
t_{i}
,
g
and
b
. By Lemma
3.21
(a)
, we have
r_{i}^{\varepsilon}=e^{-\varepsilon(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
, so
r_{i}^{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-1}\rightarrow 0
and
r_{i}^{\varepsilon}\log t_{i}\rightarrow 0
. It remains to check that
r_{i}^{\varepsilon}c\rightarrow 0
. By Lemma
3.21
(h)
, we have
(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}=t_{i}\cdot(\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime})^{-1}
, and by Lemma
3.21
(i)
,
\eta^{\prime}(w_{i})t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{\delta}l
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
and a bounded function
l
. Hence
(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}=t_{i}^{1-\delta}l^{-1}
, and therefore

c=(t_{i}(1+g\log t_{i})^{2}+g^{2})^{-1}=(t_{i}^{2-\delta}l^{-1}+g^{2})^{-1}=t_{i}^{\delta-2}l\cdot q,

where
q\coloneqq(1+g^{2}t_{i}^{\delta-2}l)^{-1}
is bounded. Hence
r_{i}^{\varepsilon}c=e^{-\varepsilon(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}t_{i}^{\delta-2}q\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, as claimed.

Thus
r_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
. Since
r_{i}^{-1}\partial_{j}r_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
by
(a)
, Lemma
3.39
(b)
implies that all derivatives
\partial_{j_{1}}\dots\partial_{j_{l}}r_{i}
flatten
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{i}\setminus T_{i}
, too, as claimed. Part
(a)
and Lemma
3.39
(c)
imply that
r_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

Recall that
t_{1}
, hence
r_{1}
, are in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.40
(d)
. We claim that
r_{1}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\cap\partial A
.

By definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
, we have
w_{1}>0
, so by Lemma
3.21
(b)
, on
U_{1}\cap\partial A
we have
t_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}=0
, and therefore
r_{1}=0
. In
(b)
, we have shown that
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
, so all elements of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
are bounded on
T_{i}
. Hence
r_{1}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
T_{i}\cap\partial A
. We claim that
r_{1}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
, too.

Define
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}_{i}+\langle t_{1}^{-1},\partial^{\alpha}t_{1}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\rangle
. Because
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
by
(b)
, so is
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
. We claim that
r_{1}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
. By Lemma
3.39
(a)
, we need to prove that all elements of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and all functions
t_{1}^{-1}
,
\partial^{\alpha}t_{1}
satisfy (
37
). By Lemma
3.21
(a)
,
(b)
, we have on
U_{1}\setminus\partial A

(39)

r_{1}=\exp(-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}t_{1}})=\exp(-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}\tfrac{w_{1}}{t})=\exp(-\tfrac{m_{i}}{m_{1}}\tfrac{1}{m_{i}}\tfrac{1}{t_{i}}\tfrac{w_{1}}{w_{i}})=r_{i}^{\frac{m_{i}}{m_{1}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{i}}}<r_{i}^{\gamma},\quad\mbox{where }\gamma=\tfrac{m_{i}}{m_{1}(n+1)}.

For the last inequality, we have used the fact that
r_{1}<1
by Definition
3.1
of a chart adapted to
f
;
w_{i}\leqslant 1
by Lemma
3.6
(c)
; and
w_{1}>\tfrac{1}{n+1}
by definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
.

We have seen that
r_{i}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
, so for every
\varepsilon>0
and every
a\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
we have
|r_{1}^{\varepsilon}a|<|r_{i}^{\gamma\varepsilon}a|\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
. Hence all elements of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
satisfy (
37
), as needed.

To prove (
37
) for
t_{1}^{-1}
, recall from Lemma
3.21
(a)
that
r_{1}=e^{-\varepsilon(m_{1}t_{1})^{-1}}
, so
t_{1}^{-1}r_{1}^{\varepsilon}=t_{1}^{-1}e^{-\varepsilon(m_{1}t_{1})^{-1}}
. By (
39
), we have
t_{1}|_{U_{1}\setminus T_{i}}=0
, so
t_{1}^{-1}r_{1}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
, as claimed. Eventually, by Lemma
3.40
(d)
, we have
t_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, so for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the function
\partial^{\alpha}t_{1}
is bounded on
U_{1}
, in particular
r_{1}^{\varepsilon}\partial^{\alpha}t_{1}\rightarrow 0
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
. Thus we have shown that
r_{1}
flattens
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
3.22
(a)
, we have
r_{1}^{-1}\partial_{j}r_{1}=m_{1}^{-1}t_{1}^{-2}\partial_{j}t_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, so by Lemma
3.39
(b)
all derivatives of
r_{1}
flatten
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
, too. Since
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
, it follows that they flatten
\mathcal{R}_{i}
, as claimed.

(e)
Follows from
(d)
by the chain rule applied as in the proof of Lemma
3.29
(d)
.

∎

3.4.5.
The Jacobian matrix of
\overline{\psi}_{1}

In Section
3.4.2
, we have used the fact that the Jacobian matrix of
\overline{\psi}_{1}=(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)
with respect to our auxiliary coordinates
\psi_{1}=(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
is a small deformation of the identity. The difference can be computed using Lemma
3.35
(b)
. We will now study this difference in more detail. In Lemma
3.47
, proved at the end of this section, we will see that this difference belongs to a particular matrix ring
\mathcal{M}
introduced in Definition
3.45
. Properties of
\mathcal{M}
will allow to conveniently express the coordinate vector fields of the smooth chart
\overline{\psi}_{1}
.

Recall that we work with a fixed
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
, where
\mathcal{U}_{X}=\{U_{X}^{p}\}_{p\in R}
is an atlas adapted to
f
, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\{\tau^{p}\}_{p\in R}
is a partition of unity inscribed in
\mathcal{U}_{X}
.
To simplify the notation, we will now restrict our attention to charts which are small in the following sense.

Lemma 3.42
.

Every point
x\in X
lies in a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
with the following property. For every
p\in R
such that
\tau^{p}
is not identically zero on
U_{X}
, we have
U_{X}\subseteq U_{X}^{p}
.

For
p\in R
let
G^{p}
be the closure of the support of
\tau^{p}
, i.e.
G^{p}=\overline{\{y\in X:\tau^{p}(y)>0\}}
. Put
R_{x}=\{p\in R:x\in G^{p}\}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is locally finite, there is an open neighborhood
V^{\prime}
of
x
such that the set
R^{\prime}=\{p\in R:\tau^{p}|_{V^{\prime}}\not\equiv 0\}
is finite. Clearly,
R_{x}\subseteq R^{\prime}
. Put
V=V^{\prime}\cap\bigcap_{p\in R_{x}}U^{p}_{X}\setminus\bigcup_{p\in R^{\prime}\setminus R_{x}}G^{p}
. Clearly
x\in V
, and since
R^{\prime}
and
R_{x}
are finite, the set
V
is open.

Now, fix
p\in R
such that
\tau^{p}
is not identically zero on
V
. Then
\tau^{p}|_{V^{\prime}}\not\equiv 0
, so
p\in R^{\prime}
by definition of
R^{\prime}
. Definition of
V
implies that
p\in R_{x}
, and therefore,
V\subseteq U^{p}_{X}
. We conclude that any chart adapted to
f
contained in
V
has the required property.

∎

From now on, we fix a chart
U_{X}
adapted to
f
, satisfying the statements of Lemmas
3.30
and
3.42
, and use Notation
3.36
for this chart.

Put
R_{0}=\{p\in R:\tau^{p}|_{U_{X}}\not\equiv 0\}
. Then the statement of Lemma
3.42
asserts that
U_{X}\subseteq U_{X}^{p}
for every
p\in R_{0}
. Thus for every
p\in R_{0}
and
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have a
“transition”
function
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
defined in Lemma
3.9
(c)
. We define
\mathcal{T}_{i}
as the set of those functions
a
, as
p
ranges among the set
R_{0}
. By definition,
\mathcal{T}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, so by Lemma
3.41
(e)
we have
\mathcal{T}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, and let
\mathcal{R}_{i}
,
\mathcal{W}_{i}
be the
\mathbb{R}
-algebras defined in (
38
). We define

(40)

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{i}&=\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}+\langle\partial^{\alpha}h,\ \partial^{\alpha}r_{j},\ \cos(2\pi\theta_{j}),\ \sin(2\pi\theta_{j}),\ (1+at_{i})^{-1},\ \log(1+at_{i}),\\
&\phantom{=\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}+\langle\ }(1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i}))^{-1}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k},\ h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}),\ j\in\{1,i\},\ a\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\rangle,\\
\mathcal{I}_{i}&=\langle\partial^{\alpha}r_{1},\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\rangle\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}.\end{split}

This way,
\mathcal{I}_{i}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
generated by all the derivatives of
r_{1}
,
r_{i}
. Intuitively speaking, the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}
consists of elements which decay exponentially fast with respect to
t_{i}
. In fact, we have
r_{i}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}
by Lemma
3.21
(a)
; and we have seen in (
39
) that
r_{1}<r_{i}^{\gamma}
for some constant
\gamma>0
.

We now list basic properties of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and its ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

Lemma 3.43
.

For each
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, the following hold.

(a)

We have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
.

(b)

Both the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and its ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}
are closed under
\partial
.

(c)

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the functions
\partial^{\alpha}w_{i}
,
\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}
and
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}
flatten
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
,
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
and on
U_{1}\cap\partial A
, respectively.

(d)

We have
\mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(e)

For every
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, we have
\partial_{i}h\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

(f)

For every
j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
such that
j\neq i
we have
\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{j}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

Recall that
t_{i},u_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Indeed, by definition (
38
) of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
\mathcal{R}_{i}
we have
t_{i},u_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}
and
t_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
; and by Lemma
3.41
(a)
we have
u_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

(a)
We have
\mathcal{W}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
by Lemma
3.40
(b)
and
\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
by Lemma
3.41
(b)
, so
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
. In particular, the above remark shows that
t_{i},u_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
.

By definition of the coordinate chart (
36
), we have
\cos(2\pi\theta_{j}),\sin(2\pi\theta_{j})\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
. By Lemma
3.41
(d)
,
(e)
we have
r_{j}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, so
\partial^{\alpha}r_{j},\partial^{\alpha}h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
for all
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
; in particular
a\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
for every
a\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
. By Lemma
3.26
(b)
we have
1+at_{i}\neq 0
and
1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i})\neq 0
on
U_{1}
, so the fact that
t_{i},u_{i},a\in\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
implies that the remaining generators
(1+at_{i})^{-1}
,
\log(1+at_{i})
and
(1-u_{i}\log(1+at_{i}))^{-1}
are in
\mathcal{S}(W_{i}\cup T_{i})
, as claimed.

(b)
The algebras
\mathcal{W}_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i}
are closed under
\partial
by Lemmas
3.40
(b)
and
3.41
(b)
, respectively. Hence their intersection
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
, too.

To show that
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
, it remains to check that for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
\partial_{j}
maps the remaining generators of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. For all derivatives of
r_{j}
and functions
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, this is clear by definition of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Moreover,
\partial_{j}\cos(2\pi\theta_{l})=0
and
\partial_{j}\sin(2\pi\theta_{l})=0
for all
l
by definition of the chart (
36
). Fix
a\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
, and put
b=1+at_{i}
. Then
b^{-1}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Since
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, we have
\partial_{j}a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Since
t_{i}
belongs to the algebra
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
which is closed under
\partial
, we have
\partial_{j}t_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Hence
\partial_{j}b=a\partial_{j}t_{i}+t_{i}\partial_{j}a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. As a consequence, we have
\partial_{j}b^{-1}=-b^{-2}\partial_{j}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\partial_{j}\log b=b^{-1}\partial_{j}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. It remains to prove that
\partial_{j}(1-u_{i}\log b)^{-1}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. To see this, recall that
u_{i}
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, which is closed under
\partial
, so
\partial_{j}u_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Now
\partial_{j}(1-u_{i}\log b)^{-1}=(1-u_{i}\log b)^{-2}\cdot(u_{i}\partial_{j}\log b+\log b\,\partial_{j}u_{i})\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, as needed.

Thus
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
. Closedness of its ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}
follows by the Leibniz rule.

(c)
By Lemma
3.40
(c)
, all derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}w_{i}
flatten
\mathcal{W}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
(and vanish there), so in particular they flatten there a subalgebra
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Similarly, Lemma
3.41
(c)
implies that
\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}
,
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}
flatten
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
and
U_{1}\cap\partial A
, respectively. By Lemma
3.39
(a)
, it is sufficient to prove that all the remaining generators of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
satisfy (
37
). This is clear since they are all bounded. Indeed, by Lemma
3.41
(d)
,
(e)
,
r_{j}
and all
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{1})
are in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, so their derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}r_{j},\partial^{\alpha}h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
are bounded. Of course,
|\cos(2\pi\theta_{j})|,|\sin(2\pi\theta_{j})|\leqslant 1
. The remaining generators of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
are bounded by Lemma
3.26
(b)
.

(d)
We need to show that, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the functions
(\partial^{\alpha}r_{1})\cdot a
and
(\partial^{\alpha}r_{i})\cdot a
are in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
. By
(a)
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(T_{i})
. By
(c)
, both
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}
and
\partial^{\alpha}r_{i}
flatten
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus T_{i}
. Thus the claim follows from Lemma
3.39
(c)
.

(e)
Since
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
, by the chain rule it is sufficient to prove that the coordinate functions
x_{j}=r_{j}\cos(2\pi\theta_{j})
,
y_{j}=r_{j}\sin(2\pi\theta_{j})
satisfy
\partial_{i}x_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
\partial_{i}y_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
. By definition of the chart (
36
), we have
\partial_{i}x_{j}
,
\partial_{i}y_{j}=0
for
j\neq 1,i
. If
j\in\{1,i\}
then
\partial_{i}\theta_{j}=0
, so
\partial_{i}x_{j}=\cos(2\pi\theta_{j})\cdot\partial_{i}r_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
; and similarly
\partial_{i}y_{j}=\sin(2\pi\theta_{j})\cdot\partial_{i}r_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

(f)
Assume
j\neq 1,i
.
By Lemma
3.28
, we have
\partial_{j}t_{i}=0
,
\partial_{j}u_{i}=0
, and more generally,
\partial_{j}c=0
for all
c\in\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Thus
\partial_{j}(\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i})=\{0\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}
. By
(e)
, for any
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
we have
\partial_{j}h\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
. Since by
(b)
the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{j}
is closed under
\partial
, we get
\partial_{j}\partial^{\alpha}h=\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{j}h\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
for all
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.
Moreover,
\partial_{j}\cos(2\pi\theta_{l})
,
\partial_{j}\sin(2\pi\theta_{l})=0
for
l\in\{1,i\}
;
\partial_{j}r_{i}=0
and
\partial_{j}r_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
by definition of
\mathcal{I}_{j}
.

Let
b
be one of the remaining generators of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Since
\partial_{j}t_{i}=0
and
\partial_{j}u_{i}=0
, when computing
\partial_{j}b
by the chain rule, we get an element of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
multiplied by
\partial_{j}a
, where
a\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
. By
(e)
we have
\partial_{j}a\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
, so
\partial_{j}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}
, as claimed.

∎

We will now introduce additional notation. For
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, we put

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}=\langle w_{i}^{(s)}:s\geqslant 0\rangle,\quad\mbox{where}\quad w_{i}^{(s)}=\partial_{i}^{s}w_{i}.

Moreover, we put

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{i},\quad\mathcal{J}=\langle\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\rangle\cdot\mathcal{A},\quad\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{R}+\mathcal{J}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathcal{I}_{i}.

Then
\mathcal{J}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{P}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
, containing
1
. One should think of
\mathcal{J}
as an ideal whose elements decay exponentially with respect to all
t_{i}
, see (
39
), so they can be used to flatten elements of
\mathcal{A}
. In turn, viewing the elements of
\mathcal{I}_{i}
as those which decay exponentially with respect to
t_{i}
, we can view the elements of
\mathcal{P}
as those which do not require further flattening. In particular, we will see in Lemma
3.44
(c)
below that
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

Lemma 3.44
.

The algebras defined above have the following properties.

(a)

The algebras
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{P}
are closed under
\partial
.

(b)

For every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

(c)

We have
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(d)

For every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

(a)
Follows from Lemma
3.43
(b)

(b)
By
(a)
,
\mathcal{J}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}
which is closed under
\partial
, so
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. It remains to show that for every
j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

Assume
j=i
. By Lemma
3.43
(a)
the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}
of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
, so we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

Assume
j\neq i
. We claim that
\partial_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}
. Let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. We have
a\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}\in\mathcal{J}
, so
\partial_{i}(a\partial^{\alpha}r_{1})\in\mathcal{J}
because
\mathcal{J}
is closed under
\partial
by
(a)
. For the second generator, we compute
\partial_{i}(a\partial^{\alpha}r_{j})=a\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{i}r_{j}+\partial_{i}a\cdot\partial^{\alpha}r_{j}=\partial_{i}a\cdot\partial^{\alpha}r_{j}
, because
\partial_{i}r_{j}=0
by Lemma
3.28
. By Lemma
3.43
(f)
, we have
\partial_{i}a\in\mathcal{I}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}
, so
\partial_{i}a\cdot\partial^{\alpha}r_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}=\mathcal{I}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}
. Hence
\partial_{i}(a\partial^{\alpha}r_{j})\in\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}
, as claimed.

Now
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, as needed.

(c)
We need to show that
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
and
\mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. The second assertion follows from Lemma
3.43
(d)
. For the first one, recall from Lemma
3.43
(c)
that each generator
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}
of
\mathcal{J}
flattens
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
U_{1}\cap\partial A
, for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, so it flattens the whole
\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{i}
there. Thus by Lemma
3.39
(c)
we have
\partial^{\alpha}r_{1}\cdot\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, and therefore
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

(d)
By Lemma
3.43
(c)
, for any
s
the function
w_{i}^{(s)}
flattens
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
U_{1}\setminus W_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(W_{i})
by Lemma
3.43
(a)
, Lemma
3.39
implies that
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, as needed.

∎

Definition 3.45
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the set of
k\times k
matrices
[a_{ij}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant k}
satisfying the following properties.

(a)

For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
a_{1j}=0
(i.e. the first row is zero),

(b)

For every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{P}
,

(c)

For every
i,j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
such that
i\neq j
we have
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}\cdot\mathcal{P}
,

Lemma 3.46
.

The set
\mathcal{M}
is a ring.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
. Clearly,
M+M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
. To show that
M\cdot M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, write
M=[a_{il}]
,
M^{\prime}=[a^{\prime}_{lj}]
,
M\cdot M^{\prime}=[b_{ij}]
. Since
M^{\prime}
satisfies Definition
3.45
(a)
, we have
a^{\prime}_{1j}=0
for every
j
, so

b_{ij}=\sum_{l=2}^{k}a_{il}a_{lj}^{\prime}.

Condition
3.45
(a)
for
M
gives
a_{1l}=0
, so
b_{1j}=0
, too, i.e.
MM^{\prime}
satisfies condition
3.45
(a)
.

To check condition
3.45
(b)
, it is enough to show that
a_{il}a_{lj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
for every
i,l\in\{2,\dots,k\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Consider the case
i=l
. We have
a_{ii}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
and
a_{ij}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, so
a_{ii}a_{ij}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, too, as needed. Consider now the case
i\neq l
. Then
a_{il}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}
and
a_{lj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}
. Since
\mathcal{I}_{l}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}_{l}
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}=\mathcal{I}_{l}
. Thus
a_{il}a_{lj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\cdot\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
because
\mathcal{I}_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, as needed.

Condition
3.45
(c)
is checked similarly. We claim that for every
i,l,j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
such that
i\neq j
, we have
a_{ij}a_{lj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}
. Since
i\neq j
, we have
a_{ii}a_{ij}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}
, which settles the case
i=l
. Assume
i\neq l
, so
a_{il}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}
. If
l=j
then the equality
\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{A}_{j}=\mathcal{I}_{j}
implies that
a_{ij}a_{jj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}
, as needed. Eventually, if
l\neq j
then using the equality
\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}=\mathcal{I}_{l}
we get
a_{il}a_{lj}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{P}
, because
\mathcal{I}_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, which ends the proof.

∎

Recall that our goal is to study the map
\overline{\psi}_{1}=(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{k,n}
introduced in (
36
), in the coordinate system
(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
fixed in Notation
3.36
. The Jacobian matrix of
\overline{\psi}_{1}
with respect to these coordinates has a block form

(41)

\left[\begin{matrix}\mathrm{id}-\Psi&*\\
0&\mathrm{id}\end{matrix}\right]

for some
k\times k
matrix
\Psi
. By Lemma
3.26
(e)
, the matrix
\Psi
is zero on
A_{S}^{\circ}\cap U_{1}
. Therefore, we can, and will, shrink
U_{X}
so that on
U_{1}
we have

(42)

||\Psi||<1.

Recall that we still require
U_{X}
to satisfy the statements of Lemmas
3.30
and
3.42
.

The following result is what motivates Definition
3.45
of the matrix ring
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 3.47
.

The matrix
\Psi
defined above belongs to the ring
\mathcal{M}
.

Recall that for
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the function
\overline{v}_{i}
is defined as
\sum_{p}\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p}
, where
\{\tau^{p}\}
is the fixed partition of unity, and each function
v_{i}^{p}
corresponds to the chart
U_{X}^{p}
of the fixed atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
. Since by assumption our chart
U_{X}
satisfies the statement of Lemma
3.42
, the above sum runs over the finite set
R_{0}=\{p\in R:\tau^{p}|_{U_{X}}\not\equiv 0\}
, and for each
p\in R_{0}
, the function
v^{p}_{i}
is defined on the entire
U_{1}
. We have

d(v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i})=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}\,d(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})+\sum_{p}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})d\tau^{p}

so we can write

\Psi=\sum_{p}[a_{ij}^{p}]+[b_{ij}^{p}],

where the
k\times k
matrices
[a_{ij}^{p}]
and
[b_{ij}^{p}]
are defined by

a_{1j}^{p}=0,\quad a_{ij}^{p}=\tau^{p}\cdot\partial_{j}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad b_{1j}^{p}=0,\quad b_{ij}^{p}=(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})\cdot\partial_{j}\tau^{p},

for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. We will show that
[a_{ij}^{p}]\in\mathcal{M}
and
[b_{ij}^{p}]\in\mathcal{M}
. Clearly, the first rows of these matrices are zero, so they satisfy Definition
3.45
(a)
.

We have
\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, so for every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
by definition (
40
) of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Definition of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and Lemma
3.26
(c)
imply that
v_{i}-v_{i}^{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, too. By Lemma
3.43
(b)
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
, so
\partial_{j}\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\partial_{j}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Since
1\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, and therefore the matrices
[a_{ij}^{p}]
and
[b_{ij}^{p}]
satisfy Definition
3.45
(b)
.

Assume
j\neq 1,i
. Then
\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{A}_{i}
by Lemma
3.43
(f)
. Hence
\tau^{p}\cdot\partial_{j}(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{j}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}
, and similarly
(v_{i}-v_{i}^{p})\cdot\partial_{j}\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{j}
. Again, using the fact that
1\in\mathcal{P}
we infer that
[a_{ij}^{p}]
and
[b_{ij}^{p}]
satisfy Definition
3.45
(c)
. too.

∎

3.4.6.
Differential operators induced by
\mathcal{M}

Our goal is to compare differential operators
\partial_{j}
with the ones given by the coordinate vector fields of our eventual chart
\overline{\psi}_{i}=(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)
. To make this comparison in an efficient way, we introduce the following notation.

For a matrix
M\in\mathcal{M}
, define differential operators
\partial_{1}^{M},\dots,\partial_{k}^{M}
by

[\partial_{1}^{M},\dots,\partial_{k}^{M}]^{\top}=M^{\top}\cdot[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{k}]^{\top}.

We say that an algebra
\mathcal{B}
is
closed under
\partial^{M}
if
\partial^{M}_{j}b\in\mathcal{B}
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and every
b\in\mathcal{B}
. For a multi-index
(j_{1},\dots,j_{s})\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{s}
, with
s\geqslant 0
, we write
\partial_{\boldsymbol{j}}^{M}=\partial_{j_{1}}^{M}\dots,\partial_{j_{s}}^{M}
and
|\boldsymbol{j}|=s
.

Lemma 3.48
.

For every matrix
M\in\mathcal{M}
, the following hold.

(a)

The algebras
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
, for every
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, are closed under
\partial^{M}
.

(b)

For every multi-index
\boldsymbol{j}
we have
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
and
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
for every
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
.

(a)
Write
M=[a_{lj}]
and fix
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Since
a_{1j}=0
by Definition
3.45
(a)
, we have

(43)

\partial_{j}^{M}=\sum_{l=2}^{k}a_{lj}\partial_{l}.

For every
l\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
a_{lj}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\cdot\mathcal{P}
by Definition
3.45
(b)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{l}\cdot\partial_{l}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
by Lemma
3.44
(b)
, so
a_{lj}\cdot\partial_{l}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}\cdot\partial_{l}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. By (
43
), it follows that
\partial_{j}^{M}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. Hence
\mathcal{P}
is closed under
\partial^{M}
.

Now, fix
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and an integer
s\geqslant 0
. By Lemma
3.28
, we have
\partial_{j}w_{i}^{(s)}=0
for
j\neq i,1
, so by (
43
)

\partial_{j}^{M}(b\cdot w_{i}^{(s)})=\sum_{l=2}^{k}a_{lj}\cdot(\partial_{l}b)\cdot w_{i}^{(s)}+a_{ij}\cdot b\cdot w_{i}^{(s+1)}.

To prove that
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
is closed under
\partial^{M}
, we need to show that all the above summands are in
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
. By Definition
3.45
(b)
, we have
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, so
a_{ij}bw_{i}^{(s+1)}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
, as needed. It remains to show that
a_{lj}\cdot\partial_{l}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
for all
l\in\{2,\dots,k\}
.

Fix
l\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. By Definition
3.45
(b)
, we have
a_{lj}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}
. If
l\neq i
then
\partial_{l}b\in\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{A}_{i}
by Lemma
3.43
(f)
, so
a_{lj}\partial_{l}b\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, as needed. For the remaining case
l=i
, recall that
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is closed under
\partial
by Lemma
3.43
(b)
, so
\partial_{i}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and therefore
a_{ij}\partial_{i}b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{P}
, as needed.

(b)

Fix
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Since
w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}
and
1\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{P}
, we have
w_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
. By
(a)
, we have
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}w_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{P}
. By Lemma
3.44
(c)
,
(d)
the latter algebra is contained in
\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, so
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.29
(b)
, we conclude that
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
. Let us recall the argument here. Lemma
3.6
(c)
implies that
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}w_{1}=-\sum_{i=2}^{k}\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}w_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
. By Lemma
3.28
we have
\partial_{l}t=0
for
l\neq 1
, so by (
43
) we have
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}t=0
, for all
\boldsymbol{j}
. Since
w_{1}|_{U_{1}}>0
by (
28
), the formula
v_{1}=t_{1}-u_{1}=tw_{1}^{-1}-\eta(w_{1})
shows that
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

For the last statement, fix
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{1})
. By Lemma
3.41
(e)
we have
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, so the claim holds for
|\boldsymbol{j}|=0
. Assume
|\boldsymbol{j}|>0
. By Lemma
3.43
(e)
, for any
l\in\{2,\dots,k\}
we have
\partial_{l}h\in\mathcal{I}_{l}
. By Definition
3.45
(b)
, it follows that for any
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
a_{lj}\partial_{l}h\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{l}=\mathcal{I}_{l}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, so
\partial^{M}_{j}h\in\mathcal{P}
by (
43
). Since by
(a)
the algebra
\mathcal{P}
is closed under
\partial^{M}
, we infer that
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}h\in\mathcal{P}
. But
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.44
(c)
, so eventually
\partial^{M}_{\boldsymbol{j}}h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

∎

3.4.7.
The A’Campo space admits a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
structure.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
3.31
, which makes
A
a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
manifold with boundary, and establish its properties listed in Proposition
3.33
.

Recall that in Notation
3.36
, we have fixed a chart
U_{X}
, considered the subset
U_{1}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
with smooth coordinates
(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k},\vartheta)
, and denoted the sheaf of functions which are smooth with respect to these coordinates by
\mathcal{S}
. By Lemma
3.30
, the map
\overline{\psi}_{1}=(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism from
U_{1}
onto an open subset of
Q_{k,n}
, so it gives
another
smooth structure on
U_{1}
, which is (only)
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphic to the previous one. We denote the sheaf of smooth functions with respect to that smooth structure by
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
. As in Notation
3.27
, this structure gives differential operators

\overline{\partial}_{1}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial g}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{\partial}_{i}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{i}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad i\in\{2,\dots,k\}.

Note that
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial g}
has different meaning here than in Notation
3.27
, i.e.
\overline{\partial}_{1}
may not be equal to
\partial_{1}
: this is because the vector field
\overline{\partial}_{1}
is tangent to the fibers of
(\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k})
, which differ from the fibers of
(v_{2},\dots,v_{k})
, to which the vector field
\partial_{1}
is tangent.

Combining Lemmas
3.48
and
3.47
, we get the following.

Lemma 3.49
.

We have
\overline{v}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
h\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
for all
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
.

Let
\Psi
be the matrix introduced in (
41
). Then the definition of
\overline{\partial}_{1},\dots\overline{\partial}_{k}
reads as

[\overline{\partial}_{1},\dots,\overline{\partial}_{k}]^{\top}=((\mathrm{id}-\Psi)^{-1})^{\top}\cdot[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{k}]^{\top}=\sum_{s\geqslant 0}(\Psi^{s})^{\top}\cdot[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{k}]^{\top}.

By assumption (
42
), the above sequence is uniformly convergent.

By Lemma
3.47
we have
\Psi\in\mathcal{M}
, so by Lemma
3.46
, for any
s\geqslant 1
we have
\Psi^{s}\in\mathcal{M}
, too. Therefore, by Lemma
3.48
for all
s\geqslant 1
and all multi-indices
\boldsymbol{j}
we have
\partial^{\Psi^{s}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}v_{1},\partial^{\Psi^{s}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
, for any
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
. Recall that
v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.40
(e)
and
h\in\mathcal{S}(U_{1})
by Lemma
3.41
(e)
, so the above result holds for
s=0
, too. It follows that
\overline{\partial}_{\boldsymbol{j}}v_{1}
and
\overline{\partial}_{\boldsymbol{j}}h
, for any
\boldsymbol{j}
, are continuous on
U_{1}
. In other words,
v_{1}
and
h
are smooth with respect to the coordinates
(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)
, i.e.
v_{1},h\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
.

Taking for
U_{X}
the restriction of the
p
-th chart of the fixed atlas
\mathcal{U}_{X}
, we infer that
v_{1}^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, for all
p\in R_{0}
. Since
\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
, we have shown that
\tau^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, too. Hence
\overline{v}_{1}=\sum_{p\in R_{0}}\tau^{p}v_{i}^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
. The functions
\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k}
are coordinates of the smooth chart
\overline{\psi}_{1}
defining
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
, so they are in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, too.

It remains to show that
\overline{v}_{j}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
for all
j\in\{k+1,\dots,N\}
. We have
\overline{v}_{j}=\sum_{p\in R_{0}}\tau^{p}v_{j}^{p}
, where
\tau^{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
. Thus it is enough to show that
v_{j}^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
for all
p\in R_{0}
. Since
j>k
, our chart
U_{X}
is disjoint from
D_{j}
. Hence
t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{X}}>0
and
t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{X}},\log t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{X}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
. By definition (
19
) of
v_{j}^{p}
, we have
v_{j}^{p}=t_{j}^{p}-u_{j}^{p}
, so it remains to show that
u_{j}^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
. Formulas (
18
) and (
13
) give
u_{j}^{p}=\eta(\frac{t}{t_{j}^{p}})=(1-\log t+\log t_{j}^{p})^{-1}=(g^{-1}+\log t_{j}^{p})^{-1}=g\cdot(1+g\log t_{j}^{p})^{-1}
. Since both the coordinate
g
, and the restriction
\log t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{X}}
are in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, we infer that
u_{j}^{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, as needed.

∎

Since by Lemma
3.30
each
\overline{\psi}_{i}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism, the transition map
\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\prime}\circ\overline{\psi}_{i}^{-1}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism, too. It remains to prove that it is smooth.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
i=1
, and choose
U_{X}
so small that it satisfies the statement of Lemma
3.42
and the assumption (
42
) above. Moreover, we can assume that
U_{X}\subseteq U_{X}^{\prime}
, so the index for
U_{X}
is contained with the one for
U_{X}^{\prime}
. Call the latter
S^{\prime}
. The chart
\overline{\psi}_{j}^{\prime}
is defined in (
36
) as
(g,(\overline{v}_{l})_{l\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{j\}},\vartheta^{\prime})
. Using above notation, we need to prove that all these functions are in
\overline{S}(U_{1})
, i.e. that they are smooth with respect to the coordinates
(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\vartheta)
.

Clearly, the coordinate
g
is in
\overline{S}(U_{1})
. By Lemma
3.49
, we have
\overline{v}_{l}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
for all
l
. The first
k
functions in
\vartheta^{\prime}
are
\theta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\theta_{k}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
3.26
(a)
, they differ from coordinates
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}
of
\vartheta
by a smooth function on
U_{X}
, so by Lemma
3.49
they are in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, too. The remaining functions in
\vartheta^{\prime}
are smooth on
U_{X}
, so again by Lemma
3.49
they are in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(U_{1})
, as claimed.

∎

(a)
By Lemma
3.49
, the map
\pi
pulls back smooth functions to smooth functions, so it is smooth. Its restriction to
A\setminus\partial A
is a diffeomorphism by Lemma
3.11
.

(b)
We argue exactly as in the proof of Proposition
3.14
(b)
. The function
g
is a coordinate in each smooth chart (
36
) meeting
\partial A
; and
\theta
is a nonzero linear combination of such coordinates. Thus
(g,\theta)
is a submersion near
\partial A
. It follows that
f_{A}=(\exp(-\exp(g^{-1}-1)),\theta)
is smooth near
\partial A
. Away from
\partial A
, the result follows from the fact that
f
is a submersion.

(c)
This part was proved in Lemma
3.49
.

(d)

Recall that for every
\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, the restriction
(\pi,\mu)\colon\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}A_{I}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{I}^{\circ}\times\Delta_{I}^{\circ}
is a topological
(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\#I}
-bundle by Proposition
3.7
(c)
. It remains to prove that it is smooth. The map
\pi
is smooth by
(a)
. The
i
-th coordinate of
\mu
is
\overline{u}_{i}|_{A_{I}^{\circ}}
, so by Lemma
3.6
(e)
it is zero if
i\not\in I
and equals
-\overline{v}_{i}|_{A_{I}^{\circ}}
if
i\in I
. Thus smoothness of
\mu|_{\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}A_{I}^{\circ}}
follows from
(c)
.

∎

4.
Fiberwise symplectic form on the A’Campo space

Let us recall some notation and assumptions from Section
3
. Let
X
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
, and let
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function with only one critical value
0\in\mathbb{C}
, whose central fiber
D\coloneqq f^{-1}(0)
is snc. We denote the irreducible components of
D
by
D_{1},\dots,D_{N}
, and write
D=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}D_{i}
for some positive integers
m_{i}
.

Assume that
\log|f|<-1
, and that
f|_{X\setminus D}
is a submersion onto
\mathbb{D}_{e^{-1}}^{*}
. Using certain additional data
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
,
we have constructed in Definition
3.3
the A’Campo space
A
. In Section
3.4.1
, we have endowed
A
with a structure of a smooth manifold with boundary. By Proposition
3.33
, this manifold comes with a smooth map
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
which restricts to a diffeomorphism
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}\longrightarrow X\setminus D
; and with a submersion
(g,\theta)\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
whose fibers over
(0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
agree with those of
f
.

Fix a Kähler form
\omega_{X}\in\Omega^{2}(X)
. In this section, we will introduce, for any
\delta>0
and any open subset
W\subseteq A
so that
f_{A}|_{\overline{W}}
is proper, a
2
-form on
A
which agrees with
\omega_{X}
on
g^{-1}([\delta,1))\subseteq A\setminus\partial A=X\setminus D
, and whose restriction to
W
is fiberwise symplectic with respect to the submersion
(g,\theta)\colon A\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

The restriction to
W
is important for the compactness argument in the proof, and comes naturally in our applications. For example, if
f
is a log resolution of an isolated hypersurface singularity, we can take for
W
the preimage of a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, e.g. a ball, cf. Example
5.20
. In turn, if
f
is a projective family then one can take
W=X
, i.e. no restriction is needed at all.

4.1.
Construction of the fiberwise symplectic form

Definition 4.1
.

We say that a holomorphic chart
U_{X}
on
X
is
fine
if it satisfies the statement of Lemma
3.30
, or is disjoint from
D
.

Lemma
3.30
shows that we can choose a fine chart at any point of
X
; and any fine chart meeting
D
gives rise to a collection of smooth charts (
36
) on its preimage in
A
. Fine charts are, by definition, adapted to
f
, so they come equipped with functions
t_{i},w_{i},\theta_{i},v_{i}
introduced in Section
3.1.2
. We will now use the angular coordinates
\theta_{i}
to define
global angular
1
-forms

\alpha_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(A)
, as follows.

Fix an atlas
\mathcal{V}_{X}=\{(V^{q}_{X},\psi^{q}_{X}):q\in\widetilde{R}\}
whose all charts are fine; and a locally finite partition of unity
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=\{\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\}_{q\in\widetilde{R}}
inscribed in
\mathcal{V}_{X}
(note that
(\mathcal{V}_{X},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
may differ from the adapted pair
(\mathcal{U}_{X},\boldsymbol{\tau})
used in Section
3.4
to define the smooth structure on
A
). Since the map
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
is smooth, the pullback through
\pi
of each
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}
to
A
is smooth, too. As usual, we denote these pullbacks by the same letters.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
q\in\widetilde{R}
such that
i
lies in the index set (
12
) of the chart
V^{q}_{X}
. Let
\theta_{i}^{q}
be the corresponding angular coordinate on
V^{q}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(V_{X}^{q})
, see (
17
): it is smooth by definition (
36
) of the smooth charts on
V^{q}
. Then
d\theta_{i}^{q}
is a smooth
1
-form on
V^{q}
. Since
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}
vanishes identically away from
V^{q}
, the form
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}d\theta_{i}^{q}
extends to a global smooth form on
A
, which is zero off
V^{q}
. If
V_{X}^{q}\cap D_{i}=\emptyset
, we put
\theta_{i}^{q}=0
, so
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}d\theta_{i}^{q}=0
.
This way, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we define a
global angular
1
-form

\alpha_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{q\in\widetilde{R}}\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\,d\theta_{i}^{q}.

By Proposition
3.33
(c)
, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we have a smooth function
\overline{v}_{i}\colon A\longrightarrow[-1,1]
. Put

(44)

\lambda_{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\overline{v}_{i}\alpha_{i},\quad\omega_{E}\coloneqq d\lambda_{E}.

Given any
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
we define

(45)

\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\pi^{*}\omega_{X}+\varepsilon\omega_{E}.

For every
\delta>0
we fix a smooth function
\rho_{\delta}\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow[0,1]
satisfying
\rho_{\delta}(0)=1
and
\rho_{\delta}|_{[\delta,\infty)}\equiv 0
. We define

(46)

\lambda^{\delta}_{E}\coloneqq\rho_{\delta}(g)\lambda_{E},\quad\omega^{\delta}_{E}\coloneqq d\lambda^{\delta}_{E}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}=\pi^{*}\omega_{X}+\varepsilon\omega^{\delta}_{E}.

Note that the restrictions of
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
and
\pi^{*}\omega_{X}
to
g^{-1}([\delta,+\infty))
are equal for any
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
.

If
\omega_{X}|_{X\setminus D}=d\lambda_{X}
for some
\lambda_{X}\in\Omega^{1}(X\setminus D)
, we put

(47)

\lambda_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}+\varepsilon\lambda^{\delta}_{E},\quad\mbox{so}\quad\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}=d\lambda_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}.

In our applications, the pullback
\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}
will extend to a
1
-form on
A
, hence the forms
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
constructed above will be exact.

Proposition
4.2
below is the main result of this section. Part
(b)
shows that the symplectic monodromy induced by
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
at
“radius zero”
is particularly easy to handle. In Section
7
we will see that – assuming
D
is
m
-separating – its dynamics are exactly as for the topological monodromy constructed in
[
A’C75
]
, and
nearly
as for the symplectic monodromy associated with a model resolution of a germ of isolated singularity constructed in
[
McL19
, Example 5.14]
, see Remark
7.5
. The main difference is the fact that all iterates of our monodromy will have one more component of fixed points, whose analysis is crucial for the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
W\subseteq A
be an open set such that
f_{A}|_{\overline{W}}
is proper. Then the following hold.

(a)

There is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
W^{\prime}
of
\partial A\cap W
in
W
, such that for every
\delta>0
and every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the restriction of each of the forms
\omega^{\varepsilon}_{A}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
defined in (
45
) and (
46
) is symplectic on each fiber of
(g,\theta)|_{W^{\prime}}\colon W^{\prime}\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}

(b)

Let
U_{X}
be a fine chart such that
U\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U_{X})\subseteq W
. Let
S
be its index set (
12
). The symplectic lift, with respect to
\omega^{\varepsilon}_{A}
or
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
, of the unit angular vector field on
\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
to
U\cap\partial A
in coordinates (
36
) on
U
is equal to

(48)

\left(\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\zeta(u_{i})\right)^{-1}\cdot\sum_{i\in S}\zeta(u_{i})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}},

where for each
i\in S
, the function
u_{i}\colon U\longrightarrow[0,1]
is defined in (
18
), see Lemma
3.6
(a)
, and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}
is the coordinate vector field of the chart (
36
). The function
\zeta\colon s\mapsto s^{-2}e^{1-s^{-1}}\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
is the derivative of the inverse to (
13
). In particular,
\zeta
is smooth and all its derivatives vanish at
0
.

The proof of Proposition
4.2
will be carried out in two steps, as follows. Since
\overline{W}
is compact, we can work locally, in a preimage
U
of a fine chart
U_{X}
. In Proposition
4.3
, we prove that the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\,\cdot\,,J\,\cdot\,)
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}(\,\cdot\,,J\,\cdot\,)
are fiberwise positive definite on
U\setminus\partial A
, where
J
is the standard almost complex structure on
X
, pulled back by a diffeomorphism
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
. Unfortunately,
J
does not extend to
\partial A
, so this argument does not show non-degeneracy at radius zero. We prove the latter in Proposition
4.12
by directly computing that the symplectic orthogonal in
T\partial A
to the fibers of
(g,\theta)
is spanned by the vector field (
48
).

4.2.
Positivity away from
\partial A

Let
J
be the standard almost complex structure on
X\setminus D
. We use the same letter
J
to denote its pullback to
A\setminus\partial A
via the diffeomorphism
\pi|_{A\setminus\partial A}
. We say that a
2
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(A\setminus\partial A)

fiberwise tames
J
if
\omega(v,Jv)>0
for every nonzero vector
v
tangent to the fiber of
(g,\theta)
. In particular, if
\omega
(fiberwise) tames
J
, then it is (fiberwise) nondegenerate. Note that in this definition we do not assume that
\omega(Jv,Jw)=\omega(v,w)
, that is,
\omega
may not be (fiberwise)
J
-compatible.

In this section, we prove the following result.

Proposition 4.3
.

For every point
x_{0}\in D
there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
U_{X}
containing
x_{0}
, such that for any
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,
\delta>0
, each of the forms
\omega^{\varepsilon}_{A}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}

fiberwise tames
J
on
\pi^{-1}(U_{X})\setminus\partial A
.

Remark 4.4
.

For the proof of Proposition
4.3
it is enough to consider the case
\omega^{\varepsilon}_{A}
, for
\varepsilon>0
.

For
\varepsilon=0
we have
\omega_{A}^{\delta,0}=\omega^{0}_{A}=\pi^{*}\omega_{X}
, which tames
J
. Assume that
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
fiberwise tames
J
for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Fix
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. The restriction of
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
to the level set
g^{-1}(c)
equals
\omega_{A}^{\rho_{\delta}(c)\varepsilon}
. Since
\rho_{\delta}(c)\leqslant 1
we have
\rho_{\delta}(c)\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
, so by assumption this restriction fiberwise tames
J
, as needed.

∎

We will use the following notation. Fix
x_{0}\in D
, so
x_{0}\in X_{S}^{\circ}
for some nonempty
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
. We order the components of
D
so that
S=\{1,\dots,k\}
. By Lemma
3.30
, there is a fine chart around
x_{0}
whose associated index set (
12
) is
S
. We fix such a chart
U_{X}
, put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
, and use functions
w_{i},\theta_{i}
etc. introduced for
U_{X}
in Section
3.1.2
, cf. Lemma
3.6
(a)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

The following hold.

(a)

For every
i\in S
there is a
1
-form
\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X})
such that we have
d\theta_{i}-\alpha_{i}=\pi^{*}\beta_{i}
.

(b)

Fix
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus S
and a subset
I\subseteq S
. Then there is a bounded
1
-form
\mu_{j}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ})
which satisfies
\mu_{j}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}=d\overline{v}_{j}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}
for every
\tau\geqslant 0
.

(c)

For every
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus S
, the
1
-form
\alpha_{j}
is a pullback of a smooth
1
-form on
U_{X}
.

(d)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, the
2
-form
d\alpha_{i}
is a pullback of a smooth
2
-form on
X
.

(a)

Fix
q\in\widetilde{R}
. Put
V_{X}=V_{X}^{q}\cap U_{X}
,
V=\pi^{-1}(V_{X})
. On
V
, we have an equality
\theta_{i}=\theta_{i}^{q}+b_{i}^{q}
for some smooth map
b_{i}^{q}\colon V_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Indeed, if
V_{X}^{q}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then this follows from Lemma
3.26
(a)
, applied to
U_{X}^{\prime}=V_{X}^{q}
. In turn, if
V_{X}^{q}\cap D_{i}=\emptyset
, then by convention
\theta_{i}^{q}=0
, and the restriction
\theta_{i}|_{V}
is a pullback of a smooth function from
V_{X}
, as needed.

Since the restriction
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}|_{U_{X}}
is supported on
V_{X}
, the
1
-form
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\,db_{i}^{q}
extends to a smooth
1
-form on
U_{X}
. Thus on
U
, we have an equality
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\,d\theta_{i}=\widetilde{\tau}^{q}d\theta_{i}^{q}+\pi^{*}(\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\,db_{i}^{q})
. Since
\sum_{q\in\widetilde{R}}\widetilde{\tau}^{q}=1
, taking a sum over all
q\in\widetilde{R}
we get
d\theta_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\pi^{*}\beta_{i}
, where
\beta_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{q\in\widetilde{R}}\widetilde{\tau}^{q}\,db_{i}^{q}
is a smooth form on
U_{X}
, as needed.

(b)
By definition (
23
), we have
\overline{v}_{j}=\sum_{p\in R}\tau^{p}v_{j}^{p}
, so
d\overline{v}_{j}=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}dv_{j}^{p}+\sum_{p}v_{j}^{p}d\tau^{p}
. Since
j\not\in I
, by Lemma
3.16
(c)

v_{j}^{p}
restricts to a smooth function on
V_{X,I}^{p}\coloneqq U_{X}\cap U_{X}^{p}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
. Since each
\tau^{p}
is a smooth function on
X
, the form
\sum_{p}v_{j}^{p}d\tau^{p}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ})
is bounded. It remains to show that each
dv_{j}^{p}
is fiberwise bounded from
X
. More precisely, for each
p
we need to find a bounded
1
-form
\mu_{j}^{p}\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X,I}^{\circ})
, such that for every
\tau\geqslant 0
we have an equality of restrictions
\mu_{j}^{p}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)\cap V^{p}_{X,I}}=dv_{j}^{p}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)\cap V_{X,I}^{p}}
.

If
U_{X}^{p}\cap D_{j}=\emptyset
then by convention
v_{j}^{p}=0
, so we can take
\mu_{j}^{p}=0
. Assume
U_{X}^{p}\cap D_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Since
dg|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}=0
, Lemma
3.22
(b)
implies that
dv_{j}^{p}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}=(1+(t_{j}^{p})^{-1}\cdot(u_{j}^{p})^{2})\cdot dt_{j}^{p}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}
. By assumption
j\not\in S
, so the closure
\overline{U}_{I}^{\circ}
does not meet
D_{j}
. Hence the restrictions
(1+(t_{j}^{p})^{-1}\cdot(u_{j}^{p})^{2})|_{V_{X,I}^{p}}
and
dt_{j}^{p}|_{V_{X,I}^{p}}
are smooth and bounded. Thus
\mu_{j}^{p}\coloneqq(1+(t_{j}^{p})^{-1}\cdot(u_{j}^{p})^{2})\cdot dt_{j}^{p}|_{V_{X,I}^{p}}\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X,I}^{p})
is a bounded form, whose restriction to each fiber
g^{-1}(\tau)
is equal to
dv_{j}^{p}|_{V_{X,I}^{p}}
, as needed.

(c)
For every
q\in\widetilde{R}
we have a smooth map
\theta_{j}^{q}\colon V_{X}^{q}\setminus D_{j}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
, defined by the formula (
17
) if
V_{X}^{q}
meets
D_{j}
, and by
\theta_{j}^{q}=0
otherwise. Hence for each
q\in\widetilde{R}
, the form
\widetilde{\tau}^{q}d\theta_{j}^{q}
is smooth on
X\setminus D_{j}
, in particular, on
U_{X}
. Taking a sum over all
q\in\widetilde{R}
we infer that
\alpha_{j}
is a smooth form on
U_{X}
, as needed.

(d)
Follows from
(a)
if
i\in S
and from
(c)
if
i\not\in S
.

∎

The following easy consequence of Lemma
4.5
will be important in Section
5.5
to control various monodromies near the boundary of the Milnor fiber.

Remark 4.6
.

For
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
put
B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}=f_{A}^{-1}(0,\theta)\cap A_{i}^{\circ}
. Then
\pi_{i,\theta}\coloneqq\pi|_{B^{\circ}_{i,\theta}}\colon B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{i}^{\circ}
is an
m_{i}
-fold covering. Moreover, there is a form
\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(X_{i}^{\circ})
such that for every
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
we have
\lambda_{E}|_{B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}}=\pi_{i,\theta}^{*}\beta_{i}
. As a consequence, if
\omega_{X}|_{X_{i}^{\circ}}=d\lambda_{X}
for some
\lambda_{X}\in\Omega^{1}(X_{i}^{\circ})
, then for every
\delta,\varepsilon>0
the form
\lambda_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
defined in (
47
) satisfies the equality
\lambda_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}|_{B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}}=\pi_{i,\theta}^{*}(\lambda_{X}+\varepsilon\beta_{i}^{\delta})
for some
\beta_{i}^{\delta}\in\Omega^{1}(X_{i}^{\circ})
.

The first assertion is clear. Indeed, in coordinates (
36
) the subset
B_{i}^{\circ}
is given by
\{m_{i}\theta_{i}=0\}
, and the restriction
\pi|_{A_{i}^{\circ}}\colon A_{i}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{i}^{\circ}
is
(0,\theta_{i},z)\mapsto(0,z)
, i.e. it collapses the angular coordinate
\theta_{i}
.

To find
\beta_{i}
, recall from Lemma
3.6
(e)
that on
A_{i}^{\circ}
we have
\overline{v}_{i}=-\overline{u}_{i}=-1
and
\overline{u}_{j}=0
for all
j\neq i
. Hence by Lemma
4.5
(c)
, we have
\lambda_{E}|_{A_{i}^{\circ}}=-\alpha_{i}+\pi^{*}\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(X_{i}^{\circ})
. Choose a fine chart
V_{X}
with associated index set
\{i\}
, and let
V=\pi^{-1}(V_{X})
. By Lemma
4.5
(a)
, we have
\alpha_{i}|_{V}=d\theta_{i}+\pi^{*}\beta
for some
\beta\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X})
. Since
f_{A}|_{V}=(r_{i}^{m_{i}},m_{i}\theta_{i})
, the restriction of
d\theta_{i}
to any fiber of
f_{A}|_{V}
is zero. Hence
\alpha_{i}|_{V\cap B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}}=\pi^{*}_{i,\theta}\beta|_{V\cap B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}}
. Let
V_{X}^{\prime}
be another such chart, let
\beta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(V_{X}^{\prime})
be the corresponding
1
-form, and let
V^{\prime\prime}_{X}=V_{X}\cap V_{X}^{\prime}
,
V^{\prime\prime}=\pi^{-1}(V_{X}^{\prime\prime})
. Then we have an equality of restrictions
\pi_{i,\theta}^{*}\beta|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{X}\cap X_{i}^{\circ}}=\alpha_{i}|_{V^{\prime\prime}\cap B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}}=\pi_{i,\theta}^{*}\beta^{\prime}|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{X}\cap X_{i}^{\circ}}
. Since
\pi_{i,\theta}\colon B_{i,\theta}^{\circ}\longrightarrow X_{i}^{\circ}
is a local diffeomorphism, it follows that
\beta|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{X}\cap X_{i}^{\circ}}=\beta^{\prime}|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{X}\cap X_{i}^{\circ}}
. We conclude that the above forms glue to a form
\check{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}(X_{i}^{\circ})
. Eventually, we put
\beta_{i}=-\check{\beta}+\gamma
.

∎

We will now introduce smooth coordinates on
U_{X}\setminus D=U\setminus\partial A
which are well suited to the standard complex structure
J
. Recall that our holomorphic coordinates on
U_{X}
are
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k},z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
, for some
\{i_{k+1},\dots,i_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{>N}
. For
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
r_{i}=|z_{i}|
,
\theta_{i}=\frac{z_{i}}{|z_{i}|}
, see (
16
), (
17
). Put

s_{i}=\log r_{i}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad s_{i+k}=\theta_{i}\colon U_{X}\setminus D_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}.

For
j\in\{1,\dots,n-k\}
let
x_{2j-1},x_{2j}\colon U_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the real and imaginary part of
z_{i_{j}}
. Now

(49)

(s_{1},\dots,s_{2k},x_{1},\dots,x_{2(n-k)})\colon U\setminus\partial A\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{2(n-k)}

is a smooth chart on
U\setminus\partial A=U_{X}\setminus D
. With respect to this chart, we can define coordinate vector fields
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{2k},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{2(n-k)}
by the conditions

(50)

ds_{i}(\nu_{j})=\delta_{j}^{i},\quad ds_{i}(\xi_{a})=0,\quad dx_{a}(\nu_{j})=0,\quad dx_{a}(\xi_{b})=\delta_{b}^{a}

for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
and all
a,b\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
. For an integer
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
we put
\overline{i}=i
if
i\leqslant k
and
\overline{i}=i-k
if
i>k
. We will also use the functions
\sigma_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, defined in (
33
) by

\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}.

Note that
\sigma_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
. In particular,
\sigma_{i}
is bounded.

Lemma 4.7
.

The following hold.

(a)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
ds_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{-2}\,dt_{i}
,

(b)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
ds_{i}\circ J=-ds_{i+k}
and
ds_{i+k}\circ J=ds_{i}
.

(a)
The function
t_{i}
was defined in (
16
) as
t_{i}=-(m_{i}\log r_{i})^{-1}=-(m_{i}s_{i})^{-1}
, so
s_{i}=-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}
, and therefore
ds_{i}=m_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{-2}\,dt_{i}
, as claimed.

(b)
We have
ds_{i}=d\log r_{i}=r_{i}^{-1}\,dr_{i}
. Recall from Example
2.1
that
dr_{i}\circ J=-r_{i}\,d\theta_{i}
, so
ds_{i}\circ J=r_{i}^{-1}\cdot(-r_{i}\,d\theta_{i})=-d\theta_{i}=-ds_{i+k}
by definition. Now
ds_{i+k}\circ J=-ds_{i}\circ J\circ J=ds_{i}
, as claimed.

∎

For a
2
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(U\setminus\partial A)
, denote by
\omega^{J}
the symmetric part of
(v,w)\mapsto\omega(v,Jw)
. In the next lemma, we compute
\omega^{J}_{E}
.

Recall from Definition
3.23
that a form
\gamma\in\Omega^{*}(U\setminus\partial A)
is
bounded from
X
if
\gamma=\pi^{*}\gamma^{\prime}
for some bounded
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Omega^{*}(U_{X}\setminus D)
. For example, if
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
then
dr_{i}
is bounded from
X
, but
dt_{i}
is not.

Lemma 4.8
.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
there are bounded
b_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U\setminus\partial A)
and forms
\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U\setminus\partial A)
,
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{2}(U\setminus\partial A)
bounded from
X
, such that
for every
\tau>0
, we have the equality of restrictions

(51)

\omega_{E}^{J}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}=\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{\overline{i}}(b_{i}\,(ds_{i})^{2}+ds_{i}\cdot\beta_{i})+\omega_{1}^{J}.

Moreover, we can shrink the neighborhood
U_{X}
of
x_{0}
so that
b_{i}>\frac{1}{2}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Since we work with forms restricted to fibers of
g
, we will tacitly use the equality
dg=0
. This way, Lemma
3.22
(b)
reads as
dv_{i}=(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})dt_{i}
. Since
(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})=t_{i}^{-2}(t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2})=t_{i}^{-2}\sigma_{i}
by definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
, we get
dv_{i}=\sigma_{i}t_{i}^{-2}\,dt_{i}
. Hence by Lemma
4.7
(a)

dv_{i}=\sigma_{i}m_{i}\,ds_{i}.

Substituting
dg=0
to Lemma
3.35
(b)
, we infer that there is a bounded function
c_{i}
, and a
1
-form
\gamma_{i}
bounded from
X
, such that
d\overline{v}_{i}=(1+ct_{i})\,dv_{i}+\sigma_{i}\gamma_{i}
.
Substituting the above formula for
dv_{i}
, we get

(52)

d\overline{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}(m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})\,ds_{i}+\gamma_{i}).

By definition (
44
) of the form
\omega_{E}
, we have

\omega_{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}d\overline{v}_{i}\wedge\alpha_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{N}d\overline{v}_{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\overline{v}_{i}\,d\alpha_{i}.

By Lemma
4.5
(a)
, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
there is a
1
-form
\check{\beta}_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U)
, bounded from
X
, such that
\alpha_{i}=d\theta_{i}+\check{\beta}_{i}
. By Lemma
4.5
(b)
,
(c)
, for every
j\in\{k+1,\dots,N\}
, the
2
-form
d\overline{v}_{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}
is fiberwise equal to a
2
-form bounded from
X
. Eventually, by Lemma
4.5
(d)
, the
2
-form
d\alpha_{i}
is bounded from
X
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. We thus obtain a fiberwise equality

\omega_{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}d\overline{v}_{i}\wedge(d\theta_{i}+\check{\beta}_{i})+\check{\omega}_{1},

for some
1
-forms
\check{\beta}_{i}
and a
2
-form
\check{\omega}_{1}
, all bounded from
X
. Using the formula (
52
) for
d\overline{v}_{i}
and the notation
d\theta_{i}=ds_{k+i}
, we conclude that

(53)

\omega_{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}\left(m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})\,ds_{i}\wedge ds_{i+k}+\gamma_{i}\wedge ds_{i+k}+m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})\,ds_{i}\wedge\check{\beta}_{i}\right)+\omega_{1},

where the
2
-form
\omega_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}\gamma_{i}\wedge\check{\beta}_{i}+\check{\omega}_{1}
is bounded from
X
. By Lemma
4.7
(b)
, we have

\displaystyle(ds_{i}\wedge ds_{i+k})^{J}=(ds_{i})^{2}+(ds_{i+k})^{2},\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle(\gamma_{i}\wedge ds_{i+k})^{J}=ds_{i}\cdot\gamma_{i}-ds_{i+k}\cdot(\gamma_{i}\circ J),\quad(ds_{i}\wedge\check{\beta}_{i})^{J}=ds_{i}\cdot(\check{\beta}_{i}\circ J)+ds_{i+k}\cdot\check{\beta}_{i}.

Since the function
c_{i}
is bounded, and the forms
\check{\beta}_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}
are bounded from
X
, the forms
\beta_{i}\coloneqq\gamma_{i}+m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})(\check{\beta}_{i}\circ J)
and
\beta_{i+k}\coloneqq-(\gamma_{i}\circ J)+m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})\check{\beta}_{i}
are bounded from
X
, too.

Substituting these definitions to (
53
), we get

\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{\overline{i}}\left(m_{\overline{i}}(1+c_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}})\,(ds_{i})^{2}+ds_{i}\cdot\beta_{i}\right)+\omega_{1}^{J}.

Put
b_{i}=m_{\overline{i}}(1+c_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}})
. As we approach the point
x_{0}
, we have
t_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. Since
m_{\overline{i}}\geqslant 1
and
c_{\overline{i}}
is bounded, for
t_{\overline{i}}
sufficiently small we have
b_{i}>\tfrac{1}{2}
, as needed.

∎

From now on, we assume that our fine chart
U_{X}
is so small that the functions
b_{i}
from Lemma
4.8
satisfy
b_{i}>\tfrac{1}{2}
. For the next result, we introduce the following notation.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\setminus D)
be the algebra of bounded functions. For
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we put

\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\langle r_{i}t_{i}^{l}:l\in\mathbb{Z}\rangle\cdot\mathcal{B}.

Since by Lemma
3.21
(a)
we have
r_{i}t_{i}^{l}=e^{-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}}t_{i}^{l}\in\mathcal{B}
, the algebra
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}
. One should think of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
as the ideal of functions which decay exponentially with respect to
t_{i}
. In Section
3.4
, a similar role was played by the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

In the following lemma, we use the coordinate vector fields
\nu_{i}
,
\xi_{j}
introduced in (
50
).

Lemma 4.9
.

The ideals
\mathcal{Q}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
satisfy the following properties.

(a)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have
t_{i}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.

(b)

Let
\beta\in\Omega^{1}(U\setminus\partial A)
be a
1
-form bounded from
X
. Then for every
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
and every
j\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
we have
\beta(\nu_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
and
\beta(\xi_{j})\in\mathcal{B}
.

(c)

Let
\gamma\in\Omega^{2}(U\setminus\partial A)
be a
2
-form bounded from
X
. Then for every
i,j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
and every
l\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
we have
\gamma^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{j})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}
and
\gamma^{J}(\nu_{i},\xi_{l})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
.

(a)
The first equality follows directly from the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. For the second one, recall that
\sigma_{i}
was defined in (
33
) by
\sigma_{i}=t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2}
, so
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\sigma_{i}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.

To see the other inclusion, note that
\sigma_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{2}=(t_{i}^{2}+t_{i}u_{i}^{2})^{-1}t_{i}^{2}=(1+t_{i}^{-1}u_{i}^{2})^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\sigma_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. The first equality of
(a)
shows that
t_{i}^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, so
\sigma_{i}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{-1}t_{i}^{2}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, as needed.

(b)
By Definition
3.23
of a form bounded from
X
, we have
\beta=\pi^{*}\beta^{\prime}
for some bounded
\beta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X}\setminus D)
. Recall that our holomorphic chart on
U_{X}
is
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k},z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
. Write
y_{i}
,
y_{i}^{\prime}
for the real and imaginary parts of
z_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Then the
1
-forms
dy_{i}
,
dy^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
dx_{l}
for
l\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
give a basis of
T^{*}U_{X}
. Away from the zero locus of
z_{i}
, we can change this basis by replacing
dy_{i},dy_{i}^{\prime}
with polar coordinates
dr_{i}
,
r_{i}d\theta_{i}
. Hence there are bounded functions
p_{i},q_{l}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\setminus D)
such that

\beta^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\,dr_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{k+i}r_{i}\,d\theta_{i}+\sum_{l=1}^{2(n-k)}q_{l}\,dx_{l}.

We have
dr_{i}=de^{s_{i}}=e^{s_{i}}\,ds_{i}=r_{i}\,ds_{i}
, and
r_{i}\,d\theta_{i}=r_{i}\,ds_{i+k}=r_{\overline{i+k}}ds_{i+k}
by definition. Since we denote the functions on
U_{X}\setminus D
and their pullbacks to
U\setminus\partial A
by the same letters, we get

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{2k}p_{i}\,r_{\overline{i}}\,ds_{i}+\sum_{l=1}^{2(n-k)}q_{l}\,dx_{l}.

Now by definition (
50
) of the coordinate vector fields
\nu_{i},\xi_{l}
we get
\beta(\nu_{i})=p_{i}r_{\overline{i}}\in r_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
and
\beta(\xi_{l})=q_{l}\in\mathcal{B}
, as claimed.

(c)
By definition of a
2
-form bounded from
X
we have
\gamma=\pi^{*}\gamma^{\prime}
for some bounded
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Omega^{2}(U_{X}\setminus D)
. Using some basis of
T^{*}(U_{X}\setminus D)
, we can write
\gamma^{\prime}=\sum_{l}\gamma^{\prime}_{1l}\wedge\gamma^{\prime}_{2l}
for some bounded
\gamma^{\prime}_{1l},\gamma^{\prime}_{2l}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X}\setminus D)
. Thus
\gamma=\sum_{l}\gamma_{1l}\wedge\gamma_{2l}
for some
\gamma_{1l},\gamma_{2l}
bounded from
X
. Now
(c)
follows from
(b)
.

∎In a fixed basis of
T(U\setminus\partial A)
, we write the matrix of the symmetric
2
-form
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
introduced in (
51
) as

(54)

\left[\begin{matrix}A&Q^{\top}\\
Q&P\\
\end{matrix}\right]

for some
2k\times 2k
matrix
A=[a_{ij}]
; some
2(n-k)\times 2k
matrix
Q=[q_{ij}]
, and some
2(n-k)\times 2(n-k)
matrix
P=[p_{ij}]
. The entries of
A,Q,P
are smooth functions on
U\setminus\partial A
.

The next lemma summarizes some properties of these entries for our basis
((\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
given by coordinate vector fields (
50
). In Lemma
4.11
we will show that these properties are preserved after change of basis given by the Gaussian diagonalization of (
54
).

Lemma 4.10
.

The matrix (
54
) of
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
in basis
((\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
has the following properties:

(a)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
, there is a neighborhood
V_{X}
of
x_{0}
and a function
\check{b}_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
such that
a_{ii}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}\check{b}_{i}
and
\check{b}_{i}>\frac{1}{4}
on
\pi^{-1}(V_{X})\setminus\partial A
.

(b)

For every
i,j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
such that
i\neq j
we have
a_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
.

(c)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
and every
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
we have
q_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
.

(d)

All entries of the matrix
P
are bounded functions.

(e)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
the vector fields
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\pi_{*}\nu_{i}
and
\pi_{*}\nu_{i}
are bounded and converge to
0
as
r_{\overline{i}}
converges to
0
.

(f)

Let
\Xi
be the subbundle of
T(U\setminus\partial A)
spanned by
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{2(n-k)}
, let
||\,\cdot\,||
be the maximum norm on
\Xi
with respect to the coefficients of this basis; and let
||\,\cdot\,||_{X}
be any norm on
T\overline{U}_{X}
. Then there is a constant
K_{0}>0
such that for every
\xi\in\Xi
we have
||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\geqslant K_{0}||\xi||
.

(a)
By definition of the matrix (
54
), we have
a_{ii}=\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{i})
.
By definition (
50
) of
\nu_{i}
we have
ds_{j}(\nu_{i})=\delta_{j}^{i}
, for all
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
. Substituting this to the formula (
51
) for
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
, we get

a_{ii}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}(b_{i}+\beta_{i}(\nu_{i}))+\omega_{1}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{i}).

Since the
1
-form
\beta_{i}
is bounded from
X
, by Lemma
4.9
(b)
we have
\beta_{i}(\nu_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
, so
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\beta_{i}(\nu_{i})\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}=\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
, where the last equality holds by Lemma
4.9
(a)
. Similarly, since
\omega_{1}
is a
2
-form bounded from
X
, by Lemma
4.9
(c)
, we have
\omega_{1}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
. Hence

a_{ii}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}b_{i}+q

for some
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
. By Lemma
4.9
(a)
we have
\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
, so we can write
q=\sigma_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}}q^{\prime}
for some
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. This way,
a_{ii}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}(b_{i}+t_{\overline{i}}q^{\prime})
, with bounded
q^{\prime}
. Since we have chosen
U_{X}
so that the function
b_{i}
from Lemma
4.8
satisfies
b_{i}>\tfrac{1}{2}
, we have
\check{b}_{i}\coloneqq b_{i}+t_{\overline{i}}q^{\prime}>\frac{1}{4}
whenever
t_{\overline{i}}
is small enough. In particular, we have
\check{b}_{i}>\frac{1}{4}
sufficiently close to the point
x_{0}
, as needed.

(b)
By definition of the matrix (
54
), we have
a_{ij}=\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{j})
.
As before, using definition (
50
) of
\nu_{i},\nu_{j}
and the formula (
51
) for
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
, we get that for
i\neq j
:

a_{ij}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}\beta_{i}(\nu_{j})+\sigma_{\overline{j}}\beta_{j}(\nu_{i})+\omega_{1}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{j}).

We need to show that each of the above summands is in
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
. Since
\beta_{i}
is bounded from
X
, by Lemma
4.9
(b)
we have
\beta_{i}(\nu_{j})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}
. By Lemma
4.9
(a)
, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}=\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}\subseteq\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, so
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\beta_{i}(\nu_{j})\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, as needed. For the second summand, we apply the same argument interchanging the roles of
i
and
j
. Eventually, since the
2
-form
\omega_{1}
is bounded from
X
, by Lemma
4.9
(c)
we have
\omega_{1}^{J}(\nu_{i},\nu_{j})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}\subseteq\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, where the equality follows from Lemma
4.9
(a)
.

(c)
The entry
q_{ij}
is defined as
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi_{i},\nu_{j})
. Now, substituting (
50
) to (
51
) gives

q_{ij}=\sigma_{\overline{j}}\beta_{j}(\xi_{i})+\omega_{1}^{J}(\xi_{i},\nu_{j}).

By Lemma
4.9
(b)
, we have
\beta_{j}(\xi_{i})\in\mathcal{B}
, so
\sigma_{\overline{j}}\beta_{j}(\xi_{i})\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, as needed. Lemma
4.9
(c)
gives
\omega_{1}^{J}(\xi_{i},\nu_{j})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}
. By Lemma
4.9
(a)
, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}=\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{j}}\subseteq\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, so
\omega_{1}^{J}(\xi_{i},\nu_{j})\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}
, as claimed.

(d)
Formula (
51
) shows that the entries of
P
are
p_{ij}=\omega_{1}^{J}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})
for
i,j\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}
. By Lemma
4.8
, the form
\omega_{1}
is bounded from
X
, so these entries are bounded functions.

(e)
Fix a basis
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{2n}
of
T^{*}U_{X}
