. We need to show that for all
j\in\{1,\dots,2n\}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
, the functions
\sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})
and
\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})
are bounded and converge to zero as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. Since each
1
-form
\pi^{*}\beta_{j}
is bounded from
X
, by Lemma
4.9
(b)
we have
\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})=\pi^{*}\beta_{j}(\nu_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}
. By Lemma
4.9
(a)
, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{i}}\subseteq\sigma_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{B}
, so
\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})=\sigma_{\overline{i}}b_{ji}
for some bounded function
b_{ji}
. In particular, the functions
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})
and
\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})
are bounded. Moreover, since
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
, it follows that
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})=\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{1/2}b_{ji}\rightarrow 0
and
\beta_{j}(\pi_{*}\nu_{i})=\sigma_{\overline{i}}b_{ji}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
, as claimed.

(f)
By definition (
50
) of
\xi_{i}
, for
\xi\in\Xi
the norm
||\xi||
equals the maximum norm of
\pi_{*}\xi
with respect to the natural coordinates on
\overline{U}_{X}
. The result follows since all norms on
T\overline{U}_{X}
are equivalent.

∎

We now apply the Gaussian diagonalization to the first
2k
rows and columns of (
54
). For
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
, denote by
((\nu_{i}^{j})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i}^{j})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
the basis of
T(U\setminus\partial A)
obtained from
((\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
after the
j
-th diagonalization step.

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
we can shrink the fine chart
U_{X}
around
x_{0}
so that the matrix (
54
) of
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
in basis
((\nu_{i}^{j})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i}^{j})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
keeps the properties listed in Lemma
4.10
.

We argue by induction on
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
. Assume that we have eliminated the off-diagonal entries from rows and columns up to
j-1
; and that the resulting basis still satisfies Lemma
4.10
. While eliminating the off-diagonal entries from the
j
-th column, we perform the following operations on the columns
l\in\{j+1,\dots,2k\}
and
\check{l}\in\{2k+1,\dots,n\}
:

\begin{split}&a_{il}\rightsquigarrow a_{il}-a_{jj}^{-1}a_{jl}\cdot a_{ij},\mbox{ for all }i\in\{j+1,\dots,2k\}\\
&q_{il}\rightsquigarrow q_{il}-a_{jj}^{-1}a_{jl}\cdot q_{ij},\mbox{ for all }i\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\}.\\
\mbox{and}\quad&p_{il^{\prime}}\rightsquigarrow p_{il^{\prime}}-a_{jj}^{-1}q_{l^{\prime}j}\cdot q_{ij},\mbox{ for all }i\in\{1,\dots,2(n-k)\},\mbox{ where }l^{\prime}=\check{l}-2k.\end{split}

Put
c=a_{jj}^{-1}a_{jl}
,
c^{\prime}=a_{jj}^{-1}q_{l^{\prime}j}
. We claim that, after possibly shrinking
U_{X}
, we have

(55)

c\in\sigma_{\overline{l}}\cdot\mathcal{B}\quad\mbox{and}\quad c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}.

Indeed, by property
4.10
(a)
we can shrink
U_{X}
so that
a_{jj}=\sigma_{\overline{j}}\check{b}_{j}
for a function
\check{b}_{j}
bounded from below by a positive constant. This way,
\check{b}_{j}^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}
, so
a_{jj}^{-1}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\mathcal{B}
. Since we assume
l>j
, by property
4.10
(b)
we have
a_{jl}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\sigma_{\overline{l}}\mathcal{B}
, so
c=a_{jj}^{-1}\cdot a_{jl}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\cdot\sigma_{\overline{j}}\sigma_{\overline{l}}\cdot\mathcal{B}=\sigma_{\overline{l}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
. Similarly, since
q_{l^{\prime}j}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
by
4.10
(c)
, we have
c^{\prime}=a_{jj}^{-1}q_{l^{\prime}j}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}
. This proves (
55
).

Recall that
\sigma_{j}
was defined in (
33
) as
\sigma_{j}=t_{j}^{2}+t_{j}u_{j}^{2}
, so
\sigma_{j}\in\mathcal{B}
. Now for
i>j
, property
4.10
(b)
gives
a_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\sigma_{\overline{i}}\mathcal{B}
, so the formula (
55
) implies that

(56)

ca_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{l}}\cdot\mathcal{B},

so the property
4.10
(b)
is preserved.

Similarly, property
4.10
(c)
shows that
q_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, so formula (
55
) gives
cq_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{l}}\mathcal{B}
and
c^{\prime}q_{ij}\in\mathcal{B}
. It follows that the properties of
q_{il}
and
p_{il^{\prime}}
listed in Lemma
4.10
(c)
,
(d)
are preserved.

Thus we have shown that the off-diagonal entries in the first
2k
columns keep their properties. For the diagonal ones, we need to show that
4.10
(a)
is preserved. To do this, assume
i=l
. By (
56
), we have
ca_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{l}}\cdot\mathcal{B}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{2}\cdot\mathcal{B}
. Recall that by definition (
33
) we have
\sigma_{\overline{i}}=t_{\overline{i}}^{2}+t_{\overline{i}}u_{\overline{i}}^{2}\in t_{\overline{i}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
, so
ca_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
, i.e.
ca_{ij}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}}b
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Thus the new
(i,i)
-th entry of
A
is

a_{ii}-ca_{ij}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}\check{b}_{\overline{i}}-\sigma_{\overline{i}}t_{\overline{i}}b=\sigma_{\overline{i}}(\check{b}_{i}+t_{\overline{i}}b).

As we approach
x_{0}
, we have
t_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
, so
t_{\overline{i}}b\rightarrow 0
because
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Thus if
\check{b}_{i}>\frac{1}{4}
then
\check{b}+t_{\overline{i}}b>\frac{1}{4}
, too, for
t_{\overline{i}}
small enough. Therefore, shrinking
U_{X}
we can ensure that the diagonal entries of
A
keep their property
4.10
(a)
.

To see property
4.10
(e)
, we recall the Gaussian elimination formula

\nu_{i}^{j}=\nu_{i}^{j-1}-a_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}\cdot\nu_{j}^{j-1}\quad\mbox{for }i>j.

We need to prove that
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\pi_{*}\nu_{i}^{j}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. The convergence
\pi_{*}\nu_{i}^{j}\rightarrow 0
will follow since
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\in\mathcal{B}
.

By the inductive assumption,
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\pi_{*}\nu_{i}^{j-1}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. We now study the second summand. We have
a_{ij}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}
by property
4.10
(b)
and
a_{jj}^{-1}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{B}
by property
4.10
(a)
, so
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot a_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}\in\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\sigma_{\overline{i}}\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{B}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{1/2}\cdot\mathcal{B}
. Thus
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}a_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}=\sigma^{1/2}_{\overline{i}}\cdot b
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
. By the inductive assumption, the vector field
\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}
is bounded, hence so is
b\cdot\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}
. By definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{\overline{i}}
, we have
\sigma_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. Thus
\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot a_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}\cdot\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}=\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{1/2}\cdot b\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
, as needed.

It remains to show that the property
4.10
(f)
is preserved. Let
\Xi^{j}
be the subbundle of
T(U\setminus\partial A)
spanned by
\{\xi^{j}_{1},\dots,\xi_{2(n-k)}^{j}\}
, and let
||\,\cdot\,||^{(j)}
be the corresponding maximum norm on
\Xi^{j}
. We need to find a constant
K_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that for every
\xi\in\Xi^{j}
we have
||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\geqslant K_{0}^{\prime}||\xi||^{(j)}
. Clearly, it is enough to ensure this inequality for
\xi\in\Xi^{j}
such that
||\xi||^{(j)}=1
.

Take such
\xi\in\Xi^{j}
and write it as
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{2(n-k)}\lambda_{i}\xi_{i}^{j}
for some smooth functions
\lambda_{i}
. Then
\max_{i}||\lambda_{i}||=||\xi||^{(j)}=1
. Put
\xi^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\xi_{i}^{j-1}\in\Xi^{j-1}
. We have
||\xi^{\prime}||^{(j-1)}=1
, so by the inductive assumption,
||\pi_{*}\xi^{\prime}||_{X}\geqslant K_{0}
. Moreover,
||\pi_{*}\xi^{\prime}-\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\leqslant\sum_{i}||\pi_{*}(\xi^{j-1}_{i}-\xi^{j}_{i})||_{X}
.

The Gaussian elimination formula reads as
\xi^{j}_{i}=\xi^{j-1}_{i}-q_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}\cdot\nu_{j}^{j-1}
, so
\pi_{*}(\xi^{j-1}_{i}-\xi^{j}_{i})=q_{ij}a_{jj}^{-1}\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}
. Property
4.10
(e)
implies that
\pi_{*}\nu_{j}^{j-1}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{j}}\rightarrow 0
. Like before, combining properties
4.10
(c)
and
(a)
we infer that
q_{ij}\cdot a_{jj}^{-1}\in\sigma_{\overline{j}}\cdot\sigma_{\overline{j}}^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}
. Therefore,
\pi_{*}(\xi^{j-1}_{i}-\xi^{j}_{i})\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{j}}\rightarrow 0
. Since
r_{\overline{j}}(x_{0})=0
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,2k\}
, we can shrink the neighborhood
U_{X}
of
x_{0}
so that
||\pi_{*}(\xi^{j-1}_{i}-\xi^{j}_{i})||_{X}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2(n-k)}K_{0}
. Then
||\pi_{*}\xi^{\prime}-\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}K_{0}
, and as a consequence,
||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\geqslant||\pi_{*}\xi^{\prime}||_{X}-||\pi_{*}\xi^{\prime}-\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\geqslant\tfrac{1}{2}K_{0}
. Thus property
4.10
(f)
holds with the constant
K_{0}>0
replaced by
\tfrac{1}{2}K_{0}>0
.

∎

Consider the matrix (
54
) of
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
after the first
2k
steps of the Gaussian diagonalization, that is, in the basis
((\nu_{i}^{2k})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i}^{2k})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
. Then its off-diagonal block
Q
is zero, and the top-left block
A
is diagonal. By Lemma
4.11
, we can shrink
U_{X}
so that the properties
4.10
(a)
–
(f)
are preserved. Furthermore, we change the basis by replacing each
\nu_{i}^{2k}
with
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k}\coloneqq\sigma_{\overline{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\nu_{i}^{2k}
.

Now, property
4.10
(a)
asserts that the diagonal terms of
A
are bounded from below by
\tfrac{1}{4}
, and by
4.10
(e)
the corresponding basis vector fields
(\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k})_{i=1}^{2k}
approach zero as we approach
x_{0}
.

Any vector field
v\in T(U\setminus\partial A)
splits as
v=\nu+\xi
, where
\nu
and
\xi
are linear combinations of
\{\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k}\}_{i=1}^{2k}
and
\{\xi_{i}^{2k}\}_{i=1}^{2(n-k)}
, respectively. Since the block form (
54
) of
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}
has the off-diagonal block
Q
equal to zero, we have the equality

(57)

\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(v,v)=\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu,\nu)+\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi,\xi).

In the tangent bundle
T(U\setminus\partial A)
, we consider the maximum norm
||\,\cdot\,||
with respect to the coefficients of the basis
((\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k})_{i=1}^{2k},(\xi_{i}^{2k})_{i=1}^{2(n-k)})
. The diagonal entries of
A
are equal to
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k},\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k})
, so by property
4.10
(a)
, they are bounded from below by
\tfrac{1}{4}
. It follows that

(58)

\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu,\nu)\geqslant\tfrac{1}{4}||\nu||^{2}.

Property
4.10
(d)
asserts that the functions
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi_{i}^{2k},\xi_{j}^{2k})
, which are entries of the matrix
P
, are bounded. Thus there is a constant
K>0
such that, uniformly on
U\setminus\partial A
, we have the inequality

(59)

|\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi,\xi)|\leqslant K||\xi||^{2}.

Recall that the form
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
was defined in (
45
) as
\pi^{*}\omega_{X}+\varepsilon\omega_{E}
. Hence by Lemma
4.8
, for every
\tau>0
we have the equality of restrictions

(\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)}=(\widetilde{\omega}^{\varepsilon}_{A})^{J}|_{g^{-1}(\tau)},\quad\mbox{where}\quad(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}=\pi^{*}\omega_{X}^{J}+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}.

We need to show that there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
V_{X}
of
x_{0}
such that whenever
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, we have the inequality
(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)>0
for every nonzero
v\in T(\pi^{-1}(V_{X})\setminus\partial A)
.

We split
v=\nu+\xi
as above. Consider the case
||\nu||>2K^{\frac{1}{2}}||\xi||
. Since the form
\omega_{X}^{J}
is positive definite, we have
(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(v,v)\geqslant 0
, so
(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)\geqslant\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(v,v)
. Substituting the inequalities (
58
) and (
59
) to (
57
), we get
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(v,v)\geqslant\frac{1}{4}||\nu||^{2}-K||\xi||^{2}>0
by assumption. Thus
(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)>0
, as needed.

Hence we can assume

(60)

||\nu||\leqslant 2K^{\frac{1}{2}}||\xi||.

Using (
57
), we expand the equality
(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)=(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(v,v)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(v,v)
as

(61)

(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)=(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\nu)+2(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\xi)+(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\xi,\xi)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu,\nu)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi,\xi).

The inequality (
58
) implies that
\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu,\nu)\geqslant 0
, so since
(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}
is positive definite, for
v\neq 0
we have

(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\nu)+\tfrac{1}{3}(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\xi,\xi)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\nu,\nu)>0.

We group the remaining summands of the expansion (
61
) as follows:

\begin{split}B&\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{3}(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\xi,\xi)+2(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\xi),\\
C&\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{3}(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\xi,\xi)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\omega}_{E}^{J}(\xi,\xi).\end{split}

Let
||\,\cdot\,||_{X}
be a norm in the tangent bundle
T\overline{U}_{X}
. By property
4.10
(f)
, there is a constant
K_{0}>0
such that
||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\geqslant K_{0}||\xi||
. By positive definiteness of
\omega_{X}^{J}
there exists a constant
K_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that
\omega_{X}^{J}(\eta,\eta)\geqslant K_{1}^{\prime}||\eta||_{X}^{2}
for every
\eta
tangent to
\overline{U}_{X}
. Putting
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\prime}K_{0}^{2}
we get

(62)

(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\xi,\xi)\geqslant K_{1}||\xi||^{2}.

There are also constants
K_{2}^{\prime},K_{2}^{\prime\prime}>0
such that
|(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\xi)|\leqslant K_{2}^{\prime}\cdot||\pi_{*}\nu||_{X}\cdot||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}
and
||\pi_{*}\xi||_{X}\leqslant K_{2}^{\prime\prime}||\xi||
, so putting
K_{2}=K_{2}^{\prime}K_{2}^{\prime\prime}
we get
|(\pi^{*}\omega_{X})^{J}(\nu,\xi)|\leqslant K_{2}||\xi||\cdot||\pi_{*}\nu||_{X}
. This inequality together with (
62
) implies that

(63)

B\geqslant\tfrac{1}{3}K_{1}||\xi||^{2}-2K_{2}||\xi||\cdot||\pi_{*}\nu||_{X}.

Recall that
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k}=\sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\nu_{i}^{2k}
, so by property
4.10
(e)
, we have
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}^{2k}\rightarrow 0
as
r_{\overline{i}}\rightarrow 0
. Hence there is a continuous function
\kappa\colon[0,\infty)^{k}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\kappa(0)=0
and
||\pi_{*}\nu||_{X}\leqslant\kappa(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})||\nu||
. Together with the assumption (
60
), this implies that
||\pi_{*}\nu||_{X}\leqslant 2K^{\frac{1}{2}}\kappa(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})||\xi||
. Substituting this inequality to (
63
), we get

B\geqslant||\xi||^{2}(\tfrac{1}{3}K_{1}-4K^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{2}\cdot\kappa(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})).

Since
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
vanish at
x_{0}
, we can shrink the neighborhood
U_{X}
of
x_{0}
so that
\kappa(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})<\frac{\frac{1}{3}K_{1}}{4K^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{2}}
. After this shrinking of
U_{X}
, we get
B\geqslant 0
. Notice that this shrinking is independent of
\varepsilon
.

The inequalities (
62
) and (
59
) imply that

C\geqslant(\tfrac{1}{3}K_{1}-\varepsilon K)\cdot||\xi||^{2}.

Choose
\varepsilon_{0}\coloneqq\frac{1}{3}\frac{K_{1}}{K}
. Then for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
we have
C\geqslant 0
, so
(\widetilde{\omega}_{A}^{\varepsilon})^{J}(v,v)>0
, as needed.

∎

4.3.
Non-degeneracy on
\partial A

In Proposition
4.3
we have shown that the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
are fiberwise nondegenerate near
\partial A
. Proposition
4.12
below, which we will prove in this section, shows that the same holds on
\partial A
. Recall from Proposition
3.33
(b)
that
\theta\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a submersion extending
f/|f|\colon X\setminus D\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
to
\partial A
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Fix
x_{0}\in D
. There is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and a fine chart
U_{X}
containing
x_{0}
, such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and any
\delta>0
, the restriction of each of the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
to
U\cap\partial A
is nondegenerate on every fiber of
\theta|_{U\cap\partial A}
, where
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. Moreover, the symplectic lift to
U\cap\partial A
of the unit angular vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is independent of the choice of the form
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
defining the symplectic connection, and is given by the formula (
48
).

We notice that at
\partial A
the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
coincide, so it is enough to deal with the former one.

As in Section
4.2
, we fix
x_{0}\in X_{S}^{\circ}
for some
S\neq\emptyset
and order the components of
D
so that
S=\{1,\dots,k\}
. We fix a fine chart
U_{X}
around
x_{0}
with index set
S
; and put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})\subseteq A
.

Let us recall the definition of the smooth structure on
U
. In (
28
) we have defined an open covering
U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
by
U_{i}=\{w_{i}>\frac{1}{n+1}\}
. The smooth coordinate chart (
36
) on
U_{1}
is

(g,\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{k},\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k},z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow[0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{n-k},

where
\{i_{k+1},\dots,i_{n}\}
is some subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{>N}
. The charts on
U_{2},\dots,U_{k}
are defined analogously. The vector fields

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}

on
U_{1}
used in formula (
48
) are defined as the ones dual to
d\theta_{1},\dots,d\theta_{k}
with respect to the basis of
T^{*}U_{1}
given by the above chart (
36
).

In the proof of Proposition
4.12
, we will treat separately the subsets
U_{1}\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
lying over the strata
U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
, see (
25
). Therefore, it will be convenient to use slightly different charts over each stratum, which we introduce in formula (
64
) below. For the deepest stratum
X_{S}^{\circ}
, formula (
64
) agrees with (
36
).

Lemma 4.13
.

Fix a subset
I\subseteq S
containing
1
. Put
U_{I}^{\circ}\coloneqq U_{1}\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
, and reorder the components of
D
so that
I=\{1,\dots,l\}
. Then, after possibly shrinking
U_{X}
, the following hold.

(a)

For
i\in\{l+1,\dots,k\}
, put
s_{i}=\log r_{i}
, see (
50
). Then the map

(64)

(\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{l},s_{l+1},\dots,s_{k},\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k},z_{i_{k+1}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})\colon U_{I}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{n-k}.

is a smooth coordinate chart on a neighborhood of
U_{I}
in
\partial A
.

(b)

The vector fields
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}
are dual to
d\theta_{1},\dots,d\theta_{k}
with respect to the basis of
T^{*}U_{I}
given by coordinates (
64
).

(c)

Define vector fields
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{2}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{l}}
as dual to
d\overline{v}_{2},\dots,d\overline{v}_{l}
in the above basis. We have a splitting

TU_{I}^{\circ}=\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I},\quad\mbox{where }\mathcal{V}_{I}=\operatorname{span}\{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{2}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{l}}\},\ \Theta_{I}=\operatorname{span}\{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}},\dots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{l}}\},

and
\mathcal{Z}_{I}
is spanned by the vector fields dual to
ds_{l+1},\dots,ds_{k}
,
d\theta_{l+1},\dots,d\theta_{k}
and to the (real and imaginary parts of)
dz_{i_{k+1}},\dots,dz_{i_{n}}
, with respect to the basis of
T^{*}U_{I}
given by the chart (
64
).

We have
\pi_{*}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
, and
\pi_{*}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}
maps
\mathcal{Z}_{I}
isomorphically onto the tangent bundle to
X_{I}^{\circ}\cap\pi(U_{1})
.

(a)
On the stratum
U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
, we have a smooth coordinate system
(z_{l+1},\dots,z_{k},z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n-k}})
. Since the coordinates
z_{l+1},\dots,z_{k}
do not vanish on
U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
, we can replace each of them by polar coordinates
(r_{i},\theta_{i})
, or
(s_{i},\theta_{i})
. Since the map
\pi
is smooth, the pullback of each
s_{i}
and
\theta_{i}
to
U\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
is smooth, too. Thus the map (
64
) is smooth. To see that it defines a system of coordinates, it is enough to see that the block
[\frac{\partial s_{i}}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}}]_{l<i,j\leqslant k}
of its Jacobian matrix is invertible. By definition (
16
) of
t_{i}
, we have
s_{i}=-(m_{i}t_{i})^{-1}
, and by Lemma
3.16
(b)
, on
U_{I}^{\circ}
we have
t_{i}=v_{i}
. Thus it is enough to show that the matrix
[\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}}]_{l<i,j\leqslant k}
is invertible. By Lemma
3.35
(b)
, as we approach
x_{0}
we have
d(v_{i}-\overline{v}_{i})\rightarrow 0
, so our matrix approaches the identity; in particular it is invertible once
U_{X}
is small enough.

(b)
The coordinates of
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}
in our basis of
TU_{I}^{\circ}
is given by the
(k+i)
-th column of the above Jacobian matrix.
Its entries are
\frac{\partial\overline{v}_{j}}{\partial\theta_{i}}=0
,
\frac{\partial s_{j}}{\partial\theta_{i}}=0
,
\frac{\partial\theta_{j}}{\partial\theta_{i}}=\delta_{i}^{j}
and
\frac{\partial z_{j}}{\partial\theta_{i}}=0
, as needed.

(c)
The map
\pi
collapses
A_{I}^{\circ}
onto
X_{I}^{\circ}
. In local coordinates (
64
) on
U_{I}^{\circ}
and
(s_{l+1},\dots,s_{k},\allowbreak\theta_{l+1},\dots,\theta_{k},\allowbreak z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n-k}})
on its image in
X_{I}^{\circ}
, this map is a projection

(\overline{v}_{2},\dots,\overline{v}_{l},s_{l+1},\dots,s_{k},\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k},z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n-k}})\mapsto(s_{l+1},\dots,s_{k},\theta_{l+1},\dots,\theta_{k},z_{i_{1}},\dots,z_{i_{n-k}}).

Thus
\pi_{*}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
, and
\pi_{*}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}
is an isomorphism onto its image, as needed.

∎

Lemma 4.14
.

On
U_{I}^{\circ}
, the following hold.

(a)

For every
\beta\in\Omega^{*}(U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ})
we have
\pi^{*}\beta|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
.

(b)

For every
j>l
we have
\overline{v}_{j}=\overline{t}_{j}
for some
\overline{t}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ})
. In particular,
d\overline{v}_{j}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
.

(c)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,l\}
we have
\alpha_{i}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}}=0
and
\alpha_{i}(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{j}})=\delta_{i}^{j}
.

(d)

We have
d\overline{v}_{1}=-\sum_{i=2}^{l}\frac{\zeta(u_{i})}{\zeta(u_{1})}\,d\overline{v}_{i}
, where
\zeta=(\eta^{-1})^{\prime}\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
is as in (
48
).

(e)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
we have
d\overline{v}_{i}|_{\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=0
.

(f)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
we have

\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\,\cdot\,,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=\varepsilon\,d\overline{v}_{i}
.

(a)
By Lemma
4.13
(c)
, we have
\pi_{*}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
, so
\pi^{*}\beta|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=0
.

(b)
Recall that
\overline{v}_{j}=\sum_{p}\tau^{p}v_{j}^{p}
, where each
\tau^{p}
is a smooth function on
X
; and
v_{j}^{p}=0
for those
p\in R
such that
U_{X}^{p}\cap D_{j}=\emptyset
. Take
p\in R
such that
U_{X}^{p}\cap D_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Since
j\not\in I
, Lemma
3.6
(b)
and definition (
19
) of
v_{j}^{p}
imply that
v_{j}^{p}|_{U_{I}^{\circ}\cap U^{p}}=t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{I}^{\circ}\cap U^{p}}
. Again since
j\not\in I
, the restriction
t_{j}^{p}|_{U_{I}^{\circ}\cap U^{p}}
is a pullback of a smooth function on
(U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ})\cap U_{X}^{p}
. We conclude that
\sum_{p}\tau^{p}t_{j}^{p}
is a smooth function on
U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
, whose pullback to
U_{I}^{\circ}
equals
\overline{v}_{j}
. This proves the first statement of
(b)
; the second follows from
(a)
.

(c)
By Lemma
4.5
(a)
, we have
\alpha_{i}=d\theta_{i}+\pi^{*}\beta_{i}
for some
\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X})
. Hence part
(a)
implies that
\alpha_{i}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}=d\theta_{i}|_{\mathcal{V}_{I}\oplus\Theta_{I}}
. Now
(c)
follows from the definition of the coordinate vector fields
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{j}}
.

(d)
Note first that the restriction
u_{1}|_{U_{1}}
is nonzero by definition (
28
) of
U_{1}
, so each summand in
(d)
is well defined. By continuity, it is enough to prove
(d)
at each point
x\in\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}U_{I}^{\circ}
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
. Since
x\in\operatorname{Int}_{\partial A}U_{I}^{\circ}
, we have
t_{i}=0
on some neighborhood of
x
in
\partial A
. There,
\overline{v}_{i}=-\overline{u}_{i}
by definition (
23
) of
\overline{v}_{i}
; so
\overline{v}_{i}=-u_{i}
by Lemma
3.6
(e)
. The formula (
18
) gives
u_{i}=\eta(w_{i})
, so
w_{i}=\eta^{-1}(u_{i})
, and therefore
dw_{i}=\zeta(u_{i})du_{i}
by definition of
\zeta
. It follows that on
T_{x}U_{I}^{\circ}
we have
dw_{i}=-\zeta(u_{i})d\overline{v}_{i}
.

In particular,
d\overline{v}_{1}=-\tfrac{1}{\zeta(u_{1})}dw_{1}
. By Lemma
3.6
(c)
we have
w_{1}=-\sum_{i=2}^{l}w_{i}
, so

d\overline{v}_{1}=-\frac{1}{\zeta(u_{1})}dw_{1}=\frac{1}{\zeta(u_{1})}\sum_{i=2}^{l}dw_{i}=-\sum_{i=2}^{l}\frac{\zeta(u_{i})}{\zeta(u_{1})}d\overline{v}_{i},

as needed.

(e)
For
i\in\{2,\dots,l\}
we have
d\overline{v}_{i}|_{\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=0
by definition of
\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I}
, see Lemma
4.13
(c)
. The remaining equality
d\overline{v}_{1}|_{\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=0
follows from
(d)
.

(f)
By
(a)
, we have
\pi^{*}\omega_{X}(\,\cdot\,,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=0
, so
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\,\cdot\,,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=\varepsilon\omega_{E}(\,\cdot\,,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})
. By definition (
44
) of
\omega_{E}
, we have
\omega_{E}|_{\partial A}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}d\overline{v}_{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{v}_{j}d\alpha_{j}
. By Lemma
4.5
, the
1
-forms
\alpha_{j}
for
j>l
, and the
2
-forms
d\alpha_{j}
for all
j
are pullbacks of smooth forms from
U_{X}
. Thus parts
(a)
and
(b)
imply that
(d\overline{v}_{j}\wedge\alpha_{j})(\,\cdot\,,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=0
for
j>l
and
d\alpha_{j}(\,\cdot\,,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=0
for all
j
. We conclude that

\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}\left(\,\cdot\,,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{l}\alpha_{j}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)\cdot d\overline{v}_{j}-d\overline{v}_{j}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)\cdot\alpha_{j}=\varepsilon d\overline{v}_{i},

where in the second equality we have used identities
\alpha_{j}(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=\delta_{i}^{j}
and
d\overline{v}_{j}(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})=0
from
(c)
,
(e)
.

∎

Lemma 4.15
.

We can shrink
U_{X}
so that the following holds. There is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the restriction of the form
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
to the subbundle
\mathcal{Z}_{I}
is nondegenerate.

By Lemma
4.13
(c)
, the differential of
\pi
maps
\mathcal{Z}_{I}
isomorphically onto the tangent bundle to the stratum
X_{I}^{\circ}\cap\pi(U_{1})
. In particular, the standard almost complex structure
J
on
X
pulls back to an almost complex structure on
\mathcal{Z}_{I}
. We denote this pullback by
J
, too. Now it is enough to prove that, after possibly shrinking
U_{X}
, there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the symmetric part of the bilinear form
\mathcal{Z}_{I}\times\mathcal{Z}_{I}\ni(v,w)\mapsto\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(v,Jw)\in\mathbb{R}
is positive definite.

By Lemma
4.14
(e)
, we have
d\overline{v}_{i}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
. Hence

\omega_{E}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=\sum_{i=l+1}^{k}d\overline{v}_{i}\wedge\alpha_{i}+\check{\omega}_{1},

where
\check{\omega}_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{j=k+1}^{N}d\overline{v}_{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{v}_{j}d\alpha_{j}
. By Lemma
4.5
(b)
,
(c)
,
(d)
, the
1
-forms
d\overline{v}_{j}
,
\alpha_{j}
for
j>k
; and the
2
-forms
d\alpha_{j}
for all
j
, are pullbacks of smooth, bounded forms on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}\coloneqq U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
. By Lemma
4.14
(b)
, the functions
\overline{v}_{j}
for
j>l
are pullbacks of smooth functions on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
. Clearly the functions
\overline{v}_{j}
are bounded. We conclude that the form
\check{\omega}_{1}
is bounded in the natural coordinates of
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
.

By Lemma
3.35
(b)
, for every
i\in\{l+1,\dots,k\}
, the restriction of
d\overline{v}_{i}
to
TU_{I}^{\circ}
equals
(1+c_{i}t_{i})dv_{i}+\sigma_{i}\gamma_{i}
, where
c_{i}
(respectively,
\gamma_{i}
) is a pullback of a smooth, bounded function (respectively,
1
-form) on
U_{X,I}^{\circ}
; and
\sigma_{i}
is the function defined in (
33
). Moreover, since
u_{i}|_{U_{I}^{\circ}}=0
, we have
dv_{i}=dt_{i}=t_{i}^{2}m_{i}\,ds_{i}
by Lemma
4.7
(a)
, and
t_{i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}
by definition (
33
) of
\sigma_{i}
. Eventually, by Lemma
4.5
(a)
, we have
\alpha_{i}=d\theta_{i}+\pi^{*}\beta_{i}
for some bounded
\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X})
. As in the proof of Lemma
4.8
, we conclude that

\omega_{E}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=\sum_{i=l+1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})ds_{i}\wedge d\theta_{i}+\gamma_{i}\wedge d\theta_{i}+m_{i}(1+c_{i}t_{i})ds_{i}\wedge\check{\beta}_{i})+\omega_{1},

for some smooth, bounded
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
,
\gamma_{i},\check{\beta}_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
and
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{2}(U_{X,I}^{\circ})
.

This is precisely formula (
53
) with two modifications. First, the sum starts at
i=l+1
instead of
1
. Second, the functions and forms which in (
53
) are bounded from
X
, i.e. bounded in the natural coordinates of
U_{X}\cap X_{\emptyset}^{\circ}
, are now bounded in the natural coordinates of
U_{X}\cap X_{I}^{\circ}
.

The remaining part of the proof follows by repeating the proof of Proposition
4.3
, given in Section
4.2
, starting from formula (
53
). The only difference is that now the computations take place over the stratum
X_{I}^{\circ}
instead of
X_{\emptyset}^{\circ}
. In particular, instead of vector fields
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{2k}
on
U_{\emptyset}^{\circ}
, which were dual to
ds_{1},\dots,ds_{k}
,
d\theta_{1},\dots,d\theta_{k}
with respect to the coordinates (
50
), we now use vector fields which are dual to
ds_{l+1},\dots,ds_{k}
,
d\theta_{l+1},\dots,d\theta_{k}
with respect to the coordinates (
64
).

∎

The point
x_{0}\in D
lies in a stratum
X_{S}^{\circ}
for some nonempty
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, see (
11
). Choose a fine chart
U_{X}
around
x_{0}
, whose associated index set (
12
) equals
S
. The preimage
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
admits a finite covering
U=\bigcup_{i\in S}U_{i}
, where each
U_{i}
is the domain of a smooth chart (
36
). Therefore, it is enough to prove the following. For every
i_{0}\in S
, we can shrink the chart
U_{X}
around
x_{0}
, and find a positive number
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the form
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
is fiberwise nondegenerate on
U_{i_{0}}
; and the symplectic lift of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
to
U_{i_{0}}
is given by the formula (
48
).

As before, we reorder the components of
D
so that
S=\{1,\dots,k\}
and
i_{0}=1
. We can assume that
U_{X}
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
are small enough so that the statement of Lemma
4.15
holds for all subsets
I\subseteq U_{X}
containing
1
. We fix one such subset, say
I=\{1,\dots,l\}
, and use coordinates (
64
) on
U_{I}^{\circ}=U_{1}\cap A_{I}^{\circ}
. Also, we fix an
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
.

Fix a point
x\in U_{I}^{\circ}
. Let
F
be the fiber of
\theta
passing through
x
. Since the fine chart
U_{X}
is by definition adapted to
f
, Definition
3.1
(b)
together with the formula (
17
) for
\theta_{i}
show that

\theta|_{U_{I}^{\circ}}=m_{1}\theta_{1}+\dots+m_{k}\theta_{k},

where we use additive notation
3.2
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Hence

(65)

T_{x}F=\ker\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}d\theta_{i}\right]\subseteq T_{x}U_{I}^{\circ}.

Assume that a nonzero vector
\nu\in T_{x}U_{I}^{\circ}
satisfies
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\nu^{\prime})=0
for all
\nu^{\prime}\in T_{x}F
. To prove non-degeneracy of
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}|_{T_{x}F}
, we need to show that
\nu\not\in T_{x}F
.

Let
\nu^{\theta}
be the vector given by the formula (
48
). We note that for
i\in\{l+1,\dots,k\}
, we have
u_{i}=0
, so the sum in (
48
) actually runs from
i=1
to
l
. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
d\theta_{j}(\nu^{\theta})=(\sum_{i=1}^{l}m_{i}\zeta(u_{i}))^{-1}\cdot\zeta(u_{j})\geqslant 0
, with strict inequality for
j=1
. Hence formula (
65
) gives
\nu^{\theta}\not\in T_{x}F
. Thus to show that
\nu\not\in T_{x}F
, it is enough to show that
\nu
is proportional to (
48
).

Using coordinate vector fields
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{i}}
introduced in Lemma
4.13
(c)
, we can write

(66)

\nu=\sum_{i=2}^{l}a_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}+\xi,\quad\mbox{for some }a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\mbox{ and }\xi\in\mathcal{Z}_{I}.

First, we claim that all coefficients
a_{2},\dots,a_{l}
in (
66
) are zero.

For
i\in\{2,\dots,l\}
put
\nu^{\theta}_{i}=\tfrac{1}{m_{i}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}\in T_{x}U_{I}^{\circ}
. Formula (
65
) shows that

\nu_{i}^{\theta}\in T_{x}F
, so
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\nu^{\theta}_{i})=0
by definition of
\nu
. Using Lemma
4.14
(f)
, we compute

0=\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\nu^{\theta}_{i})=\tfrac{1}{m_{i}}\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}})-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}})=\tfrac{1}{m_{i}}\cdot\varepsilon d\overline{v}_{i}(\nu)-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}\cdot\varepsilon d\overline{v}_{1}(\nu)=\varepsilon(\tfrac{a_{i}}{m_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}d\overline{v}_{1}(\nu)),

where the last equality follows from definition of the coordinate vector fields. Thus for all
i\in\{2,\dots,l\}
, the numbers
\tfrac{a_{i}}{m_{i}}
are equal to the same number
a\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{m_{1}}d\overline{v}_{1}(\nu)
.

Suppose
a\neq 0
. Then
\tfrac{a_{i}}{a}=m_{i}
. Since
d\overline{v}_{1}|_{\Theta_{I}\oplus\mathcal{Z}_{I}}=0
by Lemma
4.14
(e)
, the formula (
66
) for
\nu
gives

m_{1}=\frac{1}{a}d\overline{v}_{1}(\nu)=\frac{1}{a}\sum_{i=2}^{l}a_{i}d\overline{v}_{1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{i}}\right)=-\sum_{i=2}^{l}m_{i}\frac{\zeta(u_{i})}{\zeta(u_{1})}\leqslant 0,

where for the last equality we used the fact that
\tfrac{a_{i}}{a}=m_{i}
, and the formula for
d\overline{v}_{1}
from Lemma
4.14
(d)
. This is a contradiction. Therefore,
a=0
, so
a_{i}=am_{i}=0
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,l\}
, as claimed.

Now, we claim that the vector
\xi\in\mathcal{Z}_{I}
in (
66
) is zero. Fix any
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{I}
. Then
\xi^{\prime}\in T_{x}F
by (
65
), so
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\xi^{\prime})=0
by definition of
\nu
. By Lemma
4.14
(f)
, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
we have
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}},\xi^{\prime})=-\varepsilon d\overline{v}_{i}(\xi^{\prime})=0
, where the last equality follows from Lemma
4.14
(e)
. Since we have shown that
a_{2}=\dots=a_{l}=0
, we infer from (
66
) that
0=\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\xi^{\prime})=\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
, for all
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{I}
. Since by Lemma
4.15
, the restriction
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{I}}
is nondegenerate, we get
\xi=0
, as claimed.

Therefore,
\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}
for some
b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since for all
j\in\{2,\dots,l\}
we have
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}}\in T_{x}F
, we compute using Lemma
4.14
(f)
that

0=\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}},\nu\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)=\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}d\overline{v}_{i}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{v}_{j}}\right)=\varepsilon\left(b_{j}-b_{1}\frac{\zeta(u_{j})}{\zeta(u_{1})}\right),

where the last equality follows from Lemma
4.14
(d)
. We conclude that for all
j\in\{1,\dots,l\}
we have
b_{j}=\zeta(u_{j})b
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}
. This shows that
\nu
is proportional to (
48
), as claimed. In particular, the form
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
is non-degenerate at
x
.

It remains to show that if
\nu
is normalized by
\theta_{*}(\nu)=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
then
\nu
equals (
48
). In this case, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}=\theta_{*}(\nu)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}m_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}=b\sum_{i=1}^{l}\zeta(u_{i})m_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta},

hence
b=(\sum_{i=1}^{l}\zeta(u_{i})m_{i})^{-1}
, as needed.

∎

Fix
x\in\partial A
. By Propositions
4.3
and
4.12
, there is an
\varepsilon_{x}>0
and an open neighborhood
V_{x}
of
x
in
A
, such that, for any
\delta>0
, each of the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
is fiberwise nondegenerate on
V_{x}
for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{x}]
, and on
V_{x}\setminus\partial A
for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{x}]
. Since
\partial A\cap\overline{W}
is compact, choosing a finite sub-cover from
\{V_{x}\}
we get an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
W^{\prime}
of
\partial A\cap\overline{W}
, such that, again, each of the forms
\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{A}^{\delta,\varepsilon}
is fiberwise nondegenerate on
W^{\prime}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. This proves part
(a)
, part
(b)
follows from Proposition
4.12
.

∎

We end this section with a simple example illustrating Definition
3.3
of the A’Campo space, and the monodromy at radius zero given by Proposition
4.2
(b)
. In Proposition
7.4
we will describe the latter more precisely, in terms of the associated abstract contact open book.

Example 4.16
.

A_{1}^{\circ}
A_{1,2}^{\circ}
A_{2}^{\circ}
\Gamma_{1}^{\circ}
\Gamma_{2}^{\circ}
\Gamma_{1,2}^{\circ}
\upsilon
(X_{1}^{\circ})_{\log}
(X_{2}^{\circ})_{\log}
D_{1}
D_{2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(a)

Radius-zero fibers in
A
,
\Gamma
,
X_{\log}
and
X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, see diagram (
24
).

A_{1}^{\circ}\colon(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
A_{1,2}^{\circ}\colon(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\theta_{1}-\upsilon,\theta_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\upsilon)
A_{2}^{\circ}\colon(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(b)

Monodromy at radius zero, see Propositions
4.2
(b)
and
7.4
.

Figure 4
.
Example
4.16
: the A’Campo space for the function
f(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}z_{2}^{2}
.

Take
f\colon X=\mathbb{C}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by
f(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}z_{2}^{2}
.
Then
f^{-1}(0)=D_{1}+2D_{2}
, where
D_{i}=\{z_{i}=0\}
. In the bottom-right of Figure
4(a)
, the disks
D_{1},D_{2}
meeting at the origin are shown as the vertical and horizontal cone, respectively. The fiber
f_{\log}^{-1}(0,1)\subseteq X_{\log}
is shown in the bottom-left. The restriction of the map
X_{\log}\longrightarrow X
to this fiber is an isomorphism in the vertical cylinder, is a double cover in the horizontal one, and collapses the middle circle to the origin. Hence a natural monodromy at radius zero in the Kato–Nakayama space
X_{\log}
is not continuous: indeed, in this example it is the identity in
(X_{1}^{\circ})_{\log}
, and a half-twist in
(X_{2}^{\circ})_{\log}
.

The top-right picture in Figure
4(a)
is the fiber over
0
in
\Gamma=\operatorname{graph}(\mu)
: there, the origin of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is replaced by a
1
-simplex
\Gamma_{1,2}^{\circ}
. Eventually, in the fiber
f_{A}^{-1}(0,1)\subseteq A
, shown in the top-left, we multiply this simplex by the middle circle of
f_{\log}^{-1}(0,1)
. The radius-zero monodromy
\phi
in
A
interpolates between the identity in
A_{1}^{\circ}
and half-twist in
A_{2}^{\circ}
, using a parameter of the
1
-simplex
\Gamma_{1,2}^{\circ}
, call it
\upsilon\in[0,1]
. Figure
4(b)
shows how the bold ruling is transformed by
\phi
. A precise formula for the parameter
\upsilon
can be obtained from equation (
48
): in this example, we have
\upsilon=\frac{2\zeta(u_{2})}{\zeta(u_{1})+2\zeta(u_{2})}
.

5.
Symplectic monodromy and abstract contact open books

We now recall further notions concerning Liouville domains and their fibrations. For a general introduction, see
[
CE12
,
MS17
,
Gei08
,
Gir20
,
Oh15
,
Sei08
]
and references therein. Our aim is to establish a natural setting
5.12
where we can produce or extend symplectic monodromies.

5.1.
Hamiltonian vector fields

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A
Hamiltonian
is a smooth function
H\colon M\times\mathbb{R}\ni(x,t)\mapsto H_{t}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
. A
time-independent Hamiltonian
is a smooth function
H\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which we view as a Hamiltonian
M\times\mathbb{R}\ni(x,t)\mapsto H(x)\in\mathbb{R}
. In other words, our Hamiltonians are always time-dependent, unless explicitly stated otherwise.

Fix a Hamiltonian
H\colon M\times\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Since
\omega
is non-degenerate, there exists a unique vector field
X^{H}
, called a
Hamiltonian vector field
of
H
, satisfying the equality

\omega(X^{H},\,\cdot\,)=dH.

The
Hamiltonian flow
of
H
is a family of symplectomorphisms
\psi_{t}^{H}\colon M\longrightarrow M
, parametrized by
t\in\mathbb{R}
, defined by the formulas

\psi^{H}_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{M},\quad\tfrac{d}{dt}\psi_{t}^{H}=X^{H}.

Our definition of
X^{H}
coincides with the one used in
[
Ulj17
,
MS17
,
DS94
,
Oh15
]
. However,
[
AD14
,
McL19
]
use the opposite one, namely
\omega(\,\cdot\,,X^{H})=dH
: thus for a given Hamiltonian
H
, our vector field
X^{H}
and flow
\psi^{H}_{t}
are equal to
-X^{H}
and
(\psi^{H}_{t})^{-1}
from
[
AD14
,
McL19
]
.

So, when we quote
[
Ulj17
]
we need no adaptations. However, we will quote results from
[
AD14
,
McL19
]
too. To avoid sign mistakes, whenever we do this, we replace
H
in loc. cit. by
-H
: this way, we get the same vector field
X^{H}
and flow
\psi^{H}_{t}
as in loc. cit; so no further modifications are needed. In particular, to get the same Floer Cauchy–Riemann equation (
100
) as used in
[
McL19
, Definition 4.3]
, we will need to perturb
\phi
by an opposite Hamiltonian, so we will chose it
negative
near
\partial M
, despite the symbol
“
+
”
; see Section
6.2.3
for details.

5.2.
Liouville and symplectic fibrations

Notation 5.1
.

Fix a map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
. If
f
is clear from the context, we will use the following, simplified notation for its fibers. For
y\in Y
, we put
X_{y}=f^{-1}(y)
. Given a form
\lambda\in\Omega^{*}(X)
, we write
\lambda_{y}\coloneqq\lambda|_{X_{y}}
for the restriction of
\lambda
to a fiber. For subsets
U\subseteq X
,
V\subseteq Y
we put
U|_{V}\coloneqq U\cap f^{-1}(V)
.

A subset
B
of a manifold
M
is a
codimension zero submanifold with corners
if for every point
p\in B
there is a coordinate chart
\varphi\colon U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of
M
around
p
such that
\varphi(B\cap U)
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geqslant 0}^{k}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d-k}
for some
k\geqslant 0
. A
manifold with corners
is a topological space which embeds into a smooth manifold as a codimension zero manifold with corners. This definition is compatible with the one given in
[
BS73
, Appendice]
by Proposition 3.1 loc. cit. We say that a map (or a form) is smooth on
B
if it extends to a smooth map (or form) on some neighborhood of
B
in the ambient manifold
M
.

For example, the closure of each
A_{i}^{\circ}
in Figure
2(b)
is a codimension zero submanifold with corners of
\partial A
, cf. Proposition
7.4
. Another example is the total space of the Milnor fibration

(67)

f\colon\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*},

where
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a holomorphic function with an isolated critical point at
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Given a smooth map
f\colon M\longrightarrow B
between manifolds with corners, we split the boundary
\partial M
as:

\partial M=\partial_{\mathrm{vert}}M\cup\partial_{\mathrm{hor}}M,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\partial_{\mathrm{vert}}M=f^{-1}(\partial B)\cap\partial M\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{\mathrm{hor}}M=\overline{\partial M\setminus\partial_{\mathrm{vert}}M}.

We say that
f
is a
fibration
if it is smoothly locally trivial, i.e. every
b\in B
has a neighborhood
U
and a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon U\times f^{-1}(p)\longrightarrow f^{-1}(U)
such that
f\circ\varphi=\mathrm{pr}_{U}
. By the Ehressmann lemma, see e.g.
[
CMLS20
, 6.2.10]
, a proper map
f
is a fibration if both
f
and
f|_{\partial_{\mathrm{hor}}M}
are surjective and submersive.

Let
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
be a
1
-form. The pair
(f,\lambda)
is called a
Liouville fibration
if
f\colon M\longrightarrow B
is a fibration and
(M_{b},\lambda|_{M_{b}})
is a Liouville domain for each
b\in B
, see Section
2.2
. The similar notion of a
symplectic fibration
is defined replacing
\lambda
by a closed, fiberwise symplectic form
\omega
, see Section
2.2
. A Liouville fibration induces a symplectic fibration by taking
\omega=d\lambda
.

Note that in the definition of a symplectic fibration we require that the form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is
closed
, and consider it a part of the data. This definition, while convenient for our purposes, is
not
a standard one. For example, in
[
Oh15
, §4.2]
, what we call a symplectic fibration is called a
Hamiltonian fibration
with a choice of a coupling form, cf.
[
MS17
, §6.4]
. We impose this more restrictive condition since it holds in all our applications, and guarantees that the flows of symplectic lifts are symplectomorphisms.

5.3.
Definition of a symplectic monodromy

In this section we will modify the Milnor fibration (
67
) so that its monodromy becomes a compactly supported symplectomorphism. Note that such a modification is needed: symplectomorphisms preserve volume, and there is no reason why two fibers of (
67
) should have the same volume. The following notion, introduced by McLean in
[
McL19
, Definition 3.12]
, abstracts the properties of symplectic monodromy that we need.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
(M,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain. We say that
(M,\lambda,\phi)
is an
abstract contact open book
if
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
is a diffeomorphism that is
compactly supported
, i.e. satisfies
\phi|_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{C}
for some neighborhood
C
of
\partial M
, and
exact
, that is:

(68)

\phi^{*}\lambda-\lambda=-d{a}

for some function
{a}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, called
action
of
\phi
. Note that the formula (
68
) defines
{a}
uniquely, up to an additive constant. Moreover, it implies that
\phi
is a symplectomorphism.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold, let
f\colon M\longrightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth map, and let
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
be a
1
-form such that
(f,\lambda)
is a Liouville fibration. Let
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
be a diffeomorphism which preserves each fiber of
f
and is equal to the identity close to
\partial_{\mathrm{hor}}M
. Put
\phi_{t}=\phi|_{M_{t}}
, see Notation
5.1
. If for every
t\in[0,1]
the triple
(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})
is an abstract contact open book, we say that the family
\{(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is an
isotopy of abstract contact open books
. In this case, we say that the abstract contact open books
(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})
are
isotopic
, and write
(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})\sim(M_{s},\lambda_{s},\phi_{s})
for
t,s\in[0,1]
.

Clearly, if
(M,\lambda,\phi)
is an abstract contact open book, then so is
(M,\lambda,\phi^{m})
for any iterate
\phi^{m}
of
\phi
; and if
(M_{0},\lambda_{0},\phi_{0})\sim(M_{1},\lambda_{1},\phi_{1})
then
(M_{0},\lambda_{0},\phi_{0}^{m})\sim(M_{1},\lambda_{1},\phi_{1}^{m})
for every
m\geqslant 1
.

In order to turn monodromies into abstract open books and compare their isotopy classes, we will need the following notion.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow B\times P
be a fibration from a manifold with corners to a product of manifolds with boundary. Fix
b_{0}\in B
and let
M_{b_{0}}\coloneqq(\mathrm{pr}_{B}\circ f)^{-1}(b_{0})
, see Notation
5.1
.
A
P
-fiberwise trivialization
of
f
is a diffeomorphism

(69)

\psi\colon M_{b_{0}}\times B\longrightarrow M

such that for all points
x\in M_{b_{0}}
, and all
b\in B
, we have

f(\psi(x,b))=(b,\mathrm{pr}_{P}(f(x))).

A
P
-fiberwise collar trivialization
of
f
is an open neighborhood
C
of
\partial_{\mathrm{hor}}M
in
M
, together with a
P
-fiberwise trivialization of
f|_{C}
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be a
2
-form such that
(f,\omega)
is a symplectic fibration. A
P
-fiberwise trivialization (
69
) is called
(B\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic
if for any
(b,p)\in B\times P
we have

(70)

\psi^{*}(\omega_{b,p})=\omega_{b_{0},p},

where
\omega_{b,p}
is the restriction of
\omega
to the fiber
M_{b,p}=f^{-1}(b,p)
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be such that
(f,\omega)
is a symplectic fibration. A
symplectic monodromy trivialization
is a smooth map
\Phi:M_{1}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M
such that
f(\Phi(x,t))=e^{2\pi\imath t}
for all
(x,t)\in M\times[0,1]
, and such that for all
t\in[0,1]
, the restriction
\Phi_{t}:=\Phi|_{M_{1}\times\{t\}}
defines a symplectomorphism from
(M_{1},\omega_{1})
to
(M_{\theta},\omega_{\theta})
, where
\theta=e^{2\pi\imath t}
,
\omega_{\theta}=\omega|_{M_{\theta}}
, and
\Phi_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{M_{1}}
. The corresponding
symplectic monodromy
is the symplectomorphism
\phi\coloneqq\Phi_{1}
.

Assume that
\omega=d\lambda
, and that
(f,\lambda)
is a Liouville fibration. Fix an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization
\psi\colon C\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow M
of
f
. A
symplectic monodromy trivialization relative to
\psi
is a symplectic monodromy trivialization
\Phi
satisfying
\Phi(x,t)=\psi(x,e^{2\pi\imath t})
for all
(x,t)\in C\times[0,1]
. A
symplectic monodromy relative to
\psi
is the symplectomorphism
\phi:=\Phi_{1}
for a symplectic monodromy trivialization relative to
\psi
. Observe that in this case
\phi
is the identity close to the boundary
\partial M_{1}
, so if
\phi
is exact, then
(M_{1},\lambda_{1},\phi)
is an abstract contact open book.

Remark 5.5
.

Fix
f\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
such that
(f,\omega)
is a symplectic fibration. If a symplectic monodromy exits, then it is unique as an element of the group of symplectomorphisms up to a symplectic isotopy. Indeed,
given two symplectic monodromy trivializations
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
, the needed isotopy is
\Phi^{\prime}_{t}{\circ}\Phi_{t}^{-1}{\circ}\Phi_{1}
.

Fix
f\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that
(f,\lambda)
is a Liouville fibration. Fix an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization
\psi
of
f
. Then as before, if a symplectic monodromy relative to
\psi
exists, it is unique in the group of compactly supported symplectomorphisms of
\partial M_{1}
, up to a compactly supported symplectic isotopy. In particular, if every symplectic monodromy
\phi
relative to
\psi
is exact, then the isotopy class of abstract contact open books
(M_{1},\lambda_{1},\phi)
does not depend on the choice of
\phi
.

We now distinguish a class of
cohomologically trivial
collar trivializations. In Proposition
5.7
we will use this technical condition to construct symplectic monodromy via a Moser argument.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow B\times P
as in Definition
5.3
and let
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
be such that
(f,\lambda)
is a Liouville fibration. Put
\omega=d\lambda
. Assume that we are given a
P
-fiberwise collar trivialization

\psi\colon C_{b_{0}}\times B\longrightarrow C,

that is
(B\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic, i.e. satisfies condition (
70
). For any
b\in B
choose an open neighborhood
U_{b}
of
b
in
B
and a
P
-fiberwise local trivialization

\Theta:M_{b}\times U_{b}\longrightarrow f^{-1}(U_{b}\times P)

which is induced by
\psi
on the collar
C
, that is, such that for any
x\in M_{b}\cap C
and any
b^{\prime}\in U_{b}
we have

\Theta(x,b^{\prime})=\psi(\mathrm{pr}_{C_{b_{0}}}(\psi^{-1}(x)),b^{\prime}).

For every
p\in P
the form
\Theta^{*}\omega
can be interpreted as a family of symplectic forms on the fiber
M_{b,p}
parametrized by
U_{b}
; for any
b^{\prime}\in U_{b}
, denote by
(\Theta^{*}\omega)_{b^{\prime},p}\in\Omega^{2}(M_{b,p})
the corresponding form.
Since the collar trivialization
\psi
is
(B\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic, for every
b,b^{\prime}\in U_{b}
we have the equality of restrictions

(\Theta^{*}\omega)_{b,p}|_{C_{b}}=(\Theta^{*}\omega)_{b^{\prime},p}|_{C_{b}}
,
hence the difference

(71)

\eta^{\Theta}_{b,b^{\prime};p}\coloneqq(\Theta^{*}\omega)_{b,p}-(\Theta^{*}\omega)_{b^{\prime},p}

vanishes in a neighborhood of the boundary
\partial M_{b,p}
. Thus we obtain a relative cohomology class
[\eta^{\Theta}_{b,b^{\prime};p}]\in H^{2}(M_{b,p},\partial M_{b,p};{\mathbb{R}})
. We claim that this cohomology class does not depend on the choice of the trivialization
\Theta
, and hence denote it by
[\eta_{b,b^{\prime};p}]
.

To prove the claim, we choose any class
[z]\in H_{2}(M_{b,p},\partial M_{b,p};{\mathbb{R}})
. We have
\eta^{\Theta}_{b,b^{\prime};p}(z)=\omega(\Theta(z,b))-\omega(\Theta(z,b^{\prime}))
. Hence, if
\Theta^{\prime}
is a different trivialization we have

\eta^{\Theta}_{b,b^{\prime};p}(z)-\eta^{\Theta^{\prime}}_{b,b^{\prime};p}(z)=\omega(\Theta(z,b)-\Theta^{\prime}(z,b))+\omega(-\Theta(z,b^{\prime})+\Theta^{\prime}(z,b^{\prime})).

Since
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
coincide close to
\partial M_{p,b}
, the chain
\Theta(z,b)-\Theta^{\prime}(z,b)
is closed. Therefore, since
\omega
is exact we have the vanishing
\omega(\Theta(z,b)-\Theta^{\prime}(z,b))=0
. The second summand vanishes for the same reasons. This proves the claim.

Definition 5.6
.

Fix a Liouville fibration
(f,\lambda)
and a
(B\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization
\psi
as in the discussion above. Fix
p\in P
. We say that
(f,\lambda)
is
cohomologically trivial with respect to
\psi
at
p
if for every
b\in B
and a trivialization
\Theta
as above, the cohomology class
[\eta_{p;b,b^{\prime}}]
vanishes in
H^{2}(M_{b,p},\partial M_{b,p};{\mathbb{R}})
for any
b^{\prime}\in U_{b}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
be as in Definition
5.3
and let
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
be such that
(f,\lambda)
is a Liouville fibration. Let
\psi\colon C_{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow C
be its
P
-fiberwise collar trivialization which is
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic.
Assume that
(f,\lambda)
is cohomologically trivial with respect to
\psi
at every point
p\in P
, see Definition
5.6
. Then there exists an open covering
\{Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
P
, and, for each
i\in I
, a smooth map

\Theta^{i}:M_{1}|_{Q_{i}}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M|_{Q_{i}},

such that for every
(x,t)\in M_{1}|_{Q_{i}}\times[0,1]
we have
(\mathrm{pr}_{P}\circ f\circ\Theta^{i})(x,t)=(\mathrm{pr}_{P}\circ f)(x)
, and for every
p\in Q_{i}
the restriction
\Theta^{i}|_{M_{1,p}\times[0,1]}
is a symplectic monodromy trivialization relative to the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization
\psi|_{C_{1,p}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
.

For
i\in I
and
p\in Q_{i}
, denote by
\phi_{p}^{i}:M_{1,p}\longrightarrow M_{1,p}
the associated symplectic monodromy. Assume that
P
is connected, and for every
i\in I
,
p\in Q_{i}
the symplectomorphism
\phi^{i}_{p}
is exact. Then for every
i\in I
,
p\in Q_{i}
, the triple
(M_{1,p},\lambda_{1,p},\phi_{p}^{i})
is an abstract contact open book, whose isotopy type does not depend on the choice of
i\in I
,
p\in Q_{i}
.

For the first assertion, we will use a parametrized Moser argument. Put
\omega=d\lambda
.

Since
f
is a fibration and admits a
P
-fiberwise collar trivialization
\psi
, there exists a smooth map

\Theta^{\prime}:M_{1}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M

such that we have
(\mathrm{pr}_{P}\circ f\circ\Theta^{\prime})(x,t)=(\mathrm{pr}_{P}\circ f)(x)
for any
(x,t)\in M_{1}\times[0,1]
, and
\Theta^{\prime}(x,t)=\psi(x,e^{2\pi\imath t})
for any
(x,t)\in C\cap M_{1}\times[0,1]
. We view the pullback
\omega^{\prime}:=(\Theta^{\prime})^{*}\omega
as a family of closed
2
-forms in
\Omega^{2}(M_{1})
parametrized by
t\in[0,1]
which are symplectic fiberwise over
P
. We denote by
\omega^{\prime}_{t}
the restriction of
\omega^{\prime}
to
M_{1}\times\{t\}
. Since
\psi
is
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic,
\Theta^{\prime}
can be chosen so that the restriction
\omega^{\prime}_{t}|_{C_{1,p}}
is independent of
t
for any
p\in P
. We now follow the strategy of the proof of
[
MS17
, Theorem 3.2.4]
, taking care of the fact that our construction is parametrized over
P
and compatible with the collar trivialization.

Since the assertion is local in the base
P
, we may assume that
M_{1}
is of the form
M_{1}=P\times N_{1}
. Then we can view
\omega^{\prime}_{t}
as a family of closed forms
\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}\in\Omega^{2}(N_{1})
which are independent of
t
near
\partial N_{1}
, for each fixed
p\in P
. Since
(f,\lambda)
is cohomologically trivial with respect to
\psi
, the relative cohomology class
[\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}-\omega^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},p}]
vanishes in
H^{2}(N_{1},\partial N_{1},{\mathbb{R}})
for every fixed
p\in P
, and every
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. Therefore, the class
[\frac{d}{dt}\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}]
vanishes in the same group. This means that for any
p\in P
and any
t\in[0,1]
there exists a
1
-form
\sigma_{t,p}\in\Omega^{1}(N_{1})
which vanishes near
\partial N_{1}
and satisfies
d\sigma_{t,p}=\frac{d}{dt}\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}
.

We will produce such a family of
\sigma_{t,p}
smoothly depending on
t
and
p
. For this, out of the two methods suggested in the proof of
[
MS17
, Theorem 3.2.4]
to construct
\sigma_{t,p}
, we adapt the first one, mimicking the proof of the compactly supported Mayer-Vietoris sequence in de Rham cohomology.

To be precise: denote by
\Omega_{c}^{*}(N_{1}\times[0,1]\times P)|_{[0,1]\times P}
the chain complex formed by families
\xi_{t,p}
of compactly supported forms in
N_{1}\setminus\partial N_{1}
smoothly depending on
(t,p)
. Let
H_{c}^{*}(N_{1}\times[0,1]\times P)|_{[0,1]\times P}
be its cohomology. Define a homomorphism

\gamma\colon H_{c}^{*}(N_{1}\times[0,1]\times P)|_{[0,1]\times P}\longrightarrow H_{c}^{*}(N_{1}\setminus\partial N_{1})\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,1]\times P)

as follows: choose a basis
[\Gamma_{1}],\dots,[\Gamma_{r}]
of
H_{*}(N_{1},\partial N_{1})
and define

\gamma\left([\xi_{t,p}]\right)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(\int_{\Gamma_{j}}\xi_{t,p}\right)\cdot\left[\Gamma_{j}\right]^{*},

where
\{[\Gamma_{j}]^{*}\}_{j=1}^{r}
is the basis of
H_{c}^{*}(N_{1}\setminus\partial N_{1})
dual to
\{[\Gamma_{j}]\}_{j=1}^{r}
.

In order to produce the needed family it is enough to prove that
\gamma
is an isomorphism. This is proved by induction on the minimal number of open subsets of a good cover of
N_{1}
, where a good cover is a cover by contractible open subsets such that any finite intersection is contractible. Existence of good covers is established in
[
BT82
, Theorem 5.1]
. The initial step of the induction follows the proof of the compactly supported Poincaré Lemma
[
BT82
, Propositions 4.6, 4.7]
. The inductive step follows the Mayer-Vietoris argument of
[
BT82
, §I.5]
.

After the smooth family
\sigma_{t,p}
has been constructed, applying Moser trick to the family
\sigma_{t,p}
as in
[
MS17
, §3.2]
we produce a family of diffeomorphisms
\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{t,p}\colon N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{1}
that are equal to the identity near
\partial N_{1}
and such that
(\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{t,p})^{*}\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}=\omega^{\prime}_{0,p}
for any
t\in[0,1]
and
p\in P
. Then the required smooth map
\Theta
is defined by
\Theta_{t,p}:=\Theta^{\prime}_{t,p}{\circ}\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{t,p}
.

For the second assertion, we note that for any
p\in Q_{i}
the monodromy diffeomorphism
\phi_{p}^{i}
induced by
\Theta^{i}
is compactly supported, so if it is exact, then
(M_{1,p},\lambda_{1,p},\phi_{p}^{i})
is an abstract contact open book. Clearly,
(M_{1,p},\lambda_{1,p},\phi_{p}^{i})\sim(M_{1,q},\lambda_{1,q},\phi_{q}^{i})
if
p,q\in Q_{i}
and
Q_{i}
is connected. If
p\in Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}
then since
\phi_{p}^{i}
and
\phi_{p}^{j}
are both symplectic monodromies relative to the same collar trivialization, we have
(M_{1,p},\lambda_{1,p},\phi_{p}^{i})\sim(M_{1,p},\lambda_{1,p},\phi_{p}^{j})
by Remark
5.5
.

∎

Definition 5.8
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow P\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
be a Liouville fibration, with a connected
P
, and let
\psi
be its fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization.
The abstract contact open book from Proposition
5.7
will be called the
monodromy abstract contact open book
of
f,\psi
. It is unique up to an isotopy, and exists if
\psi
is cohomologically trivial and the monodromy
\phi
is exact.

5.4.
Grading and Conley–Zehnder index

In order to define a grading of the Floer complex, we need to consider
graded
abstract contact open books. We now recall basic notions leading to their definition, following
[
McL19
, Appendix A]
.

5.4.1.
Graded principal bundles

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal cover
\widetilde{G}
, and let
p\colon F\longrightarrow M
be a principal
G
-bundle. A
grading
of
F
is a principal
\widetilde{G}
bundle
\widetilde{F}\longrightarrow M
together with an isomorphism
\alpha\colon\widetilde{F}\times_{\widetilde{G}}G\longrightarrow F
. Thus there is a natural covering map
\alpha^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{F}\longrightarrow F
. An isotopy between two gradings
\alpha_{0}
,
\alpha_{1}
is a principal
G
-bundle over
M\times[0,1]
, together with a grading which restricts to
\alpha_{i}
over
M\times\{i\}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Choose a base point
f\in F
. Then by
[
McL19
, Lemma A.3]
, there is a one-to-one correspondence

(72)

\begin{split}&\{\mbox{gradings of }p\colon F\longrightarrow M\}/_{\mbox{\tiny{isotopy}}}\longleftrightarrow\\
&\longleftrightarrow\{N\vartriangleleft\pi_{1}(F,f):p_{*}|_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(M,p(f))\mbox{ is an isomorphism}\}\end{split}

sending a grading
(\widetilde{p}\colon\widetilde{F}\longrightarrow M,\alpha)
to
\alpha^{\prime}_{*}(\pi_{1}(\widetilde{F},\widetilde{f}))\vartriangleleft\pi_{1}(F,f)
, where
\widetilde{f}\in(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}(f)
.

5.4.2.
Graded symplectic manifolds

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold of dimension
2d
. A
grading
of
M
is a grading of its symplectic frame bundle
\operatorname{Fr}(M)\longrightarrow M
, which is a principal
\operatorname{Sp}(2d)
-bundle, see
[
McL19
, Definition A.6]
. Choose an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
, so that
TM
becomes a complex vector bundle of rank
d
. Let
K^{*}_{M}\coloneqq\Lambda^{d}TM
be the corresponding anti-canonical bundle. The natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}(2d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det}

induce isomorphisms between fundamental groups, hence (
72
) gives a one-to-one correspondence between gradings of
M
and gradings of the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-bundle of
K^{*}_{M}
. By
[
McL19
, Lemma A.5]
, the latter are in one-to-one correspondence with homotopy classes of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
trivializations of
K^{*}_{M}
, or, equivalently, of
K_{M}\coloneqq\Lambda^{d}T^{*}M
. Recall that
K_{M}
is a complex line bundle, so its
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
trivializations are given by nonvanishing
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
sections. To summarize, we have a one-to-one correspondence

(73)

\{\mbox{gradings of }M\}/_{\mbox{\tiny{isotopy}}}\longleftrightarrow\{\mbox{nonvanishing smooth sections of }K_{M}\}/_{\mbox{\tiny{homotopy}}}.

Likewise, given a symplectic fibration
M\longrightarrow P
with a fiberwise symplectic form
\omega
and an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
of the relative tangent bundle, let
F\longrightarrow M
be the vertical symplectic frame bundle, and let
K_{\mathrm{vert}}
be the relative canonical bundle. We have a one-to-one correspondence

(74)

\{\mbox{gradings of }F\}/_{\mbox{\tiny{isotopy}}}\longleftrightarrow\{\mbox{nonvanishing smooth sections of }K_{\mathrm{vert}}\}/_{\mbox{\tiny{homotopy}}}.

5.4.3.
Graded symplectomorphisms and graded abstract contact open books

Let
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
be a symplectomorphism. Let
\beta\colon\operatorname{Fr}(M)\longrightarrow\phi^{*}\operatorname{Fr}(M)
be the induced automorphism of the symplectic frame bundle. A
grading
of
\phi
is an isomorphism
\widetilde{\beta}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)\longrightarrow\phi^{*}\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)
which lifts
\beta
via the grading isomorphism
\alpha\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)\times_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(2d)}\operatorname{Sp}(2d)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Fr}(M)
, i.e. satisfies
\widetilde{\beta}\times_{\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(2d)}\operatorname{Sp}(2d)=\beta
.

A
grading
of an abstract contact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi)
is a grading of the symplectic manifold
(M,d\lambda)
and a compatible grading of the symplectomorphism
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
. An isotopy of abstract contact open books
\{(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is
graded
if there is a family
\widetilde{\beta_{t}}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M_{t})\longrightarrow\phi_{t}^{*}\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M_{t})
of gradings of
\phi_{t}
depending smoothly on
t
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
f\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
be a symplectic fibration with a closed fiberwise symplectic form
\omega
, and let
F\longrightarrow M
be the vertical symplectic frame bundle. Let
\Phi\colon M_{1}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M
be a map that preserves fibers over
P
, and is a symplectic monodromy trivialization fiberwise over
P
. Any grading
\widetilde{F}\longrightarrow M
of
F
induces a grading
\widetilde{\beta}_{p}
of the symplectic monodromy
\phi_{p}\colon M_{1,p}\longrightarrow M_{1,p}
defined by
\Phi
, which depends smoothly on
p
.

By the symplectic monodromy construction, given any symplectic monodromy
\phi\colon M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{1}
there exists a closed fiberwise symplectic form
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
such that
\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}=\omega_{t,p}
for all
(t,p)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
and
\phi
is the time
1
flow of the lifting of the unit vector field
(0,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta})
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
by the symplectic connection associated with
\omega^{\prime}
. Indeed, let
\overline{M}
be the mapping cylinder of
\phi
, i.e.
\overline{M}=M_{1}\times[0,1]/_{\sim}
with
(x,1)\sim(\phi(x),0)
. We have an induced map
\overline{M}\ni(x,t)\mapsto(e^{2\pi\imath t},\mathrm{pr}_{P}\circ f(x))\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
, and a diffeomorphism
\overline{\Phi}\colon\overline{M}\ni(x,t)\mapsto\Phi(x,t)\in M
. Since
\phi^{*}\omega_{1}=\omega_{1}
, the form
\mathrm{pr}_{M_{1}}^{*}\omega_{1}
descends to a fiberwise symplectic form
\overline{\omega}
on
\overline{M}
. Put
\omega^{\prime}=(\overline{\Phi}^{-1})^{*}\overline{\omega}
. Since
\Phi
is fiberwise a symplectic monodromy trivialization, its restrictions
\Phi_{t,p}\colon M_{1,p}\longrightarrow M_{t,p}
are symplectomorphisms. It follows that
\omega^{\prime}_{t,p}=\omega_{t,p}
. The symplectic lift of
(0,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta})
from
P\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
to
M
is
\overline{\Phi}_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
, and its time one flow is
\phi
, as needed.

Since the statement only concerns fibers we may assume
\omega=\omega^{\prime}
for the rest of the proof. The symplectic connection defines a lift
\mathcal{X}
of the vector field
(0,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta})
to
F
, and the automorphism
\beta\colon F\longrightarrow F
induced by
\phi
is the time
1
flow of
\mathcal{X}
. Since
\widetilde{F}
is a covering space of
F
, there is a unique lifting of
\mathcal{X}
to a vector field
\mathcal{Y}
in
\widetilde{F}
, and
\widetilde{\beta}
is the time
1
flow of
\mathcal{Y}
.

∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Given a symplectic fibration
M\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and a grading
\widetilde{F}\longrightarrow M
of the vertical symplectic frame bundle, any symplectic monodromy inherits a grading, and the graded symplectic monodromy is unique up to graded isotopy. Furthermore, in the situation of Proposition
5.7
, a grading of the vertical symplectic frame bundle of
M\longrightarrow P\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
induces a grading of each symplectic monodromy
\phi_{p}
, and of each isotopy connecting them.

5.4.4.
Conley–Zehnder index

Let
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M

be a graded symplectomorphism. Let
x\in M
be a fixed point of
\phi
. The grading
\widetilde{\beta}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)\longrightarrow\phi^{*}\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)
sends the fiber
\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{x}
over
x
to itself. Its restriction
\widetilde{\beta}_{x}
is thus an element of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(2d)
, well defined up to a conjugation. It can be viewed as a homotopy class (relative to the ends) of a path
\gamma
of symplectic matrices, starting from
\mathrm{id}
; well defined up to a conjugation. The
Conley–Zehnder index of
x
, denoted by
\operatorname{CZ}_{\phi}(x)
, is the Maslov index of
\gamma
, see
[
RS93
, §4]
for its definition. This number depends only on the conjugacy class of this path. Indeed, by
[
RS93
, Theorem 4.1]
the Maslov index is additive under concatenation of paths, so it is invariant under conjugation. In Section
6
we will need the following, known result.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a graded symplectic manifold of dimension
2d
. Let
H\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a time-independent Hamiltonian, and let
\psi^{H}_{1}\colon M\longrightarrow M
be its time-one flow, see Section
5.1
. Let
x
be a Morse point of
H
, of Morse index
\operatorname{Ind}(x)
. Assume furthermore that
H
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-small at
x
, more explicitly,
||\operatorname{Hess}_{x}(H)||<2\pi
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The point
x
is a fixed point of
\psi^{H}_{1}
of Conley–Zehnder index
d-\operatorname{Ind}(x)
.

(b)

Let
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
be a graded symplectomorphism such that
x
is a fixed point of
\phi
. Then
x
is a fixed point of
\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}
, of Conley–Zehnder index
\operatorname{CZ}_{\phi}(x)+d-\operatorname{Ind}(x)
.

Part
(a)
is proved in
[
AD14
, Corollary 7.2.2]
. Part
(b)
follows from
(a)
since the Maslov index of a concatenation of paths is a sum of their Maslov indices
[
RS93
, Theorem 4.1]
.

∎

5.5.
Constructing symplectic monodromies

The aim of this section is to give a general construction of symplectic monodromy for families of degenerations of complex manifolds over a punctured disc. Later, we will apply it to a
\mu
-constant family of isolated hypersurface singularities, see Example
5.23
; and a log resolution of a single isolated hypersurface singularity, see Section
7.2
. Now, we work in the following general Setting
5.12
.

Setting 5.12

(see Figure
5
)
.

Let
X
be a complex variety (possibly singular). Let
P
be a compact domain in a manifold, and let
(f_{p})_{p\in P}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a smooth family of regular maps. Put

F:X\times P\ni(x,p)\mapsto(f_{p}(x),p)\in\mathbb{C}\times P.

Assume that for some
\delta>0
, the restriction of
F
over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P
is a submersion. In particular, all fibers
f_{p}^{-1}(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
,
p\in P
are smooth and contained in the smooth part
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
of
X
. Let
U\subseteq X
be an open subset whose closure is compact and whose boundary is a manifold, and such that for every
p\in P
, we have
f_{p}^{-1}(0)\pitchfork\partial\overline{U}
. Assume that we have open subsets
V,W\subseteq U
with the same transversality property, and such that
\overline{V}\subset W\subset\overline{W}\subset U
.
Put
C^{\prime}=((\overline{U}\setminus V)\times P)\cap F^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P)
. As usual, we write
C^{\prime}_{z,p}=(\overline{U}\setminus V)\cap f_{p}^{-1}(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
,
p\in P
, see Notation
5.1
.

Assume that
\mathrm{pr}_{X}(C^{\prime})\subseteq X^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and that the restriction
F|_{C^{\prime}}\colon C^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P
is a submersion. Thus for every
p\in P
, the fiber
C^{\prime}_{0,p}
is smooth and contained in
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Furthermore, we assume that the restriction of
F
gives a locally trivial fibration of pairs

(75)

F\colon(C^{\prime},(\partial\overline{W}\times P)\cap C^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P.

By Ehressmann Lemma, the latter condition can always be achieved by shrinking
\delta>0
. Now
for each
(z,p)\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P
, each connected component of
C^{\prime}_{z,p}\setminus\partial\overline{W}
cannot meet
\partial\overline{V}
and
\partial\overline{U}
at the same time.

Let
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(C^{\prime}\cup F^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P))
be a
1
-form whose restriction to each collar
C^{\prime}_{0,p}
and to each smooth fiber
f_{p}^{-1}(z)\cap\overline{U}
for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
,
p\in P
is Liouville. Furthermore, we assume that for some
p_{0}\in P
, the Liouville vector field of
\lambda|_{C_{0,p_{0}}^{\prime}}
points outwards
f^{-1}_{p_{0}}(0)\cap\partial\overline{W}
.

\partial\overline{V}\times P
\partial\overline{W}\times P
\partial\overline{U}\times P
f_{p_{0}}
\delta
f_{p}
\delta
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
C
C_{0,p_{0}}
N_{\delta,p_{0}}
\overline{W}_{\delta,p}
C^{\prime}
P
p_{0}
p\neq p_{0}

Figure 5
.
Producing collar trivializations in Setting
5.12
.

The reader may have in mind the following example: the ambient space is
X=\mathbb{C}^{n}
, the parameter space
P
is, say,
\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
; the map
F=(f_{t},t)\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}_{\eta}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
is a family of holomorphic functions with isolated singularities and
V\subset W\subset U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
are concentric balls, such that the only critical value of
f_{t}
is
0
, and
f_{t}^{-1}(0)
is smooth in
\overline{U}\setminus V
; and
\lambda
is the standard form
\lambda=\pi\cdot\sum_{i}r_{i}^{2}\,d\theta_{i}
from Example
2.1
.

The remaining part of Section
5.5
is organized as follows. In Section
5.5.1
we show how to adjust
\partial W
within
U\setminus V
so that
F|_{W\times P}
becomes a Liouville fibration over
\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\delta}\times P
, with a cohomologically trivial collar trivialization, see Definitions
5.3
and
5.6
. This may require shrinking
\delta>0
or the parameter space
P
. Next, in Section
5.5.2
we observe that under a mild assumption (
80
), the monodromy of this Liouville fibration, see Definition
5.4
, makes the fibers into isotopic abstract contact open books, see Definition
5.2
. Next, in Section
5.5.3
, we endow those abstract contact open books with a grading, under another mild assumption (
82
). In Remarks
5.16
and
5.17
we comment that these additional assumptions (
80
) and (
82
) are satisfied in most interesting cases. Eventually, in Section
5.5.5
we extend each fibration to the A’Campo space constructed in Section
3
, and, under yet another assumption (
84
), we use forms introduced in Section
4
to get isotopic abstract contact open books at radius zero. In Section
5.5.6
we gather basic applications of this construction which will be used later.

5.5.1.
Constructing a Liouville fibration with a collar trivialization in Setting
5.12

We keep the notation and assumptions of Setting
5.12
.
Let
(r,\theta)
be the polar coordinates in the disc
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
. The radial vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
in
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
does not extend to a smooth vector field at the origin, but it does extend if restricted to a ray
R_{\theta}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
of constant argument
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{\theta}
be the symplectic lift of
(\frac{\partial}{\partial r})|_{R_{\theta}}
to
F^{-1}(R_{\theta}\times P)\cap C^{\prime}
.

Let
C_{0}
be a compact neighborhood of
F^{-1}(\{0\}\times P)\cap(\partial\overline{W}\times P)
in
F^{-1}(\{0\}\times P)\cap(\overline{W}\setminus\overline{V}\times P)
. Its slice
C_{0,p}
over
p\in P
is a compact collar neighborhood of
\partial\overline{W}\cap f_{p}^{-1}(0)
in
\overline{W}\cap f_{p}^{-1}(0)
. Let
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
be a compact subdomain such that all integral curves of
\mathcal{X}_{\theta}
with initial points in
C_{0}\cap F^{-1}(\{0\}\times P^{\prime})
exist for all times
t\in[0,\delta]
and are contained in
C^{\prime}
; one can always take
P^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Then after shrinking
P
around
P^{\prime}
, or after shrinking
\delta>0
in case
P^{\prime}=\emptyset
, we get the same property for
C_{0}\cap F^{-1}(\{0\}\times P)
. Here we use compactness of
C_{0}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
in the first case; and of
C_{0}
and
P
in the second case. This way, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
we obtain a smooth flow

\varphi_{\theta}\colon C_{0}\times[0,\delta]\ni((x,p),t)\mapsto(\varphi_{t\cdot\theta,p}(x),p)\in X\times P.

The above formula defines, for each
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
,
p\in P
, a map
\varphi_{z,p}\colon C_{0,p}\longrightarrow C_{z,p}^{\prime}
. More explicitly, we put
\varphi_{0,p}=\mathrm{id}_{C_{0,p}}
and
\varphi_{z,p}(x)=\varphi_{z/|z|}((x,p),|z|)
for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. Since the flow
\varphi_{\theta}
is defined by a symplectic connection, each map
\varphi_{z,p}
is a symplectomorphism onto its image. Put

C\coloneqq\bigcup_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi_{\theta}(C_{0}\times[0,\delta]),\quad\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C\coloneqq\bigcup_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi_{\theta}((C_{0}\cap(\partial\overline{W}\times P))\times[0,\delta])

Note that
C_{0}=F^{-1}(\{0\}\times P)\cap C
. Now
F|_{C}\colon C\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P
is a symplectic fibration, and

(76)

\psi_{0}\colon C_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\ni((x,p),z)\mapsto(\varphi_{z,p}(x),p)\in C

is a
P
-fiberwise trivialization of
F|_{C}
which is
(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic.
The map

(77)

F\colon(C^{\prime},\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C)\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P

is a locally trivial fibration of pairs, which coincides with (
75
) over
\{0\}\times P
. In particular, it also has the property that for each
(z,p)\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P
, each connected component of
C^{\prime}_{z,p}\setminus\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C_{z,p}
cannot meet
\partial\overline{V}
and
\partial\overline{U}
at the same time. We call those meeting
\partial\overline{V}
the
inner components of the fibration (
77
) over
(z,p)
.
Define

(78)

F|_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P

as the symplectic fibration whose fiber over
(z,p)
is the union of
\overline{V}_{z,p}
and the inner components of the fibration (
77
) over
(z,p)
.
Note that, by Ehresmann Lemma, the fiber
N_{z,p}=F^{-1}(z,p)\cap N
is diffeomorphic to
\overline{W}_{z,p}\coloneqq f_{p}^{-1}(z)\cap\overline{W}
, and its diffeomorphism type does not depend on
(z,p)\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P
.

Using (
76
), we get a collar trivialization of (
78
), as follows. Fix a point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
, let
C_{z_{0}}=F^{-1}(\{z_{0}\}\times P)\cap C
, and let
\widehat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}\colon C_{z_{0}}\ni(x,p)\mapsto(\varphi_{z_{0},p}^{-1}(x),p)\in C_{0}
be the fiberwise identification of
C_{z_{0}}
with
C_{0}
via the trivialization (
76
). Now, the map

(79)

\psi\coloneqq\psi_{0}\circ(\widehat{\varphi}_{z_{0}}\times\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}})\colon C_{z_{0}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\longrightarrow C

is a
P
-fiberwise collar trivialization of (
78
), which is
(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P)
-fiberwise symplectic.

Lemma 5.13
.

The collar trivialization (
79
) is cohomologically trivial at every
p\in P
.

By Definition
5.6
, (
79
) is cohomologically trivial at
p\in P
if for every
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
, and every
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
sufficiently close to
z
, the class
