[\eta_{z,z^{\prime};p}]\in H^{2}(N_{z,p},\partial N_{z,p};\mathbb{R})
is zero. This class was defined in the formula (
71
), as follows. Let
U_{z}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
be a neighborhood of
z
such that the restriction
F|_{N\cap F^{-1}(U_{z}\times P)}
admits a
P
-fiberwise trivialization
\Theta
which agrees with
\psi
in the collar. For
z^{\prime}\in U_{z}
let
\Theta_{z^{\prime},p}\colon N_{z,p}\longrightarrow N_{z^{\prime},p}
be the corresponding map identifying the fibers. Then
[\eta_{z,z^{\prime};p}]
is the class of
\omega_{z,p}-\Theta_{z^{\prime},p}^{*}\omega_{z^{\prime},p}
.

We need to show that, for every
\Delta\in H_{2}(N_{z,p},\partial N_{z,p};\mathbb{R})
, the integral

\int_{\Delta}\omega_{z,p}-\Theta^{*}_{z^{\prime},p}\omega_{z^{\prime},p}

vanishes. By Stokes theorem, this is equivalent to vanishing of

\int_{\Gamma}\lambda_{z,p}-\Theta_{z^{\prime},p}^{*}\lambda_{z^{\prime},p}

for every
1
-cycle
\Gamma
on
\partial N_{z,p}
.

The condition that
\Theta
agrees with
\psi
in the collar means that
\Theta_{z^{\prime},p}|_{C_{z^{\prime},p}}=\psi_{z,p}\circ\psi^{-1}_{z^{\prime},p}
, where
\psi_{z,p}\colon C_{z_{0},p}\longrightarrow C_{z,p}
is the symplectomorphism induced by
\psi
. Using the formula (
79
) defining
\psi
, we see that
\psi_{z,p}=\varphi_{z,p}\circ\varphi_{z_{0},p}^{-1}
, so
\Theta_{z^{\prime},p}|_{C_{z^{\prime},p}}=\varphi_{z^{\prime},p}\circ\varphi_{z,p}^{-1}
. In consequence, to prove the lemma it is enough to show the vanishing of the integral
\int_{\Gamma}\lambda_{0,p}-\varphi_{z,p}^{*}\lambda_{z,p}
for every
1
-cycle
\Gamma
on
\partial\overline{W}_{0,p}
.

Fix such a
1
-cycle
\Gamma
, and an argument
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Consider a function
\tau\colon[0,\delta]\ni r\mapsto\int_{\Gamma}\lambda_{0,p}-\varphi_{r\theta,p}^{*}\lambda_{r\theta,p}
. We need to prove that
\tau\equiv 0
. Since
\varphi_{0,p}=\mathrm{id}_{\overline{W}_{0,p}}
, we have
\tau(0)=0
. It remains to prove that
\tau^{\prime}(r)=0
for all
r\in[0,\delta]
. Because
\varphi_{t\theta,p}
is a time
t
flow of
\mathcal{X}_{\theta}
, we have
\tau^{\prime}(r)=-\int_{\Gamma}\frac{d}{dt}\varphi_{t\theta,p}^{*}\lambda_{t\theta,p}|_{t=r}=-\int_{\varphi_{r\theta,p}(\Gamma)}L_{\mathcal{X}_{\theta}}\lambda_{r\theta,p}
. By Cartan formula we have
L_{\mathcal{X}_{\theta}}\lambda_{r\theta,p}=d(\lambda_{r\theta,p}(\mathcal{X}_{\theta}))+\omega_{r\theta,p}(\mathcal{X}_{\theta},\,\cdot\,)
. The restriction of
\omega_{r\theta,p}(\mathcal{X}_{\theta},\,\cdot\,)
to
\partial N_{r\theta,p}
is zero, because
\mathcal{X}_{\theta}
is
\omega
-orthogonal to the fibers of
F
. Hence the form
L_{\mathcal{X}_{\theta}}\lambda_{r\theta,p}
is exact, so its integral along a closed cycle is zero by Stokes theorem. Thus
\tau^{\prime}(r)=0
, as needed.

∎

Remark 5.14
.

Consider a special case when
W_{z,p}
is Stein manifold of dimension
n-1
. This happens e.g. if
W
is a ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, see Example
5.22
. For
n\geqslant 4
the group

H^{2}(N_{z,p},\partial N_{z,p})=H_{2n-4}(N_{z,p})=H_{2n-4}(W_{z,p})
vanishes, so the cohomological triviality proved in Lemma
5.13
holds automatically.

In case
n=2
(e.g. for plane curves), Lemma
5.13
reduces to the observation that all fibers
N_{z,p}
have the same symplectic area. The latter holds because, by construction, the integral curves of
-\mathcal{X}_{z/|z|}
starting in
N_{z,p}
do not escape the collar
C
, hence in time
|z|
they map
N_{z,p}
to the central fiber.

We now continue the general construction, and upgrade the symplectic fibration (
78
) to a Liouville one. Assume that there is a nonempty compact subdomain
P_{0}\subseteq P
, and a number
\delta_{0}\in[0,\delta]
, such that the Liouville vector field
X_{\lambda}
of
\lambda
points outwards
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C_{z,p}
for all
p\in P_{0}
and
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{0}}
. Recall that we have assumed it for
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C_{0,p_{0}}=f_{p_{0}}^{-1}(0)\cap\partial\overline{W}
, so we can always take
P_{0}=\{p_{0}\}
,
\delta_{0}=0
; nonetheless, sometimes other choices will be convenient, too. Since
P_{0}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\delta_{0}}
are compact, after possibly
shrinking
P
around
P_{0}
and
\delta>\delta_{0}
, we can assume that
X_{\lambda}
points outwards
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C_{z,p}
for all
(z,p)\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times P
. Now the restriction (
78
) is a Liouville fibration, with a cohomologically trivial collar trivialization (
79
).

Remark 5.15
.

In the language of
[
Sei03
, §1.1]
, cf.
[
Sei08
, Remark 15.2]
, the restriction
F|_{N}
is an
exact symplectic fibration
. If
X
is smooth then the restriction
F|_{\overline{N}}
, i.e.
F|_{N}
together with the singular fiber, is an
exact symplectic fibration with singularities
in the sense of
[
Sei08
, §15a]
.

5.5.2.
Constructing symplectic monodromy in Setting
5.12

Our next goal is to introduce a monodromy making the fibers of (
78
) into an abstract contact open books.

To this end, we fix a family of loops
\Upsilon
in
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. More precisely, we choose a domain
Q
and a smooth map
\Upsilon\colon Q\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. The reader may keep in mind the example
\Upsilon\colon(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni(r,\theta)\mapsto r\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. Define
N^{\prime}
as the fibered product of the Liouville fibration
N\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P
given by (
78
) and the map
\Upsilon\times\mathrm{id}_{P}\colon Q\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P
. Then the induced map
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
together with the pullback of
\lambda|_{N}
form a Liouville fibration, which inherits the fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization (
79
) constructed above. By Lemma
5.13
, the latter is cohomologically trivial.

By Proposition
5.7
, for every
q\in Q
,
p\in P
, and
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
we have a symplectic monodromy trivialization
\Theta_{q,p}\colon N^{\prime}_{q,\theta,p}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N_{q,p}^{\prime}
, see Definition
5.4
. Now, we assume that

(80)

\mbox{each associated symplectic monodromy }\phi_{q,\theta,p}\colon N^{\prime}_{q,\theta,p}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}_{q,\theta,p}\mbox{ is exact}.

If the condition (
80
) holds then each triple

(81)

(N^{\prime}_{q,\theta,p},\lambda_{q,\theta,p},\phi_{q,\theta,p})

is a
monodromy abstract contact open book
of
\Theta_{q,p}
, see Definition
5.8
. Its isotopy type does not depend on the choice of
q,\theta,p
. The Liouville domain
(N^{\prime}_{q,\theta,p},\lambda_{q,\theta,p})
is isomorphic to the fiber
(N_{z,p},\lambda_{z,p})
for
z=\Upsilon(q,\theta)
. Its underlying manifold
N_{z,p}\subseteq f_{p}^{-1}(z)
is diffeomorphic to
\overline{W}_{z,p}=f_{p}^{-1}(z)\cap\overline{W}
.

Remark 5.16
.

We now gather some natural examples when condition (
80
) is satisfied, so the construction above yields monodromy abstract contact open books (
81
).

(a)

Clearly, (
80
) holds if for some (hence, any)
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
,
p\in P
we have
H^{1}(\overline{W}_{z,p},\partial\overline{W}_{z,p};\mathbb{R})=0
.

(b)

Assume that
W_{z,p}
is a Stein manifold of dimension
n-1\geqslant 2
. Then by Lefschetz duality
H^{1}(\overline{W}_{z,p},\partial\overline{W}_{z,p})=H_{2n-3}(W_{z,p})=0
since
2n-3>n-1
, so condition (
80
) holds by
(a)
.

(c)

Let
L_{p}\coloneqq\operatorname{Int}(C_{p}\cup N_{p})\subseteq X^{\mathrm{reg}}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}f_{p}^{-1}(0)
be the
“link”
of
\operatorname{Sing}f_{p}^{-1}(0)\subseteq X
. We claim that if
H^{1}(L_{p};\mathbb{R})=0
then condition (
80
) holds.

Indeed, the symplectic monodromies
\phi_{q,\theta,p}
constructed above, together with
\mathrm{id}_{C_{p}}
, glue to a symplectomorphism
\phi\colon L_{p}\longrightarrow L_{p}
. By assumption, the closed form
\phi^{*}\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{p}
is exact. Hence its restricted pullbacks
\phi_{q,\theta,p}^{*}\lambda_{q,\theta,p}-\lambda_{q,\theta,p}
are exact, too, i.e. the condition (
80
) holds, as claimed.

(d)

Part
(c)
can be applied in the following situation. Let
X
be an algebraic surface with an isolated singularity, whose link
L
is a rational homology sphere. Take
U
small enough so that each
L_{p}
defined above is homotopically equivalent to
L
. Now, we have
H^{1}(L_{p};\mathbb{R})=0
, so condition (
80
) holds by
(c)
.

(e)

More generally, assume that
X
is an algebraic surface whose all singularities have rational homology sphere links, and that
\lambda_{X}
is defined everywhere on
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
: for example,
X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}
. Using
(d)
we first construct compactly supported, exact monodromies around each singularity of
X
and
f^{-1}(0)
as above; and then we extend them by identity to the remaining part of the fiber.

In case
P=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
, the same construction works for
f
replaced by
f\circ h
, where
h
is a log resolution restricting to an isomorphism away from the zero fiber and in the collar.

5.5.3.
Grading in Setting
5.12
.

Recall that under the assumptions of Setting
5.12
and (
80
), we have constructed monodromy abstract contact open books (
81
). Now, we endow them with a grading.

Fix
p\in P
. Let
T_{p,\mathrm{vert}}\coloneqq\ker(f_{p})_{*}\subseteq T(X\setminus f_{p}^{-1}(0))
be the vertical tangent bundle. Fix an almost complex structure
J_{p}
on
T_{p,\mathrm{vert}}
which is compatible with the fiberwise symplectic form
d\lambda_{p}
, and assume

(82)

c_{1}(T_{p,\mathrm{vert}}^{*})=0.

This first Chern class depends only on
d\lambda_{p}
, not on
J_{p}
, see
[
MS17
, Defintion 4.1.4]
.
Let
K_{p,\mathrm{vert}}
be the top exterior power of
T_{p,\mathrm{vert}}^{*}
. Condition (
82
) means that
K_{p,\mathrm{vert}}
admits a nonvanishing
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
section. By the correspondence (
74
), its pullback induces a grading of the vertical symplectic frame bundle of the Liouville fibration
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
. By Corollary
5.10
, this grading makes each triple (
81
) a graded abstract contact open book, whose graded isotopy class does not depend on the choice of
q,\theta
. Moreover, grading for one
p\in P
determines uniquely the grading for all
p\in P
.

Remark 5.17
.

Like in Remark
5.16
, we gather some examples where condition (
82
) holds, so the monodromy abstract contact open books (
81
) are naturally graded.

(a)

Clearly, (
82
) holds if for some (hence, any)
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
,
p\in P
we have
H^{2}(W_{z,p};\mathbb{R})=0
.

(b)

Assume that
d\lambda_{p}
is Kähler. Then we can take for
J_{p}
the standard complex structure. Denoting by
K_{X_{p}^{*}}
the canonical bundle of the complex manifold
X_{p}^{*}\coloneqq X\setminus f_{p}^{-1}(0)
, we get an isomorphism

(83)

K_{X_{p}^{*}}=K_{p,\mathrm{vert}}\otimes f_{p}^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}).

Thus if
c_{1}(X_{p}^{*})=0
then condition (
82
) holds, and every
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
section of
K_{X_{p}^{*}}
gives rise to a grading of the monodromy abstract contact open books (
81
).

5.5.4.
Summary

Assume that in Setting
5.12
, conditions (
80
) and (
82
) hold. Then (after possibly shrinking
\delta>0
or
P
) the above procedure, say with
\Upsilon\colon(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni(r,\theta)\mapsto r\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
defines, for all
(r,\theta,p)\in(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times P
,
monodromy graded abstract open books
(
81
), all graded isotopic to each other. The underlying space
N_{r,\theta,p}
of (
81
) is diffeomorphic to the fiber
\overline{W}_{r\cdot\theta,p}
.

Moreover, conditions (
80
) and (
82
) hold e.g. if
X
is a Stein manifold with trivial canonical bundle, and
d\lambda
is an exact Kähler form on
X
; e.g.
(X,\lambda)=(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda_{\mathrm{std}})
, see Remarks
5.16
(b)
,
(e)
and
5.17
(b)
.

5.5.5.
Extending monodromies to radius zero.

To get monodromy abstract contact open book
“at radius zero”
, we need to perform the above construction in Setting
5.18
, which slightly modifies Setting
5.12
, replacing base
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{C}_{\log}
. Below, we only consider the case which will be needed later, leaving straightforward generalizations (e.g. to more general families of loops) for the reader.

For
\delta>0
we denote by
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
the preimage of
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
in
\mathbb{C}_{\log}
, and identify
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\setminus\partial\mathbb{C}_{\log}
with
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
.

Setting 5.18
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex manifold, let
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function, and let
U_{X},W_{X},V_{X}\subseteq X
and
C^{\prime}_{X}=\overline{U}_{X}\setminus V_{X}
be as in Setting
5.12
for
P=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
. Let
\lambda_{X}\in\Omega^{1}(C^{\prime}_{X}\cup f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}))
be a
1
-form such that
d\lambda_{X}
extends to a Kähler form on
X
.

Assume that the singular fiber
f^{-1}(0)
is snc, and let
A
be A’Campo space for
f
. It is endowed with a smooth structure introduced in Section
3.4
using some adapted atlas and a partition of unity. Let
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
and
f_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}
be the smooth maps as in the diagram (
24
), see Proposition
3.33
. Put
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
,
W=\pi^{-1}(W_{X})
,
C^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(C^{\prime}_{X})
. Assume that

(84)

\mbox{the pullback }\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}\in\Omega^{1}(A\setminus\partial A)\mbox{ extends to a $1$-form on }A.

Then for every
\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon>0
, formula (
47
) defines a
1
-form
\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{1}(A)
. Put
\omega_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}=d\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}
.

Our goal is to define, in the above Setting
5.18
, symplectic monodromy over
\partial\mathbb{C}_{\log}
with respect to
\omega_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}
, whose associated abstract contact open book will be graded isotopic with monodromy abstract contact open books (
81
) constructed above over
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
; for
\varepsilon,\delta>0
small enough.

In Section
5.5.1
we have seen that for small enough
\delta>0
, formula (
76
) defines a
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
-fiberwise symplectic collar trivialization

\psi_{X}\colon C_{X,0}\times\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\longrightarrow C_{X},

where
C_{X,0}=C_{X}\cap f^{-1}(0)
. Let
f|_{N_{X}}\colon N_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
be the corresponding Liouville fibration (
78
).

In Section
5.5.2
, we took a family of loops
\Upsilon
. Now, for simplicity, we take
\Upsilon\colon(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni(r,\theta)\mapsto r\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. Assume that the induced monodromies are exact, i.e. condition (
80
) holds. Then formula (
81
) gives, for every
r\in(0,\delta]
and
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, isotopic monodromy abstract contact open books

(85)

(N_{r,\theta},\lambda_{r,\theta},\phi_{r,\theta})

The Liouville domain
(N_{r,\theta},\lambda_{r,\theta})
is naturally identified with the fiber
N_{X}\cap f^{-1}(r\theta)
equipped with the restriction of
\lambda_{X}
. Its underlying space
N_{r,\theta}
is diffeomorphic to
\overline{W}_{X}\cap f^{-1}(z)
for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
.

By the assumptions of Setting
5.12
, the special fiber
f^{-1}(0)
is smooth along the collar
C_{X}
, so the preimage
C\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(C_{X})
is a fibered product of the restriction
f|_{C_{X}}\colon C_{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
with the natural map
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
. Thus the trivialization
\psi_{X}
pulls back to a trivialization

\psi_{0}\colon C_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\longrightarrow C,

where
C_{0}\coloneqq C\cap f_{A}^{-1}(0,1)
is the intersection of
C
with a radius-zero fiber.

By Proposition
4.2
(a)
there is a neighborhood
U^{\prime}
of
\overline{U}\cap\partial A
, and an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\delta^{\prime}>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the form
\omega_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}
is fiberwise symplectic in
U^{\prime}
. Thus after shrinking
\delta>0
if needed, the restriction
f_{A}|_{\overline{U}\cap f_{A}^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log})}\colon\overline{U}\cap f_{A}^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
, together with the form
\omega_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}
, is a symplectic fibration for every
\delta^{\prime}>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Fix one
\delta^{\prime}>0
and define a family of
1
-forms
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(A\times(0,\varepsilon_{0}]\cup C\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}])
by
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}
.

By Remark
4.6
, there is a
1
-form
\beta_{X}\in\Omega^{1}(C_{X,0})
such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}|_{C\cap\partial A}=\pi^{*}(\lambda_{X}|_{C_{X,0}}+\varepsilon\beta_{X})
. Hence

C_{0}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\ni(x,\theta,\varepsilon)\mapsto(\psi_{0}(x,0,\theta),\varepsilon)\in C\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]

is an
[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
-fiberwise trivialization of
f_{A}|_{C\cap\partial A}\times\mathrm{id}_{[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
, which is
(\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}])
-fiberwise symplectic with respect to the form
d\lambda
.

Consider the symplectic lift
\mathcal{X}
of the radial vector field
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial r}
from
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
to
C\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Note that, unlike in Section
5.5.1
, now we do not need to restrict it to rays. By definition of
C_{X}
in Section
5.5.1
, the flow lines of
\mathcal{X}
starting in
(C\cap\partial A)\times\{0\}
exist in
C^{\prime}\times\{0\}
for all times
t\in[0,\delta]
. Since
C\cap\partial A
and
[0,\delta]
are compact, shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}>0
if needed, we infer that the same is true for the flow lines starting in
(C\cap\partial A)\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. This means that the vector field
\mathcal{X}
defines a flow

(C\cap\partial A)\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\times[0,\delta]\ni(x,\varepsilon,t)\mapsto(\varphi_{\varepsilon,t}(x),\varepsilon)\in C^{\prime}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]

such that for every
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the restriction of
\varphi_{\varepsilon,t}
to
C\cap f_{A}^{-1}(0,\theta)
is a symplectomorphism onto its image; with respect to the form
d\lambda_{\varepsilon}
. The image of this restriction is contained in
C^{\prime}\cap f_{A}^{-1}(t,\theta)
.

Thus for some
\widetilde{C}\subseteq C^{\prime}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, the restriction
(f_{A}\times\mathrm{id}_{[0,\varepsilon_{0}]})|_{\widetilde{C}}
admits a
[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
-fiberwise trivialization

(86)

C_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\ni(x,t,\theta,\varepsilon)\mapsto(\varphi_{\varepsilon,t}(\psi_{0}(x,0,\theta)),\varepsilon)\in\widetilde{C}.

which is (
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
)-fiberwise symplectic. Note that this trivialization restricts to
\psi_{0}
over
\varepsilon=0
.

Exactly as in Section
5.5.1
, we define
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\widetilde{C}
and, using inner components of the locally trivial fibration
f_{A}\times\mathrm{id}_{[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}\colon(\widetilde{C},\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\widetilde{C})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, we define a locally trivial fibration

(87)

(f_{A}\times\mathrm{id}_{[0,\varepsilon_{0}]})|_{\widetilde{N}}\colon\widetilde{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]

which agrees with
f_{A}|_{\overline{W}\cap\partial A}\times\mathrm{id}_{[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}
over
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, and with the Liouville fibration (
78
) over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times\{0\}
. Moreover, the fibration (
87
) is symplectic over
B\coloneqq(\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}\times(0,\varepsilon_{0}])\cup(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}])
.

By definition of
C_{X}
, the Liouville vector field of
\lambda_{X}
points outwards
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}C_{X}
, hence the one for
\lambda_{0}=\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}
points outwards
\widetilde{N}_{r,\theta,0}
for every
(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
. Since
\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
is compact, we can shrink
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that the same is true for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Now, the restriction of (
87
) over
B
is a Liouville fibration, with a fiberwise collar trivialization obtained from (
86
) by moving the base point inside
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
like in the formula (
79
). Over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times\{0\}
, this collar trivialization agrees with the one constructed for (
78
) in Section
5.5.1
. Exactly the same argument as in Lemma
5.13
shows that the above collar trivialization is cohomologically trivial.

As before, we assume that the symplectic monodromy associated to its restriction over each loop
\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni\theta\mapsto(r,\theta,\varepsilon)\in B
is exact. This condition is satisfied in the situations described in Remark
5.16
. Eventually, we get monodromy abstract contact open books

(88)

(\widetilde{N}_{r,\theta,\varepsilon},\lambda_{r,\theta,\varepsilon},\phi_{r,\theta,\varepsilon})

for all
(r,\theta,\varepsilon)\in B
, all isotopic to each other. Note that for
r>0
and
\varepsilon=0
, the abstract contact open book (
88
) can be identified with (
85
), since both are obtained using the same collar trivialization. In turn, for
(r,\theta)=(0,1)\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta,\log}
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}>0
, the abstract contact open book (
88
) is equal to

(89)

(F,\lambda,\phi)

where
F=\overline{W}\cap f_{A}^{-1}(0,1)
is a radius-zero fiber,
\lambda=\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}|_{F}
, and
\phi\colon F\longrightarrow F
is the time one flow of the monodromy vector field given locally by the formula (
48
).

We summarize the above construction in the following corollary, which will be used in Section
7.2
.

Corollary 5.19
.

The abstract contact open books (
81
) defined in Section
5.5.2
using a family of loops
\Upsilon\colon(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni(r,\theta)\mapsto r\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
, are isotopic to the
“radius-zero”
abstract contact open book (
89
).

Moreover, if
c_{1}(X\setminus f^{-1}(0))=0
then the natural grading of (
81
) defined in Remark
5.17
(b)
by a nonvanishing section of
K_{X\setminus f^{-1}(0)}
induces a grading of (
89
), by Corollary
5.10
.

5.5.6.
Applications

A typical situation where the above constructions apply is the following.

Example 5.20
.

Let
Z
be a Stein manifold and let
\varrho\colon Z\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an exhaustive strictly plurisubharmonic function. Let
X\subseteq Z
be a closed analytic subset whose all singularities are isolated. In case
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X=2
, assume further that the links of all singularities of
X
are rational homology spheres, so the assumptions of Remark
5.16
(b)
or
(e)
hold. Choose regular values
\xi_{V}<\xi_{W}<\xi_{U}
of
\varrho|_{X}
such that
\varrho^{-1}[\xi_{V},\xi_{U}]\cap X\subseteq X^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and define
V=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{V})
,
W=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{W})
,
U=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{U})
.

Consider a family of
1
-forms
\lambda_{t}\in\Omega^{1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
smoothly dependent on
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
for some
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
, such that
\lambda_{0}=-d^{c}\varrho|_{X^{\mathrm{reg}}}
. Recall from Section
2.2
that the Liouville vector field of
\lambda_{0}
is the gradient field of
\varrho
, so it points outwards
\partial\overline{W}
.

Let
f_{s}\colon X\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be a family of holomorphic functions with isolated singularities in
X
, holomorphically depending on a parameter
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
for some
\eta\geqslant 0
. Assume that

(90)

\mbox{the only critical value of }f_{s}\mbox{ is }0.

In particular, all fibers
f_{s}^{-1}(z)
for
z\neq 0
are smooth and contained in
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Note that, since the form
d\lambda_{0}=-dd^{c}\varrho
is Kähler, it restricts to a symplectic form on each fiber
f_{s}^{-1}(z)\cap\overline{U}
for
z\neq 0
. Thus for some
\delta>0
, the restrictions
f_{s}|_{\overline{U}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})}
, for all
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
, are symplectic fibrations with respect to
d\lambda_{0}
. In case
\varepsilon>0
, we can shrink
\varepsilon>0
so that the same is true for all
d\lambda_{t}
,
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
: here we use compactness of
\overline{U}
,
\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
. Now, our situation fits into Setting
5.12
, with
P=\mathbb{D}_{\eta}\times[0,\varepsilon]
and
F\colon X\times P\ni(x,s,t)\mapsto(f_{s}(x),s,t)\in\mathbb{C}\times P
.

The construction in Section
5.5.1
shows that we can shrink
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon,\eta>0
(in case they are nonzero) so that the map
F
restricts to a Liouville fibration
F|_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times P
defined in (
78
), with a collar trivialization (
79
). By construction, the special fiber
\overline{N}_{0,s,t}
equals
\overline{W}_{0,s,t}
for all
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
,
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
.

In the construction of Section
5.5.2
, we take the standard family of loops
\Upsilon\colon(0,\delta]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni(r,\theta)\mapsto r\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. Recall that condition (
80
) holds by Remark
5.16
(b)
,
(e)
. Thus for every
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*},s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta},t\in[0,\varepsilon]
formula (
81
) defines isotopic abstract contact open books

(91)

(N_{z,s,t},\lambda_{z,s,t},\phi_{z,s,t}).

Here
N_{z,s,t}
is a subset of
\overline{U}\cap f_{s}^{-1}(z)
diffeomorphic to
\overline{W}\cap f_{s}^{-1}(z)
, and
\lambda_{z,s,t}
is the restriction of
\lambda_{t}
.

If furthermore
c_{1}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})=0
then by Remark
5.17
(b)
, a choice of a nonvanishing section of
K_{X^{\mathrm{reg}}}
endows all abstract contact open books (
91
) with a grading, making them graded isotopic.

Remark 5.21
.

In the situation of Example
5.20
, if
X
is smooth and near each critical point of
f_{0}
the family
f_{s}
has constant Milnor number, then condition (
90
) is satisfied.

Example
5.20
covers the case of
\mu
-constant families considered in Theorem
1.1
. We will make it precise in Example
5.23
below. To do this, we first recall some known facts about the Milnor fibration.

Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
be a holomorphic germ with an isolated singularity. By
[
Mil68
, Corollary 2.9]
, there is a radius
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the ordinary sphere
\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
of radius
\varepsilon
centered at
0
is transversal to
f^{-1}(0)
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
is called
a Milnor radius
, and
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
is called a
Milnor ball
.

Fix a Milnor radius
\varepsilon>0
for
f
. There exists
\delta>0
such that
f|_{\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})}\colon\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
is a locally trivial fibration. Its restriction over
\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta}
is, up to a diffeomorphism, independent on the choices
[
Mil68
, Theorem 2.10]
. It is called the
Milnor fibration
of
f
, see formula (
3
) in the introduction.

Example 5.22
.

Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
be a germ of isolated hypersurface singularity. Put
\varrho(z)=\frac{\pi}{2}||z||^{2}
, and let
\lambda=-d^{c}\varrho
be the standard Liouville form, see Example
2.1
. Let
\xi_{V}<\xi_{W}<\xi_{U}
be Milnor radii for
f
. Then Example
5.20
(with
\varepsilon=\eta=0
) gives a subset
N\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{\xi_{U}}
and a
\delta>0
such that the restriction
f|_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
, is a Liouville fibration, diffeomorphic to
f|_{\mathbb{B}_{\xi_{U}}\cap f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})}
. Moreover, for each
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
, we get a monodromy abstract contact open book
(N_{z},\lambda_{z},\phi_{z})
. Using the standard holomorphic volume on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we endow each
(N_{z},\lambda_{z},\phi_{z})
with a grading.

We remark that, if
n\geqslant 3
, then by
[
Mil68
, Theorem 6.4]
the Milnor fiber is simply connected, so by the correspondence (
72
) the above grading of
(N_{z},\lambda_{z},\phi_{z})
is the unique one.

Example 5.23
.

More generally, let
\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}_{\eta}\ni(x,s)\mapsto f_{s}(x)\in\mathbb{C}
be a
\mu
-constant family of holomorphic functions with isolated singularities. As before, take
\varrho(z)=\frac{\pi}{2}||z||^{2}
, and
\lambda=-d^{c}\varrho
.

Let
\xi_{V}<\xi_{W}<\xi_{U}
be Milnor radii for
f_{0}
. Then for
\delta,\eta>0
small enough, formula (
91
) in Example
5.20
(with
\varepsilon=0
) defines graded isotopic abstract contact open books
(N_{z,s},\lambda_{z,s},\phi_{z,s})
for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
,
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
. For
s=0
, they agree with the ones constructed in Example
5.22
for
f_{0}
.

Note that the Milnor radii chosen in Example
5.23
for
f_{0}
are not necessarily Milnor radii for
f_{s}
,
s\neq 0
. In fact, it is a long-standing, open question if a Milnor radius for
f_{s}
can be chosen independently of
s
; even modifying the distance function
\varrho
, see
[
O’S83
]
.

In particular, the graded abstract contact open books produced in Example
5.23
for
f_{0}
, and the analogous ones produced using a Milnor radius for
f_{s}
, might not be isotopic.
To prove Theorem
1.1
, we will compare their Floer homology. A crucial ingredient of this comparison will be the fact that they differ by a homologically trivial cobordism. More precisely, the following result is known, see Figure
6
.

Proposition 5.24

(
[
LR76
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}_{\eta}\ni(x,s)\mapsto f_{s}(x)\in\mathbb{C}
be a
\mu
-constant family of isolated hypersurface singularities. Let
\varepsilon_{0}
a Milnor radius for
f_{0}
. For any
s
small enough and any Milnor radius
\varepsilon_{s}<\varepsilon_{0}
for
f_{s}
, the cobordism

\overline{f_{s}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}}

is homologically trivial, that is, we have the vanishing

H_{*}(\overline{f_{s}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}},f_{s}^{-1}(0)\cap\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}};{\mathbb{Z}})=0.

s=0
s\neq 0
s
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
Milnor ball for
f_{s}

homologically trivial cobordism
[
LR76
]

f_{s}^{-1}(\delta)
f_{s}^{-1}(0)
collar trivialization
f_{0}^{-1}(0)
f_{0}^{-1}(\delta)
Milnor ball for
f_{0}

Figure 6
.
\mu
-constant family, see Example
5.23

5.6.
Codimension zero families of fixed points

We end this section by introducing a notion of
codimension zero families of fixed points
, slightly extending the one introduced by McLean in
[
McL19
, p. 978]
. In Proposition
6.3
we will see that if all fixed points of
\phi
come in such families, then their topology gives information about Floer homology groups
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
. In Section
7
we will see that this is the case for the
“radius zero”
monodromy on the boundary of the A’Campo space.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
(M,\lambda,\phi)
be an abstract contact open book.
A
codimension zero family of fixed points

B\subseteq M
is a compact, connected codimension zero submanifold of
M
with corners, such that there exist an open neighborhood
N_{B}
of
B
in
M
and a time-independent Hamiltonian
H_{B}\colon N_{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

B=H_{B}^{-1}(0)=\operatorname{Fix}\phi|_{N_{B}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\phi|_{N_{B}}=\psi^{H_{B}}_{1}|_{N_{B}}\colon N_{B}\longrightarrow N_{B}.

The action function
{a}
associated with
(M,\lambda,\phi)
is constant in
B
, and we denote its value by
{a}(B)
. If
(M,\lambda,\phi)
is graded then the function
\operatorname{CZ}
is also constant in
B
, and we write
\operatorname{CZ}(B)
for its value.

Put
\partial^{M}B=B\cap\partial M
. Since
\phi=\mathrm{id}
near
\partial M
, the set
\partial^{M}B
is either empty, or a union of some connected components of
\partial M
.

Since
B=H_{B}^{-1}(0)
is a codimension zero submanifold with corners, at every point
p\in\partial B\setminus\partial^{M}B
there is a chart
U_{p}\subseteq N_{B}
such that
H_{B}|_{U_{p}\setminus B}
is either positive or negative. Denote by
\partial^{+}B
(resp.
\partial^{-}B
) the set of those points
p
such that
H_{B}|_{U_{p}\setminus B}>0
(resp.
<0
). This way, we get a decomposition

(92)

\partial B=\partial^{M}B\sqcup\partial^{+}B\sqcup\partial^{-}B.

where each factor is a union of connected components of
\partial B
.

Remark 5.26
.

Definition
5.25
generalizes the one given in
[
McL19
, p. 978]
. Namely, a codimension zero family
B
of fixed points satisfies the definition of loc. cit. if
\partial^{-}B=\emptyset
, i.e. if the Hamiltonian
H_{B}
is nonnegative (we warn the reader that, due to the opposite sign convention explained in Section
5.1
, the Hamiltonian in loc. cit. is nonpositive). As a result of this more general definition, our version of McLean’s axiom (HF3), stated in Proposition
6.3
, involves relative homology. This more general setting is needed to treat the additional fixed point component
B_{0}
, discussed in Section
1.1
.

In practice, codimension zero families of fixed points will have corners. For example, in Figure
2(b)
they are the quadrants
\overline{A_{j}^{\circ}}
, see Proposition
7.4
. The following lemma allows to get rid of these corners.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
(M,\lambda,\phi)
be an abstract contact open book, and let
B\subseteq M
be a codimension zero family of fixed points. Then there is an abstract contact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi_{1})\sim(M,\lambda,\phi)
such that
\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{1}=B_{1}\sqcup(\operatorname{Fix}\phi\setminus B)
, where
B_{1}
is a codimension zero family of fixed points, which is a manifold with boundary (but no corners), and the triple
(B_{1},\partial^{+}B_{1},\partial^{-}B_{1})
is homeomorphic to
(B,\partial^{+}B,\partial^{-}B)
.

Let
H_{B}\colon N_{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the time independent Hamiltonian from Definition
5.25
. Then
H_{B}|_{N_{B}\setminus B}
has no critical points, so
H_{B}\colon N_{B}\setminus B\longrightarrow(-\delta,\delta)
is a submersion for some
\delta>0
(if
\partial^{-}B
or
\partial^{+}B
is empty, the image
(-\delta,\delta)
should be replaced by
[0,\delta)
or
(-\delta,0]
, respectively). By assumption, the Hamiltonian flow
\psi_{s}^{H_{B}}
has no
1
-periodic orbits in
N_{B}
. Reducing
\delta
(hence shrinking
N_{B}
) we can assume that it has no
t
-periodic orbits for any
t\in(0,4]
.

Define a smooth family of functions
\rho_{t}:[-\delta,\delta]\longrightarrow[-\delta,\delta]
, parametrized by
t\in[0,1]
, such that
\rho_{0}=\mathrm{id}
,
\rho_{t}
vanishes identically in
[-\frac{1}{4}t\delta,\frac{1}{4}t\delta]
, equals the identity outside
[-\frac{1}{2}\delta,\frac{1}{2}\delta]
and is strictly increasing away from
[-\frac{1}{4}t\delta,\frac{1}{4}t\delta]
with derivative bounded by
4
. For each fixed
t\in[0,1]
define a time independent Hamiltonian
H^{t}\coloneqq\rho_{t}{\circ}H_{B}\colon N_{B}\longrightarrow(-\delta,\delta)
.

Let
\phi_{t}\coloneqq\psi_{1}^{H^{t}}\colon N_{B}\longrightarrow N_{B}
be its time one flow. Recall that
\phi|_{N_{B}}
is the time one flow of the Hamiltonian
H_{B}=H^{0}
, so
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{N_{B}}
, and
\phi_{t}
agrees with
\phi
off
H_{B}^{-1}([-\frac{1}{2}\delta,\frac{1}{2}\delta])\subsetneq N_{B}
, so we can extend
\phi_{t}
to an exact symplectomorphism
\phi_{t}\colon M\longrightarrow M
, equal to
\phi
away from
N_{B}
. In particular,
\phi_{t}=\mathrm{id}
near
\partial M
. Thus
(M,\lambda,\phi_{t})
is an isotopy of abstract contact open books, and
\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{t}\setminus N_{B}=\operatorname{Fix}\phi\setminus B
.

Put
B_{t}=H_{B}^{-1}([-\frac{1}{4}t\delta,\frac{1}{4}t\delta])
. Because
\pm\frac{1}{4}t\delta
are regular values of
H_{B}
, the subset
B_{t}\subseteq M
is a submanifold with boundary, of codimension zero. We claim that
B_{t}=\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{t}|_{N_{B}}
. Clearly,
B_{t}\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{t}
. Outside
B_{t}
, the Hamiltonian vector field associated with
H^{t}
coincides with the Hamiltonian vector field of
H_{B_{i}}
, multiplied by the derivative of
\rho_{t}
, which is bounded by
4
. The claim follows because, by assumption, the time
s
Hamiltonian flow
\psi^{H_{B}}_{s}
has no fixed points in
N_{B}\setminus B
for
s\in(0,4]
.

Therefore,
\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{t}=B_{t}\sqcup(\operatorname{Fix}\phi\setminus N_{B})
, and
B_{t}
is a codimension zero family of fixed points, without corners. It remains to see that
(B_{t},\partial^{+}B_{t},\partial^{-}B_{t})
is homeomorphic to
(B,\partial^{+}B,\partial^{-}B)
, for
t>0
small enough. Write
N_{B}=N_{B}^{+}\sqcup N_{B}^{-}\sqcup B
, where
H_{B}>0
on
N_{B}^{+}
and
H_{B}<0
on
N_{B}^{-}
. Define a smooth vector field
X_{1}
as
-\nabla H_{B}
on
N_{B}^{+}
,
\nabla H_{B}
on
N_{B}^{-}
, and
0
on
B
. Fix a vector field
X_{0}
on
N_{B}
, pointing inwards
\partial^{+}B\sqcup\partial^{-}B
, see
[
BS73
, Appendice, §2]
. Since
X_{1}
vanishes on
B
, we can choose
t>0
small enough so that
X\coloneqq X_{0}+X_{1}
does not vanish on a neighborhood
N
of
\overline{B_{t}\setminus B}
, and points inwards both
\partial^{+}B_{t}\sqcup\partial^{-}B_{t}
and
\partial^{+}B\sqcup\partial^{-}B
. As in
[
BS73
, Appendice, Proposition 6.1]
, we conclude that both
N\cap B_{t}
and
N\cap B
are homeomorphic to
(\partial^{+}B_{t}\sqcup\partial^{-}B_{t})\times[0,\infty)
, and we can extend this homeomorphism to the required homeomorphism of triples.

∎

6.
The McLean spectral sequence in fixed point Floer homology

In this section, we introduce fixed point Floer homology
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
of an abstract contact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi)
, following Uljarevic and McLean
[
Ulj17
,
McL19
]
. This definition is a variant of
[
DS94
]
, cf.
[
Her09
]
. It was first introduced in a slightly different way in Seidel’s thesis
[
Sei97
]
, see
[
Sei01
, §4]
.

After some preparations in Section
6.1
, we introduce Floer complexes in Section
6.2
. In Proposition
6.3
, we construct a spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
in case
\operatorname{Fix}\phi
is a disjoint union of codimension zero families of fixed points. This spectral sequence is a slight generalization of the axiom
[
McL19
, (HF3)]
, proved in Appendix C of loc. cit. Our arguments are essentially the same, but we give a less compressed presentation for convenience of those readers who are not experts in Floer theory.

6.1.
Completions of Liouville domains

A
contact manifold

(N,\alpha)
is a compact manifold
N
of dimension
2k-1
with a
1
-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(N)
such that
\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{k-1}\neq 0
. The
Reeb vector field

R_{\alpha}
is defined by
\alpha(R_{\alpha})=1
,
d\alpha(R_{\alpha},\,\cdot\,)=0
.

Let
(M,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain. Then
(\partial M,\lambda|_{\partial M})
is a contact manifold. For
\eta>0
, the backwards flow of the Liouville vector field
X_{\lambda}
defines a diffeomorphism onto its image

(93)

\Psi\colon(-\eta,0]\times\partial M\longrightarrow M

such that
\Psi^{*}\lambda=e^{r}\lambda|_{\partial M}
, where
r
is a coordinate on
(-\eta,0]
. The
completion
of
(M,\lambda)
, see
[
CE12
, p. 239]
or
[
Ulj17
, §1.1]
, is a manifold
\widehat{M}=(M\sqcup(-\eta,\infty)\times\partial M)/_{\sim}
, where
\sim
identifies
(-\eta,0]\times\partial M
with its image under
\Psi
, together with a Liouville form defined as
\lambda
in
M
and
e^{r}\lambda|_{\partial M}
in the
cylindrical end

(-\eta,\infty)\times\partial M
. Abusing notation, we denote this
1
-form by
\lambda
, too.

Let
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
be the vector field corresponding to the coordinate
r
of the cylindrical end
(-\eta,\infty)\times\partial M\subseteq\widehat{M}
. The restriction
\lambda|_{\{r\}\times\partial M}
is a contact form, and we denote by
R_{\lambda}
its Reeb vector field. Therefore,
R_{\lambda}
is a vector field on
(-\eta,\infty)\times\partial M\subseteq\widehat{M}
, which is tangent to the level sets of the coordinate
r
.

An almost complex structure
J
on
M
or
\widehat{M}
is
cylindrical
if there is
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
r\geqslant-\varepsilon
, the restriction
J|_{\{r\}\times\partial M}
leaves invariant the contact distribution
\ker(\lambda|_{\{r\}\times M})
and satisfies
J(\frac{\partial}{\partial r})=R_{\lambda}
. This notion coincides with
[
McL19
, Definition 4.1]
and
[
Ulj17
, (2.7), (2.8)]
.

Fix
a\in\mathbb{R}
. A (time-dependent) Hamiltonian
H_{t}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is of
slope

a
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
H_{t}{\circ}\Psi(r,y)=ar
for
r\geqslant-\varepsilon
, where
\Psi
is the trivialization (
93
), see
[
McL19
, p. 977]
or
[
Ulj17
, (2.6)]
. A Hamiltonian of slope
a
is extended to the completion
\widehat{M}
by the formula
H_{t}(r,y)=ar
at
(-\varepsilon,\infty)\times\partial M
. At the locus where
H_{t}(r,y)=ar
the Hamiltonian flow at time
1
coincides with the Reeb flow of
R_{\lambda}
at time
a
. A nonzero slope
a
is
small
if
|a|
is strictly smaller than the minimal period of a Reeb orbit. In this case,
\psi^{H}_{1}
has no fixed points in the cylindrical end of
\widehat{M}
.

6.2.
The Floer complexes

Let
(M,\phi,\lambda)
be a graded abstract contact open book, see Definition
5.2
and Section
5.4.3
. We now define the
\phi
-twisted loop Floer complex

\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}
and the
\check{\phi}
-fixed point Floer complex

\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}
, using the Floer Cauchy–Riemann equation in a perturbed (
98
) and unperturbed (
100
) version, respectively. The first setting is adapted from
[
Ulj17
]
, and mirrors
[
DS94
]
in the compact case, which in turn specifies to the classical Floer theory for
\phi=\mathrm{id}
. The second one is used in
[
McL19
]
: here
\check{\phi}
is a small Hamiltonian perturbation of
\phi
. It is known how to pass from one setting to the other: we outline the method in Section
6.2.6
. In particular, both complexes have the same homology
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
. Nonetheless, to derive the spectral sequence in Proposition
6.3
, it will be convenient to use both approaches at different parts of the argument.

6.2.1.
Periodic almost complex structures and Hamiltonians

Let
(M,\lambda,\phi)
be a graded abstract contact open book. Extending
\phi
to the identity in
[0,\infty)\times\partial M
we obtain a compactly supported exact symplectomorphism
\widehat{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}
, which we denote by
\phi
, too.

A family of almost complex structures
J_{t}
on
M
or
\widehat{M}
, smoothly parametrized by
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is
\phi
-periodic
if
J_{t+1}=\phi^{*}J_{t}
, see
[
Ulj17
, (2.5)]
. A (time dependent) Hamiltonian
H_{t}
is
\phi
-periodic
if it satisfies the equality
H_{t+1}=H_{t}{\circ}\phi
, see
[
Ulj17
, (2.4)]
. A
\phi
-twisted loop
in
M
is a path
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow M
such that
\phi(\gamma(t+1))=\gamma(t)
, see
[
Ulj17
, §2.2]
.

We warn the reader that in
[
DS94
, §2.2]
the periodicity conditions are defined in an opposite way, i.e.
\gamma(t+1)=\phi(\gamma(t))
, etc. Also, the symbols
t,s
play the opposite roles in
[
DS94
]
than in the discussion below, which follows
[
Ulj17
]
.

Let
H
be a
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian. It satisfies a basic equality

(94)

\psi_{t+1}^{H}=\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H},

cf.
[
DS94
, p. 586]
.
To prove (
94
), note that
\psi_{t+1}^{H}=\psi_{t}^{H^{\prime}}\circ\psi_{1}^{H}
, where
H_{t}^{\prime}(x)=H_{t+1}(x)
. By
\phi
-periodicity,
H_{t}^{\prime}=H_{t}\circ\phi
, so
\psi_{t}^{H^{\prime}}=\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi
, see
[
MS17
, Exercise 3.1.14(iii)]
. This proves (
94
).

We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
the space of
\phi
-twisted loops which are
Hamiltonian
, i.e. of the form
t\mapsto\psi_{t}^{H}(x)
for some
x\in M
. We have a one-to-one correspondence

(95)

\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Fix}(\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})\quad\mbox{given by}\quad\gamma\mapsto\gamma(0).

Indeed, if
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
then
(\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})(\gamma(0))=\phi(\gamma(1))=\gamma(0)
, so
\gamma(0)\in\operatorname{Fix}\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}
. For the converse, let
x\in\operatorname{Fix}(\phi\circ\psi_{1})
and
define
\gamma(t)=\psi_{t}^{H}(x)
. We have

\phi(\gamma(t+1))=\phi\circ\psi_{t+1}^{H}(x)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{\eqref{eq:basic}}}}{=}\phi\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}(x)=\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}(x)=\psi_{t}^{H}(x)=\gamma(t),

where the first and last equality follow from the definition of
\gamma
, and the second-to-last holds because
x\in\operatorname{Fix}(\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})
. Thus
\gamma
is a
\phi
-twisted loop, as needed.

Note that if a
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian
H
has small negative (or positive) slope, then all fixed points of
\phi{\circ}\psi^{H}_{1}
are away from
\partial M
, and away from the cylindrical end of of
\widehat{M}
.

6.2.2.
The Conley–Zehnder index of a Hamiltonian twisted loop

Fix a Hamiltonian
\phi
-twisted loop
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
. By (
95
), the point
\gamma(0)
is fixed by the symplectomorphism
\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}
. The latter has a unique grading, induced from the grading of
\phi
by the isotopy
(\psi_{t}^{H})
. We define
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma)
as the Conley–Zehnder index of
\gamma(0)\in\operatorname{Fix}(\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})
, see Section
5.4.4
.

This definition lifts the relative Conley–Zehnder index introduced in
[
Ulj17
, Definition 2.10]
to an absolute one. To see this, let us recall how
\operatorname{CZ}
was defined in Section
5.4.4
. Since
\gamma
is an integral curve of the Hamiltonian flow
\psi^{H}_{t}
, for any time
t\in[0,1]
the differential of
\psi^{H}_{t}
at time
t
induces a mapping
\operatorname{Fr}(M)_{\gamma(0)}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Fr}(M)_{\gamma(t)}
, which lifts to a mapping
\widetilde{\psi}^{H}_{t}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(0)}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(t)}
. The grading of
\phi
defines a mapping
\widetilde{\beta}_{\gamma(1)}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(1)}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(0)}
. So, composing we obtain a mapping

\widetilde{\beta}_{\gamma(1)}\circ\widetilde{\psi}^{H}_{1}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(0)}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(0)}.

which gives rise to a homotopy class of paths in the symplectic group relative to its ends as in Section
5.4.4
. The Conley–Zehnder index
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma)
is now the Maslov index of this path.

Given two frames
f_{0}\in\operatorname{Fr}(M)_{\gamma(0)}
and
f_{1}\in\operatorname{Fr}(M)_{\gamma(1)}
such that
(\psi_{1}^{H})_{*}f_{0}=f_{1}
,
any homotopy class (relative to its ends) of frames
f_{t}\in\operatorname{Fr}(M)_{\gamma(t)}
connecting
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
determines a map
\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(1)}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Fr}}(M)_{\gamma(0)}
. Let us call the homotopy class
admissible
if the map equals
\widetilde{\beta}_{\gamma(1)}
.

Fix
\gamma_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H_{1}}
,
\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H_{2}}
for two Hamiltonians
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
, and fix
u\colon{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow M
satisfying
\phi(u(s,t+1))=u(s,t)
,
u(s,t)=\gamma_{1}(t)
for
s\leqslant 0
and
u(s,t)=\gamma_{2}(t)
for
s\geqslant 1
. Then the grading induces, via the choice of a homotopy of admissible classes of framings along
u(s,\,\cdot\,)
for any
s
, a symplectic trivialization
T_{s,t}
of
u^{*}TM
such that
\phi^{*}T_{s,t+1}=T_{s,t}
. Now, the relative grading of
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is defined in
[
Ulj17
, Definition 2.10]
as the difference between the Maslov indices
\mu(\Psi^{2})-\mu(\Psi^{1})
, where
\Psi^{i}\colon t\mapsto T_{i,t}^{-1}\circ(\psi_{t}^{H})^{*}T_{i,0}\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n)
. The path
\Psi^{i}
represents the homotopy class used above to define
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma_{i})
, as claimed.

6.2.3.
The
\phi
-twisted loop Floer complex

Now, we introduce the fixed point Floer homology
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
using
\phi
-twisted loops, following
[
Ulj17
]
.

Recall from Definition
5.2
that associated with an abstract contact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi)
we have an action function
{a}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, defined (up to a constant) by
\phi^{*}\lambda-\lambda=-d{a}
. We fix a
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian
H
of small
negative
slope. Taking
H
of small
positive
slope would lead to a definition of another Floer homology group,
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
, which is not relevant for us: hence for the sake of clarity, we focus on
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
, and leave the analogous discussion of
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
to the reader. The relation between
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
and
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
is outlined in
[
Sei01
, Remark 4.1]
.

The
action functional
on the space of
\phi
-twisted loops is given in
[
Ulj17
, Definition 2.6]
by the formula

(96)

\mathcal{A}_{\phi,H}(\gamma)\coloneqq-\int_{0}^{1}(\gamma^{*}\lambda+H_{t}(\gamma)\,dt)+{a}(\gamma(1)).

Note that if
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
is a constant path, and
H
is time-independent, then the image of
\gamma
is a fixed point of
\phi
, say
p
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\phi,H}(\gamma)=-H(p)+{a}(p)
.

The set of critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{\phi,H}
equals exactly
\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
, see
[
Ulj17
, Lemma 2.7]
. A
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian
H
is
nondegenerate
if
\det(D(\phi{\circ}\psi^{H}_{1})(x)-\mathrm{id})\neq 0
at any fixed point
x
of
\phi{\circ}\psi^{H_{t}}_{1}
, see
[
Ulj17
, Definition 2.5]
. If a
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian
H
is nondegenerate then the set
\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
is finite.

Let
J_{t}
be a family of cylindrical,
(d\lambda)
-compatible and
\phi
-periodic almost complex structures on
\widehat{M}
. Fix
\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
. A
Floer trajectory
between
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
is a smooth map
u\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}
satisfying

(97)

\phi(u(s,t+1))=u(s,t),\quad\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}u(s,t)=\gamma_{-}(t),\quad\lim_{s\rightarrow+\infty}u(s,t)=\gamma_{+}(t)

and the
perturbed Floer Cauchy–Riemann equation
:

(98)

\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J_{t}(u)\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X^{H}(u)\right)=0,

see
[
Ulj17
, (2.10)]
. Whenever additional precision is needed, we refer to such a Floer trajectory as a
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
-Floer trajectory from
\gamma_{-}
to
\gamma_{+}
. Note that if
u(s,t)
is such a Floer trajectory then so is
u(c+s,t)
for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
. So, the additive group
{\mathbb{R}}
acts on the Floer trajectories.

Definition 6.1

(
[
Ulj17
, 2.5]
)
.

A pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
is
regular for
\phi
if
H_{t}
is nondegenerate and the linearization of the operator (
98
) for every Floer trajectory is surjective.

It is known that a generic pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
is regular. In particular, regular pairs exist, cf.
[
AD14
, Theorem 8.1.1]
or
[
Sei17
, p. 316]
.

The
\phi
-twisted loop Floer complex
associated with a graded abstract compact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi)
and a regular pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-vector space
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
generated by the Hamiltonian
\phi
-twisted loops, graded by
-\operatorname{CZ}
, whose differential
\partial
is defined as follows. Given
\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
such that
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma_{+})=\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma_{-})+1
, the coefficient of
\gamma_{+}
in
\partial(\gamma_{-})
is the number (mod
2
) of Floer trajectories between
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
, up to the additive action of
{\mathbb{R}}
. By
[
Ulj17
, Theorem 2.18]
, this differential is well defined and its square is zero, so
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}
is indeed a chain complex. By
[
Ulj17
, Corollary 2.22 and Lemma 2.25]
, the homology groups of this complex are independent of the choice of a regular pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
, up to unique isomorphism, as long as
H
remains of small negative slope. The reader should note that we have improved the relative grading used by Uljarevic to the absolute grading defined in Section
6.2.2
using the grading of
\phi
as in
[
McL19
]
; the proofs of
[
Ulj17
]
work in this setting.

As a consequence of the above independence, we can associate to each graded abstract contact open book
(M,\lambda,\phi)
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded Floer homology group
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
, given by
H
of small negative slope. The sign
“
+
”
comes from the notation of
[
McL19
]
, where, as explained in Section
5.1
, Hamiltonians have opposite signs. Taking
H
of small
positive
slope would lead to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
.

6.2.4.
The action filtration

Let
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
be the
\phi
-twisted loop Floer complex introduced in Section
6.2.3
for a regular pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
. For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
F^{\leqslant a}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
be the subspace of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
spanned by those Hamiltonian
\phi
-twisted loops
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
whose action satisfies
\mathcal{A}_{\phi,H}(\gamma)\leqslant a
. It is a subcomplex of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
, see
[
Ulj17
, §2.6]
. To see this, recall that for
\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
, the coefficient of
\gamma_{+}
in the expression of
\partial(\gamma_{-})
is the count (mod
2
) of the Floer trajectories connecting
\gamma_{-}
with
\gamma_{+}
. By
[
Ulj17
, p. 867]
, these trajectories are anti-gradient flow lines of the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{\phi,H}
. In particular, the differential
\partial
decreases the value of the action, so it maps
F^{\leqslant a}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
to itself, as claimed.

6.2.5.
The
\check{\phi}
-fixed point Floer complex

Now we review the definition of Floer homology given in
[
McL19
]
. In Section
6.2.6
we will see that it is equivalent to the one recalled in Section
6.2.3
.

Let again
(M,\lambda,\phi)
be a graded abstract compact open book. Consider a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-small
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian
H
which is of small negative slope. Assume that all fixed points of
\check{\phi}\coloneqq\phi{\circ}\psi^{H}_{1}
are nondegenerate (hence there is finitely many such points). Let
J_{t}
be a family of cylindrical,
(d\lambda)
-compatible and
\check{\phi}
-periodic almost complex structures on
M
. For
p_{-},p_{+}\in\operatorname{Fix}\check{\phi}
, a
Floer trajectory
(or, more precisely: a
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi},J_{t})
-Floer trajectory
) from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
is a smooth map
v\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\longrightarrow M
satisfying

(99)

\check{\phi}(v(s,t+1))=v(s,t),\quad\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}v(s,t)=p_{-},\quad\lim_{s\rightarrow+\infty}v(s,t)=p_{+}

and the
unperturbed Floer Cauchy–Riemann equation
:

(100)

\frac{\partial v}{\partial s}+J_{t}(v)\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}\right)=0,

see
[
McL19
, Definition 4.3]
.

We define the
\check{\phi}
-fixed point Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi},J_{t})
as the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-vector space freely generated by the fixed points of
\check{\phi}
, graded by the minus Conley–Zehnder index, and equipped with the following differential: given
p_{+},p_{-}\in\operatorname{Fix}\check{\phi}
such that
\operatorname{CZ}(p_{+})=\operatorname{CZ}(p_{-})+1
, the coefficient of
p_{+}
in
\partial(p_{-})
is the number of Floer trajectories between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
, up to the additive action of
{\mathbb{R}}
in the
s
variable of
u
. As we will see in Section
6.2.6
below, for generic choice of
J_{t}
and
H
, this differential is well defined, its square is zero and the resulting homology groups coincide with
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
introduced in Section
6.2.3
above. In particular, they do not depend on the choice of the almost complex structure
J_{t}
, and of the Hamiltonian
H
of small negative slope, cf.
[
McL19
, p. 980]
.

6.2.6.
Equivalence of the two Floer complexes

Here we explain the equivalence of Uljarevic’s and McLean’s definitions of the Floer complex, recalled in Sections
6.2.3
and
6.2.5
above.

Let
H
be a
\phi
-periodic Hamiltonian and let
(J_{t})
be a family of cylindrical,
(d\lambda)
-compatible,
\phi
-periodic almost complex structures such that
(H_{t},J_{t})
is regular for
\phi
, see Definition
6.1
. As in Section
6.2.5
put
\check{\phi}\coloneqq\phi{\circ}\psi^{H}_{1}
. We claim that there is an isomorphism of chain complexes
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})\cong\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}(\check{\phi},\widetilde{J}_{t})
for a suitable cylindrical,
(d\lambda)
-compatible and
\check{\phi}
-periodic family
\widetilde{J}_{t}
.

The correspondence (
95
), together with our definition of
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma)
, give a natural identification of the underlying graded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector spaces. For the equality of differential we use the following, known argument from
[
AD14
, Proposition 8.6.1]
.

Fix
\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,H}
, and let
u:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}
be a
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
-Floer trajectory from
\gamma_{-}
to
\gamma_{+}
. Let
p_{-},p_{+}
be the corresponding fixed points of
\check{\phi}
. By
[
Ulj17
, Lemma 2.15]
, the image of
u
is contained in
M
. Define
v(s,t):=(\psi^{H}_{t})^{-1}(u(s,t))
and

(101)

\widetilde{J}_{t}\coloneqq(\psi^{H}_{t})^{*}J_{t}=D(\psi^{H}_{t})^{-1}\circ J_{t}\circ D(\psi^{H}_{t}).

We claim that
v
is a
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi},\widetilde{J}_{t})
-Floer trajectory from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
. The proof of
[
AD14
, Proposition 8.6.1]
precisely shows that
v
satisfies the unperturbed Cauchy–Riemann Floer equation (
100
), with
J_{t}
replaced by
\widetilde{J}_{t}
. Clearly,
\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}v(s,t)=p_{\pm}
. It remains to show that
\check{\phi}(v(s,t+1))=v(s,t)
and that
\widetilde{J}_{t}
is a
\check{\phi}
-periodic family of cylindrical almost complex structures. This follows by elementary computation, using the basic equality (
94
). Indeed, (
94
) and definition of
\check{\phi}
give

\check{\phi}\circ(\psi_{t+1}^{H})^{-1}=\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}\circ(\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})^{-1}=(\psi_{t}^{H})^{-1}\circ\phi

Hence by definition of
v
, we have

\check{\phi}(v(s,t+1))=\check{\phi}\circ(\psi_{t+1}^{H})^{-1}(u(s,t+1))=(\psi_{t}^{H})^{-1}\circ\phi(u(s,t+1))\overset{\mbox{\tiny{\eqref{eq:boundulj}}}}{=}(\psi_{t}^{H})^{-1}(u(s,t))=v(s,t),

which proves the periodicity condition (
99
). To see that
\widetilde{J}_{t}
is
\check{\phi}
-periodic, we write

\widetilde{J}_{t+1}=(\psi_{t+1}^{H})^{*}J_{t+1}\overset{\tiny{\mbox{\eqref{eq:basic}}}}{=}(\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})^{*}J_{t+1}\overset{\tiny{(*)}}{=}(\psi_{t}^{H}\circ\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})^{*}J_{t}=(\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H})^{*}\widetilde{J}_{t}=\check{\phi}^{*}\widetilde{J}_{t},

as needed. The equality
(*)
above holds by the
\phi
-periodicity condition
J_{t+1}=\phi^{*}J_{t}
.

Since the pair
(H_{t},J_{t})
is regular for
\phi
, the linearized Floer equation for
u
is surjective, so the same happens for
v
and the family of almost complex structures
\widetilde{J}_{t}
. This way we get a one to one correspondence between the
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
-Floer trajectories from
\gamma_{-}
to
\gamma_{+}
, and
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi}_{t},\widetilde{J}_{t})
-Floer trajectories from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
, modulo the
\mathbb{R}
-action. Hence the differentials in both
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})
and
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi},\widetilde{J}_{t})
coincide. In particular, the results of
[
Ulj17
]
quoted in Section
6.2.3
can be used to prove that both differentials are well defined and square to zero. We conclude that the complexes
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,H_{t},J_{t})=\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi},\widetilde{J}_{t})
constructed in Sections
6.2.3
and
6.2.5
, respectively, are naturally isomorphic. In particular, both constructions give the same Floer homology groups
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
.

6.2.7.
Invariance by isotopy of abstract contact open books

The following result, crucial for us, was proved in
[
McL19
, Lemma B.3]
. We include a proof for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\{(M_{t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a graded isotopy of abstract contact open books. Then the groups
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{t},+)
and
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{t},-)
are independent of
t
.

Let
\pi\colon M\longrightarrow[0,1]
be the total space of the isotopy, and let
\phi\colon M\longrightarrow M
be the diffeomorphism which restricts to
\phi_{t}
on each fiber, see Definition
5.2
.

Given such an isotopy
(M,\pi,\lambda)
of Liouville domains, in
[
Kea14
, §9.1]
, an isotopy of completions
(\widehat{M},\pi,\lambda)
is constructed. Since
\phi
is fiberwise compactly supported, it extends to a diffeomorphism
\phi\colon\widehat{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}
which respects fibers and at each fiber is a compactly supported exact symplectomorphism. By
[
Kea14
, §9.1]
,
\pi\colon\widehat{M}\longrightarrow[0,1]
admits a trivialization by an exact symplectomorphism, i.e. there is a diffeomorphism
\Phi:\widehat{M_{0}}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\widehat{M}
such that
\Phi^{*}\lambda_{t}=\lambda_{0}+dG_{t}
, where
G_{t}
is the restriction to
M_{0}\times\{t\}
of a smooth function
G\colon M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
. So, given an isotopy of abstract contact open books
(M,\pi,\lambda,\phi)
, we have an isotopy
(\widehat{M}_{0}\times[0,1],\mathrm{pr}_{[0,1]},\lambda,\phi)
of their completions, such that
\lambda_{t}=\lambda_{0}+dG_{t}
, and
\phi
is fiberwise an exact compactly supported symplectomorphism. If the isotopy
(M,\pi,\lambda,\phi)
is graded, then this grading is inherited to
(\widehat{M}_{0}\times[0,1],\mathrm{pr}_{[0,1]},\lambda,\phi)
.

By construction, the Floer homology of
(\widehat{M}_{0},\lambda_{0}+dG_{t},\phi_{t})
coincides with the Floer homology of
(\widehat{M}_{0},\lambda_{0},\phi_{t})
. The result now follows from
[
Ulj17
, Theorem 2.34]
.

∎

6.3.
The McLean spectral sequence

The remaining part of this section is devoted to the proof of the following result, which is a (slightly more general) version of the axiom (HF3) from
[
McL19
, p. 980]
, proved in Appendix C of loc. cit; see Remark
6.5
for comparison. Our proof follows the same path, but we provide a more detailed exposition for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(M,\lambda,\phi)
be a graded abstract contact open book. Assume that

\operatorname{Fix}\phi=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}B_{i},

where each
B_{i}
is a codimension zero families of fixed points, see Definition
5.25
. Choose an action
{a}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, see (
68
), and a function
\iota\colon\{1,\dots,N\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with the following property: the function
\widehat{\iota}
from the discrete set
{a}(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}B_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{Z}
, defined by
\widehat{\iota}({a}(B_{i}))=\iota(i)
, is strictly increasing. Assume further that one of the following holds

(a)

H^{1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})=0
, or

(b)

for every
i
, the codimension zero family
B_{i}
has either
\partial^{-}B_{i}=\emptyset
or
\partial^{+}B_{i}=\emptyset
, see (
92
).

Then there is a spectral sequence
E_{p,q}^{r}
converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
, whose first page equals

(102)

E_{p,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{\{i:\iota(i)=p\}}H_{d+p+q+\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

where
d=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim M
. Moreover, there is an analogous spectral sequence converging to the Floer homology
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
, whose first page is as above with
\partial^{+}B_{i}
replaced by
\partial^{+}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{M}B_{i}
.

Remark 6.4
.

The technical assumptions
(a)
or
(b)
are introduced to simplify the construction below, and most likely can be omitted. Nonetheless, working under either of these assumptions is sufficient for our purposes. Indeed, as we will see in Proposition
7.4
(a)
, if
\phi
is the
“radius zero”
symplectic monodromy, given by the flow of the vector field (
48
), then all its codimension zero families of fixed points satisfy
\partial^{-}B_{i}=\emptyset
, so condition
(b)
holds. Moreover, in the proof of Theorem
1.1
the Liouville domain
M
will be (diffeomorphic to) the Milnor fiber of an isolated hypersurface singularity
f\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
, so for
n\geqslant 3
it will also satisfy
H^{1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})=0
, i.e. condition
(a)
.

We note that each of these assumptions can be replaced by the following, weaker one. Consider a directed graph
\Gamma
whose vertices are connected components of
M\setminus\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
, and for two such connected components, say
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, there is an edge from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
whenever there is an
i
such that the closure
\overline{C}_{1}
meets
\partial^{-}B_{i}
, and the closure
\overline{C}_{2}
meets
\partial^{+}B_{i}
. Now, we assume that the graph
\Gamma
has no loops.

Remark 6.5
.

The original McLean spectral sequence introduced in
[
McL19
, (HF3)]
converges to Floer cohomology. Its first page is
E_{1}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\{i:\iota(i)=p\}}H_{d-p-q-\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(B_{i};\mathbb{Z})
. In the setting of
[
McL19
]
, this first page (tensored by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) is dual to the first page (
102
).

To see this, we recall from Remark
5.26
that in
[
McL19
]
each codimension zero family
B_{i}
has by assumption
\partial^{-}B_{i}=\emptyset
. Moreover,
[
McL19
, (HF3)]
is formulated for a small slope deformation of
\phi
, which forces
\partial^{M}B_{i}=\emptyset
. Thus each
B_{i}
in loc. cit. has
\partial B_{i}=\partial^{+}B_{i}
, so the group
H_{d-p-q-\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(B_{i})
is isomorphic, by Lefschetz duality, to
H^{d+p+q+\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i})
. By the universal coefficient theorem, the latter is dual to
H_{d+p+q+\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i})
, which is the group in (
102
), as claimed.

We have chosen the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
coefficients for convenience, since the same choice is made in our references
[
Ulj17
,
AD14
]
. It is enough for most applications, including the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

For the proof of Proposition
6.3
, the general idea is the following: choose a Hamiltonian perturbation of
\phi
such that the fixed points become non-degenerate and contained in the union
\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
. Prove that any Floer trajectory connecting two fixed points contained in the same component
B_{i}
is contained in
B_{i}
too. Then, since
\phi|_{B_{i}}
equals the identity, following the technique of the classical proof that Hamiltonian Floer homology coincides with ordinary homology, it is possible to identify the pieces of the first page of the spectral sequence. For the precise implementation of this scheme a couple of technicalities need to be taken into account.

A confinement lemma
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
of pseudo-holomorphic curves due to McLean is used: it allows to confine Floer trajectories in a small neighborhood
N_{B_{i}}
of
B_{i}
rather than in
B_{i}
. Since the almost complex structure
J_{t}
needs to be
\phi
-periodic, and
\phi|_{N_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}}
is not the identity, it is not possible to choose
J_{t}
independent of time in
\phi|_{N_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}}
. On the other hand, for the proof of the classical fact that Hamiltonian Floer homology coincides with ordinary homology, an almost complex structure independent of time is used in order to prove that Floer trajectories associated with small time independent Hamiltonians are in fact Morse trajectories. In order to deal with this we will produce first a Hamiltonian deformation of
\phi
whose fixed points is a disjoint union of codimension zero families of fixed points
B^{\prime}_{i}
, and each
B^{\prime}_{i}
is strictly contained in the interior of
B_{i}
. Then a comparably smaller second deformation will produce non-degenerate fixed points inside
\bigsqcup_{i}B^{\prime}_{i}
, and such that any Floer trajectory connecting two fixed points in the same
B^{\prime}_{i}
is contained in
B^{\prime}_{i}
. This way, a
\phi
-periodic complex structure independent of
t
in
\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
will suffice to identify the groups appearing in the first page of the spectral sequence. In the rest of this section we carry out the proof following the program just hinted.

6.3.1.
Technical preparations

Our first aim is to provide a small Hamiltonian perturbation of
\phi
, which is well suited to apply the confinement lemma
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
; and a similar perturbation of the identity, which we will later use to compare the Floer complex with pieces of the Morse complex.

Step 1

By Lemma
5.27
, we can and do assume that each
B_{i}
is a connected codimension zero submanifold of
M
with boundary, i.e. it does not have corners.

Choose a collar structure
\Psi:(-\eta,0]\times\partial M\longrightarrow M
near the boundary of
M
such that
\Psi^{*}\lambda=e^{r}\lambda|_{\partial M}
, see (
93
), and
\phi=\mathrm{id}
in the image of
\Psi
. Choose a small negative slope
a
and consider a smooth decreasing function
\sigma\colon[-\eta,0]\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that is constant near
-\eta
and equal to the linear function
r\mapsto ar
near
0
. Define a time-independent Hamiltonian
H
by
H=\sigma\circ\mathrm{pr}_{(-\eta,0]}\circ\Psi^{-1}
in the image of
\Psi
, and
H=\sigma(-\eta)
otherwise. It has small negative slope; and is
\phi
-periodic since it is constant whenever
\phi\neq\mathrm{id}
. Let
\phi^{\prime}\coloneqq\phi\circ\psi_{1}^{H}
be the corresponding small negative slope deformation
\phi^{\prime}
of
\phi
. Now,
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi^{\prime})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}B_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, where each
B_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is a codimension zero family of fixed points, contained in
B_{i}
in such way that
\partial B_{i}^{\prime\prime}
does not intersect
\partial M
anymore. In terms of the decomposition (
92
), we have

\partial B_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\partial^{+}B_{i}^{\prime\prime}\sqcup\partial^{-}B_{i}^{\prime\prime},

\partial^{+}B_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\partial^{+}B_{i}
, and the triple
(B_{i}^{\prime\prime},\partial^{+}B_{i}^{\prime\prime},\partial^{-}B_{i}^{\prime\prime})
is homeomorphic to
(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i},\partial^{-}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{M}B_{i})
.

Notation 6.6
.

In the remaining part of the proof we will abuse notation and rename
B^{\prime\prime}_{i}=B_{i}
. This way,
\partial B_{i}=\partial^{+}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{-}B_{i}
.

Remark 6.7
.

In terms of Notation
6.6
, assumption
(b)
of Proposition
6.3
implies the following. Take
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
\partial^{+}B_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Then there is a collar structure
\Psi\colon(-\eta,0]\times\partial M\longrightarrow M
, and a level
c\in(-\eta,0)
, such that every connected component of
\partial^{-}B_{i}
is a connected component of
\Psi(\{c\}\times\partial M)
; and
\partial^{+}B_{i}
is disjoint from the collar
\Psi((-\eta,0]\times\partial M)
. Hence if some connected component of
\overline{M\setminus B_{i}}
, say
C
, meets
\partial^{-}B_{i}
, then
C
is contained in a collar near
\partial M
; in particular
C
does not meet
\partial^{+}B_{i}
.

For the next steps we need the following notion.

Definition 6.8
.

Let
F,G\colon U\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be smooth functions on an open subset
U\subset M
. We say that
F
and
G

grow compatibly in
U
if the level sets of
F
coincide with the level sets of
G
, and there is a smooth positive function
\sigma\colon U\longrightarrow(0,\infty)
, constant on each of those level sets, such that
dF=\sigma\cdot dG
.

Step 2

Let
H_{B_{i}}\colon N_{B_{i}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be the time independent Hamiltonian associated to a codimension zero family
B_{i}
of fixed points of
\phi^{\prime}
, see Figure
7
. Choose a compact collar neighborhood
C_{B_{i}}
of
\partial B_{i}
contained in
N_{B_{i}}
such that the restriction

H_{B_{i}}|_{\overline{C_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}}}:\overline{C_{B_{i}}\setminus{B_{i}}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

is topologically locally trivial. We claim that there is a smooth function
H^{\prime}_{i}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(a)

the restriction
H_{i}^{\prime}|_{C_{B_{i}}}
is topologically locally trivial and a submersion at the interior of
C_{B_{i}}
,

(b)

the functions
H^{\prime}_{i}
and
H_{B_{i}}
grow compatibly in the interior of
C_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}
, see Definition
6.8
,

(c)

the restriction of
H_{i}^{\prime}
to each connected component of
M\setminus C_{B_{i}}
is constant,

(d)

the function
H^{\prime}_{i}
is constant in
B^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\overline{B_{i}\setminus C_{B_{i}}}
and in each connected component of
\partial B_{i}
. Furthermore, we have
\min\{H^{\prime}_{i}(x):x\in\partial^{+}B_{i}\}>H^{\prime}_{i}(B^{\prime}_{i})>\max\{H^{\prime}_{i}(x):x\in\partial^{-}B_{i}\}
.

It is easy to produce a function
\widetilde{H}_{i}\colon U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying properties
(a)
–
(c)
in some neighborhood
U
of
C_{B_{i}}
. In order to obtain
H_{i}^{\prime}
satisfying
(a)
–
(c)
and defined everywhere on
M
, we consider two cases.

First, we assume that condition
(a)
holds, that is,
H^{1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})=0
. By property
(c)
, the
1
-form
d\widetilde{H}_{i}
extends by zero to a closed
1
-form on
M
, supported away from
\partial M
. Since
H^{1}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})=0
, this
1
-form is exact, i.e. there is a smooth function
H_{i}^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
dH_{i}^{\prime}=d\widetilde{H}_{i}^{\prime}
on
U
, and
dH_{i}^{\prime}=0
on
M\setminus U
. Thus
H_{i}^{\prime}
satisfies properties
(a)
–
(c)
, as needed.

Now, we assume that condition
(b)
holds. We can choose
\widetilde{H}_{i}
so that it is constant on
B_{i}\cap\partial C_{B_{i}}
. This way,
\widetilde{H}_{i}
extends to a smooth function
B_{i}\cup C_{B_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, constant on
B_{i}^{\prime}
. Write
\partial(B_{i}\cup C_{B_{i}})=\partial^{+}\sqcup\partial^{-}
, where
\partial^{+}
(respectively,
\partial^{-}
) lies in the union of connected components of
C_{B_{i}}
containing
\partial^{+}B_{i}
(respectively,
\partial^{-}B_{i}
). We can furthermore choose
\widetilde{H}_{i}
so that it is constant on
\partial^{+}
and on
\partial^{-}
. If
\partial^{+}B_{i}=\emptyset
then
\partial^{+}=\emptyset
, so
\widetilde{H}_{i}
is constant on
\partial(B_{i}\cup C_{B_{i}})
, and therefore it extends to a smooth function
H_{i}^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
(a)
–
(c)
, as needed. Assume
\partial^{+}B_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Then by Remark
6.7
, no connected component of
M\setminus(B_{i}\cup\operatorname{Int}C_{B_{i}})
meets both
\partial^{+}
and
\partial^{-}
. Hence
\widetilde{H}_{i}
is constant on the boundary of each connected component
M\setminus(B_{i}\cup\operatorname{Int}C_{B_{i}})
, so again it extends to a smooth function
H_{i}^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
(a)
–
(c)
, as required. Property
(d)
now follows from
(a)
–
(c)
.

B_{i}
B_{i}^{\prime}
C_{B_{i}}
C_{B_{i}}
\partial^{-}B_{i}
\partial^{+}B_{i}
H_{B_{i}}
H_{i}^{\prime}
B_{i}
B_{i}^{\prime}
C_{B_{i}}
C_{B_{i}}
\partial^{-}B_{i}
\partial^{+}B_{i}
H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
F_{i}

Figure 7
.
Auxiliary functions
H_{i}^{\prime}
,
H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
and
F_{i}
introduced in Steps
2
and
3
.

Since the restrictions of
H^{\prime}_{i}
and
H_{B_{i}}
to
\operatorname{Int}C_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}
grow compatibly, there is a function
g\colon\operatorname{Int}C_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}\longrightarrow(0,\infty)
, constant on the level sets of
H^{\prime}_{i}
, such that
gX^{H_{i}^{\prime}}=X^{H_{B_{i}}}
. Since
X^{H_{B_{i}}}
is zero on
B_{i}
, and
X^{H_{i}^{\prime}}
does not vanish on
\partial B_{i}
, the function
g
extends by zero to a smooth function
N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
, where
N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}\coloneqq B_{i}\cup\operatorname{Int}C_{B_{i}}
.

Fix
r_{i}>0
and put
H^{\prime\prime}_{i}=(r_{i}+g)H^{\prime}_{i}
. Then
H^{\prime\prime}_{i}\colon N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a time independent Hamiltonian such that we have the equality
\psi^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}_{1}=\psi^{H_{B_{i}}}_{1}{\circ}\psi^{r_{i}H^{\prime}_{i}}_{1}
. Indeed, at a level set of
H^{\prime}_{i}
the composition on the right hand side moves a point first time
r_{i}
along an integral curve of
X^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
, and then time
1
along an integral curve of
X^{H_{B_{i}}}
. Moving time
1
along an integral curve of
X^{H_{B_{i}}}
is the same than moving time
g
along an integral curve of
X^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
(recall that
g
is constant on those integral curves), so the total effect is moving time
r_{i}+g
along an integral curve of
X^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
.

We claim that if
r_{i}
is positive and small enough the set of fixed points of
\psi^{X^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}}_{1}
in
N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}
is equal to
B^{\prime}_{i}
. Indeed,
B^{\prime}_{i}
is obviously contained in the fixed point set of
\psi^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}_{1}
. To check the opposite inclusion we notice that, since the time
1
flow of
X^{H_{B_{i}}}
coincides with the time
1
flow of
X^{gH^{\prime}_{i}}
, and has no fixed points in
C_{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}
, there exists a positive
r_{i}
such that the time
1
flow of
X^{(r_{i}+g)H^{\prime}_{i}}
has no fixed points in
C_{B_{i}}
, either. We fix such
r_{i}
for the rest of the section.

Define
H^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}H^{\prime}_{i}
. The composition
\phi^{\prime\prime}:=\phi^{\prime}{\circ}\psi_{1}^{H^{\prime}}
is an exact symplectomorphism whose fixed point set is the disjoint union of codimension zero families of fixed points
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{\prime}_{i}
. For every
i
we have constructed a Hamiltonian
H^{\prime\prime}_{i}\colon N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\phi^{\prime\prime}|_{N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}}
is equal to the time
1
flow
\psi^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}_{1}
.

We note that, under the weaker assumption of Remark
6.4
, it might not be possible to construct individual functions
H_{i}^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. However, it is equally easy to construct a global function
H^{\prime}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and local
H_{i}^{\prime\prime}\colon N_{B_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
; and this is all we need in the sequel. We leave the details to the reader.

Step 3

For each
i
choose a function
F_{i}:B_{i}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(a)

F_{i}
admits a smooth extension
F_{i}:M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
by a constant function equal to
0
in
M\setminus B_{i}
,

(b)

the restriction of
F_{i}
to
\operatorname{Int}(C_{B_{i}}\cap{B}_{i})
has no critical points,

(c)

the functions
F_{i}
and
H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
grow compatibly in
\operatorname{Int}(C_{B_{i}}\cap{B}_{i})
,

(d)

the critical points of
F_{i}|_{B_{i}}
are of Morse type,

(e)

for every
t\in(0,1]
, the only
t
-periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian flow of
F_{i}
at the interior of
B_{i}
are constant and equal to the Morse points.

It is easy to produce a function with properties
(a)
–
(c)
. A small generic perturbation of such a function which does not modify it at
\operatorname{Int}(C_{B_{i}}\cap{B}_{i})
achieves property
(d)
. Property
(e)
is achieved multiplying any function with properties
(a)
–
(d)
by a sufficiently small positive constant, as a consequence of
[
AD14
, Proposition 6.1.5]
.

Define
F:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}F_{i}
. For
\delta>0
small enough define
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}\coloneqq\phi^{\prime\prime}{\circ}\psi_{1}^{\delta F}
. In the same way that we proved above the existence of a time independent Hamiltonian
H^{\prime\prime}_{i}
satisfying
\psi^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}_{1}=\psi^{H_{B_{i}}}_{1}{\circ}\psi^{r_{i}H^{\prime}_{i}}_{1}
, here we prove that there exists a time independent Hamiltonian
H_{\delta}
such that
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}=\phi{\circ}\psi_{1}^{H_{\delta}}
. So, we have that
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a negative slope Hamiltonian perturbation of
\phi
. Moreover, if
\delta>0
is small enough, that slope is small, too, and the fixed point set of
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}
