is the disjoint union of the Morse points of
F_{i}
at the interior of
B^{\prime}_{i}
. Indeed, by property
(e)
of
F_{i}
the fixed points of
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}
contained in
B_{i}^{\prime}
are the Morse points of
F_{i}
. Outside
\bigsqcup_{i}N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}
,
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}
coincides with
\phi^{\prime}
, so it has no fixed points there. It remains to show that
\phi_{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has no fixed points in
\bigsqcup_{i}C_{B_{i}^{\prime\prime}}
. This follows, if
\delta>0
is small enough, by the same arguments that proved above that if
r_{i}>0
is small enough the set of fixed points of
\psi^{H^{\prime\prime}_{i}}_{1}
is equal to
B^{\prime}_{i}
.

Step 4

In the three steps described up to now we have provided a Hamiltonian perturbation of
\phi
that is well suited to analyze McLean’s spectral sequence. In the next one we run a similar procedure for the identity symplectomorphism in
M
, with the later aim of producing a spectral sequence similar to McLean’s. To prove Proposition
6.3
, we will compare the first pages of both spectral sequences.

Consider a smooth function
G\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with the following properties:

(a)

we have
G|_{N_{B_{i}}^{\prime}}=H^{\prime\prime}_{i}
,

(b)

the restriction of
G
to
M\setminus(\bigsqcup_{i}N_{B_{i}}^{\prime})
is a Morse function, and moreover the set of values of
G
at the Morse points of
G|_{M\setminus(\bigsqcup_{i}N_{B_{i}}^{\prime})}
is disjoint from the finite set
\{G(B^{\prime}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
.

We consider the exact symplectomorphism
\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}:=\psi^{\delta G}_{1}{\circ}\psi_{1}^{\delta F}
for
\delta>0
. As before, we prove that there is a time independent Hamiltonian
K_{\delta}
such that
\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}=\psi^{K_{\delta}}_{1}
, so that
\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}
is a small negative slope Hamiltonian deformation of
\mathrm{id}_{M}
. Moreover,
K_{\delta}
is a Morse function in
M
which satisfies
K_{\delta^{\prime}}=\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\delta}K_{\delta}
. Therefore, by choosing
\delta
small enough we may assume that the fixed points
\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}
coincide with the Morse points of
K_{\delta}

[
AD14
, Proposition 6.1.5]
. The function
K_{\delta}
satisfies also the following properties:

(c)

\min\{K_{\delta}(x):x\in\partial^{+}B_{i}\}>\max\{K_{\delta}(x):x\in\partial^{-}B_{i}\}
.

(d)

The functions
K_{\delta}
and
H^{\prime\prime}_{i}
grow compatibly in
\operatorname{Int}(C_{B_{i}}\cap B_{i})
.

For
(c)
notice that since
F_{i}|_{\partial B_{i}}\equiv 0
, we have
K_{\delta}|_{\partial B_{i}}=\delta G|_{\partial B_{i}}=\delta H^{\prime\prime}_{i}|_{\partial B_{i}}=\delta(r_{i}+g)H^{\prime}_{i}|_{\partial B_{i}}=\delta r_{i}H^{\prime}_{i}|_{\partial B_{i}}
. Hence property
(d)
of Step
2
implies
(c)
. Property
(d)
is a consequence of property
(c)
of Step
3
.

Step 5

Let
J_{t},J^{\prime}
be cylindrical
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures such that
J_{t}
is
\phi
-periodic,
J^{\prime}
is time-independent and they have the following additional properties:

(a)

J_{t}
is independent of
t
in
\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
and we have the equality

(103)

J_{t}|_{\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}}=J^{\prime}|_{\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}}.

(b)

the pair
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is Morse-Smale for any
\delta>0
(that is, the linearized Morse operator at each gradient line connecting two Morse points of
K_{\delta}
is surjective, see
[
AD14
, Theorem 10.1.5]
),

(c)

for each
i
the pair
(F|_{B^{\prime}_{i}},J|_{B^{\prime}_{i}})
is Morse-Smale at the interior of
B^{\prime}_{i}

In order to construct
J_{t}
and
J^{\prime}
we choose, following
[
AD14
, §10]
, a time independent
J^{\prime}
satisfying property
(b)
and then choose any cylindrical
\omega
-compatible and
\phi
-periodic
J_{t}
satisfying property
(a)
. Property
(c)
holds because
F|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
is proportional by a non-zero constant factor to
K_{\delta}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
.

6.3.2.
The two Floer complexes

Having fixed
H_{\delta}
(for
\delta>0
small enough) and
J_{t}
with the properties of the previous section, following
[
AD14
, §8]
we find that there exists a time dependent perturbation
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
of the Hamiltonian
H_{\delta}
, which is as close as we wish to
H_{\delta}
, does not modify
H_{\delta}
in a neighborhood of the fixed points of
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}
and in a neighborhood of
\partial M
, and such that
(\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
is regular for
\phi
, see Definition
6.1
. Then the
\phi
-twisted loop Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
introduced in Section
6.2.3
is well defined and, by Proposition
6.2
, its homology is
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
.

We define
\check{\phi}_{\delta}:=\phi{\circ}\psi_{1}^{\check{H}_{\delta,t}}
and
\widetilde{J}_{t}\coloneqq(\psi_{1}^{\check{H}_{\delta,t}})^{*}J_{t}
, see formula (
101
). Then the
\check{\phi}_{\delta}
-fixed point Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\check{\phi}_{\delta},\widetilde{J}_{t})
introduced in Section
6.2.5
is well defined and, as we have seen in Section
6.2.6
, its homology is
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
, too.

Similarly, there is a time dependent perturbation
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
of
K_{\delta}
which does not modify
K_{\delta}
near the critical points of
G
, such that
(\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
is regular for the identity, see Definition
6.1
. Then the
\mathrm{id}
-twisted loop Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
is well defined, and its homology is
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\mathrm{id},+)
.

As before, putting
\widetilde{J}_{t}^{\prime}=(\psi_{1}^{\check{K}_{\delta,t}})^{*}J^{\prime}
, we see that the
\psi_{1}^{\check{K}_{\delta,t}}
-fixed point Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}(\psi_{1}^{\check{K}_{\delta,t}},\widetilde{J}^{\prime}_{t})
is well defined and computes the same homology as
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
.

The perturbation procedure described in
[
AD14
, §8]
makes clear that the perturbations
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
and
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
can be chosen so that they coincide in
\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
, that is,

(104)

\check{K}_{\delta,t}|_{\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}}=\check{H}_{\delta,t}|_{\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}}.

The idea is to replace the space of perturbations of the Hamiltonian, denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}(H_{0})
in
[
AD14
, §8]
, by a Banach space of perturbations
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}(H_{\delta},K_{\delta})
whose elements are pairs
(h,k)
where
h:M\times{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a
\phi
-periodic time dependent Hamiltonian,
k:M\times{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a periodic time dependent Hamiltonian and
h
and
k
coincide in
\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
. Then it is possible to prove like in
[
AD14
, §8]
that the locus of
(h,k)
such that
(H_{\delta}+h,J_{t})
and
(K_{\delta}+k,J^{\prime})
are both regular pairs is dense in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}(H_{\delta},K_{\delta})
.

6.3.3.
The McLean spectral sequence

We now use the action filtration introduced in Section
6.2.4
to define the McLean spectral sequence.

Recall that the action function
{a}
of
\phi
is constant in each codimension zero family
B_{i}
of fixed points of
\phi
. We have denoted its value by
{a}(B_{i})
. Choose a positive number
\xi
such that
\xi<\tfrac{1}{3}\min\{|{a}(B_{i})-{a}(B_{j})|:{a}(B_{i})\neq{a}(B_{j})\}
.
Let
\iota
be the indexing function from the statement of Proposition
6.3
. For an integer
p
put
I_{p}=\iota^{-1}(p)
and

a_{p}\coloneqq\max\{{a}(B_{i}):\iota(i)\leqslant p\}
. Then for every
i\in I_{p}
we have
{a}(B_{i})=a_{p}
. Put
b_{p}=a_{p}+\xi
. Clearly,
b_{p}\leqslant b_{q}
if
p\leqslant q
.

Put
F_{p}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})\coloneqq F^{\leqslant b_{p}}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
, where the right hand side is defined in Section
6.2.4
. This is an increasing filtration by subcomplexes of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
. The
McLean spectral sequence

E_{p,q}^{r}
is the spectral sequence associated with this filtration. We have
E_{p,q}^{r}\Rightarrow\operatorname{HF}_{p+q}(\phi,+)
.

Recall from Section
6.2.3
that the complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
is generated as a graded vector space by the set
\mathcal{P}_{\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t}}
of Hamiltonian
\phi
-twisted loops. Since we have chosen
\delta
and
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
small, the image of every
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t}}
is contained in some
B^{\prime}_{i}
.

We have that
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\delta}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
is equal to the time
1
Hamiltonian flow of
H_{\delta}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
, which is equal to
\delta F_{i}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
. Then, by property
(e)
of
F_{i}
, the only
1
-periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian flow of
H_{\delta}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
are constant and equal to the Morse points of
F_{i}
. According to the procedure used to define the small perturbation
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
following
[
AD14
, §8]
, we have that the only
\phi
-twisted Hamiltonian loops for the Hamiltonian
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
which are contained in
B^{\prime}_{i}
are the same as those of
H_{\delta}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
. Hence each
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t}}
is constant and equal to a Morse point of
F_{i}
in
B_{i}^{\prime}
, for some index
i
. Formula (
96
) shows that the action
\mathcal{A}_{\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t}}
of such point belongs to
({a}(B_{i})-\xi,{a}(B_{i})+\xi)
. We thus get an isomorphism of graded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector spaces

(105)

F_{p}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})/F_{p-1}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})\cong\bigoplus_{i\in I_{p}}C^{i}_{*},

where
C^{i}_{*}
is a subspace of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
generated by the Morse points of
F_{i}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
. Lemma
6.9
below implies that each
C^{i}_{*}
is in fact a subcomplex of
F_{p}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})/F_{p-1}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
, hence (
105
) is an isomorphism of chain complexes.

In order to compute the first page of McLean’s spectral sequence we will compare it with the first page of a similar spectral sequence associated with a filtration in the
\mathrm{id}
-twisted loop Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
which we introduce now. Let
a^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<a^{\prime}_{m}
be the critical values of
G
; notice that some of these values are equal to
G(B_{i}^{\prime})
. Let
I_{j}^{\prime}
be the set of those indices
i
such that
a_{j}^{\prime}=G(B_{i}^{\prime})
.

Since
\delta
is as small as we wish, we have that
K_{\delta}
and
G
are as close as we wish in the maximum norm. Therefore we may assume that the critical values of
K_{\delta}
belong to
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}(a^{\prime}_{i}-\xi^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{i}+\xi^{\prime})
, for a fixed
\xi^{\prime}>0
such that
\xi^{\prime}<\tfrac{1}{3}\min\{|a^{\prime}_{i}-a^{\prime}_{j}|:i\neq j\}
.

Fix
b^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<b^{\prime}_{m}
such that
b^{\prime}_{m}>a^{\prime}_{m}+\xi^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(a^{\prime}_{i}-a^{\prime}_{i-1})
for
i<m^{\prime}
. We define an increasing filtration by subcomplexes of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
by
F_{j}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})\coloneqq F^{\leqslant b^{\prime}_{j}}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
, where as in Section
6.2.4
,
F^{\leqslant b^{\prime}_{j}}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
is the subcomplex generated by Hamiltonian
\mathrm{id}
-twisted loops whose action is bounded from above by
b^{\prime}_{j}
.

As before, since the perturbations
K_{\delta}
and
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
are small, we conclude that the set of Hamiltonian
\mathrm{id}
-twisted loops
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t}}
coincides with the set of Morse points of
K_{\delta}
. Since the action function for the identity symplectomorphism can be taken vanishing identically, Formula (
96
) shows that the action
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t}}
of such a point
x
belongs to
(a^{\prime}_{j}-\xi^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{j}+\xi^{\prime})
for a certain index
j
. If
I^{\prime}_{j}\neq\emptyset
, property
(b)
of
G
shows that
x
is a critical point of
F_{i}|_{B_{i}^{\prime}}
for some
i\in I_{j}^{\prime}
. We conclude that, whenever
I_{j}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we have an isomorphism of graded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector spaces

(106)

F_{j}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})/F_{j-1}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})\cong\bigoplus_{i\in I_{j}^{\prime}}D^{i}_{*},

where
D^{i}_{*}
is a subspace of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
generated by the Morse points of
F_{i}|_{B^{\prime}_{i}}
. As before, Lemma
6.9
shows that (
106
) is an isomorphism of chain complexes.

We claim that we have an isomorphism of chain complexes

(107)

D_{*}^{i}=C_{*-\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}^{i}.

By definition, both underlying
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector spaces are spanned by the Morse points of
F_{i}|_{B_{i}^{\prime}}
, so they coincide. The grading of such a point, say
x
, in
C_{*}^{i}
is
-\operatorname{CZ}_{\phi\circ\psi}(x)
, where
\psi
is the time one flow of
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
. By (
104
),
\psi
coincides in
B_{i}
with the time one flow of
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
. Hence the grading of
x
in
D_{*}^{i}
is
-\operatorname{CZ}_{\psi}(x)
.
Lemma
5.11
(b)
shows that
\operatorname{CZ}_{\phi\circ\psi}(x)=\operatorname{CZ}_{\phi}(x)+\operatorname{CZ}_{\psi}(x)=\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})+\operatorname{CZ}_{\psi}(x)
. Thus the grading of
x
in
C_{*}^{i}
equals the grading of
x
in
D_{*}^{i}
minus
\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})
, which gives the required degree shift.

It remains to show that the differentials in complexes
D^{i}_{*}
and
C^{i}_{*}
are equal. They are computed by counting, respectively,
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
- and
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
- Floer trajectories connecting the Morse points of
F_{i}|_{B_{i}^{\prime}}
. By the confinement Lemma
6.9
below, these trajectories are contained in
B_{i}
. There, we have equalities
\phi|_{B_{i}}=\mathrm{id}_{B_{i}}
,
J_{t}|_{B_{i}}=J^{\prime}|_{B_{i}}
, see (
103
), and
\check{K}_{\delta,t}|_{B_{i}}=\check{H}_{\delta,t}|_{B_{i}}
, see (
104
). Hence the Floer trajectories counted by both differentials coincide, and we get isomorphism (
107
), as needed.

In the above proof, we have used the following confinement lemma for pseudo-holomorphic curves due to McLean, see
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Fix any index
p
. If
\delta>0
and the perturbation
\check{H}_{\delta,t}
are chosen small enough, for any two Morse points
q_{1},q_{2}
of
F
which are contained in
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
, we have that any
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\phi,\check{H}_{\delta,t},J_{t})
-Floer trajectory from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
has image completely contained in
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B_{i}
.

Similarly, if
\delta>0
and the perturbation
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
are chosen small enough, for any two Morse points
q_{1},q_{2}
of
F
which are contained in
\bigsqcup_{i\in I^{\prime}_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
, any
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
-Floer trajectory from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
has image completely contained in
\bigsqcup_{i\in I^{\prime}_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
.

For the first case the Lemma coincides exactly with
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
, applied to
\phi^{\prime\prime}
, where
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
plays the role of codimension zero family
B
of fixed points and
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B_{i}
is the neighborhood where
\phi^{\prime}
is Hamiltonian, except that loc. cit. is formulated for
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{pt}}_{*}
-Floer trajectories. The case of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}
-Floer trajectories is reduced to McLean’s setting by the procedure described in Section
6.2.6
. Moreover, in loc. cit.
B
is assumed to be connected, which is not the case for
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
. However, what is actually used in the proof is not the connectedness of
B
, but the fact that the action function
{a}
is constant on
B
: this is satisfied by
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{p}}B^{\prime}_{i}
by definition of
I_{p}
.

For the second case notice that by
[
AD14
, Proposition 10.1.9]
, if
\delta
is small enough the solutions of the perturbed Floer Cauchy–Riemann equation (
98
) for
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
, i.e.
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
-Floer trajectories, are time-independent, and hence equal to gradient trajectories for
-K_{\delta}
. By Property
(c)
of Step
4
a gradient trajectory starting and ending in
B^{\prime}_{i}
can not escape
B_{i}
. Moreover, by Property
(d)
of Step
4
, a gradient trajectory cannot escape
B^{\prime}_{i}
either, and since the critical points of
K_{\delta}
are contained at the interior of
B^{\prime}_{i}
we conclude that all gradient trajectories are contained at the interior of
B^{\prime}_{i}
. This proves the confinement of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
-Floer trajectories. Now if the perturbation
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
is chosen small enough, confinement of
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})
-Floer trajectories follows from Gromov compactness.

∎

Remark 6.10
.

The proof of
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
uses a version of Gromov compactness due to Fish
[
Fis11
, Theorem 3.1]
. The latter is a substantial generalization of
[
IS00
]
. However, the proof of
[
McL19
, Lemma C.2]
remains valid with the reference to
[
Fis11
]
replaced by
[
IS00
]
.

6.3.4.
Computation of the first page

To prove Proposition
6.3
, we need to compute the homology of the complex
C_{*}^{i}
appearing in the formula (
105
). Using the isomorphism (
107
), it is enough to compute the homology of the complex
D_{*}^{i}
, defined in (
106
).

We need to show that the homology of
D_{*}^{i}
is the relative homology of
(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i})
, up to a degree shift. For this we follow the proof that
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\mathrm{id}_{M},+)=H_{*-d}(M,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
via continuation maps in Floer theory. In
[
Ulj17
]
it is remarked that our case (in which
M
is a Liouville domain) is treated like in the classical proof that Hamiltonian Floer homology for compact
M
with vanishing second homotopy group, equals ordinary homology shifted by the dimension. This proof is fully explained in
[
AD14
]
.

We recall the needed details here. In
[
AD14
, §10]
it is proved that since the pair
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is Morse-Smale, the Morse complex of
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is well defined. Since we have
\xi K_{\delta}=K_{\xi\delta}
, if
\delta
is chosen small enough, the
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
-Floer trajectories are independent of
t
, see
[
AD14
, Proposition 10.1.9]
. Hence the pair
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is regular for
\mathrm{id}
. The
\mathrm{id}
-twisted Floer complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is well defined and coincides with the Morse complex of
(K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
.

In
[
AD14
, §11]
, using Floer’s continuation maps, a quasi-isomorphism

(108)

\Phi\colon\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})\longrightarrow\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})

is defined. The action filtration
F_{j}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
is defined for the complex
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
in a similar way as above. We claim that the quasi-isomorphism (
108
) can be defined in such a way that it respects the action filtration in both sides. To prove the claim we recall some ingredients involved in the construction of the continuation quasi-isomorphism. First we define continuation data
L:M\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, where
L
is a function such that
{L}(p,t,s)=K_{\delta}(p)
for
s\leqslant-1
and
{L}(p,t,s)\coloneqq\check{K}_{\delta,t}(p)
for
s\geqslant-1
. Then a small generic perturbation
\widetilde{L}:M\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is constructed, without modifying outside
s\in[-1,1]
, and from it
\Phi
is constructed by counting pseudo-holomorphic curves
u\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow M\times\mathbb{R}
. The point is that the pseudo-holomorphic curves satisfy the energy bound provided in
[
AD14
, Proposition 11.1.2]
. If a pseudo-holomorphic curve
u
connects
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
then its energy is bounded by

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{id},K_{\delta}}(q_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t}}(q_{2})+c,

where
c
is a constant determined in
[
AD14
, p. 387]
: it is enough that
c
bounds
\partial\widetilde{L}/\partial s
. Choosing
K_{\delta}
and its perturbation
\check{K}_{\delta,t}
small, the perturbation
\widetilde{L}
can be chosen small, too. So we can set
c
as small as we wish, and doing so makes it clear that
\Phi
preserves the action filtration.

As a consequence we obtain a quasi-isomorphism

F_{j}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})/F_{j-1}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},K_{\delta},J^{\prime})\longrightarrow F_{j}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime})/F_{j-1}\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}(\mathrm{id},\check{K}_{\delta,t},J^{\prime}).

Assume
I^{\prime}_{j}\neq\emptyset
. The complex on the right-hand side equals
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{j}^{\prime}}D^{i}_{*}
, see (
106
). The complex on the left-hand side is defined analogously, as a vector space spanned by those critical points of
K_{\delta}
whose action lies in the interval
(a_{j}^{\prime}-\xi,a_{j}^{\prime}+\xi)
. They are precisely the Morse points of
K_{\delta}
contained in
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}^{\prime}}B_{i}^{\prime}
. Since
K_{\delta}
and
J^{\prime}
are time-independent, the Floer equation (
98
) used to define the differential in
\operatorname{CF}^{\textnormal{loop}}_{*}
reduces to the Morse equation used to define the differential in the Morse complex for
(-K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
, see
[
AD14
, §10.1]
or
[
SZ92
, Theorems 7.1 and 7.3]
: note that the sign of the Hamiltonian gets switched as explained in Section
5.1
. Therefore,
D_{*}^{i}
corresponds to a piece of the Morse complex for
(-K_{\delta},J^{\prime})
generated by the Morse points contained in
B_{i}
. This piece computes the relative homology of
(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i})
. Indeed, in the decomposition (
92
), the gradient of
-K_{\delta}
points inwards
\partial^{+}B
and outwards
\partial^{-}B_{i}
, so the claim follows from
[
Rot14
, (2.2)]
. Using Lemma
5.11
(a)
to compute the grading, we get

H_{*}(D^{i}_{*})=H_{*+d}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i}),

cf.
[
Sei96
, p. 834]
.
Together with the formulas (
105
) and (
107
), this gives

E_{p,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{p}}H_{p+q}(C_{*}^{i})=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{p}}H_{p+q+\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})}(D^{i}_{*})=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{p}}H_{p+q+\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})+d}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i}),

as claimed in Proposition
6.3
.

To get the analogous spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,-)
, we need to replace the small negative slope by a small positive one. This way, in Step
1

\partial^{M}B_{i}
becomes a part of
\partial^{+}B_{i}
, see Notation
6.6
, so at the end of the day we get relative homology of
B_{i}
modulo
\partial^{+}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{M}B_{i}
, as claimed.

7.
The A’Campo abstract contact open book

In this section, we generalize McLean spectral sequence
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
, which converges to the fixed point Floer homology of the monodromy. We will formulate it in Proposition
7.1
after some preparations. To prove it, we will phrase Proposition
4.2
in the language of graded abstract contact open books. This way, in Section
7.2
we will get an isotopy between the natural abstract contact open book, constructed at (any small) positive radius in Section
5.5.2
(see Example
5.22
), and the one at radius zero, given by the flow of (
48
), which has good dynamical properties just like the topological A’Campo model
[
A’C75
]
, or McLean model resolution
[
McL19
]
, see Remark
7.5
. We summarize those properties in Proposition
7.4
, which is the main result of this section. In particular, we will see that the radius zero monodromy satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
6.3
, which provides the required spectral sequence.

7.1.
The generalized McLean spectral sequence for isolated singularities

We work with the monodromy graded abstract contact open book constructed in Example
5.20
for a single function (without parameters). Let us briefly recall its definition.

Let
Z
be a Stein manifold and let
\varrho\colon Z\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an exhaustive strictly plurisubharmonic function. Consider the Liouville form
\lambda_{Z}:=-d^{c}\varrho
, see Section
2.2
. Let
Y\subseteq Z
be a closed analytic subset whose all singularities are isolated, and, in case
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y=2
, their links are rational homology spheres. Let

f\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

be a holomorphic function such that
\operatorname{Sing}Y\subseteq f^{-1}(0)
. Assume that
0\in\mathbb{C}
is the only critical value of
f
, and all singularities of
f^{-1}(0)
are isolated. Then
f|_{f^{-1}(\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*})}
is a submersion for some
\delta>0
.

We choose regular values
\xi_{V}<\xi_{W}<\xi_{U}
of
\varrho|_{Y}
, and put
U_{Y}=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{U})\cap Y
,
V_{Y}=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{V})\cap Y
and
W_{Y}=\varrho^{-1}(-\infty,\xi_{W})\cap Y
. Now, Example
5.20
(with
\varepsilon=\eta=0
) provides a subset
N\subseteq Y
such that
f^{-1}(0)\cap\overline{N}=f^{-1}(0)\cap\overline{W}_{Y}
, and the restriction
f|_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
is a Liouville fibration, whose monodromy yields abstract contact open books

(109)

(N_{z},\lambda_{Z},\phi_{z}),

see (
91
). Note that the fiber
N_{z}
is diffeomorphic to
f^{-1}(z)\cap\overline{W}_{Y}
. If
Y=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\varrho(z)=\tfrac{\pi}{2}||z||^{2}
, and
W_{Y}
is a Milnor ball, then (
109
) is the abstract contact open book constructed in Example
5.22
.

Note also that in the formula (
109
) we abuse the notation and write
\lambda_{Z}
instead of its restriction
\lambda_{Z}|_{N_{z}}
. We will do so several times in this section, to keep the formulas concise.

We assume furthermore that

(110)

c_{1}(Y\setminus f^{-1}(0))=0.

Then, as explained in Remark
5.17
(b)
, a nonvanishing section of
K_{Y\setminus f^{-1}(0)}
endows the abstract contact open book (
109
) with a grading. Its graded isotopy type does not depend on
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
.

To state our results, we need to fix some additional data. Let
h^{\prime}\colon Z^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
be a log resolution of
(f,Y)
. That is,
h^{\prime}
is a proper modification such that, if
X
denotes the proper transform of
Y
, then
h\coloneqq h^{\prime}|_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a resolution of
f
. Put
D=(f\circ h)^{-1}(0)
. Then
X
is a smooth complex manifold, and
D_{\mathrm{red}}
is snc. We assume that
h
is an isomorphism away from
D
and near the preimage of the collar
\overline{U}_{Y}\setminus V_{Y}
. We write the irreducible decomposition of
D
as

D=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}}D_{i}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}}m_{i}D_{i},

where the first sum runs through the components of the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
, and the second one through exceptional components of
h
. For
i\in\mathcal{P}
we put
m_{i}=1
.

Fix an integer
m\geqslant 1
and assume that the log resolution
h
is
m
-separating
, that is,

(111)

\mbox{if }i\neq j\mbox{ and }D_{i}\cap D_{j}\neq\emptyset\mbox{ then }m_{i}+m_{j}>m.

It can always be achieved by composing
h^{\prime}
with further blowups, see
[
BFdB+22
, Lemma 2.9]
.

Since
Y
is Stein, we can fix an ample divisor
H
on
X
such that

(112)

H=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}}b_{i}D_{i}

for some negative integers
b_{i}
. For
i\in\mathcal{P}
we put
b_{i}=0
.

We will also need some additional notation. First, by assumption (
110
), we can write the canonical bundle of
X
as

(113)

K_{X}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}}a_{i}D_{i},

for some
discrepancies

a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that if
c_{1}(Y)=0
then we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\in\mathcal{P}
. We put

(114)

S_{m}=\{i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}:m_{i}|m\},\quad S_{m,p}=\{i\in S_{m}:p=\frac{m}{m_{i}}b_{i}\}.

Note that
\mathcal{P}\subseteq S_{m,0}
.

Fix
i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}
. Recall from (
11
) the notation
X_{i}^{\circ}=D_{i}\setminus(D-D_{i})
. Put
D_{i}^{\circ}=X_{i}^{\circ}\cap\overline{W}_{X}
, where
W_{X}=h^{-1}(W_{Y})
. We now introduce a natural
m_{i}
-fold covering

\nu_{i}\colon B_{i}^{\circ}\longrightarrow D_{i}^{\circ},

following
[
DL02
, §2.3]
. It can be thought of as a piece of the fiber
f^{-1}(\delta)\cap\overline{W}_{Y}
which
“corresponds to”
the component
D_{i}
. To define
\nu_{i}
, fix any holomorphic chart
G_{X}\subseteq X
around a point of
X_{i}^{\circ}
, and let
\{z_{i}=0\}
be a local equation of
D_{i}
in
G_{X}
. Then
f|_{G_{X}}=\mu\cdot z_{i}^{m_{i}}
for some
\mu\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}(G_{X})
. Define
\widetilde{G}_{X}=\{(z,x)\in\mathbb{C}\times(G_{X}\cap D_{i}^{\circ}):\mu(x)\cdot z^{m_{i}}=1\}
. Gluing these charts, one gets a topological covering
\nu_{i}\colon B_{i}^{\circ}\longrightarrow D_{i}^{\circ}
with Galois group
\mathbb{Z}_{m_{i}}
, as needed.

Note that if
i\in\mathcal{E}
, i.e.
D_{i}
is contracted by
h
, then
\nu_{i}
is exactly the
m_{i}
-fold covering of
X_{i}^{\circ}
considered in
[
DL02
, §2.3]
or
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
. If
i\in\mathcal{P}
, i.e.
D_{i}
is a proper transform of some component of
f^{-1}(0)
, then
\nu_{i}
is an isomorphism; and
B_{i}^{\circ}
can be identified with a submanifold
X_{i}^{\circ}\cap\overline{W}_{X}
of
X_{i}^{\circ}
, with boundary
D_{i}\cap\partial\overline{W}_{X}
. In other words, if
i\in\mathcal{P}
then
B_{i}^{\circ}
is diffeomorphic to
(h(D_{i})\cap\overline{W}_{Y})\setminus\operatorname{Bs}h^{-1}
, where
\operatorname{Bs}h^{-1}
is the locus where
h
is not an isomorphism.

With this notation at hand, we can formulate the first result of this section. We denote by
H^{BM}_{*}
the Borel–Moore homology with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

There is a spectral sequence
E_{p,q}^{r}
converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{z}^{m},+)
whose first page equals

(115)

E_{p,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{i\in S_{m,p}}H^{BM}_{n-1+p+q+2\frac{m}{m_{i}}(a_{i}+1)-2m}(B_{i}^{\circ}).

Proposition
7.1
will be proved in two steps. First, in Section
7.2
, we will use the lift of the radial vector field on
\mathbb{C}_{\log}
to isotope the abstract contact open book (
109
) to the abstract contact open book
(F,\lambda,\phi)
at radius zero, i.e. at the boundary of the A’Campo space; where
\phi
is the time one flow of the vector field (
48
). Next, in Section
7.3
we will identify
B_{i}^{\circ}
with the intersections
F\cap A_{i}^{\circ}
, see (
25
), which, as we will see, are codimension zero families of fixed points of
\phi
whenever
m_{i}|m
. The
m
-separating condition (
111
) will guarantee that
\phi
has no more fixed points. Once this is done, Proposition
7.1
will follow from Proposition
6.3
.

Remark 7.2
.

The spectral sequence (
115
) depends on the choice of the
m
-separated resolution
h
and on the ample divisor
H
. Since the limit
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{m},+)
does not depend on
h
and
H
, flexibility of these choices suggests that some Floer differentials in (
115
) should be zero.

One reason for such vanishing can be seen as follows. Since the action functional decreases along the Floer trajectories, see Section
6.2.4
, if we have an inequality of actions
{a}(B_{i}^{\circ})<{a}(B_{j}^{\circ})
then there are no Floer trajectories from
B_{i}^{\circ}
to
B_{j}^{\circ}
. Hence any differential from (a sub-quotient of)
H^{BM}_{*}(B_{i}^{\circ})
to
H^{BM}_{*}(B_{j}^{\circ})
vanishes. By Proposition
7.4
(d)
, the inequality
{a}(B_{i}^{\circ})<{a}(B_{j}^{\circ})
is equivalent to
\frac{b_{i}}{m_{i}}<\frac{b_{j}}{m_{j}}
.

For example, let
f
be an irreducible plane curve germ. Then the dual graph of
D
is a tree, with the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
as a root. Following the resolution process it is easy to see that, for any
H
, the quotients
\frac{b_{i}}{m_{i}}
decrease as we move away from the root. Thus if
D_{i}
lies closer to the root than
D_{j}
, all differentials in (
115
) from
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{i}^{\circ})
to
H^{BM}_{*}(B_{j}^{\circ})
vanish. However, in the opposite situation, that is if
D_{j}
lies closer to the root than
D_{i}
, the ampleness condition is restrictive enough so that we cannot guarantee the vanishing of differentials from
H^{BM}_{*}(B_{i}^{\circ})
to
H^{BM}_{*}(B_{j}^{\circ})
, see Example
7.3
.

This result is compatible with the corresponding one concerning topology of the
m
-th contact locus
{X}_{m}
of
f
. Recall that
[
BFdB+22
, Theorem 1.1]
provides a spectral sequence converging to
H^{*}_{c}({X}_{m})
whose page is dual (up to a shift) to (
115
), which leads to the
Arc-Floer conjecture

\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{m},+)\cong H^{*}_{c}({X}_{m})
, see Conjecture 1.5 loc. cit. The differentials
H_{*}(B_{j})\longrightarrow H_{*}(B_{i})
in the above spectral sequence count, roughly speaking, families of arcs lifting to
D_{i}
, whose limit lifts to
D_{j}
. In case of irreducible plane curves, the proof of
[
BLP23
, Theorem 3.4]
shows that there are no such families if
D_{i}
lies closer to the root than
D_{j}
, so the corresponding differential, again, is zero. But, in the opposite situation, if
D_{j}
lies closer to the root than
D_{i}
, the differential can be non-zero.

Example 7.3
.

Let
f
be a simple cusp
x^{2}-y^{3}
. By
[
BLP23
, p. 26]
we have
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{6},+)=H_{*-1}^{BM}(B^{\circ})
, where
B^{\circ}
is the entire Milnor fiber, i.e. a punctured torus. Thus
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{6},+)
equals
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in degrees
2
and
3
, and
0
in the remaining degrees. Let
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
be the minimal log resolution of
f
; it is
6
-separated. We now describe the spectral sequence (
115
) for various choices of ample divisors
H
on
X
.

Write
D=D_{0}+2D_{1}+3D_{2}+6D_{3}
, where
D_{0}
is the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
,
D_{3}
is the component of
\operatorname{Exc}h
meeting
D_{0}
, and
D_{1},D_{2}
are tips of
\operatorname{Exc}h
. Then
B_{1}^{\circ}
and
B_{2}^{\circ}
are disjoint unions of
3
and
2
disks, respectively; and
B_{3}^{\circ}
is a torus with six disks removed. Since
K_{X}=D_{1}+2D_{2}+4D_{3}
, by Proposition
7.4
(c)
we have
\operatorname{CZ}(B_{1}^{\circ})=\operatorname{CZ}(B_{2}^{\circ})=0
and
\operatorname{CZ}(B_{3}^{\circ})=-2
. A direct computation shows that a divisor
H=\sum_{i=1}^{3}b_{i}D_{i}
is ample if and only if
\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}},\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}<\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}
, see Remark
7.2
, and
2\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}}+3\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}>6\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}
.

Choose
H
so that
\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}}=\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}=\frac{-13}{6}
and
\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}=\frac{-12}{6}
(strictly speaking,
H
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor, but it is easy to see that (
115
) works as long as
6\cdot\frac{b_{i}}{m_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}
). Then the nonzero entries of the
E^{1}
page of (
115
) are
E^{1}_{-13,14}=H^{BM}_{2}(B_{1}^{\circ})\oplus H^{BM}_{2}(B_{2}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5}
,
E^{1}_{-12,14}=H^{BM}_{1}(B_{3}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{6}
, and
E^{1}_{-12,15}=H^{BM}_{2}(B_{3}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In particular, in agreement with Remark
7.2
, there is no differential from
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{1}^{\circ})
or
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{2}^{\circ})
to
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{3}^{\circ})
. On the other hand, there is a non-trivial differential in the opposite direction. Indeed, since we know that the limit
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{6},+)
is
0
in degree
1
, we see that the differential
E^{1}_{-12,14}\longrightarrow E^{1}_{-13,14}
is surjective, and the spectral sequence degenerates on the second page.

Similarly, choose
H
so that
\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}}=\frac{-13}{6}
,
\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}=\frac{-14}{6}
and
\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}=\frac{-12}{6}
. Then the nonzero entries on the
E^{1}
-page are
E^{1}_{-14,15}=H^{BM}_{2}(B_{2}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
,
E^{1}_{-13,14}=H^{BM}_{2}(B_{1}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
; and as before
E^{1}_{-12,14}=H^{BM}_{1}(B_{3}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{6}
,
E^{1}_{-12,15}=H^{BM}_{2}(B_{3}^{\circ})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The only nonzero differential on
E^{1}
is
E^{1}_{-12,14}\twoheadrightarrow E^{1}_{-13,14}
; on
E^{2}
it is
E^{2}_{-12,14}\twoheadrightarrow E^{2}_{-14,15}
and on
E^{3}
the spectral sequence degenerates. These differentials count Floer trajectories from
B_{3}^{\circ}
to
B_{1}^{\circ}
and
B_{2}^{\circ}
; there are no Floer trajectories from
B_{1}^{\circ}\sqcup B_{2}^{\circ}
to
B_{3}^{\circ}
.

For
\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}}=\frac{-14}{6}
,
\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}=\frac{-13}{6}
,
\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}=\frac{-12}{6}
, we get an analogous spectral sequence, with the roles of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
interchanged. For
\frac{b_{1}}{m_{1}}=\frac{-15}{6}
,
\frac{b_{2}}{m_{2}}=\frac{-13}{6}
,
\frac{b_{3}}{m_{3}}=\frac{-12}{6}
, the spectral sequence degenerates on
E^{4}
.

7.2.
Isotopy to radius zero

We will now construct the
“radius zero”
graded abstract contact open book
(F,\lambda,\phi)
, and show that it is isotopic to our original one (
109
). We keep the notation and assumptions from Section
7.1
.

Write
\widetilde{f}\coloneqq f\circ h
. Put
\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}=-d^{c}(\varrho\circ h)\in\Omega^{1}(X)
, i.e.
\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}
is the suitable restriction of the pullback of the
1
-form
\lambda_{Z}
.
Since
h|_{X\setminus D}
is an isomorphism, the form
\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}
makes
\widetilde{f}|_{h^{-1}(N)}
a Liouville fibration. Moreover, since
h
is an isomorphism near the collar, the collar trivialization chosen in Section
7.1
lifts to
X
. In particular, for each
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
we have a graded abstract contact open book
(\widetilde{N}_{z},\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y},\widetilde{\phi}_{z})\coloneqq(h^{-1}(N_{z}),\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y},h^{-1}\circ\phi_{z}\circ h)
isomorphic to (
109
). We will now modify the form
\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}|_{X\setminus D}
to a form
\lambda_{X}
such that
d\lambda_{X}
extends to a Kähler form on
X
.

Recall that, in formula (
112
), we have fixed an ample divisor
H
. Fix an integer
b>0
such that
bH
is very ample, and let
\jmath\colon X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\dim|bH|}
be the embedding given by
|bH|
. Let
\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}\in\Omega^{2}(X)
be the pullback of the Fubini-Study form through
\jmath
. The basis of
H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(bH))
used to define
\jmath
gives a hermitian metric on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(bH)
, see
[
Huy05
, Example 4.1.2(i)]
. Let
||\,\cdot\,||
be the corresponding norm. Choose a section
s
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(bH)
which is supported on
H
, i.e. with a pole of order
-bb_{i}>0
along
D_{i}
for each
i\in\mathcal{E}
, and no zeros or poles elsewhere.
Then by
[
Huy05
, Examples 4.3.9(iii) and 4.3.12]
, we have
\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=2\imath\cdot\overline{\partial}\partial\log||s||
. Since
d^{c}=\imath(\partial-\overline{\partial})
, see Section
2.2
, we have
dd^{c}=2\imath\cdot\overline{\partial}\partial
, so

\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=d\lambda_{\mathrm{FS}},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\lambda_{\mathrm{FS}}\coloneqq d^{c}\log||s||\in\Omega^{1}(X\setminus\operatorname{Exc}h).

We remark that the
1
-form
\lambda_{\mathrm{FS}}
is, up to scaling, the same form as the one used in
[
McL19
, Example 5.14]
: the sign difference results from varying definitions of
d^{c}
, explained in Section
2.2
.

For
t\geqslant 0
put
\lambda_{t}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}+t\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{FS}}\in\Omega^{1}(X\setminus\operatorname{Exc}h)
, and put
\omega_{t}\coloneqq d\lambda_{t}
. Then
\omega_{t}
is a Kähler form on
X\setminus D
for every
t\geqslant 0
, and on
X
for every
t>0
. In particular, since
f
is holomorphic, these forms are fiberwise symplectic over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
. Now, the map
(\widetilde{f},\mathrm{pr}_{[0,t_{0}]})\colon X\times[0,t_{0}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\times[0,t_{0}]
fits into Setting
5.12
, with
P=[0,t_{0}]
,
P^{\prime}=\{0\}
. Hence after possibly shrinking
t_{0}>0
, we get a subset
M\subseteq X\times[0,t_{0}]
such that the above map restricted to
M\setminus(D\times[0,t_{0}])
is a Liouville fibration over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}\times[0,t_{0}]
, with a collar trivialization which extends over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times[0,t_{0}]
, and agrees over
\mathbb{D}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
with the one chosen in Section
7.1
to define (
109
). Taking for
\Upsilon
the standard family of loops, the construction in Section
5.5
provides monodromy graded abstract contact open books
(M_{z,t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{z,t})
, see formula (
81
).

Since
(M_{z,0},\lambda_{0},\phi_{z,0})=(\widetilde{N}_{z},\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y},\widetilde{\phi}_{z})
, we infer that for every
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}^{*}
and every
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, the abstract contact open book
(M_{z,t},\lambda_{t},\phi_{z,t})
is graded isotopic to (
109
).

Put
\lambda_{X}=\lambda_{t_{0}}
,
\omega_{X}=d\lambda_{X}
. Then
\omega_{X}
extends to a Kähler form on
X
. Let
A
be the A’Campo space associated to the function
\widetilde{f}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, with natural smooth maps
\pi\colon A\longrightarrow X
and
\widetilde{f}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\log}
as in Proposition
3.33
, see diagram (
24
).

We note that the pullback
\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}
extends to a
1
-form on
A
, i.e. assumption (
84
) of Setting
5.18
holds. To see this, we give a local description of
\lambda_{X}
, which will be useful in the proof of Proposition
7.4
(d)
, too. Choose a fine chart
U_{X}
(see Definition
4.1
) with associated index set
S
(see (
12
)), and a trivialization of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(bH)|_{U_{X}}
. In this trivialization, we can write our section
s
as
\xi^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}^{bb_{i}}
for some nonvanishing holomorphic function
\xi^{\prime}
. The formula for
||\,\cdot\,||
from
[
Huy05
, Example 4.1.2(i)]
shows that
\log||s||=\sum_{i\in S}bb_{i}\log r_{i}+\xi
for some
\xi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{X})
. Thus
\lambda_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{U_{X}}=d^{c}\log||s||=-\sum_{i\in S}bb_{i}d\theta_{i}+d^{c}\xi
, see Example
2.1
. Since
\widetilde{\lambda}_{Y}\in\Omega^{1}(X)
, we get

(116)

\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}|_{U_{X}}=-\sum_{i\in S}t_{0}bb_{i}\,d\theta_{i}+\pi^{*}\beta\quad\mbox{for some }\beta\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X}).

Because the functions
\theta_{i}
are coordinates of each chart (
36
) covering
\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
, the formula (
116
) shows that
\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}
extends to a smooth form on
A
, as claimed.

We are now in Setting
5.18
. Let
F=\widetilde{f}_{A}^{-1}(0,1)\cap\pi^{-1}(\overline{W}_{X})
be a radius-zero fiber, and for
\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon>0
let
\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{1}(A)
be the
1
-form defined in (
47
). By Corollary
5.19
, there is an arbitrarily small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that, for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
, the abstract contact open book
(M_{z,t_{0}},\lambda_{t_{0}},\phi_{z,t_{0}})
constructed above is isotopic to

(117)

(F,\lambda,\phi),

where
\lambda=\lambda_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}|_{F}
, and
\phi\colon F\longrightarrow F
is the time-one flow of the monodromy vector field (
48
).
Moreover, this isotopy endows (
117
) with a grading. We conclude that the natural monodromy abstract contact open book (
109
) is graded isotopic to the radius-zero abstract contact open book (
117
). The properties of the latter are listed in Proposition
7.4
below.

7.3.
Dynamics at radius zero

In Section
7.2
above, we have constructed an isotopy joining the natural monodromy abstract contact open book (
109
) with an abstract contact open book
(F,\lambda,\phi)
defined after the formula (
117
). Dynamical properties of
\phi
are summarized in the Proposition
7.4
, which is a key step in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. We will state it using notation from Sections
7.1
and
7.2
, which we now briefly recall.

The fiber
F
in (
117
) is the intersection of the radius-zero fiber
\widetilde{f}_{A}^{-1}(0,1)
with the preimage
\overline{W}
of the sub-level set
\overline{W}_{Y}
of a strictly plurisubharmonic function
\varrho\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
chosen in the beginning of Section
7.1
. This sub-level set should be thought of as a compact
“ball”
around the singularities of
f^{-1}(0)
and
Y
, not necessarily a small one. The subset
A_{i}^{\circ}\subseteq\partial A
is a piece of the
“radius-zero tube”

\partial A
lying over
D_{i}\setminus(D-D_{i})
, see the formula (
25
). The manifold
B_{i}^{\circ}
was introduced in Section
7.1
as follows: if
D_{i}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}h
then
\nu_{i}\colon B_{i}^{\circ}\longrightarrow D_{i}\setminus(D-D_{i})
is the
m_{i}
-fold covering described in
[
DL02
, §2.3]
; and if
D_{i}
is a proper transform of a component
C
of
f^{-1}(0)
then
B_{i}^{\circ}=\overline{W}_{Y}\cap C\setminus\operatorname{Bs}h^{-1}
, which should be thought of as the smooth part of
C
intersected with the closed
“ball”

\overline{W}_{Y}
. The integer
a_{i}
is the discrepancy of
D_{i}
, see (
113
) and
b_{i}
is the coefficient of
D_{i}
in the chosen ample divisor
H
, see (
112
): in particular,
b_{i}<0
if
D_{i}\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}h
and
b_{i}=0
otherwise. The integer
b>0
is chosen so that
bH
is very ample; and
t_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
are arbitrarily small positive numbers chosen in Section
7.2
in such a way that
\varepsilon_{0}
can be taken arbitrarily small compared to
t_{0}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
(F,\lambda,\phi)
be the graded abstract contact open book (
117
). Then

(118)

\operatorname{Fix}\phi^{m}=\bigsqcup_{m_{i}|m}B_{i},\quad\mbox{where}\quad B_{i}=\overline{A_{i}^{\circ}}\cap F.

Moreover, for each
i
such that
m_{i}|m
, every connected component of
B_{i}
is a codimension zero family of fixed points of
\phi^{m}
, see Definition
5.25
. Write
\partial B_{i}=\partial^{F}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{+}B_{i}\sqcup\partial^{-}B_{i}
, where
\partial^{F}B_{i}=F\cap\partial B_{i}
and
\partial^{\pm}B_{i}
is the disjoint union of
\partial^{\pm}B_{i}^{\prime}
for all connected components
B_{i}^{\prime}
of
B_{i}
, see formula (
92
). Then the following holds.

(a)

We have
\partial^{-}B_{i}=\emptyset
and
\partial^{F}B_{i}=B_{i}\cap\partial\overline{W}
.

(b)

There is a diffeomorphism
\kappa_{i}\colon B_{i}\setminus\partial^{+}B_{i}\longrightarrow B_{i}^{\circ}
such that
\nu_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}=\pi|_{F}
.

(c)

The Conley–Zehnder index of each point in
B_{i}
equals
\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})=2\frac{m}{m_{i}}(a_{i}+1)-2m
.

(d)

We can choose the action function
{a}\colon F\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, see (
68
), so that
{a}|_{B_{i}}=(t_{0}b\cdot b_{i}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}
.

Recall that
\phi^{m}\colon F\longrightarrow F
is the time
m
flow of the symplectic lift of the unit angular vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{1}=\partial\mathbb{C}_{\log}
. Fix a fine chart
U_{X}\subseteq X
, i.e. one satisfying the statement of Lemma
3.30
, let
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
, and let
S
be the associated index set (
12
). In local coordinates (
36
) on
U
, this lift is given by the formula (
48
). Therefore,
\phi^{m}|_{U}
is given by

(119)

\phi^{m}\colon\theta_{j}\mapsto\theta_{j}+m\ell_{j},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\ell_{j}=\frac{\zeta(u_{j})}{\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\zeta(u_{i})}

In other words,
\phi
is a rotation by an angle
2\pi m\ell_{j}
about the
j
-th axis, see Notation
3.2
. Note that by Lemma
3.6
(e)
, the values of
\ell_{j}
do not depend on the choice of the chart.

To prove the equality (
118
), we use the
m
-separatedness assumption (
111
), cf.
[
A’C75
, p. 242]
.

First, we claim that
\operatorname{Fix}\phi^{m}\subseteq\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}}B_{i}
. To prove this, take
x\in F
such that
x\not\in\overline{A}_{i}^{\circ}
for any
i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}
. Put
I=\{i\in S:w_{i}(x)>0\}
. By Proposition
3.7
(c)
,
x\in\overline{A}_{I}^{\circ}
, so
\#I\geqslant 2
by assumption. Choose
j\in I
such that
w_{i}(x)\geqslant w_{j}(x)
for all
i\in I
.
Since both functions
\eta
and
\zeta
are increasing, we infer that
\zeta(u_{i}(x))\geqslant\zeta(u_{j}(x))>0
for all
i\in I
, so
\frac{\zeta(u_{i}(x))}{\zeta(u_{j}(x))}\geqslant 1
if
i\in I
and
\frac{\zeta(u_{i}(x))}{\zeta(u_{j}(x))}=0
if
i\not\in I
. Hence

0<m\ell_{j}(x)=m\left(\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\frac{\zeta(u_{i}(x))}{\zeta(u_{j}(x))}\right)^{-1}\leqslant m\left(\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}\right)^{-1}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{\eqref{eq:m-separatedness}}}}{<}1,

In particular,
m\ell_{j}(x)\not\in\mathbb{Z}
. Formula (
119
) shows that
x\not\in\operatorname{Fix}\phi^{m}
, as claimed.

Therefore,
\operatorname{Fix}\phi^{m}\subseteq\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}}B_{i}
. Take
x\in B_{i}
. By Proposition
3.7
(c)
we have
w_{i}(x)=1
and
w_{j}(x)=0
for all
j\neq i
. Thus
m\ell_{i}=\tfrac{m}{m_{i}}
and
\ell_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
, so formula (
119
) shows that
x\in\operatorname{Fix}\phi^{m}
if and only if
m_{i}|m
. This ends the proof of (
118
).

Now, we claim that each
B_{i}
is a codimension zero family of fixed points, see Definition
5.25
. Say that
i=1
. First, we claim that
B_{1}\subseteq F
is a codimension zero submanifold with corners.

Fix a point
x\in B_{1}
, let
U_{X}
be a fine chart around
\pi(x)
, let
S
be its index set (
12
), and let
U=\pi^{-1}(U_{X})
. Recall that by Lemma
3.6
(e)
, we have
\overline{u}_{j}|_{U\cap F}=u_{j}|_{U\cap F}
for
j\in S
, where
\overline{u}_{j}
are global functions introduced in (
23
), and
u_{j}
are the local ones, introduced in (
18
). By Proposition
3.7
(c)
, we have
\overline{A}_{1}^{\circ}\cap U=\{u_{1}=1\}
. In particular,
\overline{A}_{1}^{\circ}\cap U
is contained in the open subset
U_{1}\subseteq U
introduced in (
28
). There, we have a smooth coordinate system
(g,(\overline{v}_{i})_{i\in S\setminus\{1\}},\vartheta)
, see (
36
), where
U_{1}\cap\partial A=\{g=0\}
.

By Lemma
3.6
(c)
, we have
\overline{A}_{1}^{\circ}\cap U=\{u_{1}=1\}=\{u_{i}=0:i\in S\setminus\{1\}\}
. For
x\in U\cap\partial A
, we have
u_{i}(x)=0
if and only if
\overline{v}_{i}(x)\geqslant 0
. Indeed, if
u_{i}(x)=0
then by Lemma
3.6
(d)

u_{i}^{p}(x)=0
for every
p\in R
such that
\tau^{p}(x)\neq 0
, which by (
23
) implies that
\overline{v}_{i}(x)\geqslant 0
. Conversely, if
\overline{v}_{i}(x)\geqslant 0
then
v_{i}^{p}(x)\geqslant 0
for some
p\in R
, which by Lemma
3.16
(b)
implies that
u_{i}^{p}(x)=0
, so again by Lemma
3.6
(d)
we get
u_{i}(x)=0
. We conclude that the subset
\overline{A}_{1}^{\circ}\cap U_{1}\subseteq\partial A\cap U_{1}
is given by the inequalities
\{\overline{v}_{i}\geqslant 0:i\in S\setminus\{1\}\}
, see Figure
2(b)
. The fiber
F\cap U_{1}
is given in
\partial A\cap U_{1}
by
\sum_{i\in S}m_{i}\theta_{i}=0
. Therefore,
B_{1}\subseteq F
is a codimension zero submanifold with corners, as claimed.

Now, according to Definition
5.25
, we need to find a neighborhood
N_{1}
of
B_{1}
, and a time independent Hamiltonian
H_{1}\colon N_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi|_{N_{1}}=\psi_{1}^{H_{1}}
. Define
N_{1}\subseteq F
as the union of all the sets
U_{1}\cap F
, where
U_{1}
is the set (
28
) defined for an adapted chart
U_{X}
, and
U_{X}
ranges through all fine charts meeting
D_{1}
. We have seen that
N_{1}
is a neighborhood of
B_{1}
in
F
. We claim that

H_{1}\coloneqq\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}\varepsilon_{0}(\overline{v}_{1}+1)|_{N_{1}}\colon N_{1}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)

is the required time-independent Hamiltonian.

To prove this, once again we restrict to a chart
U_{1}
with coordinates (
36
). There, the monodromy
\phi^{m}
is the time one-flow of a vector field
mX_{\theta}
, where
X_{\theta}
is the symplectic lift of
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
, given by the formula (
48
). Recall that
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}
is the coordinate vector field of the chart (
36
). Put

X_{1}\coloneqq mX_{\theta}-\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}.

We claim that
X_{1}
is the Hamiltonian vector field
X^{H_{1}}
. Since
\theta_{*}(X_{\theta})=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
by definition of
X_{\theta}
, we have
\theta_{*}(X_{1})=0
, so
X_{1}
is tangent to the fiber
F
. Because
X_{\theta}
is orthogonal to
F
, in the tangent space to
F
we have

\omega_{A}^{\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}(X_{1},\,\cdot\,)=\omega_{A}^{\varepsilon_{0}}(-\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}},\,\cdot\,)=\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}\varepsilon_{0}d\overline{v}_{1}=dH_{1},

where the second equality holds by Lemma
4.14
(f)
. Thus
X_{1}=X^{H_{1}}
, as claimed.

To verify the conditions of Definition
5.25
, we need to check that
\phi^{m}|_{N_{1}}=\psi^{H_{1}}_{1}
. Recall that
\phi^{m}
is the time one flow of
mX_{\theta}
, so it differs from
\psi^{H_{1}}_{1}
by a translation by
\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}
in the
\theta_{1}
-direction. Since
\tfrac{m}{m_{1}}\in\mathbb{Z}
, these time one flows are equal, as needed.

It remains to verify the properties
(a)
–
(d)
. Part
(a)
is clear since the Hamiltonian
H_{i}
defined above for each codimension zero family
B_{i}
satisfies
H_{i}=\frac{m}{m_{1}}\varepsilon_{0}(\overline{v}_{i}+1)\geqslant 0
and
H_{i}|_{B_{i}}\equiv 0
.

(b)

Locally on
A_{i}^{\circ}
, the fiber
F
is given by
\{m_{i}\theta_{i}=0\}
, and the restriction of
\pi
collapses the angular coordinate
\theta_{i}
. This yields the required covering, cf. Remark
4.6
.

(c)
Say
i=1
. Recall that the Conley–Zehnder index is constant in a codimension zero family of fixed points, so it is enough to compute it for a single point
x\in F\cap A_{1}^{\circ}
. Choose a fine a chart
U_{X}\subseteq X
around
\pi(x)
whose associated index set is
\{1\}
, i.e.
U_{X}
does not meet
D\setminus D_{1}
. The smooth coordinates (
36
) on
U\cap\partial A
are
(\theta_{1},z_{i_{2}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
: we have skipped
g
since it cuts out
\partial A
. We will compute
\operatorname{CZ}(x)
using
[
McL19
, Lemma A.8]
. To do this, recall that the grading of
(F,\lambda,\phi)
is induced by isotopy from the one on
(M_{z,t_{0}},\lambda_{z},\phi_{z,t_{0}})
. The latter is given by a section
s_{\mathrm{vert}}
of the relative canonical bundle
K_{\mathrm{vert}}
, such that
s_{\mathrm{vert}}\otimes d\widetilde{f}
maps under (
83
) to the section
s_{K}
of
K_{X\setminus D}
chosen in Section
5.5.3
. In this case,
s_{K}
is simply the pullback of the holomorphic volume form on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, see Example
5.22
.

Since
s_{K}
has zero of order
a_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
along
D_{1}
, we can choose the coordinates
(z_{i_{2}},\dots,z_{i_{n}})
of our adapted chart
U_{X}
so that
s_{K}|_{U_{X}}=z_{1}^{a_{1}}\,dz_{1}\wedge dz_{i_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge dz_{i_{n}}
. We have
df|_{U_{X}}=m_{1}z_{1}^{m_{1}-1}\,dz_{1}
, so
s_{\mathrm{vert}}|_{U_{X}}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}z_{1}^{a_{1}-m_{1}+1}\,dz_{i_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge dz_{i_{n}}
. By (
119
), the symplectomorphism
\phi^{m}
is given by
\theta_{1}\mapsto\theta_{1}+m\ell_{1}
, where
\ell_{1}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}
. It follows that the winding number used in
[
McL19
, Lemma A.8]
equals
-(a_{1}-m_{1}+1)\cdot\frac{m}{m_{1}}=-\frac{m}{m_{1}}(a_{1}+1)+m
, so
\operatorname{CZ}(x)=2\frac{m}{m_{1}}(a_{1}+1)-2m
, as needed.

(d)
For every
i\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}
, choose a point
x_{i}\in A_{i}^{\circ}\cap F
. Recall that the function
{a}
is defined up to an additive constant, and is constant in each
B_{i}
. Therefore, to prove
(d)
it is sufficient to show that for every
i,j\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{E}
we have

{a}(x_{j})-{a}(x_{i})=(t_{0}bb_{j}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{j}}-(t_{0}bb_{i}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}.

By definition,
{a}
satisfies
(\phi^{m})^{*}\lambda-\lambda=-d{a}
. Therefore, it is enough to prove that for some path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow F
such that
\gamma(0)=x_{i}
,
\gamma(1)=x_{j}
, we have

(120)

\int_{\gamma}(\phi^{m})^{*}\lambda-\lambda=-(t_{0}bb_{j}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{j}}+(t_{0}bb_{i}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}.

We can choose
\gamma
in such a way that it avoids triple intersections, i.e.
\gamma([0,1])\cap\bigsqcup_{\#I\geqslant 3}A_{I}^{\circ}=\emptyset
. Then
\gamma
can be split into segments with images lying over adapted charts whose associated index sets (
12
) have at most two elements. Thus considering each piece of
\gamma
separately, it is sufficient to prove (
120
) for
\gamma\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow U
, where
U
is the preimage of an adapted chart
U_{X}
with index set
S\coloneqq\{i,j\}
.

By the formula (
116
), we have
\pi^{*}\lambda_{X}|_{U_{X}}=-\sum_{l\in S}t_{0}bb_{l}\,d\theta_{l}+\pi^{*}\beta^{\prime}
for some
\beta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X})
. Using Lemma
4.5
and
4.14
(b)
, we conclude that
\lambda|_{U\cap F}=\sum_{l\in S}(-t_{0}bb_{l}+\varepsilon_{0}\overline{v}_{l})\,d\theta_{l}+\pi^{*}\beta
for some
\beta\in\Omega^{1}(U_{X})
. The formula (
119
) shows that
\pi|_{F}\circ\phi=\pi|_{F}
, so
(\phi^{m})^{*}(\pi^{*}\beta)-(\pi^{*}\beta)=0
. Now by (
119
) we have

(121)

\int_{\gamma}(\phi^{m})^{*}\lambda-\lambda=\int_{\gamma}\textstyle\sum_{l\in S}(-t_{0}bb_{l}+\varepsilon_{0}\overline{v}_{l})m\,d\ell_{l}.

If
S=\{i\}
then the image of
\gamma
is contained in
A_{i}^{\circ}
, where by (
119
) the function
\ell_{i}
is constant. Hence the above integral is zero, which proves (
120
) in this case.

Assume that
S=\{i,j\}
for
i\neq j
. We claim that, along
\gamma
, we have the equality

(122)

\overline{v}_{i}d\ell_{i}+\overline{v}_{j}d\ell_{j}=d(\overline{v}_{i}\ell_{i}+\overline{v}_{j}\ell_{j}).

Inside
A_{i}^{\circ}
, the functions
\overline{v}_{i}
,
\ell_{i}
are constant, and
\ell_{j}=0
, so
\overline{v}_{j}\ell_{j}=0
is constant, too. Hence both forms are zero in
A_{i}^{\circ}
, and by symmetry the same holds in
A_{j}^{\circ}
. It remains to prove the equality (
122
) in
A^{\circ}_{i,j}
.

Fix
l\in\{i,j\}
. By Lemma
3.6
(e)
, in
A_{i,j}^{\circ}
we have
\overline{v}_{l}=-u_{l}
. Recall that, by definition (
18
) of
u_{l}
we have
w_{l}=\eta^{-1}(u_{l})
, so
dw_{l}=\zeta(u_{l})\,du_{l}
by definition of
\zeta
. We conclude that
\zeta(u_{l})d\overline{v}_{l}=-\zeta(u_{l})du_{l}=-dw_{l}
. By Lemma
3.6
(c)
we have
w_{i}+w_{j}=1
, so
\zeta(u_{i})d\overline{v}_{i}+\zeta(u_{j})d\overline{v}_{j}=-dw_{i}-dw_{j}=0
. Hence
\ell_{i}d\overline{v}_{i}+\ell_{j}d\overline{v}_{j}=0
by definition (
119
) of
\ell_{i}
,
\ell_{j}
. This proves (
122
).

Now, the equality (
122
) implies that the integral (
121
) is equal to the difference of values of
\sum_{l\in S}(-t_{0}bb_{l}+\varepsilon_{0}\overline{v}_{l})m\ell_{l}
at the endpoints
x_{j}
,
x_{i}
of
\gamma
. Since
x_{j}\in A_{j}^{\circ}
, we have
\ell_{i}(x_{j})=0
, and similarly
\ell_{j}(x_{i})=0
. Moreover,
\overline{v}_{j}(x_{j})=\overline{v}_{i}(x_{i})=-1
and
\ell_{j}(x_{j})=\frac{1}{m_{j}}
,
\ell_{i}(x_{i})=\frac{1}{m_{i}}
. Thus

\int_{\gamma}(\phi^{m})^{*}\lambda-\lambda=(-t_{0}bb_{j}-\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}-(-t_{0}bb_{i}-\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}

as claimed in the formula (
120
).

∎

The monodromy abstract contact open book (
109
) is graded isotopic to (
117
). Hence by Proposition
6.2
, their fixed point Floer homology groups are the same, i.e.
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{z},+)=\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
. By Proposition
7.4
,
\phi
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
6.3
, so we get a spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi,+)
, whose first page
E_{p,q}^{1}
is given by (
102
).

By Proposition
7.4
(d)
, we can choose the action function
{a}
for
\phi
in such a way that
{a}|_{B_{i}}=(t_{0}bb_{i}+\varepsilon_{0})\frac{m}{m_{i}}
for every
i\in S_{m}
. Moreover, the number
\varepsilon_{0}>0
can be chosen arbitrarily small compared to
t_{0}b>0
. Therefore, we can choose the indexing function
\iota\colon S_{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
in Proposition
6.3
to be
\iota(i)=\frac{m}{m_{i}}b_{i}
. Hence the indexing set
\{i:\iota(i)=p\}
in (
102
) is the set
S_{m,p}
defined in (
114
). Substituting to (
102
) the formula
\operatorname{CZ}(B_{i})=2\frac{m}{m_{i}}(a_{i}+1)-2m
from Proposition
7.4
(c)
, we get

E_{p,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{i\in S_{m,p}}H_{n-1+p+q+2\frac{m}{m_{i}}(a_{i}+1)-2m}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

By definition,
B_{i}
is compact, and by Proposition
7.4
(b)
, the manifold
B_{i}\setminus\partial^{+}B_{i}
is diffeomorphic to
B_{i}^{\circ}
. Thus
H_{*}(B_{i},\partial^{+}B_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=H_{*}^{BM}(B_{i}^{\circ})
, as required by the formula (
115
).

∎

Remarks
7.5
,
7.6
and
7.7
below compare our results with the ones of
[
McL19
]
.

Remark 7.5
.

Assume that
Y=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
h\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
is an
m
-separating resolution of an isolated singularity
0\in f^{-1}(0)
, and
W_{Y}=\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
is a Milnor ball. Then, Proposition
7.4
shows that the radius zero monodromy satisfies almost the same dynamical properties as McLean’s
model resolution
, see
[
McL19
, Theorem 5.41]
.

The key difference is that our monodromy has codimension zero family of fixed points corresponding to the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
. If
W_{Y}
is a Milnor ball, they can be avoided by a small perturbation near the boundary, as done in loc. cit. However, to prove Theorem
1.1
we will need to apply Proposition
7.4
in more generality (namely, keeping the radius of the ball fixed within the family, see Example
5.23
).

Note that Proposition
7.4
is much more general: for example, it holds for an embedded resolution of
f
with multiple isolated singularities in
f^{-1}(0)
. Moreover, with minor modifications one can formulate a result analogous to Proposition
7.4
(a)
,
(b)
for a degeneration of projective varieties. In this setting, the Conley–Zehnder index computation in Proposition
7.4
(c)
can be carried out, too, provided
\phi
admits a grading: this happens when fibers are Calabi–Yau.

Eventually, we note that the abstract contact open book in Proposition
7.4
is actually isotopic to the symplectic monodromy of
f
(defined in a natural way in Example
5.20
). Meanwhile, the model resolution constructed in
[
McL19
, Example 5.14]
just shares the same associated graded contact pair, which in this case is
(\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon},\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\cap f^{-1}(0))
.

Remark 7.6
.

The formula for Conley–Zehnder index in Proposition
7.4
(c)
differs from the one in
[
McL19
, Theorem 5.41(3)]
by
2m
.

This shift most likely arises due to a mistake in the computation carried out in
[
McL19
, p. 1025]
. Let us indicate a possible source of this mistake. The Conley–Zehnder index is computed in loc. cit. from the winding number associated to a trivialization of the vertical canonical bundle
K_{\mathrm{vert}}
, using Lemma A.8 loc. cit. At the bottom of p. 1025 loc. cit., it is claimed that this winding number is the same as the one for the trivialization of
K_{X\setminus D}
. To be more precise, loc. cit. uses an analogue of canonical bundles on the model resolutions, but these two coincide locally, when
X
is a chart adapted to
f
.

The claimed equality is false. To see this, take
X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
with coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2})
, let
f(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}
, and let
dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}
be a trivialization of
K_{X\setminus D}
. As in the formula (
83
), write
K_{X\setminus D}=K_{\mathrm{vert}}\otimes f^{*}K_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}
. The trivialization of
f^{*}K_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}
is given by
df=dz_{1}
, so the compatible trivialization of
K_{\mathrm{vert}}
is
dz_{2}
. To compute the winding number, as in the proof Proposition
7.4
(c)
, we pull back these forms by
\Phi_{m\theta}^{-1}\colon(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(e^{-2\pi\imath\cdot m\theta}z_{1},z_{2})
, and trace the path in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
made by the leading coefficient. For
K_{X\setminus D}
, the pullback is
e^{-2\pi\imath\cdot m\theta}dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}
, so the winding number is
-m
. In turn, for
K_{\mathrm{vert}}
, the pullback is
dz_{2}
, so the winding number is
0
. Hence
[
McL19
, Lemma A.8]
shows that to get the correct Conley–Zehnder index, one needs to subtract
2m
from the one computed in loc. cit.

Remark 7.7
.

Let
Y=\mathbb{C}^{n}
, let
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function with an isolated critical point
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and let
\overline{U}=\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
,
\overline{W}=\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
be Milnor balls. Then Proposition
7.1
provides a spectral sequence converging to Floer homology of the symplectic monodromy of the Milnor fibration (
3
) in the tube, introduced in Example
5.22
.

In this case, the term of (
115
) corresponding to the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
vanishes. To see this, note that
\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}}B_{i}^{\circ}=f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{*}
. By the conical structure theorem
[
Mil68
, 2.10]
,
f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
is a cone over
f^{-1}(0)\cap\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
, so
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{i}^{\circ})=0
for every
i\in\mathcal{P}
.

Therefore, in this case the first page (
115
) is similar to the one in
[
McL19
, Theorem 1.2]
, cf. Remark
6.5
. The spectral sequence in loc. cit. converges to the Floer cohomology of the symplectic monodromy of the Milnor fibration in the sphere, i.e.
f/|f|\colon\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\setminus f^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Remark 7.8
.

The exact values of the action and Conley Zehnder indices, computed in Proposition
7.4
(d)
,
(c)
were used to write a precise formula for the degree shift in (
115
). However, this formula will not be used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, where we will only be interested in vanishing of
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{m},+)
.

To conclude this section, we note the following consequence of Proposition
7.4
. It is not needed in the sequel, but might become of independent interest.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
f\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},0)
be a singular hypersurface germ. Let
(N,\lambda_{\mathrm{std}},\phi_{\mathrm{std}})
be the abstract contact open book associated to the Milnor fibration of
f
in the tube
f^{-1}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{\delta})\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
, constructed in Example
5.22
. Then
(N,\lambda_{\mathrm{std}},\phi_{\mathrm{std}})
is isotopic to abstract contact open books
(F,\lambda,\phi_{1})
and
(F,\lambda,\phi_{2})
such that
\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{1}=\emptyset
and
\operatorname{Fix}\phi_{2}=\partial F
.

Choose a resolution
h\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
of
f
which is an isomorphism away from the origin and factors through a blowup of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
at
0
. Since the germ
f
is singular, all components of
\operatorname{Exc}h
have multiplicity at least
2
. In particular,
h
is
1
-separated, i.e. satisfies condition (
111
) for
m=1
.
Let
(F,\lambda,\phi)
be the radius-zero abstract contact open book (
117
) associated to this resolution. Since
(N,\lambda_{\mathrm{std}},\phi_{\mathrm{std}})
is isotopic to
(F,\lambda,\phi)
, it is enough to prove the claim for
(F,\lambda,\phi)
.

Put
B_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}}B_{i}
, where
B_{i}=\overline{A}_{i}^{\circ}\cap F
is as in formula (
118
), and the sum runs over all components of the proper transform of
f^{-1}(0)
. Applying Proposition
7.4
for
m=1
, we get
\operatorname{Fix}\phi=B_{0}
and
\partial B_{0}=\partial^{+}B_{0}\sqcup\partial F
, where
\partial^{+}B_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}}\partial^{+}B_{i}
. Applying an isotopy from Lemma
5.27
, we can assume that
B_{0}
is a smooth codimension zero manifold with boundary (and no corners).

The above isotopy does not change the diffeomorphism type of
B_{0}^{\circ}\coloneqq B_{0}\setminus\partial^{+}B_{0}
. Thus
B_{0}^{\circ}\cong\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}}X_{i}^{\circ}\cong(f^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}
. By the conical structure theorem
[
Mil68
, Theorem 2.10]
, the latter is diffeomorphic to
L\times[-1,0)
, where
L\coloneqq f^{-1}(0)\cap\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
is the link of
f
. Combining the above diffeomorphisms with some trivialization of a collar neighborhood of
\partial^{+}B_{0}
, we conclude that there is an open neighborhood
U
of
B_{0}
and a diffeomorphism
U\longrightarrow L\times[-1,\eta)
for some
\eta>0
.

Now, we argue like in
[
McL19
, p. 1023]
. By Definition
5.25
, the condition
\partial B_{0}=\partial^{+}B_{0}\sqcup\partial F
means that, after shrinking
U
if needed, the restriction
\phi|_{U}
is the time one flow of some Hamiltonian
v\colon U\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
satisfying
v^{-1}(0)=B_{0}
. Shrinking
U
further we can assume that
v
extends to a smooth function on a compact closure
\overline{U}
, such that
dv
vanishes precisely on
B_{0}
.

Using the above diffeomorphism
L\times[-1,\eta)\longrightarrow U
, we can define a Hamiltonian
H\colon F\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
which is constant on
F\setminus U
, has no critical points in
U
, and grows compatibly with
v
on
U\setminus B_{0}
, see Definition
6.8
. Put
\check{\phi}_{\tau}\coloneqq\psi^{H}_{\tau}\circ\phi
, so
\check{\phi}_{\tau}
is isotopic to
\phi
. We claim that
\check{\phi}_{\tau}
has no fixed points for small
\tau>0
.

Since
H
is constant on
F\setminus U
, we have
\psi^{H}_{\tau}|_{F\setminus U}=\mathrm{id}_{F\setminus U}
and thus
\check{\phi}_{\tau}
has no fixed points in
F\setminus U
. Since
H
has no critical points in
U
, there is a time
\tau_{0}>0
such that
\psi^{H}_{\tau}
has no fixed points in
U
for all
\tau\in(0,2\tau_{0}]
. In particular, since
\phi|_{B_{0}}=\mathrm{id}_{B_{0}}
, we see that
\check{\phi}_{\tau}
has no fixed points in
B_{0}
. On
U\setminus B_{0}
, by Definition
6.8
the level sets of
H
and
v
agree, and there is a positive function
\sigma
, constant on those level sets, such that
dv=\sigma\cdot dH
, so
\check{\phi}_{\tau}=\psi^{H}_{\tau}\circ\psi_{1}^{v}=\psi_{\tau+\sigma}^{H}
. Since
dv
vanishes on
B_{0}
and
dH
does not, there is an open neighborhood
V
of
B_{0}
in
U
such that
\sigma|_{V}<\tau_{0}
, so
\check{\phi}_{\tau}
has no fixed points in
V
for all
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
. Similarly, since
dv
does not vanish on the compact set
\overline{U}\setminus V
, the function
\sigma^{-1}
is bounded on
U\setminus V
, so since
\psi_{1}^{v}=\phi
has no fixed points on
U\setminus V
, neither does
\check{\phi}_{\tau}=\psi_{\tau}^{H}\circ\psi_{1}^{v}=\psi^{v}_{\tau\sigma^{-1}+1}
for small enough
\tau>0
; as claimed.

This way, we get the required isotopy
(F,\lambda,\phi)\sim(F,\lambda,\phi_{1})
. To get
\phi_{2}
, we replace
H
by a composition
\rho\circ H
for some smooth, increasing function
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\rho^{\prime}
vanishes precisely at
H(\partial F)
.

∎

8.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we apply the techniques developed above to prove Theorem
1.1
, which asserts that any
\mu
-constant family of isolated hypersurface singularities is equimultiple.

Let us recall the definition of a
\mu
-constant family. Consider a family of power series
f=\sum_{\iota\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}a_{\iota}z^{\iota}
, where
a_{\iota}\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
are continuous functions. For
t\in[0,1]
we write
f_{t}=\sum_{\iota\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}a_{\iota}(t)z^{\iota}\in\mathbb{C}[\![z_{1},\dots,z_{n}]\!]
, and let
\mu(f_{t})
be the Milnor number of
f_{t}
. We say that
f
is a
\mu
-constant family
if all numbers
\mu(f_{t})
are finite and equal to each other. We say that
f
is
equimultiple
if the multiplicities
\nu(f_{t})
of
f_{t}
are equal for all
t
.

Note that the assumption
\mu(f_{t})<\infty
, which we include in the definition for convenience, ensures that the singular germ
\{f_{t}=0\}
is isolated.

We begin the proof by recalling a known reduction.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume that every
\mu
-constant family
f
satisfying the conditions
(a)
–
(b)
below, is equimultiple. Then every
\mu
-constant family is equimultiple.

(a)

There is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a_{\iota}\equiv 0
for
|\iota|>N
, i.e. each
f_{t}
is a polynomial of degree at most
N
.

(b)

The functions
a_{\iota}
extend to holomorphic maps
\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}

(a)
Let
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[\![z_{1},...,z_{n}]\!]
. If
\mu(f)
is finite then
f
is
(\mu(f)+1)
-determined
[
GLS07
, I.2.24]
. This means the following: for any
g\in\mathfrak{m}^{\mu(f)+2}
there exists a formal change of variables
\varphi
such that
(f+g){\circ}\varphi=f
; if moreover
f
and
g
are holomorphic then
\varphi
can be chosen to be holomorphic. As a consequence,
\mu(f+g)=\mu(g)
and
\nu(f+g)=\nu(g)
.

Now if
f_{t}:=\sum_{I}a_{I}z^{I}
is a continuous family with constant Milnor number
\mu
, then for
h_{t}:=\sum_{|I|\leqslant\mu+1}a_{I}z^{I}
we have
\mu(f_{t})=\mu(h_{t})
and
\nu(f_{t})=\nu(h_{t})
, so to prove Theorem
1.1
for
(f_{t})
it suffices to show it for
(h_{t})
such that each
h_{t}
is a polynomial.

(b)
Polynomials whose germs at the origin have Milnor numbers precisely
\mu
form a Zariski-locally-closed subset of the space of polynomials of degree
\mu+1
, called the
\mu
-constant stratum. Denote it by
S
. The family
f_{t}
describes a continuous map
\alpha\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow S
. Choose a resolution of singularities
\pi\colon\widetilde{S}\longrightarrow S
, and let
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
be points which are in the same irreducible component of
\widetilde{S}
and such that
\pi(p_{0})=\alpha(0)
and
\pi(p_{1})=\alpha(1)
. Then there exists a continuous path joining
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
. Because
\widetilde{S}
is smooth, there are points
p_{0}=q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{k}=p_{1}
and holomorphic parametrizations
\varphi_{i}:\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\widetilde{S}
such that
q_{i}
and
q_{i+1}
are contained in
\varphi_{i}(\mathbb{D})
for any
i
. The result follows after replacing
\alpha
by each
\varphi_{i}
.

∎

By Lemma
8.1
, we can assume that our
\mu
-constant family of power series defines a family of holomorphic functions, holomorphically parametrized by a disk
\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
. Clearly, it is enough to show that the multiplicity remains constant for
\eta>0
sufficiently small.

In Example
5.23
, we have chosen three Milnor radii for
f_{0}
, say
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, and constructed an isotopy of graded abstract contact open books
(F_{s},\lambda_{s},\phi^{m}_{s})
, parametrized by
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
, such that
F_{s}
is diffeomorphic to
f^{-1}_{s}(\delta)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}
for small
\delta>0
.

By Proposition
6.2
, for each
m\geqslant 1
, the Floer homology groups
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{s}^{m},+)
do not depend on
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
. We will now study the first page (
115
) of the spectral sequence converging to
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{s}^{m},+)
.

Let
\nu(f_{s})
be the multiplicity of
f_{s}
. Fix
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
, a number
m\geqslant 1
, and an
m
-separating resolution
h
of
f_{s}^{-1}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We can assume that
h
factors as
h=h_{1}\circ h_{2}
, where
h_{1}
is a blowup at the origin, and
h_{2}
is a morphism such that the base locus of
h_{2}^{-1}
is contained in the singular locus of
(f_{s}\circ h_{1})^{-1}(0)_{\mathrm{red}}
. Write the irreducible decomposition of
(f_{s}\circ h)^{-1}(0)
as
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}}D_{i}+\nu(f_{s})D_{1}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\{1\}}m_{i}D_{i}
, where
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{P}}D_{i}
is the proper transform of
f_{s}^{-1}(0)
, and
D_{1}
is the proper transform of
\operatorname{Exc}h_{1}
. The assumption on
h
implies that
m_{i}>\nu(f_{s})
for every
i\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\{1\}
. Thus the sets
S_{m}
defined in (
114
) satisfy:

(123)

S_{m}=\mathcal{P}\quad\mbox{if}\quad m<\nu(f_{s})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S_{\nu(f_{s})}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{1\}.

Put
B_{0}^{\circ}=\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{P}}B_{i}^{\circ}
. We claim that

(124)

H_{*}^{BM}(B_{0}^{\circ})=0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad H_{*}^{BM}(B_{1}^{\circ})\neq 0,

i.e. the contribution of the proper transform of
f_{s}^{-1}(0)
(respectively, of
\operatorname{Exc}h_{1}
) to the first page (
115
) is zero (respectively, nonzero).

Recall that
B_{0}^{\circ}\cong f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{*}
, where
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is a Milnor radius for
f_{0}
chosen in Example
5.23
. Let
\varepsilon_{s}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
be a Milnor radius for
f_{s}
. By the Conical Structure Theorem
[
Mil68
, Theorem 2.10]

we have that
f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
is homeomorphic to the cone over
f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
. Therefore,

H^{BM}_{*}(B_{0}^{\circ})=H^{BM}_{*}(f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{*})=H_{*}(\overline{f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}},f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\partial\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}})=0,

because the cobordism
\overline{f^{-1}_{s}(0)\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}}
is homologically trivial by Proposition
5.24
. This proves the first claim in (
124
). Notice that when
s=0
, the above cobordism does not appear, so the vanishing of
H_{*}^{BM}(B_{0}^{\circ})
reduces to Remark
7.7
.

For the second claim in (
124
), recall that
B_{1}^{\circ}
is a covering of
D_{1}\setminus(D-D_{1})
, which is a manifold without boundary, so its Borel–Moore homology cannot vanish.

Now, the formulas (
123
) and (
124
) imply that the first page (
115
) is zero for
m<\nu(f_{s})
, and for
m=\nu(f_{s})
, it has exactly one nonzero column. We conclude that

(125)

\nu(f_{s})=\min\{m\geqslant 1:\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{m}_{s},+)\neq 0\},

which is independent of
s\in\mathbb{D}_{\eta}
since
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi^{m}_{s},+)
is independent of
s
.

∎

Remark 8.2

(cf.
[
BFdB+22
, Proposition 1.6]
)
.

Let
F_{s}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneqq\{\operatorname{in}(f_{s})=1\}
be the Milnor fiber of the tangent cone to
f_{s}
. As we will explain in more detail below, the proof of Theorem
1.1
shows that

(126)

\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{s}^{\nu},+)=H_{*+3n-1-2\nu}^{BM}(F_{s}^{\mathrm{in}}),

where
\nu
is the multiplicity of
f_{s}
. By Theorem
1.1
, the number
\nu
does not depend on
s
, so by Proposition
6.2
, the group
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{s}^{\nu},+)
does not depend on
s
, either. Thus the formula (
126
) implies that

H_{*}^{BM}(F_{s}^{\mathrm{in}})=H_{*}^{BM}(F_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}).

The above equality can be seen as an evidence for
[
BFdB+22
, Conjecture 1.7]
, which asks whether the homotopy type of the Milnor fiber of the tangent cone is a topological invariant. Recall, however, that by
[
FdB05
, Theorem A(ii)]
, the projectivized tangent cones
\{\operatorname{in}(f_{s})=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
may not be homotopically equivalent.

To prove the equality (
126
), recall that for
m=\nu
, the first page (
115
) has only one nonzero column, which corresponds to the unique component of
(h\circ f_{s})^{-1}(0)
of multiplicity
\nu
. This component is (the proper transform of) the exceptional divisor of the first blowup over
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Call this exceptional divisor
D_{1}
, and define
D_{1}^{\circ}
as
D_{1}
minus the proper transform of
f_{s}^{-1}(0)
. Then, we have
\operatorname{HF}_{*}(\phi_{s}^{\nu},+)=H_{*+n+1+2a_{1}-2\nu}^{BM}(B_{1}^{\circ})
, where
B_{1}^{\circ}\longrightarrow D_{1}^{\circ}
is a
\nu
-fold covering constructed in
[
DL02
, §2.3]
, see Section
7.1
. We have
D_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, so by adjunction, the discrepancy
a_{1}
equals
n-1
. Moreover,
D_{1}^{\circ}=\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\setminus\{\mathrm{in}(f_{s})=0\}
, and the covering
B_{1}^{\circ}\longrightarrow D_{1}^{\circ}
can be realized as the restriction of the quotient morphism
\mathbb{C}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
to
F_{s}^{\mathrm{in}}
; so
B_{1}^{\circ}=F_{s}^{\mathrm{in}}
, which proves (
126
).

Remark 8.3

(cf. Remark
7.7
)
.

When
s=0
